Local existence theory for a class of CMC gauges for the Einstein-non-linear scalar field equations
Hans Ringström

Abstract
. The purpose of this article is to develop a local existence theory for a class of CMC gauges for the Einstein-non-linear scalar field equations. We do so in the context of closed and parallelisable initial manifolds. The assumption that the initial manifold is parallelisable is not a topological restriction in the case of closed oriented 3 3 -manifolds, but it is a restriction in higher dimensions. The results include local existence, uniqueness, a continuation criterion and Cauchy stability. Previous results concerning related gauges have been restricted to the case of 𝕋 n \mathbb{T}^{n} spatial topology. Moreover, they have not covered the Einstein-non-linear scalar field setting. The main motivation for developing this theory is that it forms the basis for a family of past global non-linear stability results we derive in a separate article.



1.
Introduction

In a sequence of works,
[
24
,
25
,
26
,
28
]
, Rodnianski and Speck use what they refer to as CMC transported spatial coordinates
to prove stable big bang formation. They consider the Einstein-scalar field, stiff fluid and higher dimensional vacuum equations. With the
exception of
[
28
]
, treating the case of
\mathbb{S}^{3}
-spatial topology, they assume
\mathbb{T}^{n}
-spatial topology. The articles
[
25
,
28
]

are concerned with with the stability of spatially homogeneous solutions, in the
3+1
-dimensional setting, that are close to isotropy. However,

[
26
]
concerns the stability of Kasner solutions in higher dimensions; i.e., spatially homogeneous vacuum solutions with spatial topology

\mathbb{T}^{n}
that are, by necessity, anisotropic. Finally,
[
10
]
, written in collaboration with Fournodavlos, demonstrates stability of spatially
homogeneous solutions with
\mathbb{T}^{n}
-spatial topology for the maximal range in which stability can hold. The matter models considered in
[
10
]

are vacuum and scalar fields. However, in
[
10
]
, the authors consider a different system of equations, including a Fermi Walker transported frame.
A related result is to be found in
[
9
]
. In this article, the authors prove stable big bang formation for the FLRW solutions to the Einstein
scalar field equations with compact hyperbolic spatial topology. Moreover, in this setting, the authors are able to prove future global non-linear
stability. In other words, they obtain a global non-linear stability result for the solutions they consider. It is important to note that all the
results mentioned so far are based on CMC gauges. One issue associated with this choice is that it is non-local, even though the causal structure
close to the big bang is local. The CMC gauges thus introduce a connection between different regions of spacetime that should, from the point of
view of physics, be independent. For these reasons, it is of interest to mention
[
2
,
3
,
4
]
, in which the authors do prove stable big bang
formation using a gauge that can be localised.

It is natural to try to extend the results of
[
10
]
to more general spatial topologies. Moreover, it is of interest to find a condition on
initial data that ensures big bang formation, in particular conclusions concerning the singularity similar to those of
[
10
]
, but which
is independent of any background solution. There is also a natural notion of initial data on the singularity introduced in
[
22
]
.
If one wishes to prove stable big bang formation, the most natural class of solutions to prove stability of is those with asymptotics corresponding
to data on the singularity. Finally, it is natural to wish to prove stable big bang formation generally for spatially locally homogeneous solutions
with quiescent asymptotics. As always, the Einstein-scalar field equations is a natural system to consider. However, given the interest in the physics
literature in both inflation and different models of dark energy, it is natural to consider the Einstein-non-linear scalar field equations.
In this case, it is reasonable to expect a large class of solutions with quiescent singularities as well as future asymptotics that can be demonstrated
to be stable. In other words, it is natural to expect a large class of solutions for which one can demonstrate both future and past global non-linear
stability.

We address all the questions raised in the previous paragraph in separate articles; one concerning the asymptotics of spatially homogeneous solutions
to the Einstein-non-linear scalar field equations, see
[
23
]
, and one concerning finding stable regimes etc., see
[
13
]
. However, the
results require a local existence theory for the relevant gauge fixed equations. The purpose of this article is to provide such a theory.

The local existence theory we develop here concerns a class of gauge fixed equations which is similar to the equations used in
[
10
]
. However,
we here allow additional degrees of freedom: an antisymmetric matrix depending on the spacetime coordinates and the unknowns can be specified
freely etc. Moreover, we consider more general manifolds. In particular, we only require the spatial manifold to be closed and parallelisable.
Finally, we address the Einstein-non-linear scalar field setting. The main issues in proving local existence in this setting are similar to those
already addressed in
[
25
, Section 14.3, p. 4412-4420]
. However, in
[
25
]
, the authors refer to
[
7
]
as the main source of the
relevant ideas. The main complication is due to the appearance of too many derivatives of the metric components (in our case, the frame coefficients)
in the second order equation for the components of the second fundamental form. However, this issue can be addressed by integrating by parts in both
space and time; this idea seems to be common to all the local existence proofs and seems to originate with
[
7
]
.

The actual equations that appear in the study of the Einstein-non-linear scalar field system are quite lengthy. For this reason, it is natural to
develop a local existence theory for an abstract model system and then to demonstrate that the Einstein-non-linear scalar field system can be
considered to be a special case of the general theory. Next, we describe the abstract model systems we consider and state the relevant well
posedness results.

1.1.
Model systems

Let
\bar{M}
be a closed, connected and oriented
n
-dimensional manifold,
\mathcal{I}\subset\mathbb{R}
be an open interval,
\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}}
be a Riemannian metric
on
\bar{M}
and assume that there is a global orthonormal frame
\{E_{i}\}
on
\bar{M}
with respect to
\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}}
. Let
M:=\bar{M}\times\mathcal{I}
and

n_{i}\in\mathbb{N}
,
i=1,2
; here and in what follows we use the notation
\mathbb{N}_{0}
for the set of non-negative integers (i.e.,
0,1,2,...
) and

\mathbb{N}
for the set of positive integers (i.e.,
1,2,3,\dots
). Let
f_{i}
,
i=1,2,3,4
, be smooth functions of the following types:

\displaystyle f_{1}:M\times\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}+n_{2}+n+1}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}},

\displaystyle f_{2}:M\times\mathbb{R}^{(n+1)n_{1}+(n+2)n_{2}+(n+2)(n+1)}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n_{2}},

\displaystyle f_{3}:M\times\mathbb{R}^{(n+1)n_{1}+n_{2}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R},

\displaystyle f_{4}:M\times\mathbb{R}^{(n+1)n_{1}+(n+2)n_{2}+(n+2)(n+1)/2}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}.

Next, for
i,j=1,\dots,n
, and
l=1,2
, let
\bar{h}_{l}^{ij}
be smooth functions of the following types:

\bar{h}_{1}^{ij}:M\times\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}+n+1}\rightarrow\mathbb{R},\ \ \ \bar{h}_{2}^{ij}:M\times\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}.

Assume also that
\bar{h}_{l}^{ij}=\bar{h}_{l}^{ji}
for
i,j=1,\dots,n
and
l=1,2
. Assume, moreover, that there is a
1<\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
such that if

\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, then

(1)

\lambda^{-1}|\xi|^{2}\leq\bar{h}_{1}^{ij}(p)\xi_{i}\xi_{j}\leq\lambda|\xi|^{2},\ \ \ \lambda^{-1}|\xi|^{2}\leq\bar{h}_{2}^{ij}(q)\xi_{i}\xi_{j}\leq\lambda|\xi|^{2}

for all
p\in M\times\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}+n+1}
and
q\in M\times\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}}
, where we use the Einstein summation convention. Next, for
i,j=1,\dots,n
,
let
\gamma^{ij}
be smooth functions of the following type:

\gamma^{ij}:M\times\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}+n_{2}+n+1}\rightarrow M_{n_{2}\times n_{1}}(\mathbb{R});

here
M_{k\times l}(\mathbb{K})
denotes the
k\times l
-matrices with entries in
\mathbb{K}
. Finally, let
\zeta
be a smooth function of the
following type:

\zeta:M\times\mathbb{R}^{(n+1)n_{1}+n_{2}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R},

where we, in addition, assume that there is a
1<\lambda_{\zeta}\in\mathbb{R}
such that

(2)

\lambda_{\zeta}^{-1}\leq\zeta(p)\leq\lambda_{\zeta},

for all
p\in M\times\mathbb{R}^{(n+1)n_{1}+n_{2}}
. In addition to the above, assume
f_{i}
,
\bar{h}_{l}^{ij}
,
\gamma^{ij}
and
\zeta
and all their
partial derivatives to be globally bounded (where we by partial derivatives in the
\bar{M}
direction mean applications of
E_{\mathbf{I}}
, where

E_{\mathbf{I}}\psi:=E_{i_{1}}\cdots E_{i_{k}}\psi
for
\mathbf{I}=(i_{1},\dots,i_{k})
and
1\leq i_{j}\leq n
for
1\leq j\leq k
).

Given the above functions, consider the following system of equations (the meaning of the notation
f_{i}[u,v,N_{1}]
etc. is clarified in connection with
(
5
) below):

(3a)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}u

\displaystyle=f_{1}[u,v,N_{1}],

(3b)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}^{2}v

\displaystyle=\bar{h}^{ij}_{1}[u,N_{1}]E_{i}E_{j}v+\gamma^{ij}[u,v,N_{1}]E_{i}E_{j}u+f_{2}[u,v,N_{1},N_{2}],

(3c)

\displaystyle\Delta_{\bar{h}_{2}[u]}N_{1}=

\displaystyle\zeta[u,v]N_{1}+f_{3}[u,v],

(3d)

\displaystyle\Delta_{\bar{h}_{2}[u]}N_{2}=

\displaystyle\zeta[u,v]N_{2}+f_{4}[u,v,N_{1}],

where
\bar{h}_{2}[u]
is the Riemannian metric such that
\bar{h}_{2}^{ij}[u]
are the components of the inverse of the matrix with components

\bar{h}_{2}[u](E_{i},E_{j})
. We are interested in solving the initial value problem for these equations. In other words, given
t_{0}\in\mathcal{I}

and initial data
u_{0}\in C^{\infty}(\bar{M},\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}})
and
v_{0,i}\in C^{\infty}(\bar{M},\mathbb{R}^{n_{2}})
,
i=0,1
, we wish to find an open interval

\mathcal{I}_{0}\subseteq\mathcal{I}
containing
t_{0}
and a solution
(u,v,N_{1},N_{2})
to (
3
) on
M_{0}:=\bar{M}\times\mathcal{I}_{0}
, with

(4)

u\in C^{\infty}(M_{0},\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}}),\ \ \ v\in C^{\infty}(M_{0},\mathbb{R}^{n_{2}}),\ \ \ N_{1},N_{2}\in C^{\infty}(M_{0},\mathbb{R}),

such that
u(\cdot,t_{0})=u_{0}
,
v(\cdot,t_{0})=v_{0,0}
and
(\partial_{t}v)(\cdot,t_{0})=v_{0,1}
(in practice, we are also interested in finding solutions
in other regularity classes). In (
3
),
f_{1}[u,v,N_{1}]
should, given
u,v,N_{1}
, be thought of as the function that takes
p\in M_{0}
to

(5)

f_{1}(p,u(p),v(p),N_{1}(p),(E_{1}N_{1})(p),\dots,(E_{n}N_{1})(p)).

The interpretation of
f_{2}[u,v,N_{1},N_{2}]
is similar, only that it depends on
u
,
E_{i}u
,
v
,
E_{i}v
,
\partial_{t}v
,
N_{1}
,
N_{2}
,
E_{i}N_{1}
,

E_{i}N_{2}
,
E_{i}E_{j}N_{1}
and
E_{i}E_{j}N_{2}
(where we assume
i\leq j
in the last two expressions, so that the last two expressions only give rise
to dependence on
n(n+1)
variables). Similarly,
f_{3}
only depends on
u
,
E_{i}u
and
v
. However,
f_{4}
only depends on
u
,
v
,
E_{i}u
,
E_{i}v
,

\partial_{t}v
,
N_{1}
,
E_{i}N_{1}
and
E_{i}E_{j}N_{1}
(where we assume
i\leq j
in the last expression). Next,
\bar{h}_{1}^{ij}
only depends on
u
,
N_{1}
and

E_{i}N_{1}
and
\bar{h}_{2}^{ij}
only depends on
u
. The
\gamma^{ij}
’s only depend on
u
,
v
,
N_{1}
and
E_{i}N_{1}
. Finally,
\zeta
has the same dependence
as
f_{3}
.

Definition 1
.

An equation of the form (
3
), where
\bar{M}
;
\mathcal{I}
;
\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}}
;
\{E_{i}\}
;
f_{i}
,
i=1,2,3,4
;
\bar{h}_{l}^{ij}
,
i,j=1,\dots,n
and

l=1,2
;
\gamma^{ij}
,
i,j=1,\dots,n
; and
\zeta
satisfy the above conditions, is said to be a
model system
.

Remark 2
.

Given a model system, we below use all the notation introduced prior to the statement of Definition
1
without further comment.

Remark 3
.

We are mainly interested in the case that
N_{2}=\partial_{t}N_{1}
, so that (
3d
) can, essentially, be derived from (
3c
).
However, it is convenient to assume that
f_{4}
and all its derivatives are bounded, and the equation obtained by time differentiating
(
3c
) does not have that property. In fact, one would have to modify the resulting equation in a neighbourhood of the initial data in
order to obtain a system of the desired form. Here we therefore prefer to consider (
3
) and discuss the necessary modifications when
applying the general theory to a particular system.

We state the basic existence and uniqueness theorem in finite degree of regularity in Proposition
30
below. In
Lemma
33
, we then state the associated continuation criterion. Combining these results yields the following corollary for
smooth solutions.

Corollary 4
.

Fix a model system and

(6)

\mathcal{U}_{0}\in C^{\infty}(\bar{M},\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}}),\ \ \ \mathcal{V}_{0,0}\in C^{\infty}(\bar{M},\mathbb{R}^{n_{2}}),\ \ \ \mathcal{V}_{0,1}\in C^{\infty}(\bar{M},\mathbb{R}^{n_{2}}).

Let
t_{0}\in\mathcal{I}=:(t_{-},t_{+})
. Then there are
t_{a},t_{b}
with
t_{0}\in(t_{a},t_{b})\subseteq\mathcal{I}
, and a unique smooth solution
u,v,N_{1},N_{2}

to (
3
) on
\bar{M}\times(t_{a},t_{b})
satisfying

(7)

u(\cdot,t_{0})=\mathcal{U}_{0},\ \ \ v(\cdot,t_{0})=\mathcal{V}_{0,0},\ \ \ (\partial_{t}v)(\cdot,t_{0})=\mathcal{V}_{0,1}.

Moreover, either
t_{b}=t_{+}
or

(8)

\lim_{t\uparrow t_{b}}\sup_{t_{0}\leq\tau\leq t}\left(\|u(\cdot,\tau)\|_{C^{2}}+\|v(\cdot,\tau)\|_{C^{2}}+\|\partial_{t}v(\cdot,\tau)\|_{C^{1}}\right)=\infty.

The statement concerning
t_{a}
is similar.

Proof.

The proof is to be found at the end of Subsection
2.8
.
∎

Definition 5
.

Fix a model system, a
t_{0}\in\mathcal{I}
and initial data as in (
6
). Then the interval
(t_{a},t_{b})
obtained by appealing to Corollary
4

is called the
maximal interval of existence
corresponding to the initial data.

Next, we state the relevant Cauchy stability result.

Proposition 6
.

Fix a model system, a
t_{0}\in\mathcal{I}
and initial data as in (
6
). Let
u
,
v
,
N_{1}
and
N_{2}
denote the corresponding solution, obtained by appealing
to Corollary
4
, and
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{bg}}
be the corresponding maximal interval of existence; see Definition
5
. Fix an integer
m>n/2+1
.
Finally, fix a
t_{\mathrm{fin}}\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{bg}}
. Then, given
\epsilon>0
, there is a
\delta>0
such that if

(9)

\mathcal{U}_{0,\mathrm{per}}\in C^{\infty}(\bar{M},\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}}),\ \ \ \mathcal{V}_{0,0,\mathrm{per}}\in C^{\infty}(\bar{M},\mathbb{R}^{n_{2}}),\ \ \ \mathcal{V}_{0,1,\mathrm{per}}\in C^{\infty}(\bar{M},\mathbb{R}^{n_{2}})

are such that

\|\mathcal{U}_{0,\mathrm{per}}-\mathcal{U}_{0}\|_{H^{m+1}(\bar{M})}+\|\mathcal{V}_{0,0,\mathrm{per}}-\mathcal{V}_{0,0}\|_{H^{m+1}(\bar{M})}+\|\mathcal{V}_{0,1,\mathrm{per}}-\mathcal{V}_{0,1}\|_{H^{m}(\bar{M})}<\delta,

and if
u_{\mathrm{per}}
,
v_{\mathrm{per}}
,
N_{1,\mathrm{per}}
and
N_{2,\mathrm{per}}
is the smooth solution to (
3
) satisfying

(10)

u_{\mathrm{per}}(\cdot,t_{0})=\mathcal{U}_{0,\mathrm{per}},\ \ \ v_{\mathrm{per}}(\cdot,t_{0})=\mathcal{V}_{0,0,\mathrm{per}},\ \ \ (\partial_{t}v_{\mathrm{per}})(\cdot,t_{0})=\mathcal{V}_{0,1,\mathrm{per}},

cf. Corollary
4
, with corresponding maximal existence interval
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{per}}
, cf. Definition
5
, then

t_{\mathrm{fin}}\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{per}}
and

\|u-u_{\mathrm{per}}\|_{H^{m+1}(\bar{M}_{t_{\mathrm{fin}}})}+\|v-v_{\mathrm{per}}\|_{H^{m+1}(\bar{M}_{t_{\mathrm{fin}}})}+\|\partial_{t}v-\partial_{t}v_{\mathrm{per}}\|_{H^{m+1}(\bar{M}_{t_{\mathrm{fin}}})}<\epsilon.

Proof.

The proof is to be found in Subsection
2.9
below.
∎

1.2.
Einstein’s equations

Next, we apply the above results to the
Einstein non-linear scalar field equations
, which, given a
potential

V\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
,
can be written

(11a)

\displaystyle G=

\displaystyle T,

(11b)

\displaystyle\Box_{g}\phi=

\displaystyle V^{\prime}\circ\phi.

A solution to these equations consists of a Lorentz manifold
(M,g)
and a function
\phi\in C^{\infty}(M)
satisfying (
11
), where

\Box_{g}
is the wave operator associated with
g
;

\displaystyle G:=

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ric}-\tfrac{1}{2}Sg;

\displaystyle T:=

\displaystyle d\phi\otimes d\phi-\left[\tfrac{1}{2}|d\phi|_{g}^{2}+V\circ\phi\right]g;

and
\mathrm{Ric}
and
S
denote the Ricci tensor and scalar curvature of
g
respectively. In what follows, we also refer to
\phi
as the

scalar field
;
G
as the
Einstein tensor
; and
T
as the
stress energy tensor
. We are interested in solving
(
11
) given initial data, defined as follows.

Definition 7
.

Initial data for the Einstein non-linear scalar field equations
, with potential
V\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
, consist of the following: a connected
smooth manifold
\bar{M}
; a smooth Riemannian metric
h
on
\bar{M}
; a smooth symmetric covariant
2
-tensor field
\kappa
on
\bar{M}
; and two functions

\phi_{0}
,
\phi_{1}\in C^{\infty}(\bar{M})
satisfying the
constraint equations

(12a)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Scal}_{h}-|\kappa|_{h}^{2}+(\mathrm{tr}_{h}\kappa)^{2}=

\displaystyle\phi_{1}^{2}+|d\phi_{0}|_{h}^{2}+2V\circ\phi_{0},

(12b)

\displaystyle\mathrm{div}_{h}\kappa-d\mathrm{tr}_{h}\kappa=

\displaystyle\phi_{1}d\phi_{0}.

Here
\mathrm{Scal}_{h}
denotes the scalar curvature associated with
h
. In case
\mathrm{tr}_{h}\kappa
is constant, the initial data are said to have

constant mean curvature
or to be
CMC
.

Remark 8
.

The equations (
12a
) and (
12b
) are referred to as the
Hamiltonian
and

momentum constraint equations
respectively.

Remark 9
.

Needless to say, one can consider initial data with a lower degree of regularity.

In
[
13
]
, the system (
11
) is considered in the context of the formation of big bang singularities. However, in order to obtain
a system of equations with a well posed initial value problem and which can be used to deduce geometric conclusions concerning the asymptotics in
the direction of the singularity, it is necessary to fix the gauge. This can be done in many ways, but in
[
13
]
the equations used are a
special case of the system (
13
) we discuss next. In fact, the conclusions of this article are used in the proof of the past global
existence results in
[
13
]
and in the proof of the results concerning
the existence of developments given data on the singularity in
[
11
]
. However, the step from Corollary
4
and
Proposition
6
to the type of existence and stability results needed in, e.g.,
[
13
]
is substantial. Our next goal is
therefore to formulate local existence and Cauchy stability results for solutions to a slight generalisation of the equations in

[
13
]
. In order to formulate the equations, assume first
\bar{M}
to be a closed
n
-dimensional manifold
and assume that there is a global frame
\{E_{i}\}
of
T\bar{M}
with dual frame

\{\eta^{i}\}
. We wish to solve the equations on
M:=\bar{M}\times\mathcal{I}
, where
\mathcal{I}\subset(0,\infty)
is an open interval. Moreover, we, by standard abuse
of notation, let
t
denote both the second coordinate of a point in
\bar{M}\times\mathcal{I}
as well as the function that maps
(\bar{x},s)\in M
to
s
. In
particular,
t\in\mathcal{I}
. In this subsection we are interested in the following system of equations:

(13a)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}e_{I}^{i}=

\displaystyle f_{I}^{J}e_{J}^{i},

(13b)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\omega_{i}^{I}=

\displaystyle-f_{J}^{I}\omega_{i}^{J},

(13c)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\bar{k}_{IJ}=

\displaystyle\overline{\nabla}_{I}\overline{\nabla}_{J}N+2N\bar{k}_{IL}\bar{k}_{LJ}-Nt^{-1}\bar{k}_{IJ}+Ne_{I}(\phi)e_{J}(\phi)

\displaystyle+\tfrac{2N}{n-1}(V\circ\phi)\delta_{IJ}-N\bar{R}_{IJ}+f_{I}^{L}\bar{k}_{LJ}+\bar{k}_{IL}f_{J}^{L},

(13d)

\displaystyle\Delta_{\bar{g}}N=

\displaystyle t^{-2}(N-1)+N\big[\bar{k}_{IJ}\bar{k}_{IJ}+[e_{0}(\phi)]^{2}-\tfrac{2}{n-1}V\circ\phi-\tfrac{1}{t^{2}}\big],

(13e)

\displaystyle\Box_{g}\phi=

\displaystyle-\vartheta e_{0}(\phi)+V^{\prime}\circ\phi,

(13f)

\displaystyle(\overline{\nabla}_{e_{L}}\bar{k})(e_{L},e_{I})=

\displaystyle e_{I}(\theta)+e_{I}(\phi)e_{0}(\phi),

(13g)

\displaystyle t^{-2}=

\displaystyle\bar{k}_{IJ}\bar{k}_{IJ}+[e_{0}(\phi)]^{2}+e_{I}(\phi)e_{I}(\phi)+2V\circ\phi-\bar{S},

where
V\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
,
i,I,J\in\{1,\dots,n\}
and we sum over repeated indices, even if both are downstairs indices. The variables in
(
13
) are
e_{I}^{i}
,
\omega^{I}_{i}
,
\bar{k}_{IJ}
,
N
and
\phi
; we refer to them as the
basic variables
. A solution
is tacitly understood to consist of a manifold
M
as above, on which the basic variables are defined. Moreover, it is understood that
N>0
on

M
and that, initially,
\omega^{I}_{i}e^{i}_{J}=\delta^{I}_{J}
. In order to explain how the
constituents of (
13
) are to be interpreted, define, given
e_{I}^{i}
and
\omega^{I}_{i}
,
e_{I}:=e_{I}^{i}E_{i}
and

\omega^{I}:=\omega^{I}_{i}\eta^{i}
. Then, due to (
13a
) and (
13b
),

(14)

\partial_{t}(\omega^{J}(e_{I})-\delta_{I}^{J})=-f_{K}^{J}(\omega^{K}(e_{I})-\delta^{K}_{I})+f_{I}^{K}(\omega^{J}(e_{K})-\delta^{J}_{K}).

Since
\omega^{J}(e_{I})=\delta_{I}^{J}
initially, (
14
) yields the conclusion that
\omega^{J}(e_{I})=\delta_{I}^{J}
whenever the
solution is defined. In particular,
\{e_{I}\}
is thus a frame and
\{\omega^{I}\}
is its dual frame on the tangent spaces of the hypersurfaces

\bar{M}_{t}:=\bar{M}\times\{t\}
. We define a Lorentz metric on
M
by

(15)

g:=-N^{2}dt\otimes dt+\textstyle{\sum}_{I}\omega^{I}\otimes\omega^{I}.

Letting
e_{0}:=N^{-1}\partial_{t}
,
\{e_{\alpha}\}
is then an orthonormal frame with respect to
g
. The induced metric on the hypersurfaces

\bar{M}_{t}
is

(16)

\bar{g}:=\textstyle{\sum}_{I}\omega^{I}\otimes\omega^{I}.

In (
13
),
\overline{\nabla}
,
\bar{R}
and
\bar{S}
denote the Levi-Civita connection, the Ricci curvature and the scalar curvature of

\bar{g}
respectively. Moreover, the upper case Latin indices refer to components with respect to the frame
\{e_{I}\}
. Next,
\bar{k}_{IJ}
is required
to be symmetric. Moreover,

\bar{k}:=\bar{k}_{IJ}\omega^{I}\otimes\omega^{J};

\theta:=\bar{k}_{II}
; and
\vartheta:=\theta-t^{-1}
. Turning to (
13d
), we assume

(17)

\zeta:=\bar{k}_{IJ}\bar{k}_{IJ}+(e_{0}\phi)^{2}-\tfrac{2}{n-1}V\circ\phi

to satisfy
\zeta>0
on
M
. Combining this assumption with (
13d
) and the maximum principle, it follows that
N>0
; see
(
193
) and the adjacent text for more details. Concerning
f_{I}^{J}
, we assume

(18)

f_{I}^{J}+f_{J}^{I}=-2N\bar{k}_{IJ}

(recall that
\bar{k}_{IJ}
is symmetric). Due to this requirement,
\bar{k}_{IJ}
are the components of the second fundamental
form of the
\bar{M}_{t}
-hypersurfaces with respect to the frame
\{e_{I}\}
; see Lemma
35
. Let
a_{I}^{J}:=(f_{I}^{J}-f_{J}^{I})/2
. Then we, finally,
assume
a_{I}^{J}
to only depend on the spacetime coordinates,
e^{i}_{I}
,
\omega^{I}_{i}
,
N
,
\bar{k}_{IJ}
,
\phi
,
N^{-1}\partial_{t}\phi
,
E_{i}\phi
and
E_{i}N
.
That the condition
a_{I}^{J}=0
yields the equations considered in
[
13
]
is verified in Remark
12
.

Next, let us state the relevant well posedness results for (
13
). We use the following notation:

(19a)

\displaystyle\|e(\cdot,t)\|_{C^{k}}:=

\displaystyle\big(\textstyle{\sum}_{i,I}\sum_{|\mathbf{I}|\leq k}\sup_{\bar{x}\in\bar{M}}|(E_{\mathbf{I}}e_{I}^{i})(\bar{x},t)|^{2}\big)^{1/2},

(19b)

\displaystyle\|\omega(\cdot,t)\|_{C^{k}}:=

\displaystyle\big(\textstyle{\sum}_{i,I}\sum_{|\mathbf{I}|\leq k}\sup_{\bar{x}\in\bar{M}}|(E_{\mathbf{I}}\omega_{i}^{I})(\bar{x},t)|^{2}\big)^{1/2},

(19c)

\displaystyle\|\bar{k}(\cdot,t)\|_{C^{k}}:=

\displaystyle\big(\textstyle{\sum}_{I,J}\sum_{|\mathbf{I}|\leq k}\sup_{\bar{x}\in\bar{M}}|(E_{\mathbf{I}}\bar{k}_{IJ})(\bar{x},t)|^{2}\big)^{1/2},

(19d)

\displaystyle\|\partial_{t}\bar{k}(\cdot,t)\|_{C^{k}}:=

\displaystyle\big(\textstyle{\sum}_{I,J}\sum_{|\mathbf{I}|\leq k}\sup_{\bar{x}\in\bar{M}}|(E_{\mathbf{I}}\partial_{t}\bar{k}_{IJ})(\bar{x},t)|^{2}\big)^{1/2},

(19e)

\displaystyle\|\phi(\cdot,t)\|_{C^{k}}:=

\displaystyle\big(\textstyle{\sum}_{|\mathbf{I}|\leq k}\sup_{\bar{x}\in\bar{M}}|(E_{\mathbf{I}}\phi)(\bar{x},t)|^{2}\big)^{1/2}.

For an explanation of the meaning of
E_{\mathbf{I}}
etc., we refer the reader to Subsection
A.5
. Next, given two real numbers

t_{a}
and
t_{b}
, define
I_{t_{a},t_{b}}
to be the closed and non-empty interval with
t_{a}
and
t_{b}
as endpoints. In order to formulate the
continuation criterion, it is convenient to introduce the following expression

(20)

\begin{split}\mathcal{C}(t):=&\textstyle{\sup}_{\tau\in I_{t_{0},t}}[\|e(\cdot,\tau)\|_{C^{2}}+\|\omega(\cdot,\tau)\|_{C^{2}}+\|\bar{k}(\cdot,\tau)\|_{C^{2}}+\|\partial_{t}\bar{k}(\cdot,\tau)\|_{C^{1}}+\|\phi(\cdot,\tau)\|_{C^{3}}\\
&\phantom{\sup\ \ +xy}+\|\partial_{t}\phi(\cdot,\tau)\|_{C^{2}}+\|e_{0}\phi(\cdot,\tau)\|_{C^{2}}+\|\partial_{t}e_{0}\phi(\cdot,\tau)\|_{C^{1}}+\|1/\zeta(\cdot,\tau)\|_{C^{0}}]\end{split}

where
\zeta
is defined by (
17
).

Next, we state local existence and uniqueness of solutions to (
13
), including a continuation criterion, given initial data.

Theorem 10
.

Fix a function
V\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
and let
(\bar{M},h,\kappa,\phi_{0},\phi_{1})
be smooth CMC initial data for the Einstein-non-linear scalar
field equations with potential
V
, see Definition
7
, where
\bar{M}
is a closed, connected, oriented and parallelisable
n
-dimensional
manifold,
\mathrm{tr}_{h}\kappa>0
and

(21)

|\kappa|_{h}^{2}+\phi_{1}^{2}-\tfrac{2}{n-1}V\circ\phi_{0}>0

on
\bar{M}
. Let
t_{0}:=1/\mathrm{tr}_{h}\kappa
. Fix a frame
\{E_{i}\}
of
\bar{M}
with dual frame
\{\eta^{i}\}
. Next, let
\{\xi_{I}\}
be an orthonormal
frame of
(\bar{M},h)
with dual frame
\{\rho^{I}\}
. Let
a_{I}^{J}
, where
I,J\in\{1,\dots,n\}
, be such that
a_{I}^{J}=-a_{J}^{I}
and such that

a_{I}^{J}
only depends on the spacetime coordinates,
e^{i}_{I}
,
\omega^{I}_{i}
,
\bar{k}_{IJ}
,
\phi
,
N^{-1}\partial_{t}\phi
,
E_{i}\phi
,
N
and
E_{i}N
,
where
e^{i}_{I}
,
\omega^{I}_{i}
,
\bar{k}_{IJ}
,
\phi
and
N
are the basic variables of the system (
13
). Define
f_{I}^{J}
, where

I,J\in\{1,\dots,n\}
, by

(22)

f_{I}^{J}:=-N\bar{k}_{IJ}+a_{I}^{J}.

Let
e_{I}^{i}|_{t_{0}}:=\xi_{I}^{i}
and
\omega_{i}^{I}|_{t_{0}}:=\rho_{i}^{I}
, where
\xi_{I}^{i}
and
\rho_{i}^{I}
are defined by

\xi_{I}=\xi_{I}^{i}E_{i}
and
\rho^{I}=\rho_{i}^{I}\eta^{i}
respectively. Next, let
\bar{k}_{IJ}|_{t_{0}}:=\kappa(\xi_{I},\xi_{J})
and
N|_{t_{0}}

be defined as the unique solution to the equation that results by replacing
t
,
\phi
and
e_{0}\phi
in (
13d
) by

t_{0}
,
\phi_{0}
and
\phi_{1}
respectively. Define, finally,
\phi|_{t_{0}}:=\phi_{0}
and
(\partial_{t}\phi)|_{t_{0}}:=N|_{t_{0}}\phi_{1}
.
Then there is an open interval
\mathcal{I}\subseteq(0,\infty)
, containing
t_{0}
, and a unique solution to (
13
) on

M:=\bar{M}\times\mathcal{I}
, corresponding to these initial data, such that
N>0
and
\zeta>0
; such that
\bar{k}_{II}=1/t
; and such that

\bar{k}_{IJ}=\bar{k}_{JI}
. Moreover, if
\bar{M}_{t}:=\bar{M}\times\{t\}
for
t\in\mathcal{I}
and

(23)

g:=-N^{2}dt\otimes dt+\textstyle{\sum}\omega^{I}\otimes\omega^{I},

where
\omega^{I}:=\omega^{I}_{i}\eta^{i}
, then
(M,g,\phi)
is a solution to the Einstein-non-linear scalar field equations which induces the
initial data
(h,\kappa,\phi_{0},\phi_{1})
on
\bar{M}_{t_{0}}
; and is such that the mean curvature of
\bar{M}_{t}
is
1/t
for

t\in\mathcal{I}
. Finally, if
\mathcal{I}=(t_{-},t_{+})
, then either
t_{-}=0
or
\mathcal{C}(t)\rightarrow\infty
as
t\downarrow t_{-}
. Similarly, either

t_{+}=\infty
or
\mathcal{C}(t)\rightarrow\infty
as
t\uparrow t_{+}
.

Remark 11
.

Orientable
3
-dimensional closed manifolds are parallelisable. The requirement that
\bar{M}
be parallelisable is thus only a non-trivial
condition in case
n\neq 3
.

Remark 12
.

In case
a_{I}^{J}=0
, where
a_{I}^{J}:=(f_{I}^{J}-f_{J}^{I})/2
, the equations in (
13
) take the form of the corresponding
equations in
[
13
, Proposition 53]
, with the following caveats: In the momentum constraint equation, i.e. (
13f
), the
additional condition of constant mean curvature has to be imposed, which means that the first term on the right hand side of
(
13f
) vanishes. Second, in the lapse equation, i.e. (
13d
), it is necessary to substitute the spatial scalar curvature
from the Hamiltonian constraint in order to obtain our lapse equation from the lapse equation in
[
13
]
. We leave the verifications of these
statements to the reader. Note, however, that the main discrepancy between
[
13
]
and (
13
) is that in
[
13
]
, the authors
explicitly write out the spatial Ricci and scalar curvatures etc. Moreover, the relevant expressions for these quantities are to be found in

[
13
, Lemma 55]
. Note also that the second order evolution equation in
[
13
]
for
\phi
coincides with (
166
). In addition, the
evolution equation for
{\bar{\gamma}}^{I}_{JK}
included in
[
13
]
can be deduced by appealing to (
134
) and the evolution equation for

e_{I}\phi
can be deduced by computing the commutator
[e_{0},e_{I}]
.

Proof.

The proof is to be found in Subsection
4.2
.
∎

Finally, we state the corresponding result on Cauchy stability.

Theorem 13
.

Given notation, assumptions and conclusions as in the statement of Theorem
10
, let
e_{I}^{i}
,
\omega_{i}^{I}
,

\bar{k}_{IJ}
,
\phi
and
N
be the solution obtained in the conclusions. Let, moreover,

\mathcal{I}
be the corresponding existence interval. Given
t_{1}\in\mathcal{I}
,
m>n/2+1
and
\epsilon>0
, there is then a
\delta>0
such that the following
holds. Let
(\bar{M},\check{h},\check{\kappa},\check{\phi}_{0},\check{\phi}_{1})
be smooth CMC initial data for the Einstein-non-linear scalar field equations with potential
V
,
satisfying
\mathrm{tr}_{\check{h}}\check{\kappa}=1/t_{0}
, and let
\{\check{\xi}_{I}\}
be an orthonormal frame of
(\bar{M},\check{h})
. Let
\check{e}_{I}^{i}
,
\check{\omega}_{i}^{I}
,
\check{k}_{IJ}
,

\check{\phi}
and
\check{N}
be the corresponding solution, obtained by applying Theorem
10
. Let, moreover,
\check{\mathcal{I}}
be the
corresponding existence interval and
\check{e}_{0}=\check{N}^{-1}\partial_{t}
. Then, if

(24a)

\displaystyle\textstyle{\sum}_{I,i}\|\check{e}_{I}^{i}-e_{I}^{i}\|_{H^{m+1}(\bar{M}_{t_{0}})}+\textstyle{\sum}_{I,i}\|\check{\omega}^{I}_{i}-\omega^{I}_{i}\|_{H^{m+1}(\bar{M}_{t_{0}})}

\displaystyle<\delta,

(24b)

\displaystyle\textstyle{\sum}_{I,J}\|\bar{k}_{IJ}-\check{k}_{IJ}\|_{H^{m+1}(\bar{M}_{t_{0}})}+\textstyle{\sum}_{I,J}\|\partial_{t}\bar{k}_{IJ}-\partial_{t}\check{k}_{IJ}\|_{H^{m}(\bar{M}_{t_{0}})}

\displaystyle<\delta,

(24c)

\displaystyle\|\phi-\check{\phi}\|_{H^{m+2}(\bar{M}_{t_{0}})}+\|\partial_{t}\phi-\partial_{t}\check{\phi}\|_{H^{m+1}(\bar{M}_{t_{0}})}

(24d)

\displaystyle+\|e_{0}\phi-\check{e}_{0}\check{\phi}\|_{H^{m+1}(\bar{M}_{t_{0}})}+\|\partial_{t}e_{0}\phi-\partial_{t}\check{e}_{0}\check{\phi}\|_{H^{m}(\bar{M}_{t_{0}})}

\displaystyle<\delta,

the interval
\check{\mathcal{I}}
contains
t_{1}
and (
24
) holds with
t_{0}
replaced by
t_{1}
and
\delta
replaced by
\epsilon
.

Proof.

The proof is to be found at the end of Subsection
4.2
.
∎

1.3.
Outline

In Section
2
, we develop a local existence theory for the model systems introduced in Subsection
1.1
.
This includes local existence and uniqueness, a continuation criterion, as well as Cauchy stability. One difficulty turns out to be the elliptic estimates
for
N_{1}
and
N_{2}
(solving (
3c
) and (
3d
)). Note that, given
u
and
v
, the equation (
3c
) is a linear
elliptic equation for
N_{1}
. Moreover, since
\zeta>0
, this equation has a unique solution and there are standard elliptic Schauder and Sobolev estimates
for
N_{1}
. However, for our applications, this information is not sufficient. We need to know how the constants appearing in the Schauder and Sobolev
estimates depend on
u
and
v
(and that the dependence is continuous with respect to appropriate norms). Unfortunately, we were unable to find a
statement of the estimates including sufficiently detailed information concerning the dependence of the constants for our applications. The relevant
Schauder and Sobolev estimates are to be found in Appendices
C
,
D
and

E
. To obtain the required regularity of the lapse function, as a
function of time, we also need an appropriate implicit function theorem argument; see Appendix
F
for the
details. Once the local existence theory for solutions to the model system has been developed, we turn to applications to Einstein’s equations.
In Section
3
, we take the first steps in relating the model system to the equations we actually
wish to solve. In the end, the system we wish to solve is (
13
). Unfortunately, this system cannot be solved directly. In
fact, we have to solve an auxiliary system which, as opposed to (
13
), is second order in the coefficients of the second fundamental
form. The logic is then that we use initial data for (
13
) to generate initial data for the auxiliary second order system.
The second order system can then be solved by appealing to the local existence theory for the model system. However, at the end we wish to return
to (
13
). To this end we, in Section
3
and using the auxiliary second order system, derive a system
of equations for expressions quantifying the violations of the CMC condition, of the Hamiltonian constraint, of the momentum constraint and of the
first order equation for the coefficients of the second fundamental form. We also demonstrate that given a solution to the auxiliary second order
system with appropriate initial data, the expressions quantifying the violations all vanish. This yields a solution to the original system
(
13
), which is also a solution to the Einstein-non-linear scalar field system. Finally, in Section
4
,
we prove well posedness for the original system (
13
).

1.4.
Acknowledgements

The author would like to express his gratitude to Oliver Petersen for his help in the writing of Appendix
D
.
This research was funded by Vetenskapsrådet (the Swedish Research Council), dnr. 2022-03053.

2.
Model system

2.1.
A simplified model system

In order to solve the Cauchy problem associated with (
3
), the idea is to set up an iteration: we consider a sequence of solutions to
simplified versions of the model system and prove that the sequence converges to a solution of the actual system; in fact, the overall architecture of
the local existence argument is taken from
[
16
]
. In practice, this means that we, given

u_{a}\in C^{\infty}(M,\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}})
,
v_{a}\in C^{\infty}(M,\mathbb{R}^{n_{2}})
and
N_{i,a}\in C^{\infty}(M,\mathbb{R})
,
i=1,2
, wish to solve the initial value problem for

(25a)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}u_{b}=

\displaystyle f_{1}[u_{a},v_{a},N_{1,a}],

(25b)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}^{2}v_{b}=

\displaystyle\bar{h}^{ij}_{1}[u_{b},N_{1,b}]E_{i}E_{j}v_{b}+\gamma^{ij}[u_{b},v_{a},N_{1,b}]E_{i}E_{j}u_{b},

\displaystyle+f_{2}[u_{a},v_{a},N_{1,a},N_{2,a}]

(25c)

\displaystyle\Delta_{\bar{h}_{2}[u_{b}]}N_{1,b}=

\displaystyle\zeta[u_{b},v_{a}]N_{1,b}+f_{3}[u_{b},v_{a}],

(25d)

\displaystyle\Delta_{\bar{h}_{2}[u_{b}]}N_{2,b}=

\displaystyle\zeta[u_{b},v_{a}]N_{2,b}+f_{4}[u_{b},v_{a},N_{1,b}].

Note, to begin with, that there is no problem in solving (
25
). In fact, the following lemma holds.

Lemma 14
.

Fix a model system in the sense of Definition
1
. Fix
u_{a}\in C^{\infty}(M,\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}})
,
v_{a}\in C^{\infty}(M,\mathbb{R}^{n_{2}})
and

N_{i,a}\in C^{\infty}(M,\mathbb{R})
,
i=1,2
. Fix, moreover,
t_{0}\in\mathcal{I}
,
u_{0}\in C^{\infty}(\bar{M},\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}})
and
v_{0,i}\in C^{\infty}(\bar{M},\mathbb{R}^{n_{2}})
,

i=0,1
. Then there is a unique solution
u_{b}\in C^{\infty}(M,\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}})
,
v_{b}\in C^{\infty}(M,\mathbb{R}^{n_{2}})
and
N_{i,b}\in C^{\infty}(M,\mathbb{R})
,

i=1,2
, to (
25
) such that
u_{b}(\cdot,t_{0})=u_{0}
,
v_{b}(\cdot,t_{0})=v_{0,0}
and
(\partial_{t}v_{b})(\cdot,t_{0})=v_{0,1}
.

Proof.

Given
u_{a}
,
v_{a}
,
N_{1,a}
and
u_{0}
, it is clear that the initial value problem for (
25a
) has a unique smooth solution
on
M
. In (
25b
)–(
25d
),
u_{b}
can then be considered to be a given smooth function on
M
. Next,
since
\zeta
has a strictly positive lower bound and
u_{b}
is a given smooth function, it is clear that for each
t\in\mathcal{I}
, the equation
(
25c
) has a unique smooth solution
N_{1,b}(\cdot,t)
; cf. Lemma
101
and
Remark
104
. However, we also need to know that the dependence on
t
is smooth. To this end, we appeal to
Corollary
109
. In order to verify that the conditions are satisfied, note that for every
k,m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
with

k>n/2+1
, we can think of

t\mapsto[\bar{h}_{2}[u_{b}](\cdot,t),\zeta[u_{b},v_{a}](\cdot,t),f_{3}[u_{b},v_{a}](\cdot,t)]

as being a
C^{m}
map from
\mathcal{I}
to space required in Corollary
109
. It then follows from Corollary
109

that
N_{1,b}
is a
C^{m}
map from
\mathcal{I}
to
H^{k+2}(\bar{M})
. Since
k>n/2+1
and
m
are arbitrary, we conclude that
N_{1,b}\in C^{\infty}(M)
. At this
stage
u_{b}
and

N_{1,b}
have been determined. They are also smooth on
M
. That the equation (
25b
) has a unique smooth solution, given initial data,
then follows from, e.g.,
[
18
, Theorem 12.19, p. 144]
. Finally, since
u_{b}
,
v_{b}
and
N_{1,b}
are now given smooth functions, the proof
of the fact that the equation (
25d
) has a unique solution
N_{2,b}\in C^{\infty}(M)
is identical to the proof in the case of
N_{1,b}
.
∎

2.2.
Schauder estimates for the lapse functions

In the proof of local existence, and in the derivation of a continuation criterion, we need Schauder estimates. The general form of the estimates
we need is standard. However, we need more detailed information concerning the dependence of the constants than is normally given. In
Appendix
C
below, we therefore derive the relevant estimates for a model equation, including the needed information concerning
the constants. Here we state the conclusions obtained by applying the general estimates to (
25c
) and (
25d
).

Lemma 15
.

Let
n_{1},n_{2}\in\mathbb{N}
,
(\bar{M},\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}})
be a closed, connected and oriented Riemannian manifold, and assume
(\bar{M},\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}})
to have a
global orthonormal frame

\{E_{i}\}
. Assume, in addition,
\mathcal{I}
,
\bar{h}_{2}^{ij}
,
\zeta
,
f_{3}
and
f_{4}
to have the properties described at the beginning of
Subsection
1.1
. Let
\mathcal{J}\subset\mathcal{I}
be an interval and assume
u_{b}:\bar{M}\times\mathcal{J}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}}
and

v_{a}:\bar{M}\times\mathcal{J}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n_{2}}
to be such that
\partial_{t}v_{a}
is well defined on
\bar{M}\times\mathcal{J}
; such that
u_{b}(\cdot,t)\in C^{2}
for all

t\in\mathcal{J}
; such that
v_{a}(\cdot,t)\in C^{2}
for all
t\in\mathcal{J}
; and such that
(\partial_{t}v_{a})(\cdot,t)\in C^{1}
for all
t\in\mathcal{J}
. Finally, assume

N_{1,b}(\cdot,t)
to be a
C^{2,1}
solution to (
25c
) for all
t\in\mathcal{J}
, and
N_{2,b}(\cdot,t)
to be a
C^{2,1}
solution to
(
25d
) for all
t\in\mathcal{J}
. Let
\mathbb{R}_{+}:=[0,\infty)
. Then there is a covering of
\bar{M}
(depending only on
(\bar{M},\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}})
) and
functions
\mathcal{B}_{1}:\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
and
\mathcal{B}_{2}:\mathbb{R}_{+}^{3}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
that are continuous and increasing in each of their arguments,
such that

(26a)

\displaystyle\|N_{1,b}(\cdot,t)\|_{C^{2,1}}\leq

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{1}[\|u_{b}(\cdot,t)\|_{C^{2}},\|v_{a}(\cdot,t)\|_{C^{1}}],

(26b)

\displaystyle\|N_{2,b}(\cdot,t)\|_{C^{2,1}}\leq

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{2}[\|u_{b}(\cdot,t)\|_{C^{2}},\|v_{a}(\cdot,t)\|_{C^{2}},\|(\partial_{t}v_{a})(\cdot,t)\|_{C^{1}}]

for all
t\in\mathcal{J}
. Here the functions
\mathcal{B}_{i}
only depend on bounds on
\bar{h}_{2}^{ij}
,
\zeta
,
f_{3}
and
f_{4}
and
their derivatives up to order two, one, one and one respectively;
(\bar{M},\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}})
; the covering; the frame
\{E_{i}\}
; the
\lambda
appearing in
(
1
); and the
\lambda_{\zeta}
appearing in (
2
).

Remark 16
.

In the statement of the lemma we use the notation
C^{k,\alpha}=C^{k,\alpha}(h_{\mathrm{ref}})
for the sake of brevity; see
Appendix
B
for a definition of Hölder spaces and norms.

Remark 17
.

In case
f_{3}
is such that
f_{3}(\xi)=0
for all
\xi
with the property that the last
(n+1)n_{1}+n_{2}
components of
\xi
equal zero, then
(
26a
) can be improved to

(27)

\|N_{1,b}(\cdot,t)\|_{C^{2,1}}\leq\mathcal{B}_{3}[\|u_{b}(\cdot,t)\|_{C^{2}},\|v_{a}(\cdot,t)\|_{C^{1}}]\cdot(\|u_{b}(\cdot,t)\|_{C^{2}}+\|v_{a}(\cdot,t)\|_{C^{1}}),

where
\mathcal{B}_{3}
has the same dependence and properties as
\mathcal{B}_{1}
except that it depends on one more derivative of
f_{3}
. Similarly, in case,
in addition,
f_{4}
is such that
f_{4}(\xi)=0
for all
\xi
with the property that the last
(n+1)n_{1}+(n+2)n_{2}+(n+2)(n+1)/2
components of

\xi
equal zero, then (
26b
) can be improved to

(28)

\begin{split}\|N_{2,b}(\cdot,t)\|_{C^{2,1}}\leq&\mathcal{B}_{4}[\|u_{b}(\cdot,t)\|_{C^{2}},\|v_{a}(\cdot,t)\|_{C^{2}},\|\partial_{t}v_{a}(\cdot,t)\|_{C^{1}}]\\
&\cdot(\|u_{b}(\cdot,t)\|_{C^{2}}+\|v_{a}(\cdot,t)\|_{C^{2}}+\|\partial_{t}v_{a}(\cdot,t)\|_{C^{1}}),\end{split}

where
\mathcal{B}_{4}
has the same dependence and properties as
\mathcal{B}_{2}
except that it depends on one more derivative of
f_{3}
and
f_{4}
.

Proof.

We are here only interested in considering one
\bar{M}_{t}:=\bar{M}\times\{t\}
at a time; i.e., we focus on
N_{1,b}(\cdot,t)
etc. However,
for the sake of brevity, we consistently omit the argument
(\cdot,t)
in what follows. Since
\bar{h}_{2}^{ij}
only depends on
u
, the
metric
\bar{h}_{2}[u_{b}]
is a
C^{2}
metric on
\bar{M}_{t}
. Since
\zeta
and
f_{3}
only depend on
u
,
E_{i}u
and
v
, it is clear that
f_{3}[u_{b},v_{a}]

and
\zeta[u_{b},v_{a}]
are both
C^{1}
. Finally, note that since
\bar{h}_{2}^{ij}
and
\zeta
satisfy (
1
) and
(
2
) respectively, (
220
) below is satisfied. By an application of Theorem
83
below
to (
25c
), we conclude that (
26a
) holds. In case
f_{3}
is such that
f_{3}(\xi)=0
for all
\xi
with the property
that the last
(n+1)n_{1}+n_{2}
components of
\xi
equal zero, we can rewrite
f_{3}[u_{b},v_{a}]
as

(29)

f_{3}[u_{b},v_{a}]=\textstyle{\int}_{0}^{1}(\partial_{u}f_{3})[su_{b},sv_{a}]ds\cdot(u_{b},E_{1}u_{b},\dots,E_{n}u_{b})+\textstyle{\int}_{0}^{1}(\partial_{v}f_{3})[su_{b},sv_{a}]ds\cdot v_{a}.

Here
\partial_{u}f_{3}
denotes the derivative of
f_{3}
with respect to all the
u
-related arguments; i.e.,
u
,
E_{1}u,\dots,E_{n}u
. Applying
Theorem
83
below to (
25c
), using the representation (
29
) when estimating the last factor
on the right hand side of (
222
), yields the improvement (
27
).

Turning to
f_{4}
, note that it depends on
N_{1,b}
,
E_{i}N_{1,b}
and
E_{i}E_{j}N_{1,b}
. However, bounds on these quantities in
C^{0,1}
follow from
(
26a
) (or (
27
), in case
f_{3}
satisfies the condition stated prior to (
27
)). Beyond these
quantities,
f_{4}
only depends on
u_{b}
,
v_{a}
,
E_{i}u_{b}
,
E_{i}v_{a}
and
\partial_{t}v_{a}
. These objects are clearly bounded in
C^{0,1}
by

\|u_{b}\|_{C^{2}}
,
\|v_{a}\|_{C^{2}}
and
\|\partial_{t}v_{a}\|_{C^{1}}
. Applying Theorem
83
to (
25d
), keeping
(
26a
) in mind, yields the conclusion that (
26b
) holds. In case
f_{4}
satisfies the condition stated prior to
(
28
), then there is a representation of
f_{4}
analogous to (
29
). The derivation of (
28
)
is therefore similar to the derivation of (
27
). There is, however, one difference:
f_{4}
depends on up to two derivatives of
N_{1,b}
.
This difference can, however, be handled by appealing to (
27
), assuming
f_{3}
satisfies the condition stated prior to
(
27
). We conclude that (
28
) holds.
∎

In the proof of local existence, we, unfortunately, also need to control
\partial_{t}N_{1,b}
in
C^{1}
.

Lemma 18
.

With assumptions, notation and conclusions as in Lemma
14
, there is a covering of
\bar{M}
(depending only on
(\bar{M},\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}})
)
and a function
\mathcal{C}:\mathbb{R}_{+}^{5}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
which is continuous and increasing in each of its arguments, such that

(30)

\begin{split}&\|\partial_{t}N_{1,b}(\cdot,t)\|_{C^{2,1}}\\
\leq&\mathcal{C}[\|u_{a}(\cdot,t)\|_{C^{2}},\|u_{b}(\cdot,t)\|_{C^{2}},\|v_{a}(\cdot,t)\|_{C^{2}},\|\partial_{t}v_{a}(\cdot,t)\|_{C^{1}},\|N_{1,a}(\cdot,t)\|_{C^{2,1}}],\end{split}

for all
t\in\mathcal{I}
. Here the function
\mathcal{C}
only depends on bounds on

\bar{h}_{2}^{ij}
,
\zeta
,
f_{1}
and
f_{3}
and their derivatives up to order three, two, two and two respectively;
(\bar{M},\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}})
; the covering;
the frame
\{E_{i}\}
; the
\lambda
appearing in (
1
); and the
\lambda_{\zeta}
appearing in (
2
).

Remark 19
.

Remark
16
is equally relevant here.

Proof.

As in the proof of Lemma
15
, we omit the argument
(\cdot,t)
in the present proof. Differentiating (
25c
) yields

\Delta_{\bar{h}_{2}[u_{b}]}(\partial_{t}N_{1,b})=\zeta[u_{b},v_{a}]\partial_{t}N_{1,b}+g[u_{a},u_{b},v_{a},N_{1,a},N_{1,b}],

where
g
is a smooth function depending only on the spacetime coordinates,
u_{a}
,
E_{i}u_{a}
,
u_{b}
,
E_{i}u_{b}
,
v_{a}
,
E_{i}v_{a}
,

\partial_{t}v_{a}
,
N_{1,a}
,
E_{i}N_{1,a}
,
E_{i}E_{j}N_{1,a}
,
N_{1,b}
,
E_{i}N_{1,b}
and
E_{i}E_{j}N_{1,b}
. In order to arrive at this conclusion
we used the fact that (
25a
) holds. Moreover,
g
is a linear combination of terms built up of the following factors: components
of
(\bar{h}_{2}^{ij})^{(m)}[u_{b}]
,

\zeta^{(l)}[u_{b},v_{a}]
,
f_{1}^{(l)}[u_{a},v_{a},N_{1,a}]
and
f_{3}^{(l)}[u_{b},v_{a}]
, where
m\in\{0,1,2\}
and
l\in\{0,1\}
(here
\zeta^{(l)}

denotes the
l
’th derivative of
\zeta
); the components of the inverse of the matrix with components
\bar{h}_{2}^{ij}[u_{b}]
; and factors on which
g

depends; i.e.,
u_{a}
,
E_{i}u_{a}
etc. In particular, this means that there is a function
\mathcal{C}_{g}:\mathbb{R}_{+}^{6}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
which is continuous
and increasing in each of its arguments such that

\begin{split}&\|g[u_{a},u_{b},v_{a},N_{1,a},N_{1,b}]\|_{C^{0,1}}\\
\leq&\mathcal{C}_{g}[\|u_{a}\|_{C^{2}},\|u_{b}\|_{C^{2}},\|v_{a}\|_{C^{2}},\|\partial_{t}v_{a}\|_{C^{1}},\|N_{1,a}\|_{C^{2,1}},\|N_{1,b}\|_{C^{2,1}}]\end{split}

for all
t\in\mathcal{I}
. Here the function
\mathcal{C}_{g}
only depends on bounds on

\bar{h}_{2}^{ij}
,
\zeta
,
f_{1}
and
f_{3}
and their derivatives up to order three, two, two and two respectively;
(\bar{M},\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}})
;
the frame
\{E_{i}\}
; and the
\lambda
appearing in (
1
). However, by appealing to (
26a
), dependence on

\|N_{1,b}\|_{C^{2,1}}
can be eliminated. The estimates for
\zeta[u_{b},v_{a}]
and
\bar{h}_{2}[u_{b}]
are the same as in the proof of
Lemma
15
. Appealing to Theorem
83
now yields the desired conclusion.
∎

2.3.
High order estimates for the lapse functions

Next, we bound the
N_{i,b}
,
i=1,2
, with respect to Sobolev norms.

Lemma 20
.

Given the assumptions and notation introduced in Lemma
14
, there is a covering of
\bar{M}
(depending only on
(\bar{M},\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}})
)
and a function
\mathcal{C}_{1}:\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
which is continuous and monotonically increasing in each of its arguments, such that

(31)

\|N_{1,b}(\cdot,t)\|_{H^{m+2}}\leq\mathcal{C}_{1}[\|u_{b}(\cdot,t)\|_{C^{2}},\|v_{a}(\cdot,t)\|_{C^{1}}]\left(\|u_{b}(\cdot,t)\|_{H^{m+1}}+\|v_{a}(\cdot,t)\|_{H^{m}}+1\right)

for all
t\in\mathcal{I}
. Here
\mathcal{C}_{1}
only depends on the covering;
(\bar{M},\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}})
;
\{E_{i}\}
;
m
;
\lambda
;
\lambda_{\zeta}
; a bound on up to

{\mathrm{max}}\{1,m\}
derivatives of
\zeta
and
f_{3}
; and up to
{\mathrm{max}}\{2,m+1\}
derivatives of
\bar{h}_{2}^{ij}
. Next, there is a function

\mathcal{C}_{2}:\mathbb{R}_{+}^{3}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
which is continuous and increasing in each of its arguments, such that

(32)

\begin{split}\|N_{2,b}(\cdot,t)\|_{H^{m+2}}\leq&\mathcal{C}_{2}[\|u_{b}(\cdot,t)\|_{C^{2}},\|v_{a}(\cdot,t)\|_{C^{2}},\|\partial_{t}v_{a}(\cdot,t)\|_{C^{1}}]\\
&\cdot\left(\|u_{b}(\cdot,t)\|_{H^{m+1}}+\|v_{a}(\cdot,t)\|_{H^{m+1}}+\|\partial_{t}v_{a}(\cdot,t)\|_{H^{m}}+1\right)\end{split}

for all
t\in\mathcal{I}
. Here
\mathcal{C}_{2}
only depends on the covering;
(\bar{M},\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}})
;
\{E_{i}\}
;
m
;
\lambda
;
\lambda_{\zeta}
; a bound on up to

{\mathrm{max}}\{1,m\}
derivatives of
\zeta
,
f_{3}
and
f_{4}
; and up to
{\mathrm{max}}\{2,m+1\}
derivatives of
\bar{h}_{2}^{ij}
.

Remark 21
.

In the proof and the statement of the lemma, we use the notation
C^{k}=C^{k}(h_{\mathrm{ref}})
,
C^{k,\alpha}=C^{k,\alpha}(h_{\mathrm{ref}})
and
H^{k}=H^{k}(h_{\mathrm{ref}})
for the sake of
brevity; see Appendix
B
for a definition of
C^{k}
- and Hölder spaces and norms, and
Appendix
A
for a definition of Sobolev spaces and norms.

Remark 22
.

If, in addition to the assumptions of the lemma,
m>n/2+1
and
f_{3}
is such that
f_{3}(\xi)=0
for all
\xi
with the property that the
last
(n+1)n_{1}+n_{2}
components of
\xi
equal zero, then (
31
) can be improved in the sense that the
+1
inside the
parenthesis on the right hand side can be removed. However, this improvement comes at the price of
\mathcal{C}_{1}
depending on one more derivative
of
\zeta
,
f_{3}
and
\bar{h}_{2}^{ij}
. Assuming, in addition,
f_{4}
to be such that
f_{4}(\xi)=0
for all
\xi
with the property that the
last
(n+1)n_{1}+(n+2)n_{2}+(n+2)(n+1)/2
components of
\xi
equal zero, then (
32
) can be improved in that the
+1

inside the parenthesis on the right can be removed. Again, this improvement comes at the price of
\mathcal{C}_{2}
depending on one more derivative
of
\zeta
,
f_{3}
,
f_{4}
and
\bar{h}_{2}^{ij}
.

Proof.

As in the proof of Lemma
15
, we omit the argument
(\cdot,t)
in the present proof.
We derive the desired estimates by appealing to Theorem
85
with
\Sigma=\bar{M}
,
h_{\mathrm{ref}}=\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}}
,
h=\bar{h}_{2}[u_{b}]
,

a=\zeta[u_{b},v_{a}]
and
f
equal to
-f_{3}[u_{b},v_{a}]
or
-f_{4}[u_{b},v_{a},N_{1,b}]
in the case of (
31
) and
(
32
) respectively. In order to verify that the conditions of Theorem
85
are satisfied, note
that (
1
) and (
2
) imply that (
224
) holds, with
c_{1}=\lambda
and

c_{2}=\lambda_{\zeta}
. The estimate (
31
) follows by combining (
227
), the norm equivalence stated in
Subsection
A.5
and the Moser estimates stated in (
202
). In order to obtain the improvement
described in Remark
22
,
we need to appeal to (
225
). Moreover, we need to estimate the terms appearing in the parenthesis on the right hand side.
When applying (
225
) to (
25c
),
f=-f_{3}[u_{b},v_{a}]
, and we can rewrite
f_{3}
as in (
29
).
Applying
E_{\mathbf{I}}
to (
29
) results in a linear combination of terms, each of which consists of one integral of a derivative of

f_{3}
(along
[su_{b},sv_{a}]
) and at least one factor consisting of a derivative of
u_{b}
or
v_{a}
. In order to estimate such a term in
L^{2}
,
we extract the integral in
L^{\infty}
and apply Moser estimates, see (
202
), to what remains. This results in an estimate of the
form (
31
), where the
+1
on the right hand side has been removed. However, the corresponding
\mathcal{C}_{1}
depends on one more
derivative of
f_{3}
(in what follows, we omit similar remarks). Next, we need to estimate the second term inside the parenthesis on the right hand
side of (
225
). In our case, this term takes the form

(33)

\begin{split}\|N_{1,b}\|_{C^{1}}\|\bar{h}_{2}[u_{b}]\|_{H^{m+1}}\leq&\|N_{1,b}\|_{C^{1}}\|\bar{h}_{2}[u_{b}]-\bar{h}_{2}[0]\|_{H^{m+1}}+\|N_{1,b}\|_{C^{1}}\|\bar{h}_{2}[0]\|_{H^{m+1}}.\end{split}

In the first term on the right hand side of (
33
), we can estimate the first factor by appealing to (
26a
)
and the second factor by an argument similar to the one used to estimate the first term inside the parenthesis on the right hand side of
(
225
); i.e.,
f_{3}[u_{b},v_{a}]
. The second factor in the second term on the right hand side of (
33
)
can be estimated by a constant depending only on
m
,
(\bar{M},\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}})
,
\{E_{i}\}
and up to
m+1
derivatives of
\bar{h}_{2}^{ij}
. In order to
estimate the first factor in the second term, it is sufficient to appeal to (
27
) and Sobolev embedding. The argument needed to
estimate the third term inside the parenthesis on the right hand side of (
225
) is essentially identical to the argument in the
case of the second term. The improvement of (
31
) described in Remark
22
follows.

In order to prove (
32
), we proceed in the same way. The only difference is that we, in this case, need to estimate

f_{4}[u_{b},v_{a},N_{1,b}]
in
C^{0,1}
and in
H^{m}
. Due to the dependence of
f_{4}
, estimating
f_{4}[u_{b},v_{a},N_{1,b}]
in
C^{0,1}
entails
estimating
u_{b}
in
C^{2}
,
v_{a}
in
C^{2}
,
\partial_{t}v_{a}
in
C^{1}
and
N_{1,b}
in
C^{2,1}
. Moreover, the dependence of the function
\mathcal{C}_{2}
on
these norms is continuous and increasing in each of the norms. However, the dependence on the
C^{2,1}
-norm of
N_{1,b}
can, by appealing to
(
26a
), be replaced by dependence on the
C^{2}
-norm of
u_{b}
and the
C^{1}
-norm of
v_{a}
. In order to estimate
f_{4}
in
H^{m}

we appeal to Moser estimates. This leads to a continuous dependence on
u_{b}
in
C^{1}
,
v_{a}
in
C^{1}
,
\partial_{t}v_{a}
in
C^{0}
and
N_{1,b}
in

C^{2}
(where the dependence on the
C^{2}
-norm of
N_{1,b}
can, by appealing to (
26a
), be replaced by continuous dependence on the

C^{2}
-norm on
u_{b}
and the
C^{1}
-norm of
v_{a}
). Moreover, we obtain linear dependence on the
H^{m+1}
-norm of
u_{b}
, the
H^{m+1}
-norm of

v_{a}
, the
H^{m}
-norm of
\partial_{t}v_{a}
and the
H^{m+2}
-norm of
N_{1,b}
. However, the latter dependence can, by appealing to
(
31
), be replaced by linear dependence on the
H^{m+1}
-norm of
u_{b}
and the
H^{m}
-norm of
v_{a}
. In order to obtain the improvement
described in Remark
22
, we, again, need to appeal to (
225
). The argument is very similar to the
proof of the improvement of (
31
). We leave the details to the reader. Let us, however, make two observations. First, when estimating

f_{4}[u_{b},v_{a},N_{1,b}]
in
H^{m}
by rewriting it in analogy with (
29
), we, in addition to the norms appearing in the
second factor on the right hand side of (
32
), need to estimate
\|N_{1,b}\|_{H^{m+2}}
. However, this can be done by
appealing to the improvement of (
31
) obtained in Remark
22
. Second, when estimating the second term inside
the parenthesis on the right hand side of (
225
), we first estimate it in analogy with (
33
) and then we
appeal to (
26b
), (
28
) and Sobolev embedding. The statements of the lemma and Remark
22
follow.
∎

2.4.
Bounding solutions to the simplified model system in the high norm; energy estimates

Next, we wish to estimate the following energy of the solution
u_{b}
to (
25a
):

E_{1,m+1}[u_{b}](t):=\tfrac{1}{2}\textstyle{\sum}_{|\mathbf{I}|\leq m+1}\int_{\bar{M}_{t}}|E_{\mathbf{I}}u_{b}|^{2}d\mu_{\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}}}=\frac{1}{2}\|u_{b}\|_{H^{m+1}(\bar{M}_{t})}^{2},

where we use the notation introduced in Appendix
A
, in particular Subsection
A.5
. Moreover,
we, for the sake of brevity, write
H^{k}(\bar{M}_{t})
instead of
H^{k}(\bar{M}_{t};\mathbb{E})
, where
\mathbb{E}:=\{E_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
. The bound we derive for

E_{1,m+1}[u_{b}]
depends on
u_{a}
,
v_{a}
and
N_{1,a}
. For that reason, it is convenient to introduce the notation

(34)

\begin{split}\mathfrak{N}_{a}(t):=&\|u_{a}\|_{H^{m+1}(\bar{M}_{t})}+\|v_{a}\|_{H^{m+1}(\bar{M}_{t})}+\|\partial_{t}v_{a}\|_{H^{m}(\bar{M}_{t})}\\
&+\|N_{1,a}\|_{H^{m+2}(\bar{M}_{t})}+\|N_{2,a}\|_{H^{m+2}(\bar{M}_{t})}+\iota_{\mathrm{ih}}.\end{split}

Here
\iota_{\mathrm{ih}}:=0
in case
f_{1}(p,0)=0
,
f_{2}(p,0)=0
,
f_{3}(p,0)=0
and
f_{4}(p,0)=0
for all
p\in M
. If one of these conditions is violated, then

\iota_{\mathrm{ih}}:=1
. Making this distinction makes it possible to apply the estimates below both to prove local existence and to prove
Cauchy stability. We include
N_{2,a}
in (
34
) since, even though the evolution of the norm of
u_{b}
does not depend on this quantity, the
evolution of the norm of
v_{b}
, e.g., does. The (time dependent) constants appearing in the estimates also depend on

(35)

\begin{split}\mathfrak{n}_{a}(t):=&\|u_{a}(\cdot,t)\|_{C^{2}}+\|v_{a}(\cdot,t)\|_{C^{2}}+\|\partial_{t}v_{a}(\cdot,t)\|_{C^{1}}\\
&+\|N_{1,a}(\cdot,t)\|_{C^{2,1}}+\|N_{2,a}(\cdot,t)\|_{C^{2,1}},\end{split}

where we use the notation
C^{k,\alpha}=C^{k,\alpha}(\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}})
; cf., e.g., (
30
), giving an estimate of
\partial_{t}N_{1,b}
which is needed to estimate
the time derivative of the energy associated with
v_{b}
. With this notation, there is a continuous and monotonically increasing function

\mathcal{C}_{a}:\mathbb{R}_{+}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that

(36)

\left|\partial_{t}E_{1,m+1}[u_{b}](t)\right|\leq\mathcal{C}_{a}[\mathfrak{n}_{a}(t)]\mathfrak{N}_{a}(t)E_{1,m+1}^{1/2}[u_{b}](t)

for all
t\in\mathcal{I}
. Here
\mathcal{C}_{a}
only depends on bounds on
m
,
\{E_{i}\}
,
(\bar{M},\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}})
,
f_{1}
and its partial derivatives up to order
m+2-\iota_{\mathrm{ih}}
.
In order to obtain this estimate, it is sufficient to appeal to (
25a
) and Moser estimates; see (
202
). In case
\iota_{\mathrm{ih}}=0
,
we rewrite
f_{1}[u_{a},v_{a},N_{1,a}]
in analogy with (
29
); this is what leads to an extra derivative in case
\iota_{\mathrm{ih}}=0
. In practice, a better
estimate than (
36
) holds, but this improvement will not be of use in our applications. Due to (
36
), we obtain bounds on

\|u_{b}\|_{H^{m+1}}
and, assuming
m>n/2+1
, on
\|u_{b}\|_{C^{2}}
. Below, we appeal to these bounds in order to control
v_{b}
and
N_{i,b}
,
i=1,2
.
For that reason, it is convenient to define

(37)

\mathfrak{N}_{a,b}(t):=\mathfrak{N}_{a}(t)+\|u_{b}\|_{H^{m+1}(\bar{M}_{t})},\ \ \ \mathfrak{n}_{a,b}(t):=\mathfrak{n}_{a}(t)+\|u_{b}(\cdot,t)\|_{C^{2}}.

Combining this notation with (
30
) yields the conclusion that there is a continuous and monotonically increasing function

\mathcal{C}_{1,b}:\mathbb{R}_{+}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that

(38)

\|\partial_{t}N_{1,b}(\cdot,t)\|_{C^{2,1}}\leq\mathcal{C}_{1,b}[\mathfrak{n}_{a,b}(t)],

where
\mathcal{C}_{1,b}
only depends on bounds on
\bar{h}_{2}^{ij}
,
\zeta
,
f_{1}
and
f_{3}
and their derivatives up to order three, two, two and two
respectively;
(\bar{M},\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}})
; the covering; the frame
\{E_{i}\}
; the
\lambda
appearing in (
1
); and the
\lambda_{\zeta}

appearing in (
2
).

Next, we wish to estimate the energy for
v_{b}
. Consider, to this end,

(39)

E_{2,m+1}[v_{b}]:=\tfrac{1}{2}\textstyle{\sum}_{|\mathbf{I}|\leq m}\int_{\bar{M}_{t}}(|\partial_{t}E_{\mathbf{I}}v_{b}|^{2}+\bar{h}^{ij}_{1}[u_{b},N_{1,b}]E_{i}E_{\mathbf{I}}v_{b}\cdot E_{j}E_{\mathbf{I}}v_{b}+|E_{\mathbf{I}}v_{b}|^{2})d\mu_{\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}}}.

Due to the second term on the right hand side of (
25b
), it is not possible to derive straightforward energy estimates for this
quantity. However,

(40)

\hat{E}_{2,m+1}[v_{b},u_{b}]:=E_{2,m+1}[v_{b}]+\textstyle{\sum}_{|\mathbf{I}|\leq m}\int_{\bar{M}_{t}}\gamma^{ij}[u_{b},v_{a},N_{1,b}]E_{j}E_{\mathbf{I}}(u_{b})\cdot E_{i}E_{\mathbf{I}}v_{b}d\mu_{\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}}}

is an object for which we can estimate the time evolution. On the other hand, this quantity has the disadvantage that it does not dominate the
Sobolev norms we wish to control. For this reason, it is convenient to let

\alpha_{\gamma}:=\textstyle{\sup}_{\xi\in M\times\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}+n_{2}+n+1}}\textstyle{\sum}_{i,j}\|\gamma^{ij}(\xi))\|;

note that we assume
\gamma^{ij}
to be globally bounded. Then

\big|\textstyle{\sum}_{|\mathbf{I}|\leq m}\int_{\bar{M}_{t}}\gamma^{ij}E_{j}E_{\mathbf{I}}(u_{b})\cdot E_{i}E_{\mathbf{I}}v_{b}d\mu_{\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}}}\big|\leq\tfrac{1}{2}E_{2,m+1}[v_{b}]+C_{\lambda,n}\alpha_{\gamma}^{2}E_{1,m+1}[u_{b}],

where
C_{\lambda,n}
only depends on
\lambda
and
n
. Introducing

(41)

\beta_{\gamma}:=C_{\lambda,n}\alpha_{\gamma}^{2}+1,

we conclude that if

(42)

\check{E}_{2,m+1}[v_{b},u_{b}]:=\hat{E}_{2,m+1}[v_{b},u_{b}]+\beta_{\gamma}E_{1,m+1}[u_{b}],

then

(43)

E_{1,m+1}[u_{b}](t)+\tfrac{1}{2}E_{2,m+1}[v_{b}](t)\leq\check{E}_{2,m+1}[v_{b},u_{b}](t).

We derive the following estimate for
\check{E}_{2,m+1}[v_{b},u_{b}]
.

Lemma 23
.

With assumptions, notation and conclusions as in Lemma
14
, fix
m\in\mathbb{N}
with
m>n/2+1
and define
\check{E}_{2,m+1}[v_{b},u_{b}]
by
(
42
). Then there is a covering of
\bar{M}
(depending only on
(\bar{M},\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}})
) and a continuous and monotonically increasing function

\mathcal{C}_{m}:\mathbb{R}_{+}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that, given
t_{1}\in\mathcal{I}
,

(44)

\begin{split}&|\check{E}_{2,m+1}[v_{b},u_{b}](t_{1})-\check{E}_{2,m+1}[v_{b},u_{b}](t_{0})|\\
\leq&\big|\textstyle{\int}_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\mathcal{C}_{m}\circ\mathfrak{n}_{a,b}\cdot\big(E_{2,m+1}[v_{b}]+\mathfrak{N}_{a,b}(\|v_{b}\|_{C^{1}}+1)E_{2,m+1}^{1/2}[v_{b}]+\mathfrak{N}_{a,b}^{2}\big)dt\big|.\end{split}

Here
\mathcal{C}_{m}
only depends on bounds on up to
{\mathrm{max}}\{3,m+2-\iota_{\mathrm{ih}}\}
derivatives of
\bar{h}_{2}^{ij}
;
m+1-\iota_{\mathrm{ih}}
derivatives of
\zeta
,
f_{1}
,
f_{2}

and
f_{3}
; and
m+1
derivatives of
\gamma^{ij}
and
\bar{h}_{1}^{ij}
;
(\bar{M},\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}})
; the covering; the frame
\{E_{i}\}
;
m
;
\lambda
; and

\lambda_{\zeta}
.

Remark 24
.

Due to (
43
), we can replace
E_{2,m+1}
with
\check{E}_{2,m+1}
on the right hand side of (
44
). Moreover,
since
m>n/2
,
\|v_{b}\|_{C^{1}}
can, due to Sobolev embedding, be replaced by the square root of
\check{E}_{2,m+1}
. Combining these observations
with (
44
), the prior bound on
\mathfrak{N}_{a,b}
and Grönwall’s lemma yields a bound on
\check{E}_{2,m+1}
.

Proof.

Time differentiating
E_{2,m+1}
yields

(45)

\begin{split}\partial_{t}E_{2,m+1}[v_{b}]:=&\textstyle{\sum}_{|\mathbf{I}|\leq m}\int_{\bar{M}_{t}}(\partial_{t}^{2}E_{\mathbf{I}}v_{b}\cdot\partial_{t}E_{\mathbf{I}}v_{b}+(\partial_{t}\bar{h}_{1}^{ij}/2)E_{i}E_{\mathbf{I}}v_{b}\cdot E_{j}E_{\mathbf{I}}v_{b}\\
&\phantom{\textstyle{\sum_{i=1}\int\bar{M}_{t}}+}+\bar{h}_{1}^{ij}E_{i}E_{\mathbf{I}}v_{b}\cdot E_{j}E_{\mathbf{I}}\partial_{t}v_{b}+E_{\mathbf{I}}v_{b}\cdot E_{\mathbf{I}}\partial_{t}v_{b})d\mu_{\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}}}.\end{split}

In order to estimate the contribution from the second term in the integrand, note that
\partial_{t}\bar{h}_{1}^{ij}
is a linear combination of terms
consisting of four types
of factors: a first derivative of
\bar{h}_{1}^{ij}
;
\partial_{t}u_{b}
;
\partial_{t}N_{1,b}
; and
E_{i}\partial_{t}N_{1,b}
. Appealing to (
25a
),
(
38
) and the assumptions, cf. Subsection
1.1
, it is clear that the contribution can be estimated by

\mathcal{C}[\mathfrak{n}_{a,b}(t)]E_{2,m+1}
, where
\mathcal{C}:\mathbb{R}_{+}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
is continuous and monotonic. Moreover,
\mathcal{C}
only depends on bounds on
\bar{h}_{2}^{ij}
,

\zeta
,
f_{1}
,
f_{3}
,
\gamma^{ij}
and
\bar{h}_{1}^{ij}
and their derivatives up to order three, two, two, two, two, one and one respectively;

(\bar{M},\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}})
; the covering; the frame
\{E_{i}\}
;
m
; the
\lambda
appearing in (
1
); and the
\lambda_{\zeta}
appearing in
(
2
). Here we include dependence on, e.g.,
\gamma^{ij}
, which is strictly speaking not necessary. The reason for this
is that we wish to include all the dependence that will appear in the course of the proof, instead of adding dependence incrementally.
The contribution from the fourth term in the integrand of the right hand side of (
45
) can be estimated by a numerical
constant times
E_{2,m+1}[v_{b}]
. Next, consider

(46)

\begin{split}\textstyle{\int}_{\bar{M}_{t}}\bar{h}_{1}^{ij}E_{i}E_{\mathbf{I}}v_{b}\cdot E_{j}E_{\mathbf{I}}\partial_{t}v_{b}d\mu_{\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}}}=&-\textstyle{\int}_{\bar{M}_{t}}E_{j}(\bar{h}_{1}^{ij})E_{i}E_{\mathbf{I}}v_{b}\cdot E_{\mathbf{I}}\partial_{t}v_{b}d\mu_{\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}}}\\
&-\textstyle{\int}_{\bar{M}_{t}}\bar{h}_{1}^{ij}E_{j}E_{i}E_{\mathbf{I}}v_{b}\cdot E_{\mathbf{I}}\partial_{t}v_{b}d\mu_{\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}}}\\
&-\textstyle{\int}_{\bar{M}_{t}}\bar{h}_{1}^{ij}E_{i}E_{\mathbf{I}}v_{b}\cdot E_{\mathbf{I}}\partial_{t}v_{b}\mathrm{div}_{\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}}}E_{j}d\mu_{\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}}};\end{split}

cf., e.g., the derivation of
[
20
, (B.7), p. 211]
.
By (
26a
) and arguments similar to the above, the first term on the right hand side can be estimated by
\mathcal{C}[\mathfrak{n}_{a,b}(t)]E_{2,m+1}
,
where
\mathcal{C}
has the same dependence as before. The contribution from the third term on the right hand side of (
46
) can be estimated
by a constant times
E_{2,m+1}
, where the constant only depends on
\lambda
,
(\bar{M},\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}})
and
\{E_{i}\}
. In order to estimate the second term
on the right hand side of (
46
), we need to consider
[\bar{h}_{1}^{ij}E_{j}E_{i},E_{\mathbf{I}}]
. This expression is a sum of terms of the form

\lambda_{\mathbf{I}_{1},\mathbf{I}_{2}}E_{\mathbf{I}_{1}}(\bar{h}_{1}^{ij})E_{\mathbf{I}_{2}}
,
where
\lambda_{\mathbf{I}_{1},\mathbf{I}_{2}}
only depends on the frame,
\mathbf{I}_{1}
and
\mathbf{I}_{2}
;
|\mathbf{I}_{1}|+|\mathbf{I}_{2}|\leq|\mathbf{I}|+2
;
|\mathbf{I}_{1}|\geq 1
if

|\mathbf{I}_{1}|+|\mathbf{I}_{2}|=|\mathbf{I}|+2
; and
|\mathbf{I}_{2}|\geq 1
. We thus need to estimate

(47)

\|E_{\mathbf{I}_{1}}(\bar{h}_{1}^{ij})E_{\mathbf{I}_{2}}v_{b}\|_{L^{2}}.

Expanding the first factor inside the norm leads to a linear combination of terms consisting of derivatives of
\bar{h}_{1}^{ij}
times spatial derivatives
applied to
u_{b}
,
N_{1,b}
and
E_{i}N_{1,b}
. Extracting the derivatives of
\bar{h}_{1}^{ij}
in the supremum norm, applying the Moser estimate (
202
)
to what remains and appealing to (
26a
) and (
31
) (and, in case
\iota_{\mathrm{ih}}=0
, to Remark
22
) yields

\|E_{\mathbf{I}_{1}}(\bar{h}_{1}^{ij})E_{\mathbf{I}_{2}}v_{b}\|_{L^{2}}\leq\mathcal{C}_{m}[\mathfrak{n}_{a,b}(t)]\big(\|v_{b}\|_{C^{1}}\mathfrak{N}_{a,b}+E_{2,m+1}^{1/2}[v_{b}]\big),

where
\mathcal{C}_{m}
, in addition to the dependence of
\mathcal{C}
, only depends on
m
; bounds on up to
m+1-\iota_{\mathrm{ih}}
derivatives of
\zeta
,
f_{1}
,
f_{2}

and
f_{3}
; bounds on up to
m+2-\iota_{\mathrm{ih}}
derivatives of
\bar{h}_{2}^{ij}
;
and bounds on up to
m+1
derivatives of
\gamma^{ij}
and
\bar{h}_{1}^{ij}
(we here add dependence that will only be necessary later on). Adding up,

(48)

\begin{split}&\big|\partial_{t}E_{2,m+1}[v_{b}]-\textstyle{\sum}_{|\mathbf{I}|\leq m}\int_{\bar{M}_{t}}E_{\mathbf{I}}(\partial_{t}^{2}v_{b}-\bar{h}_{1}^{ij}E_{i}E_{j}v_{b})\cdot\partial_{t}E_{\mathbf{I}}v_{b}d\mu_{\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}}}\big|\\
\leq&\mathcal{C}_{m}[\mathfrak{n}_{a,b}(t)]\big(E_{2,m+1}[v_{b}]+\|v_{b}\|_{C^{1}}\mathfrak{N}_{a,b}E_{2,m+1}^{1/2}[v_{b}]\big),\end{split}

where
\mathcal{C}_{m}
has the dependence described above. Next, note that

(49)

\begin{split}&\textstyle{\sum}_{|\mathbf{I}|\leq m}\int_{\bar{M}_{t}}E_{\mathbf{I}}(\partial_{t}^{2}v_{b}-\bar{h}_{1}^{ij}E_{i}E_{j}v_{b})\cdot\partial_{t}E_{\mathbf{I}}v_{b}d\mu_{\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}}}\\
=&\textstyle{\sum}_{|\mathbf{I}|\leq m}\int_{\bar{M}_{t}}E_{\mathbf{I}}(\gamma^{ij}E_{i}E_{j}u_{b})\cdot\partial_{t}E_{\mathbf{I}}v_{b}d\mu_{\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}}}+\sum_{|\mathbf{I}|\leq m}\int_{\bar{M}_{t}}E_{\mathbf{I}}f_{2}\cdot\partial_{t}E_{\mathbf{I}}v_{b}d\mu_{\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}}}.\end{split}

The second term can be estimated by appealing to Moser estimates; it is bounded in absolute value by
\mathcal{C}_{m}[\mathfrak{n}_{a,b}(t)]\mathfrak{N}_{a}E_{2,m+1}^{1/2}[v_{b}]
,
where
\mathcal{C}_{m}
has the dependence described above. Note that, in case
\iota_{\mathrm{ih}}=0
, we first rewrite
f_{2}
in analogy with (
29
).
In the first term on the right hand side of (
49
), if not all the derivatives hit
u_{b}
, the corresponding expressions can
estimated similarly to the estimate of (
47
). What we are left with is a sum of terms of the form

\begin{split}&\textstyle{\int}_{\bar{M}_{t}}\gamma^{ij}E_{i}E_{j}E_{\mathbf{I}}u_{b}\cdot\partial_{t}E_{\mathbf{I}}v_{b}d\mu_{\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}}}\\
=&-\textstyle{\int}_{\bar{M}_{t}}E_{i}(\gamma^{ij})E_{j}E_{\mathbf{I}}u_{b}\cdot\partial_{t}E_{\mathbf{I}}v_{b}d\mu_{\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}}}-\int_{\bar{M}_{t}}\gamma^{ij}E_{j}E_{\mathbf{I}}u_{b}\cdot\partial_{t}E_{i}E_{\mathbf{I}}v_{b}d\mu_{\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}}}\\
&-\textstyle{\int}_{\bar{M}_{t}}\gamma^{ij}E_{j}E_{\mathbf{I}}u_{b}\cdot\partial_{t}E_{\mathbf{I}}v_{b}\cdot(\mathrm{div}_{\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}}}E_{i})d\mu_{\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}}}.\end{split}

The first and last terms can be estimated similarly to the above. In order to estimate the second term, we also need to integrate in time. In fact,

\begin{split}&-\textstyle{\int}_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\int_{\bar{M}_{t}}\gamma^{ij}E_{j}E_{\mathbf{I}}u_{b}\cdot\partial_{t}E_{i}E_{\mathbf{I}}v_{b}d\mu_{\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}}}dt\\
=&-\big[\textstyle{\int}_{\bar{M}_{t}}\gamma^{ij}E_{j}E_{\mathbf{I}}u_{b}\cdot E_{i}E_{\mathbf{I}}v_{b}d\mu_{\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}}}\big]_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}+\textstyle{\int}_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\int_{\bar{M}_{t}}\partial_{t}(\gamma^{ij})E_{j}E_{\mathbf{I}}u_{b}\cdot E_{i}E_{\mathbf{I}}v_{b}d\mu_{\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}}}dt\\
&+\textstyle{\int}_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\int_{\bar{M}_{t}}\gamma^{ij}E_{j}E_{\mathbf{I}}(\partial_{t}u_{b})\cdot E_{i}E_{\mathbf{I}}v_{b}d\mu_{\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}}}dt.\end{split}

In order to estimate the second term on the right hand side, we proceed as in the estimate of the contribution from the second term in the
integrand on the right hand side of (
45
). The third term on the right hand side can be estimated by appealing to
(
25a
) and Moser estimates; again, we first rewrite
f_{1}
in analogy with (
29
) in case
\iota_{\mathrm{ih}}=0
. The
first term gives rise to boundary terms that have to be included in the energy. This is what leads to the energy defined in (
40
).
The above estimates lead to the conclusion that

(50)

\begin{split}&\big|\hat{E}_{2,m+1}[v_{b},u_{b}](t_{1})-\hat{E}_{2,m+1}[v_{b},u_{b}](t_{0})\big|\\
\leq&\big|\textstyle{\int}_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\mathcal{C}_{m}[\mathfrak{n}_{a,b}(t)](E_{2,m+1}[v_{b}]+(\|v_{b}\|_{C^{1}}+1)\mathfrak{N}_{a,b}E_{2,m+1}^{1/2}[v_{b}])dt\big|.\end{split}

Combining (
50
) with (
36
) and (
42
) yields (
44
). The lemma follows.
∎

2.5.
Convergence in a low norm

Next, we estimate differences of solutions in a low norm. The relevant situation to consider is the following. Fix a model system.
Then, using notation and assumptions as in Subsection
1.1
, let
\mathcal{J}\subseteq\mathcal{I}
,
M_{\mathcal{J}}:=\bar{M}\times\mathcal{J}
, and
assume that

(51a)

\displaystyle u_{a,i},u_{b,i}\in

\displaystyle C^{1}(\mathcal{J},C^{1}(\bar{M},\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}}))\cap C^{0}(\mathcal{J},C^{2}(\bar{M},\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}})),

(51b)

\displaystyle v_{a,i},v_{b,i}\in

\displaystyle C^{2}(\mathcal{J},C^{0}(\bar{M},\mathbb{R}^{n_{2}}))\cap C^{1}(\mathcal{J},C^{1}(\bar{M},\mathbb{R}^{n_{2}}))\cap C^{0}(\mathcal{J},C^{2}(\bar{M},\mathbb{R}^{n_{2}})),

(51c)

\displaystyle N_{1,a,i},N_{1,b,i}\in

\displaystyle C^{1}(\mathcal{J},C^{1}(\bar{M}))\cap C^{0}(\mathcal{J},C^{2,1}(\bar{M})),

(51d)

\displaystyle N_{2,a,i},N_{2,b,i}\in

\displaystyle C^{0}(\mathcal{J},C^{2,1}(\bar{M}))

for
i=1,2
. Assume, finally, that
u_{b,i},v_{b,i},N_{1,b,i},N_{2,b,i}
are solutions to (
25
) corresponding to

u_{a,i},v_{a,i},N_{1,a,i},N_{2,a,i}
for
i=1,2
. In practice, we are going to apply the results of the present subsection in two different settings.
One setting is when proving uniqueness of solutions. The other setting is when proving convergence of an iteration. In the latter setting, the
u_{a,i}

etc. are given smooth functions on
M
. Moreover, we specify smooth initial data
u_{0,i}
,
v_{0,0,i}
,
v_{0,1,i}
,
i=1,2
, as in the statement of
Lemma
14
. Appealing to Lemma
14
then yields corresponding solutions
u_{b,i}
,
v_{b,i}
,
N_{l,b,i}
. In what
follows, it is convenient to use the notation

(52)

U_{a}:=u_{a,2}-u_{a,1},\ \ \ V_{a}:=v_{a,2}-v_{a,1},\ \ \ L_{l,a}:=N_{l,a,2}-N_{l,a,1},

l=1,2
. We similarly define
U_{b}:=u_{b,2}-u_{b,1}
etc. In analogy with (
34
), we introduce the notation

(53)

\begin{split}\mathcal{N}_{a}(t):=&\|U_{a}\|_{H^{1}(\bar{M}_{t})}+\|V_{a}\|_{H^{1}(\bar{M}_{t})}+\|\partial_{t}V_{a}\|_{L^{2}(\bar{M}_{t})}\\
&+\|L_{1,a}\|_{H^{2}(\bar{M}_{t})}+\|L_{2,a}\|_{H^{2}(\bar{M}_{t})}.\end{split}

Since the constants appearing in the estimates depend on the norms of both
u_{a,1}
and
u_{a,2}
etc., it is also convenient to use the notation

(54)

\begin{split}\mathfrak{l}_{a}(t):=&\textstyle{\sum}_{i=1}^{2}\|u_{a,i}(\cdot,t)\|_{C^{2}}+\textstyle{\sum}_{i=1}^{2}\|v_{a,i}(\cdot,t)\|_{C^{2}}+\textstyle{\sum}_{i=1}^{2}\|\partial_{t}v_{a,i}(\cdot,t)\|_{C^{1}}\\
&+\textstyle{\sum}_{i=1}^{2}\|N_{1,a,i}(\cdot,t)\|_{C^{2,1}}+\textstyle{\sum}_{i=1}^{2}\|N_{2,a,i}(\cdot,t)\|_{C^{2,1}},\end{split}

where
C^{k,\alpha}=C^{k,\alpha}(\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}})
. Similarly to (
37
), it is also convenient to introduce the notation

(55a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{a,b}(t):=

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{a}(t)+\|U_{b}\|_{H^{1}(\bar{M}_{t})},

(55b)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{l}_{a,b}(t):=

\displaystyle\mathfrak{l}_{a}(t)+\textstyle{\sum}_{i=1}^{2}\|u_{b,i}(\cdot,t)\|_{C^{2}}.

Estimates for the lapse function.
We begin by estimating
L_{1,b}
and
L_{2,b}
.

Lemma 25
.

Given the above assumptions and notation, there is a covering of
\bar{M}
(depending only on
(\bar{M},\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}})
) and a continuous and monotonically
increasing function
\mathfrak{C}_{L}:\mathbb{R}_{+}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that

(56)

\displaystyle\|L_{1,b}\|_{H^{2}(\bar{M}_{t})}\leq

\displaystyle\mathfrak{C}_{L}[\mathfrak{l}_{a,b}(t)]\left(\|U_{b}\|_{H^{1}(\bar{M}_{t})}+\|V_{a}\|_{L^{2}(\bar{M}_{t})}\right),

(57)

\displaystyle\|L_{2,b}\|_{H^{2}(\bar{M}_{t})}\leq

\displaystyle\mathfrak{C}_{L}[\mathfrak{l}_{a,b}(t)]\left(\|U_{b}\|_{H^{1}(\bar{M}_{t})}+\|V_{a}\|_{H^{1}(\bar{M}_{t})}+\|\partial_{t}V_{a}\|_{L^{2}(\bar{M}_{t})}\right)

for
t\in\mathcal{J}
. Here
\mathfrak{C}_{L}
only depends on bounds on
\bar{h}_{2}^{ij}
,
\zeta
,
f_{3}
and
f_{4}
and their derivatives up to order two, one,
one and one respectively;
(\bar{M},\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}})
; the covering; the frame
\{E_{i}\}
;
\lambda
; and
\lambda_{\zeta}
.

Proof.

Due to Lemma
15
, there is a function
\mathfrak{C}
, with the same properties and dependence as
\mathfrak{C}_{L}
appearing in the statement,
such that

(58)

\|N_{1,b,1}\|_{C^{2,1}}+\|N_{1,b,2}\|_{C^{2,1}}+\|N_{2,b,1}\|_{C^{2,1}}+\|N_{2,b,2}\|_{C^{2,1}}\leq\mathfrak{C}[\mathfrak{l}_{a,b}(t)]

for all
t\in\mathcal{J}
; as before, we ignore the argument
(\cdot,t)
. Next, due to (
25c
),

(59)

\begin{split}\Delta_{\bar{h}_{2}[u_{b,1}]}L_{1,b}=&\zeta[u_{b,2},v_{a,2}]L_{1,b}+(\Delta_{\bar{h}_{2}[u_{b,1}]}-\Delta_{\bar{h}_{2}[u_{b,2}]})N_{1,b,2}\\
&+(\zeta[u_{b,2},v_{a,2}]-\zeta[u_{b,1},v_{a,1}])N_{1,b,1}+f_{3}[u_{b,2},v_{a,2}]-f_{3}[u_{b,1},v_{a,1}].\end{split}

At this stage, we can appeal to Proposition
97
and Remark
98
,
with
h=\bar{h}_{2}[u_{b,1}]
,
a=\zeta[u_{b,2},v_{a,2}]
and
u=L_{1,b}
. Keeping (
58
) in mind, this yields (
56
).
Next, note that (
25d
) yields

(60)

\begin{split}\Delta_{\bar{h}_{2}[u_{b,1}]}L_{2,b}=&\zeta[u_{b,2},v_{a,2}]L_{2,b}+(\Delta_{\bar{h}_{2}[u_{b,1}]}-\Delta_{\bar{h}_{2}[u_{b,2}]})N_{2,b,2}\\
&+(\zeta[u_{b,2},v_{a,2}]-\zeta[u_{b,1},v_{a,1}])N_{1,b,2}\\
&+f_{4}[u_{b,2},v_{a,2},N_{1,b,2}]-f_{4}[u_{b,1},v_{a,1},N_{1,b,1}].\end{split}

Again, we can appeal to Proposition
97
and Remark
98
,
with
h=\bar{h}_{2}[u_{b,1}]
,
a=\zeta[u_{b,2},v_{a,2}]
and
u=L_{2,b}
. Keeping (
58
) in mind, this yields an estimate
analogous to (
57
), but with the one difference that there is an additional term
\|L_{1,b}\|_{H^{2}(\bar{M}_{t})}
in the paranthesis
on the right hand side. However, this term can be eliminated by appealing to (
56
).
∎

Energy estimates.
In order to control
U_{b}
, we introduce the energy

(61)

\mathcal{E}_{1}:=\tfrac{1}{2}\textstyle{\sum}_{|\mathbf{I}|\leq 1}\int_{\bar{M}}|E_{\mathbf{I}}U_{b}|^{2}d\mu_{\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}}}.

In order to control
V_{b}
, we, in analogy with the derivation of the bounds in the high norm, introduce three energies.
To begin with, let

(62)

\mathcal{E}_{2}:=\tfrac{1}{2}\textstyle{\int}_{\bar{M}}[|\partial_{t}V_{b}|^{2}+\bar{h}^{ij}_{1}[u_{b,1},N_{1,b,1}]E_{i}V_{b}\cdot E_{j}V_{b}+|V_{b}|^{2}]d\mu_{\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}}}.

Due to the second term on the right hand side of (
25b
), we are not able to derive estimates for this energy
directly. Instead, we have to consider

(63)

\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{2}:=\mathcal{E}_{2}+\textstyle{\int}_{\bar{M}}\gamma^{ij}[u_{b,1},v_{a,1},N_{1,b,1}]E_{j}U_{b}\cdot E_{i}V_{b}d\mu_{\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}}}.

For this quantity, it is possible to derive energy estimates. However, it might be negative, and does not bound the norms we wish to
control. On the other hand, by a combination of Cauchy-Schwarz and Young’s inequality,

\big|\textstyle{\int}_{\bar{M}}\gamma^{ij}[u_{b,1},v_{a,1},N_{1,b,1}]E_{j}U_{b}\cdot E_{i}V_{b}d\mu_{\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}}}\big|\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\mathcal{E}_{2}+\mathfrak{b}_{D}\mathcal{E}_{1},

where
\mathfrak{b}_{D}
is a constant only depending on
\lambda
,
n
and bounds on the
\gamma^{ij}
’s. Introducing

(64)

\check{\mathcal{E}}_{2}:=\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{2}+(\mathfrak{b}_{D}+1)\mathcal{E}_{1}

then yields an energy which we can estimate and which is such that

(65)

\tfrac{1}{2}\mathcal{E}_{2}+\mathcal{E}_{1}\leq\check{\mathcal{E}}_{2}.

Lemma 26
.

Fix a model system and an interval
\mathcal{J}\subseteq\mathcal{I}
. Let
u_{a,i}
,
v_{a,i}
,
N_{1,a,i}
,
N_{2,a,i}
and

u_{b,i}
,
v_{b,i}
,
N_{1,b,i}
,
N_{2,b,i}
,
i=1,2
, have the regularity described by (
51
). Assume moreover, that

u_{b,i}
,
v_{b,i}
,
N_{1,b,i}
,
N_{2,b,i}
is a solution to (
25
) corresponding to
u_{a,i}
,
v_{a,i}
,
N_{1,a,i}
,
N_{2,a,i}
.
Then, using the notation introduced in (
53
)–(
55
) and (
61
)–(
64
), there is a
continuous and monotonically increasing function
\mathfrak{C}:\mathbb{R}_{+}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that

(66)

|\mathcal{E}_{1}(t_{1})-\mathcal{E}_{1}(t_{0})|\leq\big|\textstyle{\int}_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\mathfrak{C}[\mathfrak{l}_{a}(t)]\mathcal{N}_{a}(t)\mathcal{E}_{1}^{1/2}(t)dt\big|

for all
t_{0},t_{1}\in\mathcal{J}
, where
\mathfrak{C}
only depends on bounds on
f_{1}
and its partial derivatives up to order
2
. In addition, there
is a continuous and monotonically increasing function
\check{\mathfrak{C}}:\mathbb{R}_{+}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that,

(67)

\begin{split}|\check{\mathcal{E}}_{2}(t_{1})-\check{\mathcal{E}}_{2}(t_{0})|\leq&\big|\textstyle{\int}_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\check{\mathfrak{C}}[\mathfrak{l}_{a,b}(t)](\|v_{b,2}\|_{C^{2}}+\|\partial_{t}N_{1,b,1}\|_{C^{1}}+1)[\mathcal{E}_{2}(t)+\mathcal{N}_{a,b}^{2}(t)]dt\big|\end{split}

for all
t_{0},t_{1}\in\mathcal{J}
,
where
\check{\mathfrak{C}}:\mathbb{R}_{+}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
only depends on bounds on
\bar{h}_{2}^{ij}
,
\bar{h}_{1}^{ij}
,

\gamma^{ij}
,
\zeta
,
f_{1}
,
f_{2}
,
f_{3}
and
f_{4}
and their derivatives up to order two, one, one, one, two, one, one and one respectively;

(\bar{M},\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}})
; the covering;
\{E_{i}\}
;
\lambda
; and
\lambda_{\zeta}
.

Remark 27
.

The estimate (
66
) gives a bound on
\mathcal{N}_{a,b}
, which justifies the appearance of this quantity in (
67
).

Remark 28
.

Combining (
65
) and (
67
) yields

(68)

|\check{\mathcal{E}}_{2}(t_{1})-\check{\mathcal{E}}_{2}(t_{0})|\leq\big|\textstyle{\int}_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}C(\check{\mathcal{E}}_{2}+\mathcal{N}_{a}^{2})dt\big|,

where
C
depends on
\mathfrak{l}_{a,b}
,
\|\partial_{t}N_{1,b,1}\|_{C^{1}}
and
\|v_{b,2}\|_{C^{2}}
.

Proof.

Time differentiating
\mathcal{E}_{1}
introduced in (
61
) and appealing to the equations for
u_{b,i}
yields

|\partial_{t}\mathcal{E}_{1}|\leq\mathfrak{C}[\mathfrak{l}_{a}]\mathcal{N}_{a}\mathcal{E}_{1}^{1/2}

for
t\in\mathcal{J}
, where
\mathfrak{C}:\mathbb{R}_{+}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
is a continuous and monotonically increasing function depending only on bounds on

f_{1}
and its partial derivatives up to order
2
. The estimate (
66
