) follows.

Next, we time differentiate the energy introduced in (
62
). The term that results when the time derivative hits the last
term in the integrand can be estimated by the energy. Consider the expression that results
when the time derivative hits
\bar{h}_{1}^{ij}[u_{b,1},N_{1,b,1}]
. Appealing to (
25a
) leads to the conclusion that

\|\partial_{t}(\bar{h}_{1}^{ij}[u_{b,1},N_{1,b,1}])\|_{C^{0}}\leq C(\|\partial_{t}N_{1,b,1}\|_{C^{1}}+1),

where
C
is a constant depending only on
(\bar{M},\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}})
,
\{E_{i}\}
, bounds on
f_{1}
and bounds on the derivative of

\bar{h}_{1}^{ij}
. Next, it is natural to integrate by parts in analogy with (
46
). The analogue of the last term on the
right hand side can be estimated by
C\mathcal{E}_{2}
, where
C
only depends on
\lambda
,
(\bar{M},\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}})
and
\{E_{i}\}
. In order to estimate
the first term on the right hand side, we need to estimate

\|E_{j}(\bar{h}_{1}^{ij}[u_{b,1},N_{1,b,1}])\|_{C^{0}}\leq C(\|u_{b,1}\|_{C^{1}}+\|N_{1,b,1}\|_{C^{2}}+1),

where
C
only depends on bounds on the first derivative of
\bar{h}_{1}^{ij}
,
(\bar{M},\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}})
and
\{E_{i}\}
. Combining this
estimate with (
58
) yields the conclusion that there is a continuous and monotonically increasing function

\mathfrak{C}:\mathbb{R}_{+}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that

\begin{split}\big|\partial_{t}\mathcal{E}_{2}-\textstyle{\int}_{\bar{M}}(\partial_{t}^{2}V_{b}-\bar{h}^{ij}_{1}[u_{b,1},N_{1,b,1}]E_{j}E_{i}V_{b})\cdot\partial_{t}V_{b}d\mu_{\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}}}\big|\leq&\mathfrak{C}[\mathfrak{l}_{a,b}](\|\partial_{t}N_{1,b,1}\|_{C^{1}}+1)\mathcal{E}_{2}.\end{split}

Moreover,
\mathfrak{C}
only depends on bounds on
\bar{h}_{2}^{ij}
,
\bar{h}_{1}^{ij}
,
\zeta
,
f_{1}
,
f_{3}
and
f_{4}
and their derivatives up
to order two, one, one, one, one and one respectively;
(\bar{M},\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}})
; the covering;
\{E_{i}\}
;
\lambda
; and

\lambda_{\zeta}
. On the other hand,

(69)

\begin{split}&\partial_{t}^{2}V_{b}-\bar{h}^{ij}_{1}[u_{b,1},N_{1,b,1}]E_{j}E_{i}V_{b}\\
=&\gamma^{ij}[u_{b,2},v_{a,2},N_{1,b,2}]E_{i}E_{j}u_{b,2}-\gamma^{ij}[u_{b,1},v_{a,1},N_{1,b,1}]E_{i}E_{j}u_{b,1}\\
&+f_{2}[u_{a,2},v_{a,2},N_{1,a,2},N_{2,a,2}]-f_{2}[u_{a,1},v_{a,1},N_{1,a,1},N_{2,a,1}]\\
&+\big(\bar{h}^{ij}_{1}[u_{b,2},N_{1,b,2}]-\bar{h}^{ij}_{1}[u_{b,1},N_{1,b,1}]\big)E_{i}E_{j}v_{b,2}.\end{split}

The second line on the right hand side can be estimated in
L^{2}
by
C\mathcal{N}_{a}
, where
C
is a constant depending only on bounds on the first
derivative of
f_{2}
,
(\bar{M},\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}})
and
\{E_{i}\}
. In order to estimate the third line on the right hand side, note that

\|\bar{h}^{ij}_{1}[u_{b,2},N_{1,b,2}]-\bar{h}^{ij}_{1}[u_{b,1},N_{1,b,1}]\|_{2}\leq C\left(\|U_{b}\|_{L^{2}(\bar{M}_{t})}+\|L_{1,b}\|_{H^{1}(\bar{M}_{t})}\right),

where
C
is a constant depending only on bounds on the first derivative of
\bar{h}_{1}^{ij}
. Combining this estimate with
(
56
) yields a continuous and monotonically increasing function
\mathfrak{C}:\mathbb{R}_{+}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that the third line on
the right hand side of (
69
) can be estimated by

\mathfrak{C}[\mathfrak{l}_{a,b}(t)]\|v_{b,2}\|_{C^{2}}\mathcal{N}_{a,b}(t).

Here
\mathfrak{C}
only depends on bounds on
\bar{h}_{2}^{ij}
,
\bar{h}_{1}^{ij}
,
\zeta
,
f_{3}
and
f_{4}
and their derivatives up to order two, one, one,
one and one respectively;
(\bar{M},\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}})
; the covering;
\{E_{i}\}
;
\lambda
; and
\lambda_{\zeta}
. Finally, the first
term on the right hand side of (
69
) can be written

(70)

\begin{split}&\gamma^{ij}[u_{b,2},v_{a,2},N_{1,b,2}]E_{i}E_{j}u_{b,2}-\gamma^{ij}[u_{b,1},v_{a,1},N_{1,b,1}]E_{i}E_{j}u_{b,1}\\
=&\left(\gamma^{ij}[u_{b,2},v_{a,2},N_{1,b,2}]-\gamma^{ij}[u_{b,1},v_{a,1},N_{1,b,1}]\right)E_{i}E_{j}u_{b,2}+\gamma^{ij}[u_{b,1},v_{a,1},N_{1,b,1}]E_{i}E_{j}U_{b}.\end{split}

The first term on the right hand side can be estimated by
\mathfrak{C}[\mathfrak{l}_{a,b}]\mathcal{N}_{a,b}
in
L^{2}
by an argument similar to the one required to estimate
the third line on the right hand side of (
69
). Here
\mathfrak{C}:\mathbb{R}_{+}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
is a continuous and monotonically increasing
function depending only on bounds on
\bar{h}_{2}^{ij}
,
\gamma^{ij}
,
\zeta
,
f_{3}
and
f_{4}
and their derivatives up to order two, one, one, one and
one respectively;
(\bar{M},\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}})
; the covering;
\{E_{i}\}
;
\lambda
; and
\lambda_{\zeta}
. However, the second term on the right hand
side of (
70
) cannot be estimated directly in
L^{2}
. Instead we, in analogy with the proof of Lemma
23
,
have to consider

(71)

\begin{split}&\textstyle{\int}_{\bar{M}}\gamma^{ij}[u_{b,1},v_{a,1},N_{1,b,1}]E_{i}E_{j}U_{b}\cdot\partial_{t}V_{b}d\mu_{\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}}}\\
=&-\textstyle{\int}_{\bar{M}}E_{i}\left(\gamma^{ij}[u_{b,1},v_{a,1},N_{1,b,1}]\right)E_{j}U_{b}\cdot\partial_{t}V_{b}d\mu_{\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}}}\\
&-\textstyle{\int}_{\bar{M}}\gamma^{ij}[u_{b,1},v_{a,1},N_{1,b,1}]E_{j}U_{b}\cdot\partial_{t}E_{i}V_{b}d\mu_{\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}}}\\
&-\textstyle{\int}_{\bar{M}}\gamma^{ij}[u_{b,1},v_{a,1},N_{1,b,1}]E_{j}U_{b}\cdot\partial_{t}V_{b}\cdot\mathrm{div}_{\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}}}E_{i}d\mu_{\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}}}\end{split}

in greater detail. Due to (
58
), the first and the last term can be estimated by
\mathfrak{C}[\mathfrak{l}_{a,b}]\mathcal{N}_{a,b}\mathcal{E}_{2}^{1/2}
, where

\mathfrak{C}:\mathbb{R}_{+}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
is a continuous and monotonically increasing function depending only on bounds on
\bar{h}_{2}^{ij}
,
\gamma^{ij}
,
\zeta
,

f_{3}
and
f_{4}
and their derivatives up to order two, one, one, one and one respectively;
(\bar{M},\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}})
; the covering;

\{E_{i}\}
;
\lambda
; and
\lambda_{\zeta}
. In order to handle the second term on the right hand side of (
71
), we need to
integrate in time as well. Consider, to this end

(72)

\begin{split}&-\textstyle{\int}_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\int_{\bar{M}}\gamma^{ij}[u_{b,1},v_{a,1},N_{1,b,1}]E_{j}U_{b}\cdot\partial_{t}E_{i}V_{b}d\mu_{\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}}}dt\\
=&-\left[\textstyle{\int}_{\bar{M}}\gamma^{ij}[u_{b,1},v_{a,1},N_{1,b,1}]E_{j}U_{b}\cdot E_{i}V_{b}d\mu_{\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}}}\right]_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\\
&+\textstyle{\int}_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\int_{\bar{M}}\partial_{t}(\gamma^{ij}[u_{b,1},v_{a,1},N_{1,b,1}])E_{j}U_{b}\cdot E_{i}V_{b}d\mu_{\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}}}dt\\
&+\textstyle{\int}_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\int_{\bar{M}}\gamma^{ij}[u_{b,1},v_{a,1},N_{1,b,1}]E_{j}\partial_{t}U_{b}\cdot E_{i}V_{b}d\mu_{\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}}}dt.\end{split}

The last term on the right hand side can be estimated by

\big|\textstyle{\int}_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\int_{\bar{M}}\mathfrak{C}[\mathfrak{l}_{a}(t)]\mathcal{N}_{a}(t)\mathcal{E}_{2}^{1/2}(t)dt\big|,

where
\mathfrak{C}:\mathbb{R}_{+}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
is a continuous and monotonically increasing function depending only on
\lambda
, bounds on
\gamma^{ij}

and bounds on
f_{1}
and its partial derivatives up to order
2
; note that, to estimate
E_{j}\partial_{t}U_{b}
in
L^{2}
, it is sufficient to proceed
as in the proof of (
66
). The second term on the right hand side of (
72
) can be estimated by

\big|\textstyle{\int}_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\int_{\bar{M}}\mathfrak{C}[\mathfrak{l}_{a}(t)](\|\partial_{t}N_{1,b,1}\|_{C^{1}}+1)\mathcal{N}_{a,b}(t)\mathcal{E}_{2}^{1/2}(t)dt\big|,

where
\mathfrak{C}:\mathbb{R}_{+}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
is a continuous and monotonically increasing function depending only on
\lambda
, bounds on
f_{1}

and bounds on
\gamma^{ij}
and its first derivative. Here we used the fact that
u_{b,1}
satisfies (
25a
). Including the first
term on the right hand side of (
72
) in the energy yields

|\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{2}(t_{1})-\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{2}(t_{0})|\leq\big|\textstyle{\int}_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\mathfrak{C}[\mathfrak{l}_{a,b}](\|v_{b,2}\|_{C^{2}}+\|\partial_{t}N_{1,b,1}\|_{C^{1}}+1)(\mathcal{E}_{2}+\mathcal{N}_{a,b}\mathcal{E}_{2}^{1/2})dt\big|,

where
\mathfrak{C}:\mathbb{R}_{+}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
is a continuous and monotonically increasing function depending only on bounds on
\bar{h}_{2}^{ij}
,
\bar{h}_{1}^{ij}
,

\gamma^{ij}
,
\zeta
,
f_{1}
,
f_{2}
,
f_{3}
and
f_{4}
and their derivatives up to order two, one, one, one, two, one, one and one respectively;

(\bar{M},\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}})
; the covering;
\{E_{i}\}
;
\lambda
; and
\lambda_{\zeta}
. Combining this observation with (
66
) yields
(
67
).
∎

2.6.
Uniqueness

Due to the estimates derived in the previous subsection, we are in a position to prove uniqueness of solutions.

Lemma 29
.

Fix a model system and an interval
\mathcal{J}\subseteq\mathcal{I}
with non-empty interior. Assume that there are two solutions
u_{i}
,
v_{i}
and
N_{l,i}
,

i=1,2
,
l=1,2
, to (
3
) on
M_{\mathcal{J}}:=\bar{M}\times\mathcal{J}
with the following regularity:

(73a)

\displaystyle u_{i}\in

\displaystyle C^{1}(\mathcal{J},C^{1}(\bar{M},\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}}))\cap C^{0}(\mathcal{J},C^{2}(\bar{M},\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}})),

(73b)

\displaystyle v_{i}\in

\displaystyle C^{2}(\mathcal{J},C^{0}(\bar{M},\mathbb{R}^{n_{2}}))\cap C^{1}(\mathcal{J},C^{1}(\bar{M},\mathbb{R}^{n_{2}}))\cap C^{0}(\mathcal{J},C^{2}(\bar{M},\mathbb{R}^{n_{2}})),

(73c)

\displaystyle N_{1,i}\in

\displaystyle C^{1}(\mathcal{J},C^{1}(\bar{M}))\cap C^{0}(\mathcal{J},C^{2,1}(\bar{M})),

(73d)

\displaystyle N_{2,i}\in

\displaystyle C^{0}(\mathcal{J},C^{2,1}(\bar{M}))

for
i=1,2
. Assume, moreover, that
t_{0}\in\mathcal{J}
and that
u_{1}(\cdot,t_{0})=u_{2}(\cdot,t_{0})
,

v_{1}(\cdot,t_{0})=v_{2}(\cdot,t_{0})
and
(\partial_{t}v_{1})(\cdot,t_{0})=(\partial_{t}v_{2})(\cdot,t_{0})
. Then the two solutions coincide on
M_{\mathcal{J}}
.

Proof.

Using the notation introduced at the beginning of Subsection
2.5
,
u_{a,i}=u_{b,i}=u_{i}
, and similarly for
v
,
N_{1}

and
N_{2}
. We therefore use the notation
U_{a}=U_{b}=u_{2}-u_{1}=:U
, and similarly for
V
,
L_{1}
and
L_{2}
; cf. (
52
). Moreover,
we let
\mathcal{N}(t)
denote the right hand side of (
53
) with
U_{a}
replaced by
U
etc.
Then
\mathcal{N}_{a,b}\leq 2\mathcal{N}
, using the notation introduced in (
55a
). We also let
\mathfrak{l}
denote the right hand side of
(
54
). Again
\mathfrak{l}_{a,b}\leq 2\mathfrak{l}
, where
\mathfrak{l}_{a,b}
is introduced in (
55b
). Next, note that
combining the definition of
\mathcal{E}_{1}
and
\check{\mathcal{E}}_{2}
with (
65
) and the definition of
\mathcal{N}
yields

(74)

[\mathcal{N}(t)]^{2}\leq C_{1}\big[\check{\mathcal{E}}_{2}(t)+\|L_{1}(\cdot,t)\|_{H^{2}(\bar{M})}^{2}+\|L_{2}(\cdot,t)\|_{H^{2}(\bar{M})}^{2}\big]\leq\mathfrak{C}[\mathfrak{l}(t)]\check{\mathcal{E}}_{2}(t)

where
C_{1}
only depends on the
\lambda
and
n
; and
\mathfrak{C}
has the same properties and dependence as
the function
\mathfrak{C}_{L}
appearing in the statement of Lemma
25
. Combining (
74
) with (
65
) and
(
67
) yields

(75)

|\check{\mathcal{E}}_{2}(t_{1})-\check{\mathcal{E}}_{2}(t_{0})|\leq\big|\textstyle{\int}_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}C(t)\check{\mathcal{E}}_{2}(t)dt\big|,

where
C
depends on the model system and the solutions. Combining (
75
) with the fact that
\check{\mathcal{E}}_{2}(t_{0})=0
yields the
conclusion that
\check{\mathcal{E}}_{2}(t)=0
for all
t\in\mathcal{J}
, so that
\mathcal{N}(t)=0
for all
t\in\mathcal{J}
due to (
74
). This means that
u_{1}=u_{2}
,

v_{1}=v_{2}
and
N_{l,1}=N_{l,2}
for
l=1,2
. The lemma follows.
∎

2.7.
Local existence

Next, we wish to prove local existence. Fix, to this end, a model system and introduce the following inner product. Let
\mathcal{J}\subset\mathcal{I}

be an interval,
M_{\mathcal{J}}:=\bar{M}\times\mathcal{J}
,
u\in C^{0}(M_{\mathcal{J}},\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}})
,
v\in C^{0}(M_{\mathcal{J}},\mathbb{R}^{n_{2}})
and
N_{1}\in C^{0}(M_{\mathcal{J}})
.
Assume, moreover, that
N_{1}
is differentiable with respect to the
\bar{M}
-variables and that
E_{i}N_{1}\in C^{0}(M_{\mathcal{J}})
,
i=1,\dots,n
. Next, let

\xi_{i}\in H^{m+1}(\bar{M},\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}})
,
\eta_{i}\in H^{m+1}(\bar{M},\mathbb{R}^{n_{2}})
and
\chi_{i}\in H^{m}(\bar{M},\mathbb{R}^{n_{2}})
,
i=1,2
, and

(76)

\begin{split}&\langle(\xi_{1},\eta_{1},\chi_{1}),(\xi_{2},\eta_{2},\chi_{2})\rangle_{H,t}\\
=&\tfrac{1}{2}\textstyle{\sum}_{|\mathbf{I}|\leq m}\textstyle{\int}_{\bar{M}_{t}}(E_{\mathbf{I}}\chi_{1}\cdot E_{\mathbf{I}}\chi_{2}+\bar{h}^{ij}_{1}[u,N_{1}](\cdot,t)E_{i}E_{\mathbf{I}}\eta_{1}\cdot E_{j}E_{\mathbf{I}}\eta_{2}+E_{\mathbf{I}}\eta_{1}\cdot E_{\mathbf{I}}\eta_{2})d\mu_{\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}}}\\
&+\tfrac{1}{2}\beta_{\gamma}\textstyle{\sum}_{|\mathbf{I}|\leq m+1}\textstyle{\int}_{\bar{M}_{t}}E_{\mathbf{I}}\xi_{1}\cdot E_{\mathbf{I}}\xi_{2}d\mu_{\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}}}\\
&+\tfrac{1}{2}\textstyle{\sum}_{|\mathbf{I}|\leq m}\textstyle{\int}_{\bar{M}_{t}}\gamma^{ij}[u,v,N_{1}](\cdot,t)E_{j}E_{\mathbf{I}}\xi_{1}\cdot E_{i}E_{\mathbf{I}}\eta_{2}d\mu_{\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}}}\\
&+\tfrac{1}{2}\textstyle{\sum}_{|\mathbf{I}|\leq m}\textstyle{\int}_{\bar{M}_{t}}\gamma^{ij}[u,v,N_{1}](\cdot,t)E_{j}E_{\mathbf{I}}\xi_{2}\cdot E_{i}E_{\mathbf{I}}\eta_{1}d\mu_{\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}}}\end{split}

for
t\in\mathcal{J}
, where
\beta_{\gamma}
is defined by (
41
); when we speak of this inner product in what follows, the choice of the functions
u
,
v

and
N_{1}
should be clear from the context. For each
t\in\mathcal{J}
, (
76
) defines an inner product on

H:=H^{m+1}(\bar{M},\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}})\times H^{m+1}(\bar{M},\mathbb{R}^{n_{2}})\times H^{m}(\bar{M},\mathbb{R}^{n_{2}})
. To justify this statement, note that
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{H,t}
is
symmetric and linear (over the real numbers) in both arguments. Moreover, due to
(
43
), it is clear that there is a constant depending only on the
\lambda
appearing in (
1
) and bounds on the

\gamma^{ij}
, say
C_{\lambda,\gamma}>1
, such that if
\xi=(u_{1},v_{1,0},v_{1,1})
, then

(77)

C_{\lambda,\gamma}^{-1}\langle\xi,\xi\rangle_{H,t}\leq\|u_{1}\|_{H^{m+1}(\bar{M})}^{2}+\|v_{1,0}\|_{H^{m+1}(\bar{M})}^{2}+\|v_{1,1}\|_{H^{m}(\bar{M})}^{2}\leq C_{\lambda,\gamma}\langle\xi,\xi\rangle_{H,t}

for all
t\in\mathcal{J}
. This means that
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{H,t}
has the required positive definiteness properties. In fact,
H
is a Hilbert space
with this inner product, and this inner product is equivalent to the standard one.

Proposition 30
.

Fix a model system,
m>n/2+2
,
\mathcal{U}_{0}\in H^{m+1}(\bar{M},\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}})
,
\mathcal{V}_{0,0}\in H^{m+1}(\bar{M},\mathbb{R}^{n_{2}})
and

\mathcal{V}_{0,1}\in H^{m}(\bar{M},\mathbb{R}^{n_{2}})
. Given a compact interval
\mathcal{J}:=[T_{1},T_{2}]\subset\mathcal{I}
, there is a covering of
\bar{M}
(depending only on

(\bar{M},\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}})
) and a
T>0
(depending only on the model system,
\mathcal{J}
,
m
,
(\bar{M},\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}})
,
\{E_{i}\}
, the covering and an upper bound on
the sum of the
H^{m+1}
-norms of
\mathcal{U}_{0}
and
\mathcal{V}_{0,0}
and the
H^{m}
-norm of
\mathcal{V}_{0,1}
) such that if
t_{0}\in\mathcal{J}
and

\mathcal{I}_{T}:=[t_{0}-T,t_{0}+T]
, then there is a unique solution
u,v,N_{1},N_{2}
to (
3
) on
M_{T}:=\bar{M}\times\mathcal{I}_{T}
such that

(78a)

\displaystyle u\in

\displaystyle C^{1}(\mathcal{I}_{T},C^{1}(\bar{M},\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}}))\cap C^{0}(\mathcal{I}_{T},C^{2}(\bar{M},\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}})),

(78b)

\displaystyle v\in

\displaystyle C^{2}(\mathcal{I}_{T},C^{0}(\bar{M},\mathbb{R}^{n_{2}}))\cap C^{1}(\mathcal{I}_{T},C^{1}(\bar{M},\mathbb{R}^{n_{2}}))\cap C^{0}(\mathcal{I}_{T},C^{2}(\bar{M},\mathbb{R}^{n_{2}})),

(78c)

\displaystyle N_{1}\in

\displaystyle C^{1}(\mathcal{I}_{T},C^{1}(\bar{M}))\cap C^{0}(\mathcal{I}_{T},C^{2,1}(\bar{M})),

(78d)

\displaystyle N_{2}\in

\displaystyle C^{0}(\mathcal{I}_{T},C^{2,1}(\bar{M}))

and (
7
) hold. Furthermore, if
\kappa_{0}
is the smallest integer strictly larger than
n/2
,

(79a)

\displaystyle u\in

\displaystyle\textstyle{\bigcap}_{j=1}^{m+1-\kappa_{0}}C^{j}\left(\mathcal{I}_{T},H^{m+2-j}(\bar{M},\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}})\right),

(79b)

\displaystyle v\in

\displaystyle\textstyle{\bigcap}_{j=0}^{m-\kappa_{0}}C^{j}\left(\mathcal{I}_{T},H^{m+1-j}(\bar{M},\mathbb{R}^{n_{2}})\right),

(79c)

\displaystyle N_{1}\in

\displaystyle\textstyle{\bigcap}_{j=1}^{m-\kappa_{0}}C^{j}\left(\mathcal{I}_{T},H^{m+3-j}(\bar{M})\right),

(79d)

\displaystyle N_{2}\in

\displaystyle\textstyle{\bigcap}_{j=0}^{m-1-\kappa_{0}}C^{j}\left(\mathcal{I}_{T},H^{m+2-j}(\bar{M})\right).

Given these functions, define

\check{\mathfrak{E}}_{2,m+1}[v,u](t)=\langle(u(\cdot,t),v(\cdot,t),(\partial_{t}v)(\cdot,t)),(u(\cdot,t),v(\cdot,t),(\partial_{t}v)(\cdot,t))\rangle_{H,t},

where the inner product is defined by (
76
), and the functions
u
,
v
and
N_{1}
appearing in (
76
) are the
ones determined by the solution. Then there is a continuous and monotonically increasing function
\mathfrak{C}:\mathbb{R}_{+}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that

(80)

\check{\mathfrak{E}}_{2,m+1}[v,u](t_{1})+1\leq\left[\check{\mathfrak{E}}_{2,m+1}[v,u](t_{0})+1\right]\exp\big(\big|\textstyle{\int}_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\mathfrak{C}[\ell(t)]dt\big|\big)

for
t_{1}\in\mathcal{I}_{T}
. Here
\mathfrak{C}
only depends on
m
,
(\bar{M},\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}})
,
\{E_{i}\}
, the covering and the model system. Moreover,

(81)

\ell(t):=\|u(\cdot,t)\|_{C^{2}}+\|v(\cdot,t)\|_{C^{2}}+\|\partial_{t}v(\cdot,t)\|_{C^{1}}.

Finally, the bound on
\check{\mathfrak{E}}_{2,m+1}[v,u](t)
for
t\in\mathcal{I}_{T}
can be assumed to only depend on the model system,
m
,
(\bar{M},\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}})
,

\{E_{i}\}
, the covering and an upper bound on the sum of the
H^{m+1}
-norms of
\mathcal{U}_{0}
and
\mathcal{V}_{0,0}
and the
H^{m}
-norm of
\mathcal{V}_{0,1}
.

Remark 31
.

The only reason for the dependence of
T
on
\mathcal{J}
is to ensure that
\mathcal{I}_{T}\subseteq\mathcal{I}
.

Remark 32
.

It should be sufficient to assume
m>n/2+1
. However, we expect the corresponding proof to lead to technical complications that we do not wish to
address here.

Proof.

The uniqueness statement is an immediate consequence of Lemma
29
. In the proof we therefore focus on the existence statement,
the additional regularity properties of solutions and the energy estimate (
80
).

Setting up the iteration.
Let
\mathcal{U}_{0,l}\in C^{\infty}(\bar{M},\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}})
and
\mathcal{V}_{0,0,l},\mathcal{V}_{0,1,l}\in C^{\infty}(\bar{M},\mathbb{R}^{n_{2}})
,
l\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, be
such that if

\|\mathcal{U}_{0}\|_{H^{m+1}}+\|\mathcal{V}_{0,0}\|_{H^{m+1}}+\|\mathcal{V}_{0,1}\|_{H^{m}}\leq C_{0},

then

(82)

\|\mathcal{U}_{0,l}\|_{H^{m+1}}+\|\mathcal{V}_{0,0,l}\|_{H^{m+1}}+\|\mathcal{V}_{0,1,l}\|_{H^{m}}\leq C_{0}+1

and

\lim_{l\rightarrow\infty}[\|\mathcal{U}_{0,l}-\mathcal{U}_{0}\|_{H^{m+1}}+\|\mathcal{V}_{0,0,l}-\mathcal{V}_{0,0}\|_{H^{m+1}}+\|\mathcal{V}_{0,1,l}-\mathcal{V}_{0,1}\|_{H^{m}}]=0

(in what follows, we impose additional restrictions on the sequence).
Let now
u_{0}(\bar{x},t):=\mathcal{U}_{0,0}(\bar{x})
,
v_{0}(\bar{x},t):=\mathcal{V}_{0,0,0}(\bar{x})
,
N_{1,0}:=1
and
N_{2,0}:=0
. Note that
u_{0}
,
v_{0}
,
N_{1,0}
and
N_{2,0}
are
smooth functions on
M:=\bar{M}\times\mathcal{I}
. Given that
u_{l}
,
v_{l}
,
N_{1,l}
and
N_{2,l}
have been defined, define
u_{l+1}
,
v_{l+1}
,

N_{1,l+1}
and
N_{2,l+1}
by solving (
25
) with
u_{a}=u_{l}
,
v_{a}=v_{l}
,
N_{1,a}=N_{1,l}
,
N_{2,a}=N_{2,l}
, initial data for
u_{l+1}

given by
\mathcal{U}_{0,l+1}
, initial data for
v_{l+1}
given by
\mathcal{V}_{0,0,l+1}
and initial data for
\partial_{t}v_{l+1}
given by
\mathcal{V}_{0,1,l+1}
. That
the solution exists and is smooth follows from Lemma
14
.

Bounds in the high norm.
The first goal is to prove that the sequence
(u_{l},v_{l},N_{1,l},N_{2,l})
is bounded in the appropriate norm
on an interval of the form
\mathcal{I}_{T}:=[-T+t_{0},t_{0}+T]
for any
t_{0}\in\mathcal{J}
and some suitable choice of
T>0
. The relevant expression we wish
to bound is

(83)

\begin{split}\mathfrak{N}_{l}(t):=&\|u_{l}\|_{H^{m+1}(\bar{M}_{t})}+\|v_{l}\|_{H^{m+1}(\bar{M}_{t})}+\|\partial_{t}v_{l}\|_{H^{m}(\bar{M}_{t})}\\
&+\|N_{1,l}\|_{H^{m+2}(\bar{M}_{t})}+\|N_{2,l}\|_{H^{m+2}(\bar{M}_{t})}+1.\end{split}

By Sobolev embedding and the fact that
m>n/2+1
, a bound on
\mathfrak{N}_{l}(t)
yields a bound on

(84)

\begin{split}\nu_{l}(t):=&\|u_{l}(\cdot,t)\|_{C^{2}}+\|v_{l}(\cdot,t)\|_{C^{2}}+\|\partial_{t}v_{l}(\cdot,t)\|_{C^{1}}\\
&+\|N_{1,l}(\cdot,t)\|_{C^{2,1}}+\|N_{2,l}(\cdot,t)\|_{C^{2,1}}+1.\end{split}

In fact, we obtain bounds on
N_{i,l}
in
C^{3}(\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}})
, but we choose the
C^{2,1}
-norm since it fits better with Schauder estimates.
Assume, inductively, that, for some
0\leq l\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, there is a constant
\mathcal{M}
such that

(85)

\mathfrak{N}_{j}(t)\leq\mathcal{M}

for
t\in\mathcal{I}_{T}
and
0\leq j\leq l
. Note that for any
t_{0}\in\mathcal{J}
and any
T
such that
[T_{1}-T,T_{2}+T]\subset\mathcal{I}
, this statement holds for

l=0
, assuming
\mathcal{M}
to be large enough. Moreover, for
l=0
, the bound only depends on
(\bar{M},\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}})
and
C_{0}
due to (
82
)
and the definitions of
u_{0}
,
v_{0}
,
N_{1,0}
and
N_{2,0}
. Since
m>n/2+1
, combining (
85
) with Sobolev embedding yields

\nu_{j}(t)\leq C\mathcal{M}
for
t\in\mathcal{I}_{T}
. Let
0\leq j\leq l
. Then, due to (
36
),

E_{1,m+1}[u_{j+1}](t)+1\leq E_{1,m+1}[u_{j+1}](t_{0})+1+\big|\textstyle{\int}_{t_{0}}^{t}K_{1}(E_{1,m+1}[u_{j+1}](s)+1)ds\big|,

where
K_{1}
is a constant depending only on
\mathcal{M}
,
(\bar{M},\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}})
,
\{E_{i}\}
,
m
and bounds on
f_{1}
and its partial derivatives up to
order
m+1
. Appealing to Grönwall’s lemma yields

E_{1,m+1}[u_{j+1}](t)+1\leq(E_{1,m+1}[u_{j+1}](t_{0})+1)e^{K_{1}|t-t_{0}|}

for all
t\in\mathcal{I}_{T}
and
j\in\{0,\dots,l\}
. Combining this estimate with (
82
) yields

(86)

E_{1,m+1}[u_{j+1}](t)+1\leq(C_{0}^{2}+2)e^{K_{1}T}

for all
t\in\mathcal{I}_{T}
and
j\in\{0,\dots,l\}
. Assuming
T
to be small enough that
K_{1}T\leq\ln 2
, we obtain a uniform bound on
u_{j+1}
in

H^{m+1}(\bar{M}_{t})
for
t\in\mathcal{I}_{T}
and
j\in\{0,\dots,l\}
which only depends on
C_{0}
. By Sobolev embedding, we thereby obtain a uniform bound
on
u_{j+1}
in
C^{2}(\bar{M}_{t})
for
t\in\mathcal{I}_{T}
and
j\in\{0,\dots,l\}
which only depends on
C_{0}
,

(\bar{M},\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}})
, the frame
\{E_{i}\}
and
m
. At this stage, we can appeal to Lemma
23
. Note that the
\mathfrak{N}_{a,b}

appearing in this lemma is, in our setting, bounded by a constant depending only on
C_{0}
and
\mathcal{M}
, assuming
K_{1}T\leq\ln 2
. By Sobolev
embedding, this implies that
\mathfrak{n}_{a,b}
is bounded by a constant depending only on
(\bar{M},\bar{g}_{\mathrm{ref}})
, the frame
\{E_{i}\}
,
m
,
C_{0}
and

\mathcal{M}
. Due to Sobolev embedding and (
43
), the estimate (
44
) implies that

\left|\check{E}_{2,m+1}[v_{j+1},u_{j+1}](t_{1})-\check{E}_{2,m+1}[v_{j+1},u_{j+1}](t_{0})\right|\leq\big|\textstyle{\int}_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}K_{2}(\check{E}_{2,m+1}[v_{j+1},u_{j+1}]+1)dt\big|,

where
K_{2}
only depends on
(\bar{M},\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}})
, the frame
\{E_{i}\}
, the covering mentioned in Lemma
23
, the model system,

m
,
C_{0}
and
\mathcal{M}
. In analogy with the derivation of (
86
), we obtain

(87)

\check{E}_{2,m+1}[v_{j+1},u_{j+1}](t)\leq C_{\gamma,\lambda}(C_{0}^{2}+1)

for all
t\in\mathcal{I}_{T}
, assuming
T
to be such that
{\mathrm{max}}\{K_{1},K_{2}\}T\leq\ln 2
. Here
C_{\gamma,\lambda}
is a constant depending only on
n
,
m
,

\lambda
and the global bound on the
\|\gamma^{ij}\|
. Combining the above conclusions with (
1
)
yields

(88)

\|u_{k}\|_{H^{m+1}(\bar{M}_{t})}+\|v_{k}\|_{H^{m+1}(\bar{M}_{t})}+\|\partial_{t}v_{k}\|_{H^{m}(\bar{M}_{t})}\leq C_{\gamma,\lambda}\langle C_{0}\rangle

for all
t\in\mathcal{I}_{T}
and
k\in\{1,\dots,l+1\}
, assuming
T
to be such that
{\mathrm{max}}\{K_{1},K_{2}\}T\leq\ln 2
, where
C_{\gamma,\lambda}
has the same
dependence as in (
87
). Due to the choice of
u_{0}
and
v_{0}
, the estimate (
88
) also holds for

k=0
. Combining (
88
) with Sobolev embedding yields

(89)

\|u_{k}(\cdot,t)\|_{C^{2}}+\|v_{k}(\cdot,t)\|_{C^{2}}+\|\partial_{t}v_{k}(\cdot,t)\|_{C^{1}}\leq C_{2}\langle C_{0}\rangle

for all
t\in\mathcal{I}_{T}
and
k\in\{0,\dots,l+1\}
, assuming
T
to be such that
{\mathrm{max}}\{K_{1},K_{2}\}T\leq\ln 2
, where
C_{2}
only depends on

(\bar{M},\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}})
,
\{E_{i}\}
,
m
,
\lambda
and the global bound on the
\|\gamma^{ij}\|
. What remains to bound (in
H^{m+2}
) is
N_{1,l+1}
and

N_{2,l+1}
. This can be achieved by appealing to Lemma
20
with
u_{b}=u_{l+1}
and
v_{a}=v_{l}
. Combining
Lemma
20
with (
88
) and (
89
) yields

\|N_{1,l+1}\|_{H^{m+2}(\bar{M}_{t})}+\|N_{2,l+1}\|_{H^{m+2}(\bar{M}_{t})}\leq C_{3}\langle C_{0}\rangle

for all
t\in\mathcal{I}_{T}
, assuming
T
to be such that
{\mathrm{max}}\{K_{1},K_{2}\}T\leq\ln 2
, where
C_{3}
only depends on
C_{0}
,
(\bar{M},\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}})
, the
covering,
\{E_{i}\}
,
m
and the system. To summarise,

\mathfrak{N}_{l+1}(t)\leq C_{\mathfrak{N}}

for all
t\in\mathcal{I}_{T}
, assuming
{\mathrm{max}}\{K_{1},K_{2}\}T\leq\ln 2
, where
C_{\mathfrak{N}}
only depends on
(\bar{M},\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}})
, the covering,
\{E_{i}\}
, the model
system,
m
and
C_{0}
. Due to the observations made below (
85
), we can thus fix a constant
\mathcal{M}
with the same dependence as

C_{\mathfrak{N}}
and deduce that (
85
) holds for all
j
and all
t\in\mathcal{I}_{T}
, where
T\leq 1/K
for some constant
K
with the same type
of dependence as
C_{\mathfrak{N}}
.

Convergence in the low norm.
Define, for
l\geq 1
,
\mathcal{N}_{l}
in analogy with (
53
), where
U_{a}
is replaced by
u_{l}-u_{l-1}

etc. Our next goal is to prove that there is a constant
\mathcal{K}_{0}
such that

(90)

\mathcal{N}_{l}(t)\leq\mathcal{K}_{0}2^{-l}

for all
t\in\mathcal{I}_{T}
and
l\geq 1
. Note that for
l\leq 2
, this estimate holds due to (
85
), where
\mathcal{K}_{0}
only depends on

(\bar{M},\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}})
, the covering,
\{E_{i}\}
, the model system,
m
and
C_{0}
. Assume now, inductively, that (
90
) holds for some

l\geq 1
. Appealing to (
66
) then yields the conclusion that

(91)

|\mathcal{E}_{1,l+1}(t_{1})-\mathcal{E}_{1,l+1}(t_{0})|\leq\big|\textstyle{\int}_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\mathcal{K}_{1}\mathcal{K}_{0}2^{-l}\mathcal{E}_{1,l+1}^{1/2}dt\big|

for
t_{1}\in\mathcal{I}_{T}
, where
\mathcal{E}_{1,l+1}
is given by the right hand side of (
61
) with
U_{b}=u_{l+1}-u_{l}
. Moreover,
\mathcal{K}_{1}
is a
constant depending only on
(\bar{M},\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}})
, the covering,
\{E_{i}\}
, the model system,
m
and
C_{0}
. From (
91
), it follows
that

\left(\mathcal{E}_{1,l+1}(t_{1})+4^{-l-1}\right)\leq\left(\mathcal{E}_{1,l+1}(t_{0})+4^{-l-1}\right)e^{\mathcal{K}_{1}\mathcal{K}_{0}T}

for
t_{1}\in\mathcal{I}_{T}
. Assume, to begin with,
T
to be small enough, depending only on
\mathcal{K}_{0}
and
\mathcal{K}_{1}
, that
\mathcal{K}_{1}\mathcal{K}_{0}T\leq\ln 2
. Then

(92)

\mathcal{E}_{1,l+1}(t_{1})\leq 2\mathcal{E}_{1,l+1}(t_{0})+(2\mathcal{K}_{1}\mathcal{K}_{0}T)4^{-l-1}

for
t_{1}\in\mathcal{I}_{T}
. In particular, making appropriate assumptions concerning the initial sequence, the first term on the right hand side can be
assumed to be smaller than
4^{-l-1}
. Assuming
T
to be small enough that
2\mathcal{K}_{1}\mathcal{K}_{0}T\leq 1
, we conclude that
2\mathcal{E}_{1,l+1}(t)\leq 4^{-l}
for

t_{1}\in\mathcal{I}_{T}
. This means that the
\mathcal{N}_{a,b}
appearing in, e.g., (
67
) can be estimated by
(\mathcal{K}_{0}+1)2^{-l}
. Combining this
observation with (
30
), (
65
) and (
67
) yields

|\check{\mathcal{E}}_{2,l+1}(t_{1})-\check{\mathcal{E}}_{2,l+1}(t_{0})|\leq\big|\textstyle{\int}_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\mathcal{K}_{2}\langle\mathcal{K}_{0}\rangle^{2}\big(\check{\mathcal{E}}_{2,l+1}(t)+4^{-l-1}\big)dt\big|

for
t_{1}\in\mathcal{I}_{T}
, where
\mathcal{K}_{2}
is a constant depending only on
(\bar{M},\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}})
, the covering,
\{E_{i}\}
,
m
, the model system and
C_{0}
.
Here
\check{\mathcal{E}}_{2,l+1}
is defined by (
62
), (
63
) and (
64
), where
\mathcal{E}_{1}
is replaced by
\mathcal{E}_{1,l+1}
,

U_{b}=u_{l+1}-u_{l}
,
V_{b}=v_{l+1}-v_{l}
,
u_{b,1}=u_{l}
,
v_{a,1}=v_{l-1}
and
N_{1,b,1}=N_{1,l}
. In particular,

\left(\check{\mathcal{E}}_{2,l+1}(t_{1})+4^{-l-1}\right)\leq\left(\check{\mathcal{E}}_{2,l+1}(t_{0})+4^{-l-1}\right)e^{\mathcal{K}_{2}\langle\mathcal{K}_{0}\rangle^{2}T}

for
t_{1}\in\mathcal{I}_{T}
. In analogy with (
92
), we deduce that

\check{\mathcal{E}}_{2,l+1}(t_{1})\leq 2\check{\mathcal{E}}_{2,l+1}(t_{0})+(2\mathcal{K}_{2}\langle\mathcal{K}_{0}\rangle^{2}T)4^{-l-1}

for
t_{1}\in\mathcal{I}_{T}
, assuming
T
to be small enough that
\mathcal{K}_{2}\langle\mathcal{K}_{0}\rangle^{2}T\leq\ln 2
. Combining this estimate with (
62
)
and (
65
), it follows that

(93)

\|v_{l+1}-v_{l}\|_{H^{1}(\bar{M}_{t})}^{2}+\|\partial_{t}v_{l+1}-\partial_{t}v_{l}\|_{L^{2}(\bar{M}_{t})}^{2}\leq\mathcal{K}_{3}\langle\mathcal{K}_{0}\rangle^{2}(\check{\mathcal{E}}_{2,l+1}(t_{0})+4^{-l-1}T)

for
t\in\mathcal{I}_{T}
, where
\mathcal{K}_{3}
has the same dependence as
\mathcal{K}_{2}
. Note that this estimate also holds with
l
replaced by
l-1
,
assuming
l\geq 2
; note that since we know (
90
) to hold for
l=1
and
l=2
, we know (
93
) to hold for
l=1
and

l=2
. Assume now that
l\geq 2
. Then, combining (
92
); (
93
); (
93
) with
l
replaced by
l-1
;
and Lemma
25
yields

[\mathcal{N}_{l+1}(t)]^{2}\leq\mathcal{K}_{4}\langle\mathcal{K}_{0}\rangle^{2}(\check{\mathcal{E}}_{2,l+1}(t_{0})+\check{\mathcal{E}}_{2,l}(t_{0})+4^{-l-1}T)

for
t\in\mathcal{I}_{T}
, where
\mathcal{K}_{4}
has the same dependence as
\mathcal{K}_{2}
; to obtain this estimate we also used (
65
). Assuming

T
to be small enough, the bound depending only on
\mathcal{K}_{0}
and
\mathcal{K}_{4}
, and assuming the initial sequence to have been chosen appropriately,
we conclude that (
90
) holds with
l
replaced by
l+1
and
\mathcal{K}_{0}
replaced by
1
. If
\mathcal{K}_{0}^{\prime}
is the constant appearing in
(
90
) for
1\leq l\leq 2
(note that this constant can be assumed to have the same dependence as
\mathcal{K}_{2}
), let
\mathcal{K}_{0}:={\mathrm{max}}\{\mathcal{K}_{0}^{\prime},1\}
.
Due to the dependence of
\mathcal{M}
, noted above, we conclude that we only need to require
T
to be small enough, the bound depending only on

(\bar{M},\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}})
, the covering,
\{E_{i}\}
, the model system,
m
and
C_{0}
(since we here tacitly assume
T
to be small enough that
\mathcal{I}_{T}\subset\mathcal{I}
,

T
also, strictly speaking, depends on
\mathcal{J}
). This ensures that both the bounds (
85
) and (
90
) hold for all
j
and
l

respectively.

We conclude that
u_{l}
,
v_{l}
,
N_{1,l}
and
N_{2,l}
converge to limits
u
,
v
,
N_{1}
and
N_{2}
respectively, satisfying

\displaystyle u\in C^{0}(\mathcal{I}_{T},H^{1}(\bar{M})),\ \ \ v\in C^{0}(\mathcal{I}_{T},H^{1}(\bar{M}))\cap C^{1}(\mathcal{I}_{T},L^{2}(\bar{M}))

\displaystyle N_{1},N_{2}\in C^{0}(\mathcal{I}_{T},H^{2}(\bar{M})).

Below we refer to this convergence as
convergence in the low norm
. Next we wish to combine this convergence with boundedness in the high
norm, i.e. (
85
), and interpolation in order to obtain convergence in intermediate norms. To this end, recall that the

H^{k}(\bar{M};\mathbb{E})
and
H^{k}(\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}})
norms are equivalent, see Subsection
A.5
, and that (
282
) holds.
Here the
\|\cdot\|_{(s)}
norm is defined by (
273
) with
h=\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}}
. Combining convergence in the low
norm with (
85
) and standard interpolation estimates, see (
276
), yields the conclusion that
for every
0<s<m
,
u_{l}
,
v_{l}
,
N_{1,l}
and
N_{2,l}
converge to limits
u
,
v
,
N_{1}
and
N_{2}
respectively, satisfying

\displaystyle u\in C^{0}(\mathcal{I}_{T},H_{(s+1)}(\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}};\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}})),\ \ \ v\in C^{0}(\mathcal{I}_{T},H_{(s+1)}(\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}};\mathbb{R}^{n_{2}}))\cap C^{1}(\mathcal{I}_{T},H_{(s)}(\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}};\mathbb{R}^{n_{2}}))

\displaystyle N_{1},N_{2}\in C^{0}(\mathcal{I}_{T},H_{(s+2)}(\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}}));

the Sobolev spaces
H_{(s)}
and associated norms are introduced in Appendix
E.1
below. In particular, we have this regularity
for
s=m-1
. This means that
u_{l}
,
v_{l}
,
N_{1,l}
and
N_{2,l}
converge to limits
u
,
v
,
N_{1}
and
N_{2}
respectively,
satisfying

\displaystyle u\in C^{0}(\mathcal{I}_{T},H^{m}(\bar{M})),\ \ \ v\in C^{0}(\mathcal{I}_{T},H^{m}(\bar{M}))\cap C^{1}(\mathcal{I}_{T},H^{m-1}(\bar{M}))

\displaystyle N_{1},N_{2}\in C^{0}(\mathcal{I}_{T},H^{m+1}(\bar{M})).

Combining this observation with the equations satisfied by the sequence yields

u\in C^{1}(\mathcal{I}_{T},H^{m}(\bar{M})),\ \ \ v\in C^{2}(\mathcal{I}_{T},H^{m-2}(\bar{M})).

Combining the above observations with the fact that
u
,
v
and
N_{1}
solve (
3c
) (this is a consequence
of the above convergence and Sobolev embedding) and Corollary
109
(with
k=m-1
) yields

N_{1}\in C^{1}(\mathcal{I}_{T},H^{m+1}(\bar{M})).

Combining the above observations with Sobolev embedding, the solution satisfies (
78
).

Bounding the solution in the high norm.
Next, we wish to prove that
u(\cdot,t)\in H^{m+1}(\bar{M},\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}})
for all
t\in\mathcal{I}_{T}
etc. Define,
to this end,

\hat{u}(i,t):=\langle u(\cdot,t),\varphi_{i}\rangle=\textstyle{\int}_{\bar{M}}u(\cdot,t)\varphi_{i}d\mu_{\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}}},

where we use the notation introduced in Appendix
E.1
in the case that
(\Sigma,h)=(\bar{M},\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}})
. We introduce similar notation
for
v
and the
N_{j}
,
j=1,2
. Note that
\hat{u}_{l}(i,t)\rightarrow\hat{u}(i,t)
for
all
t\in\mathcal{I}_{T}
. This means that

\begin{split}&\textstyle{\sum}_{i=0}^{N}\langle\lambda_{i}\rangle^{2(m+1)}|\hat{u}(i,t)|^{2}\\
=&\textstyle{\lim}_{l\rightarrow\infty}\textstyle{\sum}_{i=0}^{N}\langle\lambda_{i}\rangle^{2(m+1)}|\hat{u}_{l}(i,t)|^{2}\leq\limsup_{l\rightarrow\infty}\|u_{l}(\cdot,t)\|_{(m+1)}^{2}\\
\leq&C\textstyle{\limsup}_{l\rightarrow\infty}\|u_{l}\|_{H^{m+1}(\bar{M}_{t})}^{2}\leq C\mathcal{M}^{2}\end{split}

for all
N\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, where
C
only depends on
m
,
\{E_{i}\}
and
(\bar{M},\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}})
.
Due to this and similar arguments, we conclude that
u(\cdot,t)\in H^{m+1}(\bar{M},\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}})
,
v(\cdot,t)\in H^{m+1}(\bar{M},\mathbb{R}^{n_{2}})
,

(\partial_{t}v)(\cdot,t)\in H^{m}(\bar{M},\mathbb{R}^{n_{2}})
and
N_{i}(\cdot,t)\in H^{m+2}(\bar{M})
,
i=1,2
. Moreover,

\|u(\cdot,t)\|_{H^{m+1}(\bar{M})}+\|v(\cdot,t)\|_{H^{m+1}(\bar{M})}+\|(\partial_{t}v)(\cdot,t)\|_{H^{m}(\bar{M})}+\textstyle{\sum}_{i=1}^{2}\|N_{i}(\cdot,t)\|_{H^{m+2}(\bar{M})}\leq C\mathcal{M}

for all
t\in\mathcal{I}_{T}
, where
C
only depends on
m
,
\{E_{i}\}
and
(\bar{M},\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}})
.

Weak continuity.
Let
f
be an element in the dual of
H_{(m+1)}(\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}};\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}})
. Then
f_{\chi}=f
, for some function

\chi\in H_{(-m-1)}(\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}};\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}})
, where
f_{\chi}:=\langle\cdot,\chi\rangle
; cf. Appendix
E.1
, in particular (
278
).
We wish to prove that
t\mapsto f(u(\cdot,t))
defines a continuous function of
t
. Let, to this end,
\chi_{j}\in C^{\infty}(\bar{M},\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}})

be such that
\chi_{j}\rightarrow\chi
in
H_{(-m-1)}(\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}};\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}})
. Consider

\begin{split}|f[u(\cdot,t)]-f[u_{l}(\cdot,t)]|=&|\langle u(\cdot,t)-u_{l}(\cdot,t),\chi\rangle|\\
\leq&C\mathcal{M}\|\chi-\chi_{j}\|_{(-m-1)}+\big|\textstyle{\int}_{\bar{M}}[u(\cdot,t)-u_{l}(\cdot,t)]\chi_{j}d\mu_{\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}}}\big|.\end{split}

Given
\epsilon>0
, choose
j
large enough that the first term on the right hand side is smaller than
\epsilon/2
. Given this
j
, choose
l
large enough
that the second term is smaller than
\epsilon/2
for all
t\in\mathcal{I}_{T}
. Then the left hand side is smaller than
\epsilon
for all
t\in\mathcal{I}_{T}
. In particular

f[u_{l}(\cdot,t)]
converges uniformly to
f[u(\cdot,t)]
. This means that the map
t\mapsto f[u(\cdot,t)]
is continuous; i.e.,
u
considered as
a map from
\mathcal{I}_{T}
to
H^{m+1}(\bar{M},\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}})
is weakly continuous. Similarly
v
can be considered to be a weakly continuous map from
\mathcal{I}_{T}
to

H^{m+1}(\bar{M},\mathbb{R}^{n_{2}})
and
\partial_{t}v
can be considered to be a weakly continuous map from
\mathcal{I}_{T}
to
H^{m}(\bar{M},\mathbb{R}^{n_{2}})
. Finally, the

N_{i}
can be considered to be weakly continuous maps from
\mathcal{I}_{T}
to
H^{m+2}(\bar{M},\mathbb{R}^{n_{2}})
.

Energy inequality.
In order to prove (
80
), it is of interest to return to Lemma
23
. Let

\begin{split}&\check{F}_{2,m+1}[v_{l+1},u_{l+1}](t)\\
:=&\langle[u_{l+1}(\cdot,t),v_{l+1}(\cdot,t),(\partial_{t}v_{l+1})(\cdot,t)],[u_{l+1}(\cdot,t),v_{l+1}(\cdot,t),(\partial_{t}v_{l+1})(\cdot,t)]\rangle_{H,t},\end{split}

where
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{H,t}
is introduced in (
76
) and the functions
u
,
v
and
N_{1}
appearing in (
76
) are
part of the solution obtained above. We define
\check{E}_{2,m+1}[v_{l+1},u_{l+1}]
in exactly the same way, but with
u
,
v
and
N_{1}
appearing in
(
76
) replaced by
u_{l+1}
,
v_{l}
and
N_{1,l+1}
respectively; note that this definition coincides with the one given in
(
42
). Due to the convergence and boundedness derived so far, we know that

(94)

\lim_{l\rightarrow\infty}(\check{F}_{2,m+1}[v_{l+1},u_{l+1}]-\check{E}_{2,m+1}[v_{l+1},u_{l+1}])=0.

In fact, the convergence is uniform on
\mathcal{I}_{T}
. Next, note that Lemma
20
yields bounds on the
N_{i,l}
in terms of

v_{l-1}
and
u_{l}
. More specifically, for
l\geq 3
, there is a continuous and monotonically increasing function
\mathcal{C}_{1}:\mathbb{R}_{+}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}

such that

(95)

\begin{split}\textstyle{\sum}_{i=1}^{2}\|N_{i,l}(\cdot,t)\|_{H^{m+2}(\bar{M})}^{2}\leq&\mathcal{C}_{1}[\ell_{1,l}(t)][\check{E}_{2,m+1}[v_{l},u_{l}](t)+\check{E}_{2,m+1}[v_{l-1},u_{l-1}](t)+1]\end{split}

for
t\in\mathcal{I}_{T}
. Here

\ell_{1,l}(t):=\|u_{l}(\cdot,t)\|_{C^{2}}+\|v_{l-1}(\cdot,t)\|_{C^{2}}+\|\partial_{t}v_{l-1}(\cdot,t)\|_{C^{1}}.

Moreover,
\mathcal{C}_{1}
only depends on
m
, the covering,
(\bar{M},\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}})
,
\{E_{i}\}
and the model system. Next, we wish to apply
Lemma
23
. Note, to this end, that
\mathfrak{N}_{a,b}
appearing in the statement of Lemma
23

can in the present setting be estimated according to

\begin{split}\mathfrak{N}_{a,b}^{2}(t)\leq&\mathcal{C}_{2}[\ell_{1,l}(t)](\check{E}_{\mathrm{tot},m+1,l}+1)\end{split}

for
t\in\mathcal{I}_{T}
, where

\check{E}_{\mathrm{tot},m+1,l}(t):=\check{E}_{2,m+1}[v_{l+1},u_{l+1}](t)+\check{E}_{2,m+1}[v_{l},u_{l}](t)+\check{E}_{2,m+1}[v_{l-1},u_{l-1}](t).

Moreover,
\mathcal{C}_{2}
has the same dependence and properties as
\mathcal{C}_{1}
. In order to obtain this estimate, we appealed to
(
95
). Next, note that in the present setting, Lemma
15
yields

\mathfrak{n}_{a,b}(t)\leq\mathcal{B}[\ell_{1,l+1}(t)+\ell_{1,l}(t)],

where
\mathcal{B}:\mathbb{R}_{+}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
is a continuous and monotonically increasing function depending only on the covering,
(\bar{M},\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}})
,

\{E_{i}\}
and the model system. Combining the above observations with Lemma
23
yields a continuous and monotonically
increasing function
\mathcal{C}_{E}:\mathbb{R}_{+}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that

(96)

\begin{split}&\check{E}_{2,m+1}[v_{l+1},u_{l+1}](t_{1})\\
\leq&\check{E}_{2,m+1}[v_{l+1},u_{l+1}](t_{0})+\big|\textstyle{\int}_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\mathcal{C}_{E}[\ell_{l}(t)](\check{E}_{\mathrm{tot},m+1,l}(t)+1)dt\big|\end{split}

for
t\in\mathcal{I}_{T}
, where

\ell_{l}(t):=\ell_{1,l+1}(t)+\ell_{1,l}(t)+\|v_{l+1}(\cdot,t)\|_{C^{1}}.

Moreover,
\mathcal{C}_{E}
only depends on
m
, the covering,
(\bar{M},\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}})
,
\{E_{i}\}
and the model system. Next, let

\check{\mathfrak{E}}_{2,m+1}[v,u]
be defined as in the statement of the lemma. Moreover, let

\check{\mathfrak{F}}_{2,m+1}:=\limsup_{l\rightarrow\infty}\check{F}_{2,m+1}[v_{l},u_{l}].

Combining this notation with (
94
), (
96
), Fatou’s lemma and an argument similar to Lebesgue’s dominated
convergence theorem yields

(97)

\check{\mathfrak{F}}_{2,m+1}(t_{1})\leq\check{\mathfrak{E}}_{2,m+1}[v,u](t_{0})+\big|\textstyle{\int}_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\mathfrak{C}[\ell(t)][\check{\mathfrak{F}}_{2,m+1}(t)+1]dt\big|

for
t\in\mathcal{I}_{T}
, where
\mathfrak{C}
is a function with the properties given in the statement of the lemma. Moreover,
\ell(t)
is defined by
(
81
). Combining (
97
) with Grönwall’s lemma yields

(98)

\check{\mathfrak{F}}_{2,m+1}(t_{1})+1\leq[\check{\mathfrak{E}}_{2,m+1}[v,u](t_{0})+1]\exp\big(\big|\textstyle{\int}_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\mathfrak{C}[\ell(t)]dt\big|\big)

for
t_{1}\in\mathcal{I}_{T}
.

Next, let
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{H,t}
be the inner product introduced in (
76
), where the functions
u
,
v
and
N_{1}
appearing in
(
76
) are part of the solution obtained above. Then

(99)

\begin{split}\check{\mathfrak{E}}_{2,m+1}[v,u](t)=&\langle(u(\cdot,t),v(\cdot,t),(\partial_{t}v)(\cdot,t)),(u(\cdot,t),v(\cdot,t),(\partial_{t}v)(\cdot,t))\rangle_{H,t}\\
=&\textstyle{\lim}_{l\rightarrow\infty}\langle(u(\cdot,t),v(\cdot,t),(\partial_{t}v)(\cdot,t)),(u_{l}(\cdot,t),v_{l}(\cdot,t),(\partial_{t}v_{l})(\cdot,t))\rangle_{H,t}\\
\leq&\check{\mathfrak{E}}_{2,m+1}^{1/2}[v,u](t)\check{\mathfrak{F}}_{2,m+1}^{1/2}(t),\end{split}

where the second equality is due to the weak convergence of
u_{l}(\cdot,t)
to
u(\cdot,t)
etc., and we used the fact that

\check{\mathfrak{F}}_{2,m+1}(t)=\limsup_{l\rightarrow\infty}\langle(u_{l}(\cdot,t),v_{l}(\cdot,t),(\partial_{t}v_{l})(\cdot,t)),(u_{l}(\cdot,t),v_{l}(\cdot,t),(\partial_{t}v_{l})(\cdot,t))\rangle_{H,t}.

Due to (
99
),
\check{\mathfrak{E}}_{2,m+1}[v,u](t)\leq\check{\mathfrak{F}}_{2,m+1}[v,u](t)
, so that (
98
) yields (
80
).

Strong continuity.
Next, we wish to prove strong continuity of the solution. Due to uniqueness, it is sufficient to prove continuity
at
t=t_{0}
. In other words, it is sufficient to prove that

\lim_{t\rightarrow t_{0}}\left(\|(u,v)(\cdot,t)-(u,v)(\cdot,t_{0})\|_{H^{m+1}(\bar{M})}+\|(\partial_{t}v)(\cdot,t)-(\partial_{t}v)(\cdot,t_{0})\|_{H^{m}(\bar{M})}\right)=0.

Let, to this end,

\xi(t):=[u(\cdot,t),v(\cdot,t),(\partial_{t}v)(\cdot,t)],\ \ \ \eta:=(\mathcal{U}_{0},\mathcal{V}_{0,0},\mathcal{V}_{0,1}).

Then

(100)

\langle\xi(t)-\eta,\xi(t)-\eta\rangle_{H,t_{0}}=\langle\xi(t),\xi(t)\rangle_{H,t_{0}}-2\langle\xi(t),\eta\rangle_{H,t_{0}}+\langle\eta,\eta\rangle_{H,t_{0}}.

Note that the last term on the right hand side is
\check{\mathfrak{E}}_{2,m+1}[v,u](t_{0})
. Due to the weak continuity of the solution, the second term
converges to
-2\check{\mathfrak{E}}_{2,m+1}[v,u](t_{0})
. In order to calculate the limit of the first term, note first that

\lim_{t\rightarrow t_{0}}[\langle\xi(t),\xi(t)\rangle_{H,t}-\langle\xi(t),\xi(t)\rangle_{H,t_{0}}]=0.

Moreover, due to (
80
),

\lim_{t\rightarrow t_{0}}\langle\xi(t),\xi(t)\rangle_{H,t}\leq\check{\mathfrak{E}}_{2,m+1}[v,u](t_{0}).

Combining these observations with (
77
) yields strong continuity at
t_{0}
and thus, by the above argument, strong continuity
on
\mathcal{I}_{T}
. More specifically,

(101)

u\in C^{0}(\mathcal{I}_{T},H^{m+1}(\bar{M},\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}})),\ \ v\in C^{0}(\mathcal{I}_{T},H^{m+1}(\bar{M},\mathbb{R}^{n_{2}})),\ \ \partial_{t}v\in C^{0}(\mathcal{I}_{T},H^{m}(\bar{M},\mathbb{R}^{n_{2}})).

Note, however, that
\partial_{t}v
here denotes the time derivative of
v
considered as a function on
M_{T}
and not the time derivative
of
v
considered as a function from
\mathcal{I}_{T}
to
H^{m}(\bar{M},\mathbb{R}^{n_{2}})
. Nevertheless, from (
101
), it can be deduced that

(102)

v\in C^{1}(\mathcal{I}_{T},H^{m}(\bar{M},\mathbb{R}^{n_{2}}));

we leave the details to the reader. In what follows, we take steps of this type without further comment. Combining (
101
),
(
102
), (
3c
) and Corollary
109
yields

(103)

N_{1}\in C^{0}(\mathcal{I}_{T},H^{m+2}(\bar{M})).

Combining (
101
), (
103
), (
3d
) and Corollary
109
yields

(104)

N_{2}\in C^{0}(\mathcal{I}_{T},H^{m+2}(\bar{M})).

Differentiability with respect to time.
In order to derive higher order regularity in time, note that combining
(
101
) and (
103
) with (
3a
) yields the conclusion that

(105)

u\in C^{1}(\mathcal{I}_{T},H^{m+1}(\bar{M},\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}})).

Due to (
101
), (
103
), (
104
) and (
3b
), it similarly follows that

(106)

v\in C^{2}(\mathcal{I}_{T},H^{m-1}(\bar{M},\mathbb{R}^{n_{2}})).

Next, combining (
102
) and (
105
) with (
3c
) and Corollary
109
yields

(107)

N_{1}\in C^{1}(\mathcal{I}_{T},H^{m+2}(\bar{M})).

Combining (
102
), (
105
), (
106
) and (
107
) with
(
3d
) and Corollary
109
yields

(108)

N_{2}\in C^{1}(\mathcal{I}_{T},H^{m+1}(\bar{M})).

Continuing as above, an inductive argument yields (
79
). The proposition follows.
∎

2.8.
Continuation criterion, smooth solutions

Next, we derive a continuation criterion and prove the existence of smooth solutions, given smooth initial data.
The statements and proofs of the results are very similar to the corresponding statements and results of

[
18
, Section 9.4, pp. 87–89]
.

Lemma 33
.

Fix a model system,
m>n/2+2
,
\mathcal{U}_{0}\in H^{m+1}(\bar{M},\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}})
,
\mathcal{V}_{0,0}\in H^{m+1}(\bar{M},\mathbb{R}^{n_{2}})
and
\mathcal{V}_{0,1}\in H^{m}(\bar{M},\mathbb{R}^{n_{2}})
.
Let
t_{0}\in\mathcal{I}
and
T>0
be such that
t_{0}+T\in\mathcal{I}
. Assume that there is a solution
u,v,N_{1},N_{2}
to (
3
) on

\mathcal{I}_{T}:=[t_{0},t_{0}+T]
satisfying
(
78
) and (
7
). Then
u
,
v
,
N_{1}
and
N_{2}
satisfy (
79
).
Moreover, (
80
) is satisfied for
t_{1}\in\mathcal{I}_{T}
. Let
T_{m}
be the supremum of the times
T
such that there is a
solution on
[t_{0},t_{0}+T]
satisfying (
7
) and (
78
)–(
79
). Then
either
T_{m}=t_{+}-t_{0}
or

(109)

\lim_{t\uparrow T_{m}}\sup_{t_{0}\leq\tau\leq t_{0}+t}\left(\|u(\cdot,\tau)\|_{C^{2}}+\|v(\cdot,\tau)\|_{C^{2}}+\|\partial_{t}v(\cdot,\tau)\|_{C^{1}}\right)=\infty,

where
t_{\pm}
are defined by
\mathcal{I}=(t_{-},t_{+})
.

Remark 34
.

Due to the characterisation of
T_{m}
, it is clear that
T_{m}
is independent of
m
. Note also that a similar argument
gives a similar conclusion in the opposite time direction.

Proof.

Consider the estimate (
80
). Due to Proposition
30
, we know that this estimate holds on an interval
containing
t_{0}
in its interior. Let
\mathscr{A}_{+}
be the set of
0\leq T_{*}\leq T
such that

(110a)

\displaystyle u\in

\displaystyle C^{1}\left([t_{0},t_{0}+T_{*}],H^{m+1}(\bar{M},\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}})\right),

(110b)

\displaystyle v\in

\displaystyle\textstyle{\bigcap}_{j=0}^{1}C^{j}\left([t_{0},t_{0}+T_{*}],H^{m+1-j}(\bar{M},\mathbb{R}^{n_{2}})\right)

and such that (
80
) holds for all
t_{1}\in[t_{0},t_{0}+T_{*}]
. Due to Proposition
30
, we know that there is a

T_{*}>0
such that
T_{*}\in\mathscr{A}_{+}
. This means that
\mathscr{A}_{+}
is a non-empty and connected subset of
[0,T]
. We need to prove that it is open and
closed. In order to prove openness,
assume
T_{*}\in\mathscr{A}_{+}
. Then we can apply Proposition
30
to the initial data induced at
t=t_{0}+T_{*}
. This leads to a solution
in a neighbourhood, say
I_{\epsilon}:=[t_{0}+T_{*}-\epsilon,t_{0}+T_{*}+\epsilon]
for some
\epsilon>0
, of
t=t_{0}+T_{*}
(which coincides with the original solution on their
common existence interval due to uniqueness; cf. Lemma
29
).
This means that (
110
) holds with
T_{*}
replaced by

T_{*}+\min\{\epsilon,T-T_{*}\}
and that (
80
) holds for
t_{1}\in[t_{0},t_{0}+T_{*}+\min\{\epsilon,T-T_{*}\}]
. Thus
\mathscr{A}_{+}
is open. To
prove that it is closed, assume
T_{*}
to belong to the closure of
\mathscr{A}_{+}
. Due to (
80
), which holds for any
t_{1}<t_{0}+T_{*}
,
it is clear that there is, on the set
[t_{0},t_{0}+T_{*})
, a uniform bound on the
H^{m+1}
-norms of
u(\cdot,t)
and
v(\cdot,t)
and the
H^{m}
-norm
of
(\partial_{t}v)(\cdot,t)
. Due to the dependence of the existence time on the data, the equation etc. in Proposition
30
, it is
clear that we obtain a solution beyond
t_{0}+T_{*}
, that (
110
) holds and that (
80
) holds for

t_{1}\in[t_{0},t_{0}+T_{*}]
. This means that
\mathscr{A}_{+}=[0,T]
.
Since
u
,
v
,
N_{1}
and
N_{2}
locally have the regularity (
79
), it is clear that they have this regularity on
\mathcal{I}_{T}
.

Finally, to prove the characterisation of
T_{m}
, assume that
T_{m}<t_{+}-t_{0}
, but that (
109
) does not hold. This means that

\|u(\cdot,t)\|_{C^{2}}
,
\|v(\cdot,t)\|_{C^{2}}
and
\|\partial_{t}v(\cdot,t)\|_{C^{1}}
are uniformly bounded on
[t_{0},t_{0}+T_{m})
.
Combining this observation with (
80
) yields the conclusion that
\check{\mathfrak{E}}_{2,m+1}[v,u]
is uniformly bounded on this interval.
However, by the local existence result, Proposition
30
, we can then extend the solution beyond
t_{0}+T_{m}
. This contradicts
the definition of
T_{m}
.
∎

Finally, we are in a position to prove Corollary
4
.

Proof of Corollary
4
.

Given smooth initial data, we can apply Proposition
30
and Lemma
33
for a fixed
m>n/2+2
. This
yields a solution with an existence interval as characterised in the statement of the corollary. Moreover, since the initial data are smooth, we can
apply Lemma
33
for every
m>n/2+2
. This means that the solution satisfies (
79
) for every

m>n/2+2
. In particular, the solution is smooth. The uniqueness follows from Lemma
29
.
∎

2.9.
Stability

Next, we prove Cauchy stability of solutions to (
3
); i.e., Proposition
6
.

Proof of Proposition
6
.

In what follows, we refer to the solution
u
,
v
,
N_{1}
,
N_{2}
to (
3
) as the
background solution
. Given initial data
as in (
9
), we refer to the corresponding solution (denoted
u_{\mathrm{per}}
,
v_{\mathrm{per}}
,
N_{1,\mathrm{per}}
and
N_{2,\mathrm{per}}
) to (
3
)
satisfying (
10
) as the
perturbed solution
. Denote the corresponding existence intervals obtained by appealing to Corollary
4

by
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{bg}}
and
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{per}}
. We are interested in controlling the distance between the two solutions. It is therefore of interest to
derive a system of equations for
\mathfrak{u}:=u_{\mathrm{per}}-u
,
\mathfrak{v}:=v_{\mathrm{per}}-v
,
\mathfrak{N}_{1}:=N_{1,\mathrm{per}}-N_{1}
and
\mathfrak{N}_{2}:=N_{2,\mathrm{per}}-N_{2}
.
Compute

(111)

\partial_{t}\mathfrak{u}=\mathfrak{f}_{1}[\mathfrak{u},\mathfrak{v},\mathfrak{N}_{1}],

where

(112)

\begin{split}\mathfrak{f}_{1}[\mathfrak{u},\mathfrak{v},\mathfrak{N}_{1}]=&\textstyle{\int}_{0}^{1}(\partial_{u}f_{1})[s\mathfrak{u}+u,\mathfrak{v}+v,\mathfrak{N}_{1}+N_{1}]ds\cdot\mathfrak{u}\\
&+\textstyle{\int}_{0}^{1}(\partial_{v}f_{1})[u,s\mathfrak{v}+v,\mathfrak{N}_{1}+N_{1}]ds\cdot\mathfrak{v}\\
&+\textstyle{\int}_{0}^{1}(\partial_{N_{1}}f_{1})[u,v,s\mathfrak{N}_{1}+N_{1}]ds\cdot(\mathfrak{N}_{1},E_{1}\mathfrak{N}_{1},\dots,E_{n}\mathfrak{N}_{1}).\end{split}

Here
\partial_{N_{1}}f_{1}
denotes the derivative of
f_{1}
with respect to all arguments corresponding to
N_{1}
; i.e.,
N_{1}
,
E_{1}N_{1},\dots,E_{n}N_{1}
.
In particular,
\mathfrak{f}_{1}
is such that
\mathfrak{f}_{1}[0,0,0]=0
(setting the third variable to zero here also entails putting the
E_{i}\mathfrak{N}_{1}
arguments
to zero). We would like to think of (
111
) as fitting into the framework introduced at the beginning of Subsection
1.1
.
However,
\mathfrak{f}_{1}
then has to satisfy the conditions imposed on
f_{1}
. To this end, we interpret the occurrences of
u
,
v
and
N_{1}
as given
functions (contributing to the (non-explicit)
t
and
\bar{x}
dependence). In order to obtain the desired bounds on the functions appearing in the system,
it is therefore natural to replace
\mathcal{I}
appearing in the definition of a model system by an open interval
\mathcal{I}_{0}
, where
\mathcal{I}_{0}
has compact closure
contained in
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{bg}}
. We can also assume that
t_{0},t_{\mathrm{fin}}\in\mathcal{I}_{0}
. However, even so,
\mathfrak{f}_{1}
and its derivatives are typically not bounded.
Nevertheless, this can be arranged by replacing the last factor in the first term on the right hand side (i.e.,
\mathfrak{u}
) by
\phi\circ\mathfrak{u}
, where

\phi:=C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}},\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}})
is such that
\phi(\xi)=\xi
for
|\xi|\leq 1
; and similarly for the last factors in the second and third terms
on the right hand side. This leads to a function
\mathfrak{g}_{1}
, satisfying the requirements introduced in Subsection
1.1
, such that

\mathfrak{g}_{1}[0,0,0]=0
, and such that if
|\mathfrak{u}|\leq 1
,
|\mathfrak{v}|\leq 1
,
|\mathfrak{N}_{1}|\leq 1
and
|E_{i}\mathfrak{N}_{1}|\leq 1
,
i=1,\dots,n
, then
\mathfrak{u}
,
\mathfrak{v}
and

\mathfrak{N}_{1}
solve (
111
) iff they solve

\partial_{t}\mathfrak{u}=\mathfrak{g}_{1}[\mathfrak{u},\mathfrak{v},\mathfrak{N}_{1}].

Next, (
3b
) yields

\begin{split}\partial_{t}^{2}\mathfrak{v}=&\bar{h}_{1}^{ij}[\mathfrak{u}+u,\mathfrak{N}_{1}+N_{1}]E_{i}E_{j}\mathfrak{v}+(\bar{h}_{1}^{ij}[\mathfrak{u}+u,\mathfrak{N}_{1}+N_{1}]-\bar{h}_{1}^{ij}[u,N_{1}])E_{i}E_{j}v\\
&+\gamma^{ij}[\mathfrak{u}+u,\mathfrak{v}+v,\mathfrak{N}_{1}+N_{1}]E_{i}E_{j}\mathfrak{u}\\
&+(\gamma^{ij}[\mathfrak{u}+u,\mathfrak{v}+v,\mathfrak{N}_{1}+N_{1}]-\gamma^{ij}[u,v,N_{1}])E_{i}E_{j}u\\
&+f_{2}[\mathfrak{u}+u,\mathfrak{v}+v,\mathfrak{N}_{1}+N_{1},\mathfrak{N}_{2}+N_{2}]-f_{2}[u,v,N_{1},N_{2}].\end{split}

By arguments similar to the above, this equation is equivalent to

\partial_{t}^{2}\mathfrak{v}=\bar{\mathfrak{h}}_{1}^{ij}[\mathfrak{u},\mathfrak{N}_{1}]E_{i}E_{j}\mathfrak{v}+\eta^{ij}[\mathfrak{u},\mathfrak{v},\mathfrak{N}_{1}]E_{i}E_{j}\mathfrak{u}+\mathfrak{g}_{2}[\mathfrak{u},\mathfrak{v},\mathfrak{N}_{1},\mathfrak{N}_{2}],

assuming
|\mathfrak{u}|\leq 1
,
|E_{i}\mathfrak{u}|\leq 1
,
|\mathfrak{v}|\leq 1
,
|E_{i}\mathfrak{v}|\leq 1
,
|\partial_{t}\mathfrak{v}|\leq 1
,
|\mathfrak{N}_{1}|\leq 1
,
|\mathfrak{N}_{2}|\leq 1
,

|E_{i}\mathfrak{N}_{1}|\leq 1
,
|E_{i}\mathfrak{N}_{2}|\leq 1
,
|E_{i}E_{j}\mathfrak{N}_{1}|\leq 1
and
|E_{i}E_{j}\mathfrak{N}_{2}|\leq 1
,
i,j=1,\dots,n
, where
\bar{\mathfrak{h}}_{1}^{ij}
,

\eta^{ij}
and
\mathfrak{g}_{2}
have the same properties as
\bar{h}_{1}^{ij}
,
\gamma^{ij}
and
f_{2}
, introduced at the beginning of
Subsection
1.1
, respectively. Moreover,
\mathfrak{g}_{2}[0,0,0,0]=0
.

Turning to the lapse equations, (
3c
) yields

\begin{split}&\Delta_{\bar{h}_{2}[\mathfrak{u}+u]}\mathfrak{N}_{1}+(\Delta_{\bar{h}_{2}[\mathfrak{u}+u]}-\Delta_{\bar{h}_{2}[u]})N_{1}\\
=&\zeta[\mathfrak{u}+u,\mathfrak{v}+v]\mathfrak{N}_{1}+(\zeta[\mathfrak{u}+u,\mathfrak{v}+v]-\zeta[u,v])N_{1}\\
&+f_{3}[\mathfrak{u}+u,\mathfrak{v}+v]-f_{3}[u,v].\end{split}

Again, due to arguments similar to the above, this equation is equivalent to

(113)

\Delta_{\bar{\mathfrak{h}}_{2}[\mathfrak{u}]}\mathfrak{N}_{1}=\theta[\mathfrak{u},\mathfrak{v}]\mathfrak{N}_{1}+\mathfrak{g}_{3}[\mathfrak{u},\mathfrak{v}],

assuming
|\mathfrak{u}|\leq 1
,
|\mathfrak{v}|\leq 1
and
|E_{i}\mathfrak{u}|\leq 1
,
i=1,\dots,n
, where
\bar{\mathfrak{h}}_{2}^{ij}
,
\theta
and
\mathfrak{g}_{3}
have the same properties
as
\bar{h}_{2}^{ij}
,
\zeta
and
f_{3}
, introduced at the beginning of Subsection
1.1
, respectively. Moreover,
\mathfrak{g}_{3}[0,0]=0
.
Finally, (
3d
) yields an equation which, due to arguments similar to the above, is equivalent to

\Delta_{\bar{\mathfrak{h}}_{2}[\mathfrak{u}]}\mathfrak{N}_{2}=\theta[\mathfrak{u},\mathfrak{v}]\mathfrak{N}_{2}+\mathfrak{g}_{4}[\mathfrak{u},\mathfrak{v},\mathfrak{N}_{1}],

assuming
|\mathfrak{u}|\leq 1
,
|\mathfrak{v}|\leq 1
,
|E_{i}\mathfrak{u}|\leq 1
,
|E_{i}\mathfrak{v}|\leq 1
,
|\partial_{t}\mathfrak{v}|\leq 1
,
|\mathfrak{N}_{1}|\leq 1
,
|E_{i}\mathfrak{N}_{1}|\leq 1
and

|E_{i}E_{j}\mathfrak{N}_{1}|\leq 1
,
i,j=1,\dots,n
, where
\bar{\mathfrak{h}}_{2}^{ij}
and
\theta
are the same functions as in the case of (
113
) and

\mathfrak{g}_{4}
has the same properties as
f_{4}
, introduced at the beginning of Subsection
1.1
. Moreover,
\mathfrak{g}_{4}[0,0,0]=0
. To
summarise, we obtain the following system

(114a)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\mathfrak{u}=

\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}_{1}[\mathfrak{u},\mathfrak{v},\mathfrak{N}_{1}],

(114b)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}^{2}\mathfrak{v}=

\displaystyle\bar{\mathfrak{h}}_{1}^{ij}[\mathfrak{u},\mathfrak{N}_{1}]E_{i}E_{j}\mathfrak{v}+\eta^{ij}[\mathfrak{u},\mathfrak{v},\mathfrak{N}_{1}]E_{i}E_{j}\mathfrak{u}+\mathfrak{g}_{2}[\mathfrak{u},\mathfrak{v},\mathfrak{N}_{1},\mathfrak{N}_{2}],

(114c)

\displaystyle\Delta_{\bar{\mathfrak{h}}_{2}[\mathfrak{u}]}\mathfrak{N}_{1}=

\displaystyle\theta[\mathfrak{u},\mathfrak{v}]\mathfrak{N}_{1}+\mathfrak{g}_{3}[\mathfrak{u},\mathfrak{v}],

(114d)

\displaystyle\Delta_{\bar{\mathfrak{h}}_{2}[\mathfrak{u}]}\mathfrak{N}_{2}=

\displaystyle\theta[\mathfrak{u},\mathfrak{v}]\mathfrak{N}_{2}+\mathfrak{g}_{4}[\mathfrak{u},\mathfrak{v},\mathfrak{N}_{1}].

Note here that
\bar{\mathfrak{h}}_{1}^{ij}
,
\bar{\mathfrak{h}}_{2}^{ij}
and
\theta
satisfy (
1
) and (
2
) with the same values
of
\lambda
and
\lambda_{\zeta}
as
\bar{h}_{1}^{ij}
,
\bar{h}_{2}^{ij}
and
\zeta
.
Since this system satisfies the conditions described at the beginning of Subsection
1.1
(with
\mathcal{I}
replaced by
\mathcal{I}_{0}
),
Corollary
4
applies. Given smooth initial data at
t_{0}\in\mathcal{I}_{0}
, say
\mathfrak{u}_{0}
,
\mathfrak{v}_{0,0}
and
\mathfrak{v}_{0,1}
, we thus obtain
a smooth solution
\mathfrak{u}
,
\mathfrak{v}
,
\mathfrak{N}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{N}_{2}
to (
114
). Denote the corresponding existence interval by
\mathcal{I}_{{\mathrm{max}}}\subset\mathcal{I}_{0}
.
Restricting the system given by (
114
) to the subinterval
\mathcal{I}_{{\mathrm{max}}}
, it still satisfies the conditions described at the beginning of
Subsection
1.1
(with
\mathcal{I}
replaced by
\mathcal{I}_{{\mathrm{max}}}
). The advantage of taking this perspective is that we can then think of the
solution
\mathfrak{u}
,
\mathfrak{v}
,
\mathfrak{N}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{N}_{2}
as arising from an application of Lemma
14
; i.e.,
u_{a}=u_{b}=\mathfrak{u}
,

v_{a}=v_{b}=\mathfrak{v}
etc. Moreover, we can appeal to (
27
) and (
28
) in order to conclude that there is a
continuous and monotonically increasing function
\mathcal{B}:\mathbb{R}_{+}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that

(115)

\begin{split}\|\mathfrak{N}_{1}(\cdot,t)\|_{C^{2,1}}+\|\mathfrak{N}_{2}(\cdot,t)\|_{C^{2,1}}\leq&\mathcal{B}[\mathfrak{l}(t)]\mathfrak{l}(t)\end{split}

for all
t\in\mathcal{I}_{{\mathrm{max}}}
, where

(116)

\mathfrak{l}(t):=\|\mathfrak{u}(\cdot,t)\|_{C^{2}}+\|\mathfrak{v}(\cdot,t)\|_{C^{2}}+\|\partial_{t}\mathfrak{v}(\cdot,t)\|_{C^{1}}.

Here
\mathcal{B}
only depends on bounds on
\bar{\mathfrak{h}}_{2}^{ij}
,
\theta
,
\mathfrak{g}_{3}
and
\mathfrak{g}_{4}
and
their derivatives up to order two, one, two and two respectively;
(\bar{M},\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}})
; the covering;
\{E_{i}\}
; the
\lambda
appearing in
(
1
); and the
\lambda_{\zeta}
appearing in (
2
). Returning to (
35
) and
(
37
), it is thus clear that
\mathfrak{n}_{a,b}
is bounded by the right hand side of (
115
) (with a trivial modification
of
\mathcal{B}
). Next, Lemma
20
and Remark
22
yield the conclusion that there is a
continuous and monotonically increasing function
\mathcal{C}:\mathbb{R}_{+}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that

(117)

\begin{split}\|\mathfrak{N}_{1}\|_{H^{m+2}(\bar{M}_{t})}+\|\mathfrak{N}_{2}\|_{H^{m+2}(\bar{M}_{t})}\leq&\mathcal{C}[\mathfrak{l}(t)](\|\mathfrak{u}\|_{H^{m+1}(\bar{M}_{t})}+\|\mathfrak{v}\|_{H^{m+1}(\bar{M}_{t})}+\|\partial_{t}\mathfrak{v}\|_{H^{m}(\bar{M}_{t})})\end{split}

for all
t\in\mathcal{I}_{{\mathrm{max}}}
. Here
\mathcal{C}
only depends on the covering;
(\bar{M},\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}})
;
\{E_{i}\}
;
m
;
\lambda
;
\lambda_{\zeta}
; a bound on up to

m+1
derivatives of
\theta
,
\mathfrak{g}_{3}
and
\mathfrak{g}_{4}
; and up to
m+2
derivatives of
\bar{\mathfrak{h}}_{2}^{ij}
.
Returning to (
34
) and
(
37
), it is thus clear that
\mathfrak{N}_{a,b}
is bounded by the right hand side of (
117
) (with a trivial modification
of
\mathcal{C}
). Returning to (
44
) with this information at hand yields a continuous and monotonically increasing function

\mathcal{C}_{m}
such that

(118)

\begin{split}|\check{E}_{2,m+1}[\mathfrak{v},\mathfrak{u}](t_{1})-\check{E}_{2,m+1}[\mathfrak{v},\mathfrak{u}](t_{0})|\leq&\big|\textstyle{\int}_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\mathcal{C}_{m}[\mathfrak{l}(t)]\check{E}_{2,m+1}[\mathfrak{v},\mathfrak{u}](t)dt\big|.\end{split}

Here
\mathcal{C}_{m}
only depends on bounds on up to
m+2
derivatives of
\theta
,
\mathfrak{g}_{1}
,
\mathfrak{g}_{2}
,
\mathfrak{g}_{3}
,
\mathfrak{g}_{4}
,
\bar{\mathfrak{h}}_{1}^{ij}
,

\bar{\mathfrak{h}}_{2}^{ij}
and
\eta^{ij}
;
(\bar{M},\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}})
; the covering;
\{E_{i}\}
;
m
;
\lambda
; and
\lambda_{\zeta}
. In what follows, it is of interest
to note that, due to Sobolev embedding, there is a constant
K_{\mathfrak{l}}\geq 1
, depending only on
\lambda
,
\{E_{i}\}
and
(\bar{M},\bar{h}_{\mathrm{ref}})
, such that

(119)

\mathfrak{l}(t)\leq K_{\mathfrak{l}}\check{E}_{2,m+1}^{1/2}[\mathfrak{v},\mathfrak{u}](t)

for all
t\in\mathcal{I}_{{\mathrm{max}}}
. Combining (
118
) with Grönwall’s lemma yields

(120)

\check{E}_{2,m+1}[\mathfrak{v},\mathfrak{u}](t_{1})\leq\check{E}_{2,m+1}[\mathfrak{v},\mathfrak{u}](t_{0})\exp\big(\big|\textstyle{\int}_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\mathcal{C}_{m}[\mathfrak{l}(t)]dt\big|\big)

for all
t\in\mathcal{I}_{{\mathrm{max}}}
. Let
C_{m}:=\mathcal{C}_{m}(1)
and let
t_{\mathrm{fin}}
be as in the statement of the proposition. Assume that
t_{\mathrm{fin}}\geq t_{0}
(the
argument in the case that
t_{\mathrm{fin}}\leq t_{0}
is similar and left to the reader) and that

(121)

\check{E}_{2,m+1}[\mathfrak{v},\mathfrak{u}](t_{0})\leq(3K_{\mathfrak{l}})^{-2}\exp(-C_{m}|t_{\mathrm{fin}}-t_{0}|).

Let
\mathscr{A}
be the set of
t\in[t_{0},t_{\mathrm{fin}}]
such that
[t_{0},t]\subset\mathcal{I}_{{\mathrm{max}}}
and such that

(122)

\check{E}_{2,m+1}[\mathfrak{v},\mathfrak{u}](s)\leq(2K_{\mathfrak{l}})^{-2}

for all
s\in[t_{0},t]
. Due to (
121
), it is clear that
\mathscr{A}
is non-empty. It is also connected by definition. What remains
is to prove that it is open and closed. In order to prove that it is open, assume
t\in\mathscr{A}
. Then
[t_{0},t]\subset\mathcal{I}_{{\mathrm{max}}}
by definition. In
particular, the solution exists beyond
t
. Moreover, combining (
119
) and (
122
) yields

\mathfrak{l}(s)\leq 1/2

for all
s\in[t_{0},t]
. By continuity, there is therefore an
\epsilon>0
such that
[t_{0},t+\epsilon)\subset\mathcal{I}_{{\mathrm{max}}}
and
\mathfrak{l}(s)\leq 1
for all

s\in[t_{0},t+\epsilon)
. This means, in particular, that
\mathcal{C}_{m}[\mathfrak{l}(s)]\leq C_{m}
for all
s\in[t_{0},t+\epsilon)
. Combining this observation
with (
120
) and (
121
) yields

\check{E}_{2,m+1}[\mathfrak{v},\mathfrak{u}](s)\leq(3K_{\mathfrak{l}})^{-2}\exp(-C_{m}|t_{\mathrm{fin}}-t_{0}|)\exp\big(\big|\textstyle{\int}_{t_{0}}^{s}\mathcal{C}_{m}[\mathfrak{l}(t)]dt\big|\big)\leq(3K_{\mathfrak{l}})^{-2}

for all
s\in[t_{0},t+\epsilon)\cap[t_{0},t_{\mathrm{fin}}]
. This means that the bootstrap assumption (
122
) is satisfied for all

s\in[t_{0},t+\epsilon)\cap[t_{0},t_{\mathrm{fin}}]
. Thus

\mathscr{A}
is open. Finally, we need to prove that it is closed. Assume, to this end that
t_{k}\in\mathscr{A}
is an increasing sequence converging to

t_{*}\in\mathcal{I}_{0}
. There are two possibilities. Either
[t,t_{*}]\subset\mathcal{I}_{{\mathrm{max}}}
or
t_{*}\notin\mathcal{I}_{{\mathrm{max}}}
. In the former case,
t_{*}\in\mathscr{A}

due to continuity. In case
t_{*}\notin\mathcal{I}_{{\mathrm{max}}}
, we have to have

(123)

\textstyle{\limsup}_{t\rightarrow t_{*}}\mathfrak{l}(t)=\infty

due to the Corollary
4
. On the other hand, since
t_{k}\in\mathscr{A}
, combining (
119
) and (
122
)
for
t\in[t_{0},t_{k}]
yields the conclusion that
\mathfrak{l}(t)\leq 1/2
for
t\in[t_{0},t_{k}]
. Since
t_{k}\rightarrow t_{*}
, this means that

\mathfrak{l}(t)\leq 1/2
for all
t\in[t_{0},t_{*})
. This conclusion contradicts (
123
). Thus
t_{*}\in\mathscr{A}
, and
\mathscr{A}
is closed.
This means that
\mathscr{A}=[t_{0},t_{\mathrm{fin}}]
. By the above arguments, it also follows that
\mathfrak{l}(t)\leq 1/2
for all
t\in[t_{0},t_{\mathrm{fin}}]
. Combining
this observation with (
120
) yields the conclusion that

(124)

\check{E}_{2,m+1}[\mathfrak{v},\mathfrak{u}](t)\leq\check{E}_{2,m+1}[\mathfrak{v},\mathfrak{u}](t_{0})\exp\left(C_{m}|t_{\mathrm{fin}}-t_{0}|\right)

for all
t\in[t_{0},t_{\mathrm{fin}}]
. Combining this observation with (
115
) and (
119
), it is clear that
there is an
\epsilon_{0}>0
such that if
\check{E}_{2,m+1}[\mathfrak{v},\mathfrak{u}](t_{0})\leq\epsilon_{0}
, then

\mathfrak{l}(t)+\|\mathfrak{N}_{1}(\cdot,t)\|_{C^{2,1}}+\|\mathfrak{N}_{2}(\cdot,t)\|_{C^{2,1}}\leq 1

for all
t\in[t_{0},t_{\mathrm{fin}}]
. This means, in particular, that the solution
\mathfrak{u}
,
\mathfrak{v}
,
\mathfrak{N}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{N}_{2}
to (
114
)
corresponds to a solution
u_{\mathrm{per}}=u+\mathfrak{u}
,
v_{\mathrm{per}}=v+\mathfrak{v}
,
N_{1,\mathrm{per}}=N_{1}+\mathfrak{N}_{1}
and
N_{2,\mathrm{per}}=N_{2}+\mathfrak{N}_{2}
to
(
3
). Combining this observation with (
124
), it is clear that given
\epsilon>0
, there is a
\delta>0

such that the statement of the proposition holds.
∎

3.
Gauge choice and constraint propagation

Our next goal is to apply the general theory to gauge fixed versions of Einstein’s equations. In order to clarify our gauge choice, let, to begin with,

(M,g)
be a Lorentz manifold, where
M:=\bar{M}\times\mathcal{I}
,
\bar{M}
is an
n
-dimensional connected (and typically oriented and closed) manifold and
\mathcal{I}
is an
open interval. Assume, moreover, the metrics induced on the leaves
\bar{M}_{t}:=\bar{M}\times\{t\}
, say
\bar{g}
, to be Riemannian. Then the

lapse function

N
and the
shift vector field

\chi
are defined by

(125)

\partial_{t}=NU+\chi,

where
\partial_{t}
denotes differentiation with respect to the second variable in the division
\bar{M}\times\mathcal{I}
,
U
denotes the future directed unit normal
to the leaves
\bar{M}_{t}
and
\chi
is required to be tangential to
\bar{M}_{t}
. The gauge choices we are interested in here are all such that the shift
vector field vanishes. This means that the spacetime metric takes the form

(126)

g=-N^{2}dt\otimes dt+\bar{g},

where the lapse function
N
is strictly positive. In what follows, we typically assume
\bar{M}
to have a global frame
\{E_{i}\}
, though this is not
absolutely necessary. Next, recall that if
\chi=0
, then

(127)

\partial_{t}\bar{k}_{ij}=\dot{\mathfrak{k}}_{ij},

where

(128)

\dot{\mathfrak{k}}_{ij}:=\overline{\nabla}_{i}\overline{\nabla}_{j}N+2N\bar{k}_{i}^{\phantom{i}m}\bar{k}_{mj}-N(\mathrm{tr}_{\bar{g}}\bar{k})\bar{k}_{ij}+NR_{ij}-N\bar{R}_{ij};

see, e.g., the first displayed equation after
[
21
, (263), p. 64]
. Here the indices refer to a fixed frame on
\bar{M}
, say
\{E_{i}\}
.
It is important to note that (
127
) holds for any foliation as above. In particular, it holds irrespective of whether Einstein’s equations
(and additional conditions, such as the CMC condition) are satisfied or not. In (
128
),
\overline{\nabla}
denotes the Levi-Civita connection of the
Riemannian metric
\bar{g}
induced on the leaves of the foliation;
\bar{k}
denotes the induced second fundamental form;
R_{ij}
denotes the spatial
components of the spacetime Ricci tensor; and
\bar{R}_{ij}
denotes the components of the Ricci tensor associated with
\bar{g}
. Specialising to the
Einstein-non-linear scalar field setting with a CMC foliation, the equation we actually wish to solve is

(129)

\partial_{t}\bar{k}_{ij}=\dot{\kappa}_{ij},

where

(130)

\dot{\kappa}_{ij}:=\overline{\nabla}_{i}\overline{\nabla}_{j}N+2N\bar{k}_{i}^{\phantom{i}m}\bar{k}_{mj}-Nt^{-1}\bar{k}_{ij}+N\overline{\nabla}_{i}\phi\overline{\nabla}_{j}\phi+\tfrac{2N}{n-1}(V\circ\phi)\bar{g}_{ij}-N\bar{R}_{ij}.

To arrive at (
129
) from (
127
), we made the substitutions
\mathrm{tr}_{\bar{g}}\bar{k}=1/t
(which is the CMC condition) and

\mathrm{Ric}=d\phi\otimes d\phi+\tfrac{2}{n-1}(V\circ\phi)g,

which are a consequence of the Einstein-non-linear scalar field equations. In order to relate (
127
) and (
129
), it
is convenient to introduce

(131)

\mathscr{D}_{ij}:=N^{-1}(\dot{\mathfrak{k}}_{ij}-\dot{\kappa}_{ij})=-\vartheta\bar{k}_{ij}+R_{ij}-\overline{\nabla}_{i}\phi\overline{\nabla}_{j}\phi-\tfrac{2}{n-1}(V\circ\phi)\bar{g}_{ij},

where
\theta:=\mathrm{tr}_{\bar{g}}\bar{k}
and
\vartheta:=\theta-1/t
. Next, we introduce an orthonormal frame with respect to which we express the equations.

3.1.
Orthonormal frame

The orthonormal frame we use here is defined by an evolution equation; see (
132
) below. We begin by deriving conditions on the
evolution equation that ensure that the frame remains orthonormal.

Lemma 35
.

Let
(M,g)
be a Lorentz manifold, where
M=\bar{M}\times\mathcal{I}
and
\mathcal{I}
is an open interval. Assume
\partial_{t}
to be timelike and orthogonal to the leaves

\bar{M}_{t}:=\bar{M}\times\{t\}
of the foliation, and let
N>0
be such that
g(\partial_{t},\partial_{t})=-N^{2}
; cf. (
125
). Assume that there
is a global frame
\{E_{i}\}
of the tangent space of
\bar{M}
. Let
\{e_{I}\}
,
I=1,\dots,n:=\mathrm{dim}{\bar{M}}
, be a frame for the leaves of the foliation.
Assume the frame to be orthonormal on one leaf and to satisfy the evolution equations

(132)

\partial_{t}e_{I}^{i}=f_{I}^{J}e_{J}^{i},

for some functions
f_{I}^{J}
, where
e_{I}^{i}
is defined by the condition that
e_{I}=e_{I}^{i}E_{i}
. Then the frame
\{e_{I}\}
is orthonormal
on all the leaves if and only if the components of the symmetric part of the matrix with components
f_{I}^{J}
are given by
-N\bar{k}_{IJ}
;
i.e., if

(133)

f_{I}^{J}+f_{J}^{I}=-2N\bar{k}_{IJ},

where
\bar{k}
denotes the second fundamental form of the leaves of the foliation and
\bar{k}_{IJ}:=\bar{k}(e_{I},e_{J})
.

Remark 36
.

It is important to note that any choice of antisymmetric part of the matrix with components
f_{I}^{J}
is compatible with the frame
\{e_{I}\}

being orthonormal. In what follows, we write
f_{I}^{J}=s_{I}^{J}+a_{I}^{J}
, where
s_{I}^{J}=s_{J}^{I}
and
a_{I}^{J}=-a_{J}^{I}
. By the lemma,

s_{I}^{J}=-N\bar{k}_{IJ}
.

Remark 37
.

If
\{\omega^{I}\}
is the frame dual to
\{e_{I}\}
and
\omega^{I}_{i}
is defined by the condition that

\omega^{I}=\omega^{I}_{i}\eta^{i}
, where
\{\eta^{i}\}
is the frame dual to
\{E_{i}\}
, then
\delta_{I}^{J}=\omega^{J}_{i}e^{i}_{I}
, so that

\partial_{t}\omega^{I}_{i}=-f^{I}_{J}\omega^{J}_{i}.

Proof.

By time differentiating the condition
\delta_{IJ}=g(e_{I},e_{J})
, it can be verified that the condition that the frame remains
orthonormal is equivalent to (
133
). The lemma follows.
∎

Next, define
{\bar{\gamma}}_{IJ}^{K}
by the relation
[e_{I},e_{J}]={\bar{\gamma}}_{IJ}^{K}e_{K}
. It is of interest to derive evolution equations for
{\bar{\gamma}}_{IJ}^{K}
.

Lemma 38
.

Given assumptions and notation as in Lemma
35
, define
{\bar{\gamma}}_{IJ}^{K}
as above. Then

(134)

\begin{split}\partial_{t}{\bar{\gamma}}_{IJ}^{K}=&e_{I}(f_{J}^{K})-e_{J}(f_{I}^{K})+f_{I}^{M}{\bar{\gamma}}_{MJ}^{K}+f_{J}^{M}{\bar{\gamma}}_{IM}^{K}-f_{M}^{K}{\bar{\gamma}}_{IJ}^{M}.\end{split}

Remark 39
.

Note also that
{\bar{\gamma}}_{IJ}^{K}
is polynomial in
\eta_{ij}^{k}
,
\omega^{I}_{i}
,
e_{J}^{j}
and
E_{k}e_{L}^{l}
(and of first order in the last
expression). Here
\eta_{ij}^{k}
is defined by
[E_{i},E_{j}]=\eta_{ij}^{k}E_{k}
.

Proof.

Note that
0=[\partial_{t},[e_{I},e_{J}]]+[e_{I},[e_{J},\partial_{t}]]+[e_{J},[\partial_{t},e_{I}]]
due to the Jacobi identity. Moreover,
[e_{J},\partial_{t}]=-f_{J}^{M}e_{M}
and

\begin{split}[\partial_{t},[e_{I},e_{J}]]=&[\partial_{t},{\bar{\gamma}}_{IJ}^{K}e_{K}]=(\partial_{t}{\bar{\gamma}}_{IJ}^{K}+{\bar{\gamma}}_{IJ}^{M}f_{M}^{K})e_{K}.\end{split}

Combining these observations yields (
134
).
∎

Our next goal is to derive a second order equation for
\bar{k}_{IJ}
. To this end, we first need to derive a first order equation.
However,

\partial_{t}\bar{k}_{IJ}=\partial_{t}(\bar{k}_{ij}e_{I}^{i}e_{J}^{j})=(\partial_{t}\bar{k}_{ij})e_{I}^{i}e_{J}^{j}+f_{I}^{M}\bar{k}_{MJ}+\bar{k}_{IM}f_{J}^{M}.

If (
129
) holds (and note, very carefully, that we most of the time do not assume (
129
) to hold, we only deduce it
at the very end)

(135)

\begin{split}\partial_{t}\bar{k}_{IJ}=&\dot{\kappa}_{IJ}+f_{I}^{M}\bar{k}_{MJ}+\bar{k}_{IM}f_{J}^{M}\\
=&\overline{\nabla}_{I}\overline{\nabla}_{J}N+2N\bar{k}_{IM}\bar{k}_{MJ}-Nt^{-1}\bar{k}_{IJ}+Ne_{I}(\phi)e_{J}(\phi)\\
&+\tfrac{2N}{n-1}(V\circ\phi)\delta_{IJ}-N\bar{R}_{IJ}+f_{I}^{M}\bar{k}_{MJ}+\bar{k}_{IM}f_{J}^{M};\end{split}

here we use the convention that we sum over repeated indices, even if both are subscripts. In order to derive a second order equation for

\bar{k}_{IJ}
, we need to differentiate (
135
). Most of the terms that result do not contribute to the symbol. However, we do need
to compute
\partial_{t}\bar{R}_{IJ}
.

Lemma 40
.

Given assumptions and notation as in Lemma
35
, assume
\mathcal{I}\subset(0,\infty)
, assume
\phi
to be a smooth function on
M
and
assume the frame
\{e_{I}\}
to be orthonormal on all the leaves of the foliation. Let
\bar{R}_{IJ}
denote the components of the spatial Ricci curvature
with respect to the orthonormal frame
\{e_{I}\}
, let
\theta:=\mathrm{tr}_{\bar{g}}\bar{k}
and let
\vartheta:=\theta-1/t
. Let, moreover,

(136)

\mathscr{C}(e_{I}):=(\overline{\nabla}_{e_{L}}\bar{k})(e_{L},e_{I})-e_{I}(\theta)-e_{0}(\phi)e_{I}(\phi),

where
e_{0}:=N^{-1}\partial_{t}
. Then

(137)

\partial_{t}\bar{R}_{IJ}-N\overline{\nabla}_{I}\mathscr{C}_{J}-N\overline{\nabla}_{J}\mathscr{C}_{I}-N\overline{\nabla}_{I}\overline{\nabla}_{J}\vartheta=-e_{M}e_{M}(N\bar{k}_{IJ})+\Upsilon_{IJ}+\mathfrak{R}_{IJ},

where
\mathfrak{R}_{IJ}
is polynomial in
f_{L}^{M}
,
e_{L}^{i}
,
\omega^{L}_{i}
,
N
,
N^{-1}
,
\bar{k}_{LM}
,
\partial_{t}\phi
,
E_{i}f_{L}^{M}
,
E_{i}e_{L}^{j}
,

E_{i}N
,
E_{i}\bar{k}_{LM}
,
E_{i}\phi
,
E_{i}\partial_{t}\phi
,
E_{i}E_{j}\phi
and
E_{i}E_{j}N
. Moreover,
\Upsilon_{IJ}
is a sum of terms, each of which
can be written as a polynomial in
f_{L}^{M}
,
e_{L}^{i}
,
\omega^{L}_{i}
and
\bar{k}_{LM}
multiplied by a single factor of the form
E_{i}E_{j}e_{K}^{k}
.

Remark 41
.

Note that if the Einstein-non-linear scalar field equations are satisfied, then
\mathscr{C}
introduced in (
136
) vanishes. Moreover, if
the CMC condition
\theta=1/t
holds, then
\vartheta=0
.

Remark 42
.

In the statement of the lemma, as well as in the proof, we consistently omit reference to dependence on spatial derivatives of the structure
coefficients of
\{E_{i}\}
; i.e.,
E_{\mathbf{I}}\eta^{i}_{jk}
, where we use the notation introduced in Remark
39
and
Subsection
A.5
. In particular, it is tacitly understood that the coefficients of the polynomials
\mathfrak{R}_{IJ}
and

\Upsilon_{IJ}
are allowed to depend on expressions of the form
E_{\mathbf{I}}\eta^{i}_{jk}
.

Remark 43
.

The expressions appearing in (
137
) are largely geometric. However, we later use them in a different, non-geometric, context
(in particular, below, the notation
\bar{k}_{IJ}
is used for variables that are only a posteriori demonstrated to be the components of the second
fundamental form with respect to a frame). In
this later context, the symmetry properties of the last objects on the right hand side of (
137
) are important. It is therefore important
to note that in the current context, we can replace
\Upsilon_{IJ}
and
\mathfrak{R}_{IJ}
with
\Upsilon_{(IJ)}
and
\mathfrak{R}_{(IJ)}
respectively, where the
parentheses denote symmetrisation, since all the terms on the left hand side and the first term on the right hand side of (
137
) are
symmetric in the geometric context.

Proof.

It is convenient to begin by expressing the spatial Ricci curvature in terms of the structure coefficients
{\bar{\gamma}}_{IJ}^{K}
and the connection
coefficients
{\bar{\Gamma}}_{IJ}^{K}
. Here
{\bar{\Gamma}}^{K}_{IJ}
is defined by the condition that

\overline{\nabla}_{e_{I}}e_{J}={\bar{\Gamma}}_{IJ}^{K}e_{K}.

Due to the Koszul formula,

(138)

{\bar{\Gamma}}_{IJ}^{K}=\langle\overline{\nabla}_{e_{I}}e_{J},e_{K}\rangle=\tfrac{1}{2}(-{\bar{\gamma}}_{JK}^{I}+{\bar{\gamma}}_{KI}^{J}+{\bar{\gamma}}_{IJ}^{K}),

where
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle:=\bar{g}
and
[e_{I},e_{J}]={\bar{\gamma}}_{IJ}^{K}e_{K}
. Note, for future reference, that
\partial_{t}{\bar{\gamma}}_{IJ}^{K}
and
\partial_{t}{\bar{\Gamma}}_{IJ}^{K}

are polynomials in
f_{L}^{M}
,
E_{i}f_{L}^{M}
,
e_{L}^{i}
,
\omega^{L}_{i}
and
E_{i}e_{L}^{j}
(keeping the convention introduced in
Remark
42
in mind); see (
134
), (
138
) and
Remark
39
. Moreover, the polynomials are of order one in
E_{i}f_{L}^{M}
and
E_{i}e_{L}^{j}
. Next, note that

(139)

\bar{R}_{IJ}=e_{I}({\bar{\Gamma}}^{J}_{MM})-e_{M}({\bar{\Gamma}}^{J}_{IM})+{\bar{\Gamma}}^{L}_{MM}{\bar{\Gamma}}_{IL}^{J}-{\bar{\Gamma}}_{IL}^{M}{\bar{\Gamma}}^{J}_{LM}-{\bar{\gamma}}^{M}_{IL}{\bar{\Gamma}}^{J}_{ML},

where we sum over all repeated indices. When time differentiating (
139
), it is clear that the only terms that cannot immediately be
included in
\mathfrak{R}_{IJ}
arise when the time derivative hits the first two terms on the right hand side of (
139
). In short, we need
to focus on

(140)

\bar{L}_{IJ}:=f_{I}^{L}e_{L}({\bar{\Gamma}}^{J}_{MM})-f_{M}^{L}e_{L}({\bar{\Gamma}}^{J}_{IM})+e_{I}(\partial_{t}{\bar{\Gamma}}^{J}_{MM})-e_{M}(\partial_{t}{\bar{\Gamma}}^{J}_{IM}).

Due to Remark
39
and (
138
), it is clear that the first two terms on the right hand side give rise to expressions
that can be included in either
\Upsilon_{IJ}
or
\mathfrak{R}_{IJ}
. We therefore need to focus on the last two terms. Note, to this end, that

{\bar{\Gamma}}^{J}_{MM}={\bar{\gamma}}^{M}_{JM}
. Combining this observation with (
134
) yields

(141)

\begin{split}\partial_{t}{\bar{\Gamma}}^{J}_{MM}=&\partial_{t}{\bar{\gamma}}^{M}_{JM}=e_{J}(f_{M}^{M})-e_{M}(f_{J}^{M})+f_{J}^{K}{\bar{\gamma}}_{KM}^{M}\\
=&-e_{J}(N\theta)+e_{M}(N\bar{k}_{MJ})-e_{M}(a_{J}^{M})+f_{J}^{K}{\bar{\gamma}}_{KM}^{M},\end{split}

where
\theta:=\mathrm{tr}_{\bar{g}}\bar{k}
. Rewriting
\theta=\vartheta+1/t
,
-e_{J}(N\theta)=-e_{J}(N)\theta-Ne_{J}(\vartheta)
, so that applying
e_{I}
to the first
term on the far right hand side of (
141
) yields
-N\overline{\nabla}_{I}\overline{\nabla}_{J}\vartheta
plus expressions that can be included in
\mathfrak{R}_{IJ}
. The terms
that result when applying
e_{I}
to the last term on the far right hand side can, similarly to the first two terms on the right hand side of
(
140
), be included in either
\Upsilon_{IJ}
or
\mathfrak{R}_{IJ}
. What remains to consider is
e_{M}(N\bar{k}_{MJ})
and
-e_{M}(a_{J}^{M})
, or, more
precisely,
e_{I}e_{M}(N\bar{k}_{MJ})
and
-e_{I}e_{M}(a_{J}^{M})
; cf. the second last term on the right hand side of (
140
). Next, consider

(142)

\begin{split}\partial_{t}{\bar{\Gamma}}^{J}_{IM}=&\tfrac{1}{2}(-\partial_{t}{\bar{\gamma}}_{MJ}^{I}+\partial_{t}{\bar{\gamma}}_{JI}^{M}+\partial_{t}{\bar{\gamma}}_{IM}^{J})\\
=&e_{M}(N\bar{k}_{IJ})-e_{J}(N\bar{k}_{IM})+e_{I}(a_{M}^{J})+\tfrac{1}{2}f_{M}^{K}({\bar{\gamma}}_{IK}^{J}-{\bar{\gamma}}_{KJ}^{I})+\tfrac{1}{2}f_{J}^{K}({\bar{\gamma}}^{M}_{KI}-{\bar{\gamma}}_{MK}^{I})\\
&+\tfrac{1}{2}f_{I}^{K}({\bar{\gamma}}_{JK}^{M}+{\bar{\gamma}}_{KM}^{J})+\tfrac{1}{2}f^{I}_{K}{\bar{\gamma}}^{K}_{MJ}-\tfrac{1}{2}f_{K}^{M}{\bar{\gamma}}^{K}_{JI}-\tfrac{1}{2}f_{K}^{J}{\bar{\gamma}}^{K}_{IM},\end{split}

where we appealed to Lemmas
35
and
38
. Similarly to the above, applying
e_{M}
to the last six terms on
the far right hand side results in expressions that can be included in either
\Upsilon_{IJ}
or
\mathfrak{R}_{IJ}
. Combining (
140
),
(
141
) and (
142
) with the above observations yields the conclusion that the important terms in
\partial_{t}\bar{R}_{IJ}
are

(143)

\begin{split}&-N\overline{\nabla}_{I}\overline{\nabla}_{J}\vartheta+e_{I}e_{M}(N\bar{k}_{MJ})-e_{I}e_{M}(a_{J}^{M})\\
&-e_{M}e_{M}(N\bar{k}_{IJ})+e_{M}e_{J}(N\bar{k}_{IM})-e_{M}e_{I}(a_{M}^{J})\\
=&-e_{M}e_{M}(N\bar{k}_{IJ})-N\overline{\nabla}_{I}\overline{\nabla}_{J}\vartheta+e_{I}e_{M}(N\bar{k}_{MJ})\\
