&+e_{J}e_{M}(N\bar{k}_{IM})+{\bar{\gamma}}_{MI}^{K}e_{K}(a_{J}^{M})+{\bar{\gamma}}_{MJ}^{K}e_{K}(N\bar{k}_{IM}).\end{split}

The last two terms give rise to expressions that can be included in
\mathfrak{R}_{IJ}
. We therefore only need to focus on the first four terms.
However, we wish to keep the first two terms; cf. (
137
). This leaves the third and fourth terms on the right hand side of
(
143
). On the other hand, the sum of these terms can, to leading order, be expressed as a symmetrised gradient of

\mathscr{C}
. Compute, in order to justify this statement, that

(144)

e_{M}(\bar{k}_{MJ})=\mathscr{C}(e_{J})+e_{J}(\theta)+e_{0}(\phi)e_{J}(\phi)+{\bar{\Gamma}}_{MM}^{K}\bar{k}_{KJ}+{\bar{\Gamma}}_{MJ}^{K}\bar{k}_{MK}.

From this equality, we can deduce two things. First,
\mathscr{C}(e_{L})
can be included in
\mathfrak{R}_{IJ}
. Second, applying
e_{I}
to (
144
)
yields

\begin{split}e_{I}e_{M}(\bar{k}_{MJ})=&\overline{\nabla}_{I}\mathscr{C}_{J}+\overline{\nabla}_{I}\overline{\nabla}_{J}\theta+\mathscr{C}_{K}{\bar{\Gamma}}^{K}_{IJ}+{\bar{\Gamma}}_{IJ}^{K}e_{K}(\theta)\\
&+e_{I}[e_{0}(\phi)e_{J}(\phi)+{\bar{\Gamma}}_{MM}^{K}\bar{k}_{KJ}+{\bar{\Gamma}}_{MJ}^{K}\bar{k}_{MK}].\end{split}

All the terms on the right hand side but the first two give rise to expressions that can be included in
\Upsilon_{IJ}
or
\mathfrak{R}_{IJ}
. Keeping
in mind that
\overline{\nabla}_{J}\theta=\overline{\nabla}_{J}\vartheta
and that
\overline{\nabla}_{I}\overline{\nabla}_{J}\vartheta=\overline{\nabla}_{J}\overline{\nabla}_{I}\vartheta
, the lemma follows.
∎

Next, we wish to calculate the time derivative of
\dot{\varkappa}_{IJ}
, where

(145)

\dot{\varkappa}_{IJ}:=\dot{\kappa}_{IJ}+f_{I}^{M}\bar{k}_{MJ}+\bar{k}_{IM}f_{J}^{M}.

Note that
\dot{\varkappa}_{IJ}
is given by the far right hand side of (
135
).

Lemma 44
.

Given assumptions and notation as in Lemma
40
, let
\dot{\varkappa}_{IJ}
be given by (
145
). Then

(146)

\partial_{t}\dot{\varkappa}_{IJ}+N^{2}\overline{\nabla}_{I}\mathscr{C}_{J}+N^{2}\overline{\nabla}_{J}\mathscr{C}_{I}+N^{2}\overline{\nabla}_{I}\overline{\nabla}_{J}\vartheta=Ne_{M}e_{M}(N\bar{k}_{IJ})-(\partial_{t}N)\bar{R}_{IJ}+\hat{\Omega}_{IJ}+\hat{\mathfrak{K}}_{IJ},

where
\hat{\mathfrak{K}}_{IJ}
is polynomial in
f_{L}^{M}
,
e_{L}^{i}
,
\omega^{L}_{i}
,
N
,
N^{-1}
,
\bar{k}_{LM}
,
\partial_{t}N
,
\partial_{t}\phi
,
\partial_{t}\bar{k}_{LM}
,

\partial_{t}f_{L}^{M}
,
E_{i}f_{L}^{M}
,
E_{i}e_{L}^{j}
,
E_{i}N
,
E_{i}\bar{k}_{LM}
,
E_{i}\phi
,
E_{i}\partial_{t}N
,
E_{i}\partial_{t}\phi
,
E_{i}E_{j}\phi
,
E_{i}E_{j}N
,

E_{i}E_{j}\partial_{t}N
and
V^{(l)}\circ\phi
(where the dependence on the last expression is of first order). Moreover,
\hat{\Omega}_{IJ}
is a
sum of terms, each of which can be written as a polynomial in
N
,
f_{L}^{M}
,
e_{L}^{i}
,
\omega^{L}_{i}
and
\bar{k}_{LM}
multiplied by
a single factor of the form
E_{i}E_{j}e_{L}^{k}
.

Remark 45
.

In the statement and the proof, explicit dependence on
t
is tacit, and we use the convention introduced in
Remark
42
.

Remark 46
.

As in Remark
43
, we can replace
\hat{\Omega}_{IJ}
and
\hat{\mathfrak{K}}_{IJ}
with
\hat{\Omega}_{(IJ)}
and
\hat{\mathfrak{K}}_{(IJ)}
respectively.

Proof.

We compute the time derivative of
\dot{\varkappa}_{IJ}
by considering one term at a time on the far right hand side of (
135
).
Note that the first term on the right hand side can be written

\overline{\nabla}_{I}\overline{\nabla}_{J}N=e_{I}(e_{J}N)-{\bar{\Gamma}}_{IJ}^{K}e_{K}(N)=e_{I}^{i}E_{i}(e_{J}^{k})E_{k}N+e_{I}^{i}e_{J}^{k}E_{i}E_{k}N-{\bar{\Gamma}}_{IJ}^{K}e_{K}(N).

Time differentiating this expression, keeping (
142
) in mind, leads to terms that can be included in
\hat{\mathfrak{K}}_{IJ}
. Time differentiating
the remaining terms (except for the sixth) on the far right hand side of (
135
) also leads to terms that can be included in
\hat{\mathfrak{K}}_{IJ}
.
The term that result when the time derivative hits
N
in the sixth term is included explicitly on the right hand side. Finally,

-N\partial_{t}\bar{R}_{IJ}+N^{2}\overline{\nabla}_{I}\mathscr{C}_{J}+N^{2}\overline{\nabla}_{J}\mathscr{C}_{I}+N^{2}\overline{\nabla}_{I}\overline{\nabla}_{J}\vartheta=Ne_{M}e_{M}(N\bar{k}_{IJ})-N\Upsilon_{IJ}-N\mathfrak{R}_{IJ},

where we appealed to (
137
). The lemma follows.
∎

3.2.
Equations for deviations from the CMC condition and from Einstein’s equations

In the end, we wish to solve the Einstein-non-linear scalar field equations. Moreover, we wish to prove that the foliation we construct has leaves
of constant mean curvature. We thus need to take the step from the equations we actually solve to the equations we wish to solve. This involves, to
begin with, the problem of deriving equations for the relevant deviations and then proving that, given appropriate initial conditions, the deviations
all vanish. We begin by deriving an evolution equation for
\mathscr{D}_{ij}
defined in (
131
).

Lemma 47
.

Given assumptions and notation as in Lemma
40
, let
\mathscr{D}_{ij}
be defined by (
131
) and
\overline{\mathscr{C}}
be defined by

\overline{\mathscr{C}}:=\mathscr{C}+\tfrac{1}{2}d\vartheta
. Assume, moreover, that

(147)

\begin{split}e_{0}^{2}(\bar{k}_{IJ})=&N^{-1}e_{M}e_{M}(N\bar{k}_{IJ})-N^{-2}(\partial_{t}N)\bar{R}_{IJ}+N^{-2}\hat{\Omega}_{(IJ)}\\
&+N^{-2}\hat{\mathfrak{K}}_{(IJ)}-e_{0}(\ln N)N^{-1}\dot{\varkappa}_{IJ}+N^{-1}f_{I}^{K}\mathscr{D}_{KJ}+N^{-1}f^{K}_{J}\mathscr{D}_{IK},\end{split}

where
\hat{\Omega}_{IJ}
and
\hat{\mathfrak{K}}_{IJ}
are the expressions arising in Lemma
44
and
\dot{\varkappa}_{IJ}
is defined by (
145
). Then

(148)

e_{0}(\mathscr{D}_{ij})=\overline{\nabla}_{i}\overline{\mathscr{C}}_{j}+\overline{\nabla}_{j}\overline{\mathscr{C}}_{i},

where the lower script Latin indices refer to the frame
\{E_{i}\}
.

Remark 48
.

For future reference, it is convenient to note that (
148
) implies that

(149)

e_{0}(\mathrm{tr}_{\bar{g}}\mathscr{D})=-2\bar{k}^{ij}\mathscr{D}_{ij}+2\overline{\nabla}^{i}\overline{\mathscr{C}}_{i}.

Remark 49
.

Assuming that the initial data for
\partial_{t}\bar{k}_{IJ}
at some time, say
t=t_{0}
, is given by

(150)

\partial_{t}\bar{k}_{IJ}|_{t=t_{0}}=\dot{\varkappa}_{IJ}|_{t=t_{0}},

then (
152
) below yields the conclusion that

(151)

\mathscr{D}_{IJ}|_{t=t_{0}}=0.

Proof.

Note, to begin with, that, for purely geometric reasons,

(152)

e_{0}(\bar{k}_{IJ})=N^{-1}\dot{\varkappa}_{IJ}+\mathscr{D}_{IJ}.

Time differentiating (
152
) and appealing to (
146
) and Remark
46
yields

\begin{split}e_{0}^{2}(\bar{k}_{IJ})=&N^{-1}e_{M}e_{M}(N\bar{k}_{IJ})-N^{-2}(\partial_{t}N)\bar{R}_{IJ}+N^{-2}\hat{\Omega}_{(IJ)}+N^{-2}\hat{\mathfrak{K}}_{(IJ)}-\overline{\nabla}_{I}\mathscr{C}_{J}-\overline{\nabla}_{J}\mathscr{C}_{I}\\
&-\overline{\nabla}_{I}\overline{\nabla}_{J}\vartheta+e_{0}(\mathscr{D}_{IJ})-e_{0}(\ln N)N^{-1}\dot{\varkappa}_{IJ}.\end{split}

Combining this equation with (
147
) yields

(153)

e_{0}(\mathscr{D}_{IJ})=N^{-1}f_{I}^{K}\mathscr{D}_{KJ}+N^{-1}\mathscr{D}_{IK}f^{K}_{J}+\overline{\nabla}_{I}\mathscr{C}_{J}+\overline{\nabla}_{J}\mathscr{C}_{I}+\overline{\nabla}_{I}\overline{\nabla}_{J}\vartheta.

Note that this can be viewed as an evolution equation for
\mathscr{D}_{IJ}
. With respect to the frame
\{E_{i}\}
, this equation reads (
148
).
∎

For future reference, it is convenient to make the following observation.

Lemma 50
.

Given assumptions and notation as in Lemma
40
, (
147
) is equivalent to

(154)

\partial_{t}^{2}\bar{k}_{IJ}=N^{2}e^{l}_{K}e^{m}_{K}E_{l}E_{m}\bar{k}_{IJ}+\Omega_{IJ}+\mathfrak{K}_{IJ},

where
\mathfrak{K}_{IJ}
is polynomial in
f_{I}^{J}
,
e_{I}^{i}
,
\omega^{I}_{i}
,
N
,
N^{-1}
,
\bar{k}_{IJ}
,
\partial_{t}N
,
\partial_{t}\phi
,
\partial_{t}\bar{k}_{IJ}
,

\partial_{t}f_{I}^{J}
,
E_{i}f_{I}^{J}
,
E_{i}e_{I}^{j}
,
E_{i}N
,
E_{i}\bar{k}_{IJ}
,
E_{i}\phi
,
E_{i}\partial_{t}N
,
E_{i}\partial_{t}\phi
,
E_{i}E_{j}\phi
,
E_{i}E_{j}N
,

E_{i}E_{j}\partial_{t}N
and
V^{(l)}\circ\phi
(where the dependence on the last expression is of first order). Moreover,
\Omega_{IJ}
is a sum of terms,
each of which can be written as a polynomial in
N
,
f_{I}^{J}
,
e_{I}^{i}
,
\omega^{I}_{i}
and
\bar{k}_{IJ}
multiplied by a single factor of the
form
E_{i}E_{j}e_{I}^{k}
.

Remark 51
.

Remark
45
is equally relevant in the present setting.

Remark 52
.

Considering (
154
) as an equation for variables
\bar{k}_{IJ}
, where the
\bar{k}_{IJ}
are not a priori the components of the second
fundamental form with respect to a frame (so that, in particular,
\bar{k}_{IJ}
does not necessarily equal
\bar{k}_{JI}
), the sum of the last two
terms on the right hand side can be written as a sum of a symmetric term and a sum of terms, each of which contains either a factor of the
form
\bar{k}_{[LM]}
or a factor of the form
\partial_{t}\bar{k}_{[LM]}
, where we use the notation introduced in (
157
).

Proof.

Multiply (
147
) by
N^{2}
. The second last term on the right hand side of the resulting equation is given by

(155)

Nf_{I}^{K}\mathscr{D}_{KJ}=f_{I}^{K}(\partial_{t}\bar{k}_{KJ}-\dot{\varkappa}_{KJ}),

where
\dot{\varkappa}_{IJ}
is given by the far right hand side of (
135
); see (
152
). Considering the far right hand
side of (
135
), it is clear that (
155
) gives rise to terms that can all be included in either
\mathfrak{K}_{IJ}
or

\Omega_{IJ}
. A similar argument yields the same conclusion for
N^{2}
times the last term on the right hand side of (
147
).
Next, writing
N^{2}e_{0}^{2}(\bar{k}_{IJ})=\partial_{t}^{2}\bar{k}_{IJ}-N^{-1}(\partial_{t}N)\partial_{t}\bar{k}_{IJ}
etc., (
147
) yields

(156)

\partial_{t}^{2}\bar{k}_{IJ}=N^{2}e^{l}_{K}e^{m}_{K}E_{l}E_{m}\bar{k}_{IJ}-(\partial_{t}N)\bar{R}_{IJ}-e_{0}(N)\dot{\varkappa}_{IJ}+\Omega_{IJ}^{\prime}+\mathfrak{K}_{IJ}^{\prime},

where
\Omega_{IJ}^{\prime}
and
\mathfrak{K}_{IJ}^{\prime}
have the same dependence as
\mathfrak{K}_{IJ}
and
\Omega_{IJ}
above. The only expression in
-e_{0}(N)\dot{\varkappa}_{IJ}

which cannot immediately be included into
\mathfrak{K}_{IJ}
is

-e_{0}(N)(-N\bar{R}_{IJ})=(\partial_{t}N)\bar{R}_{IJ}.

Combining this observation with (
156
) yields the conclusion of the lemma.

Next, in order to justify Remark
52
, we divide
\bar{k}_{IJ}
into a symmetric and antisymmetric part:

\bar{k}_{IJ}=\bar{k}_{(IJ)}+\bar{k}_{[IJ]}
, where

(157)

\bar{k}_{(IJ)}:=\tfrac{1}{2}(\bar{k}_{IJ}+\bar{k}_{JI}),\ \ \ \bar{k}_{[IJ]}:=\tfrac{1}{2}(\bar{k}_{IJ}-\bar{k}_{JI}).

Next, let
b_{IJ}
be symmetric; i.e.,
b_{IJ}=b_{JI}
. Then
f_{I}^{K}b_{KJ}+f_{J}^{K}b_{IK}
is symmetric in
I
and
J
. If
b_{IJ}
is not symmetric, then

f_{I}^{K}b_{KJ}+f_{J}^{K}b_{IK}=(f_{I}^{K}b_{(KJ)}+f_{J}^{K}b_{(IK)})+(f_{I}^{K}b_{[KJ]}+f_{J}^{K}b_{[IK]}).

Here the first term on the right hand side is symmetric, and each term in the second term on the right hand side contains a factor of
b_{[LM]}
.
Applying this observation with
b_{IJ}=\partial_{t}\bar{k}_{IJ}-\dot{\varkappa}_{IJ}
and recalling that the first, fourth, fifth and sixth terms on the far right hand
side of (
135
) are symmetric by construction, it follows that the sum of the last two terms on the right hand side of
(
147
) can be written as a sum of one expression which is symmetric in
I
and
J
and one expression which is a sum of terms,
each of which contains a factor of the form
\bar{k}_{[LM]}
or
\partial_{t}\bar{k}_{[LM]}
. The third and fourth terms on the right hand side of
(
147
) are explicitly symmetric. The second term is symmetric by construction. What remains is to consider the antisymmetric
part of
\dot{\varkappa}_{IJ}
. However, considering the far right hand side of (
135
), it is clear that each expression in
\dot{\varkappa}_{[IJ]}

contains a factor of the form
\bar{k}_{[LM]}
.
∎

The lapse equation.
Next, we derive an evolution equation for
\vartheta=\mathrm{tr}_{\bar{g}}\bar{k}-t^{-1}
. However, to obtain such an equation, we need
to impose an equation for the lapse function. Moreover, the evolution equation for
\vartheta
will depend on a function quantifying the deviation
from the Hamiltonian constraint.

Lemma 53
.

Given assumptions and notation as in Lemma
40
, let

(158)

\mathscr{H}:=-\bar{S}-t^{-2}+|\bar{k}|_{\bar{g}}^{2}+[e_{0}(\phi)]^{2}+|\overline{\nabla}\phi|_{\bar{g}}^{2}+2V\circ\phi,

where
\bar{S}
denotes the spatial scalar curvature. Assume, moreover,
N
to satisfy

(159)

-N^{-1}t^{-2}=N^{-1}\Delta_{\bar{g}}N-|\bar{k}|_{\bar{g}}^{2}-[e_{0}(\phi)]^{2}+\tfrac{2}{n-1}V\circ\phi.

Then

(160)

e_{0}(\vartheta)=-t^{-1}\vartheta+\mathscr{D}_{MM}+\mathscr{H}.

Remark 54
.

Using the notation (
158
), the normal normal component of Einstein’s equations reads
\mathscr{H}+t^{-2}-\theta^{2}=0
in the
Einstein-non-linear scalar field setting; i.e.,
\mathscr{H}+t^{-2}-\theta^{2}=0
is equivalent to the Hamiltonian constraint being satisfied.

Proof.

Using the notation
\theta:=\mathrm{tr}_{\bar{g}}\bar{k}
, note that

e_{0}(\theta)=N^{-1}\Delta_{\bar{g}}N-\theta^{2}+\bar{g}^{ij}R_{ij}-\bar{S};

see
[
21
, (258), p. 63]
. Note that this identity is geometric; it is not based on the assumption that Einstein’s equations are
satisfied. Using the notation introduced in (
131
), it follows that

\bar{g}^{ij}R_{ij}=R_{MM}=\mathscr{D}_{MM}+\theta\vartheta+|\overline{\nabla}\phi|_{\bar{g}}^{2}+\tfrac{2n}{n-1}V\circ\phi.

Moreover, combining the last two equalities with (
158
)
yields

e_{0}(\theta)=N^{-1}\Delta_{\bar{g}}N-|\bar{k}|_{\bar{g}}^{2}-[e_{0}(\phi)]^{2}+\tfrac{2}{n-1}V\circ\phi-t^{-1}\vartheta+\mathscr{D}_{MM}+\mathscr{H}.

In terms of
\vartheta
, this equality reads

e_{0}(\vartheta)-N^{-1}t^{-2}=N^{-1}\Delta_{\bar{g}}N-|\bar{k}|_{\bar{g}}^{2}-[e_{0}(\phi)]^{2}+\tfrac{2}{n-1}V\circ\phi-t^{-1}\vartheta+\mathscr{D}_{MM}+\mathscr{H}.

Combining this (general, geometric) equality with the imposed equation on
N
, i.e. (
159
), yields the conclusion that
(
160
) holds. The lemma follows.
∎

Equation for the scalar field.
Concerning the scalar field, we assume that it satisfies

(161)

\Box_{g}\phi+\vartheta e_{0}(\phi)-V^{\prime}\circ\phi=0.

This means that the stress energy tensor associated with the scalar field, i.e.,

T:=d\phi\otimes d\phi-\left[\tfrac{1}{2}\nabla^{\alpha}\phi\nabla_{\alpha}\phi+V\circ\phi\right]g

satisfies

(162)

\mathrm{div}_{g}T=-\vartheta e_{0}(\phi)d\phi.

For future reference, it is convenient to make the following observation.

Lemma 55
.

Given assumptions and notation as in Lemma
40
, (
161
) is equivalent to

(163)

\partial_{t}^{2}\phi=N^{2}e_{K}^{i}e_{K}^{j}E_{i}E_{j}\phi+\Phi,

where
\Phi
is polynomial in
N
,
N^{-1}
,
e^{i}_{I}
,
\omega^{I}_{i}
,
\partial_{t}N
,
\partial_{t}\phi
,
E_{i}N
,
E_{i}\phi
,
E_{j}e_{I}^{i}
and
V^{\prime}\circ\phi

(and of order at most one in the last expression). Moreover,

(164)

\partial_{t}^{2}(E_{k}\phi)=N^{2}e_{K}^{i}e_{K}^{j}E_{i}E_{j}(E_{k}\phi)+\Xi_{k}+\Phi_{k},

where
\Phi_{k}
is polynomial in
N
,
N^{-1}
,
e^{i}_{I}
,
\omega^{I}_{i}
,
\partial_{t}N
,
\partial_{t}\phi
,
E_{i}N
,
E_{i}\phi
,
E_{j}e_{I}^{i}
,

E_{i}\partial_{t}N
,
E_{i}\partial_{t}\phi
,
E_{i}E_{j}N
,
E_{i}E_{j}\phi
,
V^{\prime}\circ\phi
and
V^{\prime\prime}\circ\phi
(and of order at most one in the last two
expressions). Moreover,
\Xi_{k}
is a sum of terms, each of which can be written as a polynomial in
N
,
e_{I}^{i}
,
\omega^{I}_{i}
and

E_{i}\phi
multiplied by a single factor of the form
E_{i}E_{j}e_{I}^{k}
. Finally,

(165)

\partial_{t}^{2}(e_{0}\phi)=N^{2}e_{K}^{i}e_{K}^{j}E_{i}E_{j}(e_{0}\phi)+\Psi,

where
\Psi
is polynomial in
N
,
N^{-1}
,
e^{i}_{I}
,
\omega^{I}_{i}
,
f_{J}^{K}
,
\partial_{t}N
,
\partial_{t}\phi
,
E_{i}N
,
E_{i}\phi
,
E_{j}e_{I}^{i}
,

E_{i}f_{J}^{K}
,
E_{i}\partial_{t}N
,
E_{i}\partial_{t}\phi
,
\partial_{t}e_{0}(\phi)
,
E_{i}E_{j}N
,
E_{i}E_{j}\phi
,
V^{\prime}\circ\phi
and

V^{\prime\prime}\circ\phi
(and of order at most one in the last two expressions).

Remark 56
.

Remark
45
is equally relevant in the present setting.

Proof.

Note, first of all, that (
161
) is equivalent to

(166)

e_{0}^{2}(\phi)=e_{K}e_{K}(\phi)-t^{-1}e_{0}(\phi)+e_{K}(\ln N)e_{K}(\phi)-{\bar{\gamma}}_{KJ}^{J}e_{K}(\phi)-V^{\prime}\circ\phi,

where
{\bar{\gamma}}_{KJ}^{L}
is defined by
[e_{K},e_{J}]={\bar{\gamma}}_{KJ}^{L}e_{L}
; we leave the verification of this statement to the reader. Noting that

N^{2}e_{0}^{2}(\phi)=\partial_{t}^{2}\phi-N^{-1}(\partial_{t}N)\partial_{t}\phi
etc., (
166
) can be written

(167)

\begin{split}\partial_{t}^{2}\phi=&N^{2}e_{K}^{i}e_{K}^{j}E_{i}E_{j}\phi+N^{-1}(\partial_{t}N)\partial_{t}\phi+N^{2}e_{K}(e_{K}^{i})E_{i}\phi-t^{-1}N\partial_{t}\phi\\
&+Ne_{K}(N)e_{K}(\phi)-N^{2}{\bar{\gamma}}_{KJ}^{J}e_{K}(\phi)-N^{2}V^{\prime}\circ\phi.\end{split}

We conclude that (
163
) holds. Applying
E_{k}
to (
167
) yields (
164
). Next, we wish to apply

e_{0}
to (
166
). Note, to this end, that

(168)

[e_{0},e_{K}]=N^{-1}f_{K}^{J}e_{J}+N^{-1}e_{K}(N)e_{0}.

It can therefore be computed that

\begin{split}e_{0}e_{K}e_{K}(\phi)=&e_{K}e_{K}(e_{0}\phi)+N^{-1}f_{K}^{J}e_{J}e_{K}(\phi)+N^{-1}e_{K}(N)e_{0}e_{K}(\phi)\\
&+e_{K}[N^{-1}f_{K}^{J}e_{J}(\phi)+N^{-1}e_{K}(N)e_{0}(\phi)].\end{split}

After multiplication by
N^{2}
, the second and fourth terms on the right hand side can be included in
\Psi
; and after multiplying by
N^{2}
and
appealing to (
168
), the third term can also be included in
\Psi
. Multiplying the first term on the right hand side by

N^{2}
yields the first term on the right hand side of (
165
) plus terms that can be included in
\Psi
. Next,
N^{2}e_{0}^{3}(\phi)

gives rise to the term on the left hand side of (
165
) plus terms that can be included in
\Psi
. Applying
N^{2}e_{0}
to the second
and last terms on the right hand side of (
166
) yields terms that can be included in
\Psi
. Appealing to (
134
)
and (
168
), the terms that result when applying
N^{2}e_{0}
to the third and fourth terms on the right hand side of
(
166
) can be included in
\Psi
.
∎

Equations for
\mathscr{D}
and
\mathscr{H}
.
Next, note that if
G
denotes the Einstein tensor, i.e., if
G=\mathrm{Ric}-Sg/2
, where
S
denotes the
scalar curvature of
(M,g)
, then the Bianchi identities imply that
\mathrm{div}_{g}G=0
. Introduce

(169)

\mathscr{R}:=G-T.

Then
\mathrm{div}_{g}\mathscr{R}=\vartheta e_{0}(\phi)d\phi
due to the Bianchi identities combined with (
162
), where we assume (
161
)
to hold. Before deducing evolution equations for
\mathscr{H}
and
\overline{\mathscr{C}}
from this equality, let us relate
\mathscr{R}
to
\mathscr{H}
,
\overline{\mathscr{C}}
,
\vartheta
and
\mathscr{D}
.

Lemma 57
.

Given assumptions and notation as in Lemma
40
, let
\mathscr{R}
be defined by (
169
). Then

(170)

\mathscr{R}(e_{0},e_{0})=-\tfrac{1}{2}\mathscr{H}+\tfrac{1}{2}(\theta+t^{-1})\vartheta,\ \ \ \mathscr{R}(e_{0},e_{I})=\mathscr{C}(e_{I})=\overline{\mathscr{C}}_{I}-\tfrac{1}{2}e_{I}(\vartheta).

Moreover,

(171)

\mathscr{R}_{IJ}=\mathscr{D}_{IJ}+\vartheta\bar{k}_{IJ}-\tfrac{1}{2}\mathscr{H}\delta_{IJ}-\tfrac{1}{2}\vartheta^{2}\delta_{IJ}-(\mathrm{tr}_{\bar{g}}\mathscr{D})\delta_{IJ}.

Proof.

Due to the definitions of
\mathscr{R}
,
\mathscr{C}
,
\overline{\mathscr{C}}
and
\mathscr{H}
, and the Gauß and Codazzi equations
(cf., e.g.,
[
18
, Proposition 13.3, p. 149]
), the equalities (
170
) hold.
Next,
\mathscr{R}_{IJ}=\mathscr{D}_{IJ}+\vartheta\bar{k}_{IJ}
for
I\neq J
. It can also be calculated that

\mathscr{R}_{II}=\mathscr{D}_{II}+\vartheta\bar{k}_{II}+\tfrac{1}{n-1}\mathrm{tr}_{g}\mathscr{R}

(no summation on
I
). Summing over
I
in this equality yields

\mathrm{tr}_{g}\mathscr{R}-\tfrac{1}{2}\mathscr{H}+\tfrac{1}{2}(\theta+t^{-1})\vartheta=\mathrm{tr}_{g}\mathscr{R}+\mathscr{R}(e_{0},e_{0})=\mathrm{tr}_{\bar{g}}\mathscr{D}+\vartheta\theta+\tfrac{n}{n-1}\mathrm{tr}_{g}\mathscr{R}.

Combining the above observations yields (
171
).
∎

Next, we derive evolution equations for
\overline{\mathscr{C}}
and
\mathscr{H}
.

Lemma 58
.

Given assumptions and notation as in Lemma
40
, assume
\phi
to satisfy (
161
). Assume, moreover,
(
147
) and (
159
) to hold. Then

(172)

e_{0}(\mathscr{H})=\overline{\nabla}_{M}\overline{\nabla}_{M}\vartheta+2\overline{\nabla}_{I}(\ln N)\overline{\nabla}_{I}\vartheta-2\overline{\nabla}_{M}\overline{\mathscr{C}}_{M}+2\bar{k}_{IJ}\mathscr{D}_{IJ}+A_{\mathscr{H}}^{I}\overline{\mathscr{C}}_{I}+B_{\mathscr{H}}\vartheta,

where
A_{\mathscr{H}}^{I}
and
B_{\mathscr{H}}
are smooth functions on the domain of definition of the solution. Moreover,

(173)

e_{0}(\mathscr{E})=\overline{\nabla}_{M}\overline{\nabla}_{M}\vartheta+2\overline{\nabla}_{I}(\ln N)\overline{\nabla}_{I}\vartheta+A_{\mathscr{H}}^{I}\overline{\mathscr{C}}_{I}+B_{\mathscr{H}}\vartheta,

where

(174)

\mathscr{E}:=\mathscr{H}+\mathrm{tr}_{\bar{g}}\mathscr{D}.

Finally,

(175)

e_{0}(\overline{\mathscr{C}}_{I})=\overline{\nabla}_{M}\mathscr{D}_{MI}-\tfrac{1}{2}\overline{\nabla}_{I}\mathscr{D}_{MM}+\bar{k}_{IJ}\overline{\nabla}_{J}\vartheta+A^{LM}_{I}\mathscr{D}_{LM}+A^{M}_{I}\overline{\mathscr{C}}_{M}+A_{I}\mathscr{E}+B_{I}\vartheta,

where
A_{I}^{LM}
etc. are smooth functions on the domain of definition of the solution.

Proof.

As we noted in connection with (
169
),
\mathrm{div}_{g}\mathscr{R}=\vartheta e_{0}(\phi)d\phi
. In terms of the frame
\{e_{\alpha}\}
, this equality
can be written

(176)

\begin{split}\vartheta e_{0}(\phi)e_{\alpha}(\phi)=&-(\nabla_{e_{0}}\mathscr{R})(e_{0},e_{\alpha})+(\nabla_{e_{M}}\mathscr{R})(e_{M},e_{\alpha})\\
=&-e_{0}[\mathscr{R}(e_{0},e_{\alpha})]+\mathscr{R}(\nabla_{e_{0}}e_{0},e_{\alpha})+\mathscr{R}(e_{0},\nabla_{e_{0}}e_{\alpha})+e_{M}[\mathscr{R}(e_{M},e_{\alpha})]\\
&-\mathscr{R}(\nabla_{e_{M}}e_{M},e_{\alpha})-\mathscr{R}(e_{M},\nabla_{e_{M}}e_{\alpha}).\end{split}

Introducing the notation
\Gamma_{\alpha\beta}^{\gamma}
by requiring that
\nabla_{e_{\alpha}}e_{\beta}=\Gamma_{\alpha\beta}^{\gamma}e_{\gamma}
, the
0
-component of
(
176
)
reads

\begin{split}\vartheta[e_{0}(\phi)]^{2}=&-e_{0}[-\mathscr{H}/2+(\theta+t^{-1})\vartheta/2]+2\Gamma_{00}^{I}(\overline{\mathscr{C}}_{I}-\overline{\nabla}_{I}\vartheta/2)+e_{M}(\overline{\mathscr{C}}_{M}-\overline{\nabla}_{M}\vartheta/2)\\
&-\Gamma_{MM}^{0}[-\mathscr{H}/2+(\theta+t^{-1})\vartheta/2]-\Gamma_{MM}^{I}(\overline{\mathscr{C}}_{I}-e_{I}(\vartheta)/2)-\Gamma_{M0}^{I}\mathscr{R}_{MI},\end{split}

where we appealed to (
170
). Appealing to (
160
) when
e_{0}
hits
\vartheta
and ignoring terms that
contain an undifferentiated factor
\vartheta
or
\overline{\mathscr{C}}_{I}
, this equality can be written

\begin{split}\dots=&e_{0}(\mathscr{H})/2-(\theta+t^{-1})\mathscr{H}/2-(\theta+t^{-1})\mathscr{D}_{MM}/2-\overline{\nabla}_{I}(\ln N)\overline{\nabla}_{I}\vartheta+\overline{\nabla}_{M}\overline{\mathscr{C}}_{M}\\
&-\overline{\nabla}_{M}\overline{\nabla}_{M}\vartheta/2-\Gamma_{MM}^{I}\overline{\nabla}_{I}\vartheta/2+\theta\mathscr{H}/2+\Gamma_{MM}^{I}\overline{\nabla}_{I}\vartheta/2\\
&-\bar{k}_{IJ}\mathscr{D}_{IJ}+\theta\mathscr{H}/2+\theta\mathscr{D}_{MM}+\dots,\end{split}

where the dots signify terms that contain an undifferentiated factor
\vartheta
or
\overline{\mathscr{C}}_{I}
. Adding up the terms that contain a
factor
\mathscr{H}
results in
\mathscr{H}\vartheta/2
, which contains a factor
\vartheta
. Adding up the terms that contain a
factor
\mathscr{D}_{MM}
results in
\vartheta\mathscr{D}_{MM}/2
, which contains a factor
\vartheta
. To conclude, (
172
) holds.
Combining this equality with (
149
) (which holds if (
147
) is satisfied; cf. Lemma
47
)
yields (
173
).

Next, the
I
-component of
(
176
) reads

\begin{split}\vartheta e_{0}(\phi)e_{I}(\phi)=&-e_{0}(\overline{\mathscr{C}}_{I}-e_{I}(\vartheta)/2)+\Gamma_{00}^{J}\mathscr{R}_{JI}+\Gamma_{0I}^{0}[-\mathscr{H}/2+(\theta+t^{-1})\vartheta/2]\\
&+\Gamma_{0I}^{J}(\overline{\mathscr{C}}_{J}-e_{J}(\vartheta)/2)+e_{M}(\mathscr{R}_{MI})-\Gamma_{MM}^{0}(\overline{\mathscr{C}}_{I}-e_{I}(\vartheta)/2)\\
&-\Gamma_{MM}^{J}\mathscr{R}_{JI}-\Gamma_{MI}^{0}(\overline{\mathscr{C}}_{M}-e_{M}(\vartheta)/2)-\Gamma_{MI}^{J}\mathscr{R}_{MJ}.\end{split}

Ignoring terms that contain an undifferentiated factor
\vartheta
,
\mathscr{E}
,
\mathscr{D}_{IJ}
or
\overline{\mathscr{C}}_{I}
(note that, since
\mathscr{H}=\mathscr{E}-\mathscr{D}_{MM}
,
we also ignore terms containing an undifferentiated factor
\mathscr{H}
; and due to (
171
), we can ignore terms containing an
undifferentiated factor
\mathscr{R}_{IJ}
) yields

(177)

\begin{split}\dots=&-e_{0}(\overline{\mathscr{C}}_{I})+e_{0}e_{I}(\vartheta)/2-\Gamma_{0I}^{J}e_{J}(\vartheta)/2+e_{M}(\mathscr{R}_{MI})\\
&+\Gamma_{MM}^{0}e_{I}(\vartheta)/2+\Gamma_{MI}^{0}e_{M}(\vartheta)/2+\dots,\end{split}

where the dots signify the ignored terms. Note that

\begin{split}e_{0}e_{I}(\vartheta)=&(\nabla_{e_{0}}e_{I}-\nabla_{e_{I}}e_{0})(\vartheta)+e_{I}e_{0}(\vartheta)\\
=&\Gamma_{0I}^{0}e_{0}(\vartheta)+\Gamma_{0I}^{J}e_{J}(\vartheta)-\Gamma_{I0}^{J}e_{J}(\vartheta)+e_{I}e_{0}(\vartheta).\end{split}

Appealing to (
160
) yields

\begin{split}e_{0}e_{I}(\vartheta)/2-\Gamma_{0I}^{J}e_{J}(\vartheta)/2=&-\bar{k}_{IJ}e_{J}(\vartheta)/2-t^{-1}e_{I}(\vartheta)/2+e_{I}(\mathscr{D}_{MM})/2+e_{I}(\mathscr{H})/2+\dots,\end{split}

where the dots signify the ignored terms. Combining this equality with (
171
) and (
177
) yields
(
175
).
∎

Wave equations.
Combining (
160
), (
148
), (
172
),
(
173
) and (
175
) yields a homogeneous system of equations for
\vartheta
,
\mathscr{D}
,
\mathscr{H}
,

\mathscr{E}
and
\overline{\mathscr{C}}
. It is of interest to use this system to prove that if the initial data for
\vartheta
,
\mathscr{D}
,
\mathscr{H}
,
\mathscr{E}
and

\overline{\mathscr{C}}
vanish, then these quantities vanish on the time interval for which the solution is defined. However, it is not immediately
obvious how to do so. In fact, we need to derive higher order equations for some of the quantities of interest.

Lemma 59
.

Given assumptions and notation as in Lemma
40
, assume
\phi
to satisfy (
161
). Assume, moreover,
(
147
) and (
159
) to hold. Then

(178)

e_{0}^{2}(\vartheta)=\overline{\nabla}_{M}\overline{\nabla}_{M}\vartheta+A^{I}_{\vartheta}\overline{\mathscr{C}}_{I}+B_{\vartheta}^{\alpha}e_{\alpha}(\vartheta)+B_{\vartheta}\vartheta

for some functions
A^{I}_{\vartheta}
,
B_{\vartheta}^{\alpha}
and
B_{\vartheta}
which are smooth on the domain of definition of the solution.
Moreover,

(179)

e_{0}^{2}(\partial_{t}\vartheta)=\overline{\nabla}_{M}\overline{\nabla}_{M}\partial_{t}\vartheta+\mathfrak{L}_{\vartheta}[E_{\leq 2}\vartheta,E_{\leq 1}\partial_{t}\vartheta,\overline{\mathscr{C}},\partial_{t}\overline{\mathscr{C}}],

where
E_{\leq l}\vartheta
signifies all functions of the form
E_{\mathbf{I}}\vartheta
for
|\mathbf{I}|\leq l
. Moreover
\mathfrak{L}_{\vartheta}
is linear in
the arguments appearing inside the bracket. Finally, using analogous notation,

(180)

e_{0}^{2}(\overline{\mathscr{C}}_{I})=\overline{\nabla}_{M}\overline{\nabla}_{M}\overline{\mathscr{C}}_{I}+\mathfrak{L}_{\overline{\mathscr{C}}}[E_{\leq 1}\vartheta,E_{\leq 1}\partial_{t}\vartheta,\partial_{t}^{2}\vartheta,\partial_{t}\overline{\mathscr{C}},E_{\leq 1}\overline{\mathscr{C}},E_{\leq 1}\mathscr{D},\mathscr{E}].

Remark 60
.

Note that (
178
), (
179
) and (
180
) are quite similar to

[
25
, (14.75), pp. 4420–4421]
.

Proof.

Note, to begin with, that (
160
) implies
e_{0}(\vartheta)=\mathscr{E}-t^{-1}\vartheta
,
where we used the notation introduced in (
174
). Applying
e_{0}
to this equality and appealing to (
173
) yields
(
178
). Note that this equality can be used to express
e_{0}^{2}(\vartheta)
in terms of spatial derivatives of
\vartheta

of order at most two, time derivatives of
\vartheta
of order at most one and
\overline{\mathscr{C}}_{I}
. Differentiating (
178
) with respect
to time yields (
179
). Finally, applying
e_{0}
to (
175
) and appealing to (
148
),
(
173
) and (
178
) yields (
180
).
∎

Energy
. In order to prove that we have a solution to Einstein’s equations with a CMC foliation, we need to demonstrate that
\vartheta
,

\mathscr{H}
etc. vanish. Here we do so by means of an energy. In order to arrive at the relevant expression for the energy, it is convenient to first
consider the natural energies associated with (
179
) and (
180
). Let, to this end,

\mathcal{E}_{\vartheta}(t):=\textstyle{\int}_{\bar{M}_{t}}\big[|e_{0}\partial_{t}\vartheta|^{2}+|\overline{\nabla}\partial_{t}\vartheta|_{\bar{g}}^{2}\big]d\mu_{\bar{g}},\ \ \ \mathcal{E}_{\mathscr{C}}(t):=\textstyle{\int}_{\bar{M}_{t}}\big[\textstyle{\sum}_{I}|e_{0}\overline{\mathscr{C}}_{I}|^{2}+\textstyle{\sum}_{I,J}|\overline{\nabla}_{I}\overline{\mathscr{C}}_{J}|^{2}\big]d\mu_{\bar{g}}.

We also need an energy for the remaining expressions, involving fewer derivatives. Introduce, to this end,

(181)

\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{rem}}(t):=\textstyle{\int}_{\bar{M}_{t}}\big[|e_{0}\vartheta|^{2}+|\overline{\nabla}\vartheta|_{\bar{g}}^{2}+\textstyle{\sum}_{I}|\overline{\mathscr{C}}_{I}|^{2}+\textstyle{\sum}_{I,J}|\mathscr{D}_{IJ}|^{2}+\vartheta^{2}+\mathscr{H}^{2}+\mathscr{E}^{2}\big]d\mu_{\bar{g}}.

In the end, it will be convenient to combine these energies into

(182)

\mathcal{E}_{\eta}(t):=\mathcal{E}_{\vartheta}(t)+\mathcal{E}_{\mathscr{C}}(t)+\eta^{2}\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{rem}}(t)

for some
0<\eta\in\mathbb{R}
. However, as will become clear in a moment, this is not sufficient.

Lemma 61
.

Given assumptions and notation as in Lemma
40
, assume
\phi
to satisfy (
161
). Assume, moreover,
(
147
) and (
159
) to hold. Then there are non-negative continuous functions
C_{\vartheta}
,
C_{\mathscr{C}}
and

C_{\mathrm{rem}}
on
\mathcal{I}
and smooth functions
f^{ij}
and
h^{LJKi}
on
M
, for
i,j,L,J,K\in\{1,\dots,n\}
such that

(183a)

\displaystyle\big|\partial_{t}\mathcal{E}_{\vartheta}(t)-\textstyle{\int}_{\bar{M}_{t}}f^{ij}E_{i}E_{j}(\vartheta)\partial_{t}^{2}\vartheta d\mu_{\bar{g}}\big|\leq

\displaystyle C_{\vartheta}(t)\mathcal{E}_{1}(t),

(183b)

\displaystyle\big|\partial_{t}\mathcal{E}_{\mathscr{C}}(t)-\textstyle{\int}_{\bar{M}_{t}}h^{LJKi}E_{i}(\mathscr{D}_{LJ})\partial_{t}\overline{\mathscr{C}}_{K}d\mu_{\bar{g}}\big|\leq

\displaystyle C_{\vartheta}(t)\mathcal{E}_{1}(t),

(183c)

\displaystyle|\partial_{t}\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{rem}}(t)|\leq

\displaystyle C_{\mathrm{rem}}(t)\mathcal{E}_{1}(t)

for all
t\in\mathcal{I}
; here
\mathcal{E}_{1}
denotes the energy introduced in (
182
) with
\eta=1
.

Remark 62
.

The functions appearing in the statement of the lemma are allowed to depend on the solution and the coefficients of the equations.

Proof.

When differentiating the energies introduced prior to the statement of the lemma, we ignore terms that can be estimated by
C(t)\mathcal{E}_{1}(t)
for some
continuous function
C
on
\mathcal{I}
; cf. (
183
). Note, in particular, that when
\partial_{t}
hits
d\mu_{\bar{g}}
in any of the energies
introduced above, then the resulting expression can be estimated in this way. Next, note that

\partial_{t}|e_{0}\partial_{t}\vartheta|^{2}=2e_{0}^{2}(\partial_{t}\vartheta)\partial_{t}^{2}\vartheta

and that

\partial_{t}|\overline{\nabla}\partial_{t}\vartheta|_{\bar{g}}^{2}=\partial_{t}[\bar{g}^{ij}E_{i}(\partial_{t}\vartheta)E_{j}(\partial_{t}\vartheta)]=(\partial_{t}\bar{g}^{ij})E_{i}(\partial_{t}\vartheta)E_{j}(\partial_{t}\vartheta)+2\bar{g}^{ij}E_{i}(\partial_{t}^{2}\vartheta)E_{j}(\partial_{t}\vartheta).

After integration, the first term on the right hand side can be estimated by
C\mathcal{E}_{1}
. Moreover, after partial integration, the second term gives
rise to a term in the integrand of the form
-2\partial_{t}^{2}\vartheta\Delta_{\bar{g}}(\partial_{t}\vartheta)
. To summarise,

\partial_{t}\mathcal{E}_{\vartheta}(t)=2\textstyle{\int}_{\bar{M}_{t}}\big[e_{0}^{2}(\partial_{t}\vartheta)-\Delta_{\bar{g}}(\partial_{t}\vartheta)\big]\partial_{t}^{2}\vartheta d\mu_{\bar{g}}+\dots,

where the dots signify terms that can be estimated by
C\mathcal{E}_{1}
. Combining this observation with (
179
) and the definition of the
energy, we conclude that there are functions
f^{ij}
such that (
183a
) holds. By a similar argument,

\begin{split}\partial_{t}\mathcal{E}_{\mathscr{C}}(t)=&2\textstyle{\sum}_{I}\textstyle{\int}_{\bar{M}_{t}}\big[e_{0}^{2}(\overline{\mathscr{C}}_{I})-\overline{\nabla}_{J}\overline{\nabla}_{J}\overline{\mathscr{C}}_{I}\big]\partial_{t}\overline{\mathscr{C}}_{I}d\mu_{\bar{g}}+\dots,\end{split}

where the dots signify terms that can be estimated by
C\mathcal{E}_{1}
. Combining this observation with (
180
) yields smooth
functions
h^{IJKi}
such that (
183b
) holds.

Next, due to (
178
) and the definition of the energy,

\partial_{t}\textstyle{\int}_{\bar{M}_{t}}\big[|e_{0}\vartheta|^{2}+|\overline{\nabla}\vartheta|_{\bar{g}}^{2}\big]d\mu_{\bar{g}}=\dots.

Since
\partial_{t}\overline{\mathscr{C}}_{I}
is included in the energy, it is immediate that

\partial_{t}\textstyle{\int}_{\bar{M}_{t}}\textstyle{\sum}_{I}|\overline{\mathscr{C}}_{I}|^{2}d\mu_{\bar{g}}=\dots.

Next, consider

\partial_{t}\textstyle{\sum}_{I,J}\textstyle{\int}_{\bar{M}_{t}}|\mathscr{D}_{IJ}|^{2}d\mu_{\bar{g}}=\partial_{t}\textstyle{\int}_{\bar{M}_{t}}\bar{g}^{ij}\bar{g}^{kl}\mathscr{D}_{ik}\mathscr{D}_{jl}d\mu_{\bar{g}}=\dots,

where we appealed to (
148
) and the definition of the energy. Since
\partial_{t}\vartheta
is included in the energy,

\partial_{t}\textstyle{\int}_{\bar{M}_{t}}\vartheta^{2}d\mu_{\bar{g}}=\dots.

Finally, the arguments concerning the contributions
from the time derivatives of
\mathscr{H}
and
\mathscr{E}
are similar, due to (
172
), (
173
), the definition of the energy and
the fact that
\overline{\nabla}_{M}\overline{\nabla}_{M}\vartheta=\Delta_{\bar{g}}\vartheta
can be expressed in terms of
\partial_{t}^{2}\vartheta
by appealing to (
178
).
∎

Next, we need to estimate the contributions from the terms arising from
f^{ij}
and
h^{LJKi}
. Introduce, to this end,

(184)

\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{\vartheta}(t):=\mathcal{E}_{\vartheta}(t)+\textstyle{\int}_{\bar{M}_{t}}f^{ij}E_{j}(\vartheta)E_{i}\partial_{t}\vartheta d\mu_{\bar{g}},\ \ \ \hat{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathscr{C}}(t):=\mathcal{E}_{\mathscr{C}}(t)+\textstyle{\int}_{\bar{M}_{t}}h^{LJKi}\mathscr{D}_{LJ}E_{i}\overline{\mathscr{C}}_{K}d\mu_{\bar{g}}.

Lemma 63
.

Given assumptions and notation as in Lemma
61
, there are continuous functions
\hat{C}_{\vartheta}
and
\hat{C}_{\mathscr{C}}
on
\mathcal{I}
such
that if
t_{0},t_{1}\in\mathcal{I}
, then

(185)

|\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{\vartheta}(t_{1})-\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{\vartheta}(t_{0})|\leq\big|\textstyle{\int}_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\hat{C}_{\vartheta}(t)\mathcal{E}_{1}(t)dt\big|,\ \ \ |\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathscr{C}}(t_{1})-\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathscr{C}}(t_{0})|\leq\big|\textstyle{\int}_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\hat{C}_{\mathscr{C}}(t)\mathcal{E}_{1}(t)dt\big|.

Proof.

Integration by parts yields

(186)

\textstyle{\int}_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\textstyle{\int}_{\bar{M}_{t}}f^{ij}E_{i}E_{j}(\vartheta)\partial_{t}^{2}\vartheta d\mu_{\bar{g}}dt=-\textstyle{\int}_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\textstyle{\int}_{\bar{M}_{t}}f^{ij}E_{j}(\vartheta)E_{i}\partial_{t}^{2}\vartheta d\mu_{\bar{g}}dt+\dots,

where the dots signify terms that can be estimated by

\big|\textstyle{\int}_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}C(t)\mathcal{E}_{1}(t)dt\big|.

Integrating by parts in the first term on the right hand side of (
186
) yields

-\textstyle{\int}_{\bar{M}_{t_{1}}}f^{ij}E_{j}(\vartheta)E_{i}\partial_{t}\vartheta d\mu_{\bar{g}}+\textstyle{\int}_{\bar{M}_{t_{0}}}f^{ij}E_{j}(\vartheta)E_{i}\partial_{t}\vartheta d\mu_{\bar{g}}+\dots.

Combining these observations with (
183a
) and the definition of
\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{\vartheta}
yields the first estimate in (
185
).
Similarly, integration by parts yields

(187)

\textstyle{\int}_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\textstyle{\int}_{\bar{M}_{t}}h^{IJKi}E_{i}(\mathscr{D}_{IJ})\partial_{t}\overline{\mathscr{C}}_{K}d\mu_{\bar{g}}dt=-\textstyle{\int}_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\textstyle{\int}_{\bar{M}_{t}}h^{IJKi}\mathscr{D}_{IJ}\partial_{t}E_{i}\overline{\mathscr{C}}_{K}d\mu_{\bar{g}}dt+\dots.

Integrating by parts with respect to
t
in the first term on the right hand side of (
187
) yields

-\textstyle{\int}_{\bar{M}_{t_{1}}}h^{IJKi}\mathscr{D}_{IJ}E_{i}\overline{\mathscr{C}}_{K}d\mu_{\bar{g}}+\textstyle{\int}_{\bar{M}_{t_{0}}}h^{IJKi}\mathscr{D}_{IJ}E_{i}\overline{\mathscr{C}}_{K}d\mu_{\bar{g}}+\dots,

where we appealed to (
148
). Combining these observations with (
183b
) and the definition of
\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathscr{C}}
yields the
second estimate in (
185
).
∎

The estimates (
185
) are of the desired form. However, the energies do not control the quantities we wish to prove vanish.
On the other hand, this can easily be remedied.

Lemma 64
.

Given assumptions and notation as in Lemma
61
, let
K
be a compact subinterval of
\mathcal{I}
. If
1\leq\eta\in\mathbb{R}
is large
enough and

\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{\eta}(t):=\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{\vartheta}(t)+\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathscr{C}}(t)+\eta^{2}\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{rem}}(t),

cf. (
181
) and (
184
), then, using the notation introduced in (
182
),

(188)

\mathcal{E}_{\eta}(t)/2\leq\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{\eta}(t)\leq 2\mathcal{E}_{\eta}(t)

for all
t\in K
. Moreover, there is a constant
C_{K}
such that if
t_{0},t_{1}\in K
, then

(189)

|\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{\eta}(t_{1})-\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{\eta}(t_{0})|\leq C_{K}\big|\textstyle{\int}_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{\eta}(t)dt\big|.

Remark 65
.

Assume that
\vartheta|_{t_{0}}=0
,
\mathscr{H}|_{t_{0}}=0
,
\overline{\mathscr{C}}_{I}|_{t_{0}}=0
and
\mathscr{D}_{IJ}|_{t_{0}}=0
for some
t_{0}\in\mathcal{I}
. Then
\mathscr{E}|_{t_{0}}=0

due to (
174
). Moreover,
\partial_{t}\vartheta|_{t_{0}}=0
due to (
160
), so that
\partial_{t}^{2}\vartheta|_{t_{0}}=0
due to
(
178
). Finally, due to (
175
),
\partial_{t}\overline{\mathscr{C}}_{I}|_{t_{0}}=0
. Due to these observations,
\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{\eta}(t_{0})=0
, so that

\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{\eta}(t)=0
for all
t
in the existence interval of the solution; this is an immediate consequence of (
189
) and Grönwall’s
lemma. Combining this fact with (
170
) and (
171
) yields the conclusion
that
\mathscr{R}=0
, so that
G=T
; cf. the definition of
\mathscr{R}
, (
169
). Thus Einstein’s equations are fulfilled. Next, since
\vartheta=0
and
(
161
) holds, we know that
\Box_{g}\phi-V^{\prime}\circ\phi=0
; i.e., the non-linear scalar field equations are satisfied. Finally, since

\vartheta=0
, the hypersurface
\bar{M}_{t}
has constant mean curvature
1/t
.

Proof.

Due to the definition of
\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{\vartheta}
and
\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathscr{C}}
and the compactness of
K
, the estimate (
188
) follows immediately by
choosing
\eta
to be large enough. Given this estimate, (
189
) follows from (
183c
), (
185
) and the fact
that
\eta\geq 1
.
∎

4.
Local existence and Cauchy stability

The overall logic behind proving local existence of solutions to (
13
) is to prove local existence of solutions to the following
system:

(190a)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}e_{I}^{i}=

\displaystyle f_{I}^{J}e_{J}^{i},

(190b)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\omega_{i}^{I}=

\displaystyle-f_{J}^{I}\omega_{i}^{J},

(190c)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}^{2}\bar{k}_{IJ}=

\displaystyle N^{2}e^{l}_{K}e^{m}_{K}E_{l}E_{m}\bar{k}_{IJ}+\Omega_{IJ}+\mathfrak{K}_{IJ},

(190d)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}^{2}\phi=

\displaystyle N^{2}e^{l}_{K}e^{m}_{K}E_{l}E_{m}\phi+\Phi,

(190e)

\displaystyle\Delta_{\bar{g}}(N-1)=

\displaystyle\zeta(N-1)+\mathfrak{N},

where
f_{I}^{J}
is given by (
22
);
\Omega_{IJ}
and
\mathfrak{K}_{IJ}
are the expressions obtained in Lemma
50
; and

\Phi
is the expression obtained in Lemma
55
. Moreover,

(191)

\zeta:=\bar{k}_{IJ}\bar{k}_{IJ}+(N^{-1}\partial_{t}\phi)^{2}-\tfrac{2}{n-1}V\circ\phi,\ \ \ \mathfrak{N}:=\bar{k}_{IJ}\bar{k}_{IJ}+(N^{-1}\partial_{t}\phi)^{2}-\tfrac{2}{n-1}V\circ\phi-t^{-2}.

At this point we think of (
190
) as being devoid of geometric content (with the one exception that we, for convenience,
use
\Delta_{\bar{g}}
to denote the Laplace operator associated with the metric
\bar{g}
which is defined by the requirement that
\{e_{I}\}
is
an orthonormal frame, where
e_{I}:=e_{I}^{i}E_{i}
). In particular,
\bar{k}_{IJ}
need not be symmetric etc.

Initial data for the first order system.

Assume now that we have CMC initial data for (
13
). This means
e_{I}^{i}|_{t_{0}}
,
\omega^{I}_{i}|_{t_{0}}
,
\bar{k}_{IJ}|_{t_{0}}
,

N|_{t_{0}}>0
,
\phi|_{t_{0}}
and
(U\phi)|_{t_{0}}
are given, where
(U\phi)|_{t_{0}}=(e_{0}\phi)|_{t_{0}}
denotes the future directed unit normal
derivative of the scalar field at the initial hypersurface. Moreover, for
t=t_{0}
, these data satisfy
\bar{k}_{II}|_{t_{0}}=1/t_{0}
,

\bar{k}_{IJ}|_{t_{0}}=\bar{k}_{JI}|_{t_{0}}
, (
13d
), (
13f
) and (
13g
), where

(192)

e_{I}|_{t_{0}}:=e^{i}_{I}|_{t_{0}}E_{i};\ \omega^{I}|_{t_{0}}:=\omega^{I}_{i}|_{t_{0}}\eta^{i};\ \bar{g}|_{t_{0}}:=\textstyle{\sum}_{I}\omega^{I}|_{t_{0}}\otimes\omega^{I}|_{t_{0}};\ \bar{k}|_{t_{0}}:=\bar{k}_{IJ}|_{t_{0}}\omega^{I}|_{t_{0}}\otimes\omega^{J}|_{t_{0}}.

Moreover,
\theta|_{t_{0}}=\bar{k}_{II}|_{t_{0}}
and
\overline{\nabla}
and
\bar{S}
on
\bar{M}_{t_{0}}
are the Levi-Civita connection and the scalar curvature associated
with
\bar{g}|_{t_{0}}
. In (
192
),

\{\eta^{i}\}
is the dual basis of the fixed frame
\{E_{i}\}
. In addition, we require
\zeta
appearing in (
190e
) to satisfy

\zeta|_{t_{0}}>0
; note that (
13d
) is the same as (
190e
). This means that
N|_{t_{0}}
is uniquely determined by
(
190e
) and the initial data for the other unknowns, so that it does not have to be included in the initial data. Next, note
that
(\mathfrak{N}/\zeta)|_{t_{0}}=1-1/(t_{0}^{2}\zeta|_{t_{0}})
, so that

(193)

N|_{t_{0}}\geq 1-(\mathfrak{N}/\zeta)|_{t_{0}}=1/(t_{0}^{2}\zeta|_{t_{0}})>0

by the maximum principle. Note also that it is understood that

\omega^{I}_{i}|_{t_{0}}e_{J}^{i}|_{t_{0}}=\delta^{I}_{J}
, so that the initial data for
\omega^{I}_{i}
are determined by the initial data for
e^{i}_{I}
. To
summarise, CMC initial data for (
13
) are given by
e_{I}^{i}|_{t_{0}}
,
\bar{k}_{IJ}|_{t_{0}}
,
\phi|_{t_{0}}
and
(U\phi)|_{t_{0}}
satisfying

\bar{k}_{II}|_{t_{0}}=1/t_{0}
,
\bar{k}_{IJ}|_{t_{0}}=\bar{k}_{JI}|_{t_{0}}
, (
13f
), (
13g
) and the condition that
\zeta|_{t_{0}}>0
. Given these
initial data, we wish to solve (
13
) locally.

From initial data for the first order system to initial data for the second order system.

Given initial data for (
13
), we define initial data for (
190
) by the data for (
13
) complemented by

\partial_{t}\bar{k}_{KJ}|_{t_{0}}
, given by the right hand side of (
13c
), restricted to
\bar{M}_{t_{0}}
. Thus
\partial_{t}\bar{k}_{KJ}|_{t_{0}}=\partial_{t}\bar{k}_{JK}|_{t_{0}}
.
Due to this choice of initial data,

\vartheta|_{t_{0}}=0
,
\mathscr{H}|_{t_{0}}=0
,
\mathscr{C}|_{t_{0}}=0
and
\mathscr{D}_{KJ}|_{t_{0}}=0
(the latter equality follows from the fact that (
13c
) is
satisfied for
t=t_{0}
). Given these initial data for (
190
), we solve (
190
) (for which we, for the moment, assume
that there is a good local existence theory). In solving (
190
) and in the arguments below, it is understood that

\omega^{K}:=\omega^{K}_{i}\eta^{i},\ \ \ \bar{g}:=\textstyle{\sum}_{K}\omega^{K}\otimes\omega^{K},\ \ \ \bar{k}:=\bar{k}_{KJ}\omega^{K}\otimes\omega^{J},\ \ \ g:=-N^{2}dt\otimes dt+\bar{g}.

Assume the existence interval for the solution to be
\mathcal{I}\subset(0,\infty)
, yielding a solution on
M=\bar{M}\times\mathcal{I}
. Assume, moreover, that

\zeta>0
on
M
, where
\zeta
is introduced in (
191
). Then, by the argument given in connection with (
193
),
N>0

on
M
. Then
(M,g)
is a Lorentz manifold and
g(\partial_{t},\partial_{t})=-N^{2}
. Next, by our choices,
\bar{k}_{IJ}|_{t_{0}}
and
\partial_{t}\bar{k}_{IJ}|_{t_{0}}
are
symmetric. Combining this observation with (
190c
) and Remark
52
, it is clear that
\bar{k}_{[IJ]}
satisfies a
homogeneous wave equation with vanishing initial data, so that
\bar{k}_{IJ}
is symmetric. Since
\{e_{K}\}
, where
e_{K}:=e_{K}^{i}E_{i}
, is an orthonormal
frame, Lemma
35
applies and implies that
f_{K}^{J}+f_{J}^{K}=-2N\bar{\kappa}_{KJ}
, where
\bar{\kappa}_{KJ}
are the components of the second
fundamental form with respect to the frame
\{e_{I}\}
. On the other hand, due to (
22
) and the symmetry of
\bar{k}_{IJ}
, we also know that

f_{K}^{J}+f_{J}^{K}=-2N\bar{k}_{KJ}
. Thus
\bar{k}_{IJ}
are the components of the second fundamental form with respect to the frame
\{e_{I}\}
.

Remark 66
.

Due to the above observations, in our setting, the conditions of Lemma
40
are satisfied. Next, note that (
147
)
is equivalent to
(
190c
); see Lemma
50
. Moreover, (
159
) is an immediate consequence of (
190e
).
Finally, (
161
) is equivalent to (
190d
); see Lemma
55
. To conclude, the conditions of
Lemma
61
are fulfilled. Due to Remark
65
, we conclude that
\mathscr{H}=0
,
\mathscr{C}=0
,
\mathscr{D}=0
and
\vartheta=0

on
M
. Since
\mathscr{H}=0
, we conclude that (
13g
) holds on
M
; since
\mathscr{C}=0
, we conclude that (
13f
) holds on all of

M
; and since
\mathscr{D}=0
, we conclude that (
13c
) holds on all of
M
. Combining these observations with the fact that
(
190
) holds yields the conclusion that (
13
) holds on
M
. Finally, since
\vartheta=0
, we conclude that

\mathrm{tr}_{\bar{g}}\bar{k}=1/t
on the interval of existence. This demonstrates local existence of solutions to (
13
). However, it is also of
interest to note that the solutions to (
13
) solve the Einstein-non-linear scalar field system; see Remark
65
.
What remains is to demonstrate local existence of solutions to
(
190
). The idea is to do so by appealing to Corollary
4
. However, the system (
190
)
does not immediately fall into the framework considered in this corollary. The purpose of the next subsection is to reformulate
(
190
) in such a way that it does.

4.1.
Adapting the equations to the general framework

Before rewriting (
190
) so that it fits into the general framework, we need to derive an equation for the time derivative of the
lapse function. Note, to this end, that the lapse equation (
13d
) reads

(194)

e_{K}e_{K}(N)-t^{-2}(N-1)={\bar{\gamma}}^{J}_{KJ}e_{K}(N)+N\big(\bar{k}_{IJ}\bar{k}_{IJ}-t^{-2}+[e_{0}(\phi)]^{2}-\tfrac{2}{n-1}V\circ\phi\big).

Lemma 67
.

Given that (
13d
) holds,
\dot{N}:=\partial_{t}N
satisfies

(195)

\begin{split}e_{K}e_{K}(\dot{N})-t^{-2}\dot{N}=&{\bar{\gamma}}^{J}_{KJ}e_{K}(\dot{N})+\dot{N}\big(\bar{k}_{IJ}\bar{k}_{IJ}-t^{-2}+[e_{0}(\phi)]^{2}-\tfrac{2}{n-1}V\circ\phi\big)+\mathfrak{R}_{N},\end{split}

where
\mathfrak{R}_{N}
is a polynomial in
N
,
E_{i}N
,
E_{i}E_{j}N
,
e_{K}^{i}
,
\omega^{K}_{i}
,
E_{i}e_{K}^{j}
,
f_{K}^{J}
,
E_{i}f_{K}^{J}
,

\bar{k}_{JK}
,
\partial_{t}\bar{k}_{JK}
,
\partial_{t}\phi
,
e_{0}\phi
,
\partial_{t}e_{0}\phi
, and
V^{\prime}\circ\phi
(which is of first order in the last expression).
The function
\mathfrak{R}_{N}
is also allowed to depend on the spacetime coordinates and this dependence is smooth, assuming
\mathcal{I}\subset(0,\infty)
.

Remark 68
.

The equation (
195
) can also be written

(196)

\Delta_{\bar{g}}\dot{N}=\zeta\dot{N}+\mathfrak{R}_{N}.

Proof.

Note, to begin with, that
[\partial_{t},e_{K}]=f_{K}^{J}e_{J}
, so that

\partial_{t}e_{K}e_{K}(N)=e_{K}e_{K}(\dot{N})+f_{K}^{J}e_{J}e_{K}(N)+e_{K}(f_{K}^{J}e_{J}N).

Moreover,

\partial_{t}[{\bar{\gamma}}^{J}_{KJ}e_{K}(N)]=(\partial_{t}{\bar{\gamma}}^{J}_{KJ})e_{K}(N)+{\bar{\gamma}}^{J}_{KJ}f_{K}^{L}e_{L}(N)+{\bar{\gamma}}^{J}_{KJ}e_{K}(\dot{N}),

where we tacitly expand
\partial_{t}{\bar{\gamma}}^{J}_{KJ}
by appealing to (
134
). Adding up these observations yields the desired conclusion.
∎

4.2.
Local existence

Next, we wish to prove local existence of solutions to (
13
) by proving that (
190
) can be rewritten so that
Corollary
4
applies. In order to do this, we need to explain what the variables
u
,
v
,
N_{1}
and
N_{2}
are in the
present setting. In our setting,
u
collects all the functions
e_{I}^{i}
and
\omega^{I}_{i}
;
v
collects all the functions
\bar{k}_{IJ}
,

\phi
,
e_{0}\phi
and
E_{i}\phi
;
N_{1}=N-1
; and
N_{2}=\partial_{t}N
. Clearly, an equation of the form (
3a
) holds, assuming we make
appropriate assumptions concerning
a_{I}^{J}
; cf. Remark
36
. As in the statement of Theorem
10
,

f_{I}^{J}=-N\bar{k}_{IJ}+a_{I}^{J}
. The contribution of the first term to
f_{1}
only depends on
v
and
N_{1}
. Assuming
a_{K}^{J}
to only depend
on
e_{I}^{i}
,
\omega^{I}_{i}
,
\bar{k}_{IJ}
,
\phi
,
e_{0}\phi
,
E_{i}\phi
,
N
and
E_{i}N
, then
f_{1}
has the right dependence. However, it does
not a priori have the desired boundedness properties. On the other
hand, that can be arranged at a later stage; given initial data, we choose
f_{1}
so that it coincides with the function arising from the equations
in a neighbourhood of the initial data, but is such that it is constant outside a compact set. We describe the detailed modifications in the proof
of local existence. Turning to (
3b
), note that
\phi
satisfies (
163
). Since
\Phi
on the right hand side of
(
163
) can be included in
f_{2}
, we can interpret (
163
) as a subsystem of (
3b
). Next, consider
(
164
). To begin with,
\Phi_{k}
can be included in
f_{2}
. Moreover,
\Xi_{k}
can be included in the second term on the right hand
side of (
3b
). By similar arguments, it can be verified that
\Psi
appearing in (
165
) can be included in
f_{2}
, so that
(
165
) can be considered to be a subsystem of (
3b
). Next, let us turn to (
154
). By arguments similar to
the above,
\mathfrak{K}_{IJ}
can be included in
f_{2}
and
\Omega_{IJ}
can be included in the second term on the right hand side of (
3b
).
Finally, note that the first terms on the right hand sides of (
163
), (
164
), (
165
) and (
154
)
are such that they are included in the first term on the right hand side of (
3b
). Finally, (
190e
) is of the
form (
3c
) and (
196
) is of the form (
3d
). In fact,
\zeta
and
f_{3}
only depend on
v
.
With this information at hand, we are in a position to prove Theorem
10
.

Proof of Theorem
10
.

As mentioned at the beginning of the present subsection, we intend to prove the statement by appealing to Corollary
4
.
Moreover, the relation between the variables in (
13
) and the variables in (
3
) is clarified at the beginning
of the present subsection. However, we also need to specify initial data for
u
and
v
. Note, to this end, that given initial data as in
the statement of the theorem,
N|_{t_{0}}
is uniquely determined by (
13d
). The initial data for
u
are already given in the
statement of the theorem. Moreover,
\bar{k}_{IJ}|_{t_{0}}
,
\phi|_{t_{0}}
,
(\partial_{t}\phi)|_{t_{0}}:=N|_{t_{0}}(e_{0}\phi)|_{t_{0}}
,
(E_{i}\phi)|_{t_{0}}
,

(\partial_{t}E_{i}\phi)|_{t_{0}}
and
(e_{0}\phi)|_{t_{0}}
are all given. In addition,
(\partial_{t}\bar{k}_{IJ})|_{t_{0}}
is given by (
13c
). The only
initial datum left to be specified is
(\partial_{t}e_{0}\phi)|_{t_{0}}
. However, this quantity is obtained by restricting the right hand side of
(
166
) to
t=t_{0}
and then multiplying the result with
N|_{t_{0}}
. This means that
u|_{t_{0}}
,
v|_{t_{0}}
and
(\partial_{t}v)|_{t_{0}}

have all been specified. Moreover, as described at the beginning of the present subsection, the equations we wish to solve take the form
(
3
), and the functions
f_{1}
etc. have the dependence described in connection with (
3
); we refer to this
as the
original system
. However, the bounds such as (
1
) etc. also have to be satisfied in order for us to be allowed
to appeal to Corollary
4
. In order to ensure (
1
), let
\bar{h}_{1}^{ij}=N^{2}e_{K}^{i}e_{K}^{j}
and

\bar{h}_{2}^{ij}=e_{K}^{i}e_{K}^{j}
in a
neighbourhood of the initial values
N|_{t_{0}}
and
e_{K}^{i}|_{t_{0}}
, but such that
\bar{h}_{l}^{ij}
are constant outside a compact set. This means
that there is a
\lambda>0
such that (
1
) is fulfilled. Moreover, the
\bar{h}_{l}^{ij}
are smooth functions, all of whose derivatives
are bounded. We can modify the remaining ingredients of (
3
) similarly. We refer to the resulting system of equations as the

modified system
. Applying Corollary
4
to the modified system yields a local solution to (
3
). By
continuity, this yields a local solution to the original system in an open interval containing the initial time
t_{0}
.

Next, let us prove uniqueness of solutions to the original system. Assume, to this end, that we have two solutions, say
\sigma_{1}
and

\sigma_{2}
, to the original system on two open intervals, say
\mathcal{I}_{1}
and
\mathcal{I}_{2}
, containing
t_{0}
. Assume, moreover, that the associated

\mathcal{C}_{i}
, cf. (
20
), satisfy
\mathcal{C}_{i}(t)\in\mathbb{R}
for
t\in\mathcal{I}_{i}
. Let
K\subset\mathcal{I}_{1}\cap\mathcal{I}_{2}
be a compact interval
containing
t_{0}
in its interior. Then we can modify the original system in such a way that the resulting modified system satisfies the conditions
of Corollary
4
and coincides with the original system in a neighbourhood of the range of the solutions on
\bar{M}\times K
.
Then Corollary
4
applies and yields the conclusion that the solutions coincide on
\bar{M}\times K
. Since
K
was arbitrary,
we conclude that the solutions coincide on their common domain of definition. This makes it possible to define the maximal interval of existence
as the union of all the open intervals
\mathcal{I}
such that there is a solution on
\bar{M}\times\mathcal{I}
with the property that
\mathcal{C}(t)\in\mathbb{R}
for
t\in\mathcal{I}
.

Let
\mathcal{I}
be the maximal interval of existence for solutions to the original system. Next, we wish to prove the continuation criterion. Assume,
to this end, that
\mathcal{I}=(t_{-},t_{+})
, that
t_{-}>0
and that
\mathcal{C}(t)
is bounded on
(t_{-},t_{0}]
. This means that
\|u(\cdot,t)\|_{C^{2}}
,

\|v(\cdot,t)\|_{C^{2}}
and
\|\partial_{t}v(\cdot,t)\|_{C^{1}}
are uniformly bounded on
(t_{-},t_{0}]
. Moreover,
\zeta
is uniformly bounded away from zero
on
\bar{M}\times(t_{-},t_{0}]
. Combining this observation with the maximum principle, cf. (
193
), yields the conclusion that
N_{1}

is uniformly bounded away from zero. Next, note that the original
\zeta
and
f_{3}
are bounded in
C^{1}
and the original
h_{2}
is bounded in

C^{2}
. Moreover, the bounds are uniform for
t\in(t_{-},t_{0}]
. This means that
N_{1}
is bounded in
C^{2,1}
, uniformly for
t\in(t_{-},t_{0}]
;
see Theorem
83
. As a consequence, the original
f_{4}
is bounded in
C^{0,1}
, uniformly for
t\in(t_{-},t_{0}]
. Applying
Theorem
83
to the equation for
N_{2}
then yields the conclusion that
N_{2}
is bounded in
C^{2,1}
, uniformly for

t\in(t_{-},t_{0}]
. For these
reasons, it is possible to construct a modified system satisfying the conditions of Corollary
4
and such that it coincides
with the original system on an open neighbourhood of the closure of the range of the solution on
\bar{M}\times(t_{-},t_{0}]
. However, then
Corollary
4
ensures that it is possible to extend the solution to the modified system beyond
t_{-}
. Moreover, by continuity,
the solution to the modified system is a solution to the original system in a neighbourhood of
t_{-}
. This contradicts the definition of
t_{-}

and proves the continuation criterion to the past. The argument to the future is similar.

Our next goal is to prove that the solution to the original system is a solution to (
13
). The first step is to prove that
we have a solution to (
190
). In order to do so, we need to demonstrate that if
v_{\phi}
,
v_{\phi,i}
and
v_{\phi,0}
are the
components of
v
corresponding to
\phi
,
E_{i}\phi
and
e_{0}\phi
respectively, then
E_{i}v_{\phi}=v_{\phi,i}
and
e_{0}v_{\phi}=v_{\phi,0}
. We also need
to demonstrate that
\partial_{t}N_{1}=N_{2}
. In order to prove these statements, let us first eliminate the ambiguity inherent in deriving the original
system; in the derivation, we can think of
E_{i}\phi
as
E_{i}v_{\phi}
or as
v_{\phi,i}
etc. In the equation for
u
, there is no ambiguity, since

f_{1}
is only allowed to depend on
u
,
v
,
N_{1}
and
E_{i}N_{1}
. In other words, in the case of
f_{1}
, occurrences of
E_{i}\phi
and
e_{0}\phi
have
to be interpreted as
v_{\phi,i}
and
v_{\phi,0}
respectively. In fact, whenever
f_{I}^{J}
appears,
E_{i}\phi
and
e_{0}\phi
should be interpreted as

v_{\phi,i}
and
v_{\phi,0}
respectively (this also applies to the subsystem for
v_{\phi,0}
and the equation for
N_{2}
). Next, consider the equation
for
v_{\phi}
, considered as a subsystem of the equation for
v
. It arises from (
167
). Here,
N
and
\partial_{t}N
are replaced by
N_{1}+1
and

N_{2}
respectively; and
e^{i}_{I}
and
\omega^{I}_{i}
are replaced by components of
u
. However, every occurrence of
\phi
is replaced by
v_{\phi}
. When
deriving the subsystem for the
v_{\phi,i}
(corresponding to system of wave equations for
E_{i}\phi
obtained by applying
E_{k}
to (
167
),
see (
164
)), we interpret
E_{i}\partial_{t}\phi
and
\partial_{t}E_{i}\phi
as
\partial_{t}v_{\phi,i}
; and
E_{j}E_{i}\phi
as
E_{j}v_{\phi,i}
. However, occurrences of
single derivatives, such as
E_{i}\phi
and
\partial_{t}\phi
should be replaced by
E_{i}v_{\phi}
and
\partial_{t}v_{\phi}
respectively. In particular, in the subsystem
of equations for
v_{\phi,i}
, only
u
,
N_{1}
,
N_{2}
,
v_{\phi}
and
v_{\phi,i}
occur. Moreover, it follows that
E_{i}v_{\phi}
satisfies the same system of
wave equations as
v_{\phi,i}
with the same initial data. This means that
E_{i}v_{\phi}=v_{\phi,i}
. Next, consider the equation for
N_{1}
. It arises from
(
194
). When rewriting this equation in terms of the
u
,
v
and
N_{1}
variables, there is no ambiguity, since
\zeta
and
f_{3}
are only
allowed to depend on
u
,
v
and
E_{i}u
. In particular, we interpret
e_{0}\phi
as
v_{\phi,0}
. When
differentiating (
194
) with respect to
time, time derivatives of
e_{I}^{i}
occur. This leads to expressions of the form
f_{I}^{J}e_{J}^{i}
, and we interpret
f_{I}^{J}
as described above.
On the other hand, when differentiating (
194
),
\partial_{t}\phi
is interpreted as
\partial_{t}v_{\phi}
. Due to these choices,
\partial_{t}N_{1}
and

N_{2}
satisfy the same equation. This means that
N_{2}=\partial_{t}N_{1}
, and we define
N:=N_{1}+1
. Finally, we wish to prove that
e_{0}v_{\phi}=v_{\phi,0}
.
The equation for
v_{\phi,0}
is derived using (
166
) as a starting point. Moreover, (
166
) can be written (
167
). However,
once we have rewritten (
167
) as an equation for
v_{\phi}
(in terms of
u
,
v
,
N_{1}
and
N_{2}
), it is no longer obvious what the
relation between (
166
) and (
167
) is. However, now that we know that
\partial_{t}N=\partial_{t}N_{1}=N_{2}
, the equation for
v_{\phi}
implies that
(
166
) holds with
\phi
replaced by
v_{\phi}
, and where it is understood that
e_{0}=N^{-1}\partial_{t}
. When deriving the subsystem corresponding to

v_{\phi,0}
, we interpret each occurrence of
f_{I}^{J}
as before. Note that this
observation also applies when derivatives of
f_{I}^{J}
appear. Moreover, second spatial derivatives of
\phi
that appear explicitly (as opposed to
implicitly due to derivatives of
f_{I}^{J}
) are interpreted as first derivatives of
v_{\phi,i}
. Moreover, whenever mixed second order space and time
derivatives (effectively
\partial_{t}E_{i}\phi
) appear explicitly, we interpret them as
\partial_{t}v_{\phi,i}
.
In particular, if
e_{0}e_{I}\phi
or
e_{I}e_{0}\phi
occur, we expand the corresponding expression, and then apply the above convention. If single
derivatives of
\phi
occur explicitly, we interpret them as single derivatives applied to
v_{\phi}
. Only in the expressions corresponding to the
left hand side and first term on the right hand side of (
3b
) and in remaining terms involving second time derivatives (such as

e_{0}^{2}\phi
) do we introduce
v_{\phi,0}
; in particular, we write
-t^{-1}e_{0}^{2}\phi
as
-t^{-1}N_{1}^{-1}\partial_{t}v_{\phi,0}
. Due to these choices;
the fact that
\partial_{t}N=\partial_{t}N_{1}=N_{2}
; and the fact that
E_{i}v_{\phi}=v_{\phi,i}
, it follows that
e_{0}v_{\phi}
and
v_{\phi,0}
satisfy the same equation
with the same initial data. This means that
v_{\phi,0}=e_{0}v_{\phi}
.

To conclude, extracting
e_{I}^{i}
,
\omega_{i}^{I}
,
\bar{k}_{IJ}
,
\phi
and
N
from the solution to the original system yields
a solution to (
190
). Due to Remark
66
and the observations made at the beginning of the present section,
we conclude that the solution to the original system yields a solution to (
13
) on the maximal existence interval. Moreover, this
solution is a solution to the Einstein-non-linear scalar field equations with leaves of constant mean curvature. Finally,
\bar{k}_{IJ}
are the
components of the second fundamental form of the hypersurfaces
\bar{M}_{t}
with respect to the orthonormal frame
\{e_{I}\}
.

What remains to be demonstrated is uniqueness of solutions to (
13
). In order to do so, we prove that solutions to
(
13
) are also solutions to (
190
), which yields solutions to what we refer to as the original system in the
present proof. Considering (
130
) and (
145
), it is clear that (
13c
) is the same as

\partial_{t}\bar{k}_{IJ}=\dot{\varkappa}_{IJ}
. Combining this observation with (
146
) and Remark
46
yields

\partial_{t}^{2}\bar{k}_{IJ}=\partial_{t}\dot{\varkappa}_{IJ}=Ne_{M}e_{M}(N\bar{k}_{IJ})-(\partial_{t}N)\bar{R}_{IJ}+\hat{\Omega}_{(IJ)}+\hat{\mathfrak{K}}_{(IJ)},

where we used the fact that
\mathscr{C}=0
, see (
13f
), and the fact that
\vartheta=0
. This equation implies that

e_{0}^{2}(\bar{k}_{IJ})=N^{-1}e_{M}e_{M}(N\bar{k}_{IJ})-N^{-2}(\partial_{t}N)\bar{R}_{IJ}+N^{-2}\hat{\Omega}_{(IJ)}+N^{-2}\hat{\mathfrak{K}}_{(IJ)}-e_{0}(\ln N)N^{-1}\partial_{t}\bar{k}_{IJ}.

Since
\partial_{t}\bar{k}_{IJ}=\dot{\varkappa}_{IJ}
and
\mathscr{D}_{IJ}=0
in the current setting, this implies that (
147
) holds. This
means that (
190c
) holds; see Lemma
50
. This means that all the equations in (
190
)
are satisfied. Appealing to Lemmas
55
and
67
and proceeding as above demonstrates that we have a
solution to what is referred to as the original system in the present proof. Moreover, the initial data for this solution is determined
by the initial data for (and the equations of) (
13
). Due to the uniqueness of solutions to the original system, noted
above, we conclude that solutions to (
13
) are unique.
∎

Finally, we demonstrate Cauchy stability in the case of (
13
).

Proof of Theorem
13
.

The statement is an immediate consequence of Proposition
6
, given the relation between the different systems, explained in
detail at the beginning of the present subsection and in the proof of Theorem
10
.
∎

Appendix A
Sobolev spaces

In order not to interrupt the presentation of the main body of the text with technical details, we here summarise some of the notation as well
as some of the observations concerning Sobolev spaces and estimates that we need in this article.

A.1.
Measurability

Given a smooth manifold
M
, we begin by defining a
\sigma
-algebra of measurable subsets; cf.
[
19
, Definition 13.22, p. 204]
.

Definition 69
.

Let
M
be a smooth
n
-dimensional manifold. Then a subset
A\subseteq M
is
measurable
if, for every choice of local coordinates

(\mathsf{x},U)
, the set
\mathsf{x}(A\cap U)
is a Lebesgue measurable subset of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. If
X
is a topological space,
f:M\rightarrow X
is said to
be measurable if
f^{-1}(V)
is measurable for every open set
V\subseteq X
.

Remark 70
.

Note that if
U,V\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\phi:U\rightarrow V
is
C^{1}
, then
\phi
maps Lebesgue measurable sets to Lebesgue measurable sets;
cf.
[
19
, Lemma 13.21, p. 204]
.

Remark 71
.

The collection
\mathscr{A}
of measurable subsets of
M
is a
\sigma
-algebra; cf.
[
19
, Lemma 13.24, p. 204]
. Moreover, due to the
definition (in particular, the properties of the Lebesgue measure),
\mathscr{A}
includes all the Borel sets. Finally, if
(\mathsf{x}_{i},U_{i})
is a countable
collection of coordinate charts such that the
\{U_{i}\}
cover
M
, and if
\mathsf{x}_{i}(A\cap U_{i})
is measurable for all
i
, then
A\in\mathscr{A}
;
cf.
[
19
, Lemma 13.24, p. 204]
.

Let
(M,h)
be a smooth Riemannian manifold and
\mathscr{A}
be the
\sigma
-algebra of measurable subsets of
M
. We then wish to associate a measure

\mu_{h}:\mathscr{A}\rightarrow[0,\infty]
with
h
. We do so in several steps. Assume first that
A\in\mathscr{A}
and that there are local coordinates
(\mathsf{x},U)

such that
A\subset U
. We then define

(197)

\mu_{h}(A):=\textstyle{\int}_{\mathsf{x}(A)}|\det\mathsf{h}_{\mathsf{x}}|^{1/2}\circ\mathsf{x}^{-1}d\mu,

where
\mu
is the standard Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Moreover,
\mathsf{h}_{\mathsf{x}}
is the matrix valued function on
U
with components

(198)

(\mathsf{h}_{\mathsf{x}})_{ij}:=h(\partial_{\mathsf{x}^{i}},\partial_{\mathsf{x}^{j}}).

If
(\mathsf{y},V)
are local coordinates with
A\subseteq V
, then

\textstyle{\int}_{\mathsf{x}(A)}|\det\mathsf{h}_{\mathsf{x}}|^{1/2}\circ\mathsf{x}^{-1}d\mu=\textstyle{\int}_{\mathsf{y}(A)}|\det\mathsf{h}_{\mathsf{y}}|^{1/2}\circ\mathsf{y}^{-1}d\mu

due to the change of variables formula; cf.
[
27
, Theorem 7.26, pp. 153-154]
. In particular, the right hand side of (
197
) is independent of the
local coordinates. Moreover,
\mu_{h}
, given by (
197
), defines a measure on
U
; cf.
[
27
, Theorem 1.29, p. 23]
. In particular, considered as
a measure on
U
,
\mu_{h}
is countably additive. Next, let
P_{i}\subseteq M
,
i\in\mathbb{N}
, be such that each
P_{i}\in\mathscr{A}
;

P_{i}\cap P_{j}=\varnothing
for
i\neq j
and the union of the
P_{i}
equals
M
(i.e.,
\{P_{i}\}
is a
partition
of
M
); and each
P_{i}
is
contained in a coordinate chart, say
(\mathsf{x}_{i},U_{i})
; we leave it to the reader to verify that such covers exist. Define, for any
A\in\mathscr{A}
,

(199)

\mu_{h}(A):=\textstyle{\sum}_{i}\mu_{h}(A\cap P_{i}).

In order to verify that the result is independent of the partition, let
\{Q_{i}\}
be another partition of
M
with analogous properties. Then, due
to the properties we have already verified,

\textstyle{\sum}_{i}\mu_{h}(A\cap P_{i})=\textstyle{\sum}_{i}\textstyle{\sum}_{j}\mu_{h}(A\cap P_{i}\cap Q_{j})=\textstyle{\sum}_{j}\textstyle{\sum}_{i}\mu_{h}(A\cap P_{i}\cap Q_{j})=\textstyle{\sum}_{j}\mu_{h}(A\cap Q_{j}),

where we appealed to
[
27
, Corollary, p. 23]
in the second step. This means that
\mu_{h}
is well defined. We leave it to the reader to verify that

\mu_{h}
is a measure.

Let
f\in C_{c}(M)
; i.e.,
f
is a continuous function with compact support in
M
. Assume, moreover, that there is a coordinate chart
(\mathsf{x},U)
such
that
\mathrm{supp}f\subset U
. Assume, finally,
M
to be oriented;
(\mathsf{x},U)
to be a positively oriented chart; and
{\mathrm{Vol}}_{h}
to be the volume form
associated with
h
; cf.
[
15
, Proposition 15.29, p. 389]
. We then leave it to the reader to verify that

(200)

\textstyle{\int}_{M}fd\mu_{h}=\textstyle{\int}_{M}f{\mathrm{Vol}}_{h};

cf., e.g.,
[
15
, Chapter 16]
and
[
27
, Theorem 1.29, p. 23]
. Combining this observation with a partition of unity implies that
(
200
) holds for all
f\in C_{c}(M)
.

A.2.
L^{q}
-spaces

Let
r,s\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
T^{(r,s)}TM
denote the bundle of mixed tensors of type
(r,s)
; see, e.g.,

[
15
, p. 316]
. A
section
of this bundle is a function
\psi:M\rightarrow T^{(r,s)}TM
such that the base point of
\psi(p)
is
p
;
i.e., if
\pi:T^{(r,s)}TM\rightarrow M
is the natural projection to the base point, then
\pi\circ\psi={\mathrm{Id}}_{M}
. Let
\mathcal{T}^{(r,s)}(M)
denote the set
of smooth sections of
T^{(r,s)}TM
. By the definition of measurability, it is clear that a section is measurable if and only if the components of
the section with respect to local coordinates are measurable; cf.
Definition
69
. A contraction of a tensor product of two measurable sections is thus measurable. Since
\mathscr{A}
includes the
Borel sets, continuous sections are measurable. In what follows, we denote the measurable sections by
\mathcal{M}^{(r,s)}(M)
.

Let
(M,h)
be a Riemannian manifold and
u,v\in T^{(r,s)}TM
with
\pi(u)=\pi(v)
. Then, if
p:=\pi(u)
,

(u,v)_{h(p)}:=h^{i_{1}j_{1}}(p)\cdots h^{i_{s}j_{s}}(p)h_{l_{1}m_{1}}(p)\cdots h_{l_{r}m_{r}}(p)u^{l_{1}\cdots l_{r}}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{s}}v^{m_{1}\cdots m_{r}}_{j_{1}\cdots j_{s}}.

If
\phi
and
\psi
are sections of the same type, we define
(\phi,\psi)_{h}
to be the function whose value at
p\in M
is
(\phi(p),\psi(p))_{h(p)}
.
Note that if
\phi
and
\psi
are measurable, then
(\phi,\psi)_{h}
is a measurable function due to the above observations. Moreover,

(\phi,\phi)_{h}\geq 0
. Let
1\leq q\in\mathbb{R}
. In what follows we say that
\phi\in\mathcal{L}^{q}[T^{(r,s)}TM;h]
if
\phi\in\mathcal{M}^{(r,s)}(M)
and

\textstyle{\int}_{M}|\phi|_{h}^{q}d\mu_{h}<\infty,

where
|\phi|_{h}:=(\phi,\phi)_{h}^{1/2}
. Moreover, we say that
\phi\in\mathcal{L}^{\infty}[T^{(r,s)}TM;h]
if
\phi\in\mathcal{M}^{(r,s)}(M)
and

\mathrm{ess\ sup}_{M}|\phi|_{h}<\infty.

Next, we say that
\phi,\psi\in\mathcal{M}^{(r,s)}(M)
are equivalent if the set on which they differ has measure zero. Let
L^{q}[T^{(r,s)}TM;h]
denote the set
of equivalence classes of elements of
\mathcal{L}^{q}[T^{(r,s)}TM;h]
. If
u\in L^{q}[T^{(r,s)}TM;h]
,
1\leq q<\infty
and
\phi
is an element of the equivalence
class
u
, we define

\|u\|_{L^{q}(h)}:=\big(\textstyle{\int}_{M}|\phi|_{h}^{q}d\mu_{h}\big)^{1/q}.

If
u\in L^{\infty}[T^{(r,s)}TM;h]
and
\phi
is an element of the equivalence class
u
, we define

\|u\|_{L^{\infty}(h)}:=\mathrm{ess\ sup}_{M}|\phi|_{h}.

We define
\mathcal{L}^{q}_{\mathrm{loc}}[T^{(r,s)}TM;h]
and
L^{q}_{\mathrm{loc}}[T^{(r,s)}TM;h]
similarly. For the sake of brevity, we often use the simplified notation

L^{2}(h)
etc. Moreover, we will typically not distinguish between functions and equivalence classes of functions that differ on a set of measure
zero.

A.3.
Weak derivatives

Assume that
M
is oriented and that
u\in L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}[T^{(r,s)}TM;h]
. Then
u
is said to be
weakly differentiable
if there is
an element, say
D^{h}u\in L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}[T^{(r,s+1)}TM;h]
, such that for every
\phi\in\mathcal{T}^{(r,s+1)}(M)
with compact support,

\textstyle{\int}_{M}(D^{h}u,\phi)_{h}d\mu_{h}=-\textstyle{\int}_{M}(u,\mathrm{div}_{h}\phi)_{h}d\mu_{h},

where

(\mathrm{div}_{h}\phi)_{i_{1}\cdots i_{s}}^{j_{1}\dots j_{r}}=\nabla^{h,i}\phi_{ii_{1}\cdots i_{s}}^{j_{1}\dots j_{r}}

and
\nabla^{h}
denotes the Levi-Civita connection of
(M,h)
. If
u
is smooth, then
D^{h}u=\nabla^{h}u
; this follows from the fact that if

X
is a smooth vector field with compact support on
M
, then

\textstyle{\int}_{M}\mathrm{div}_{h}X\,d\mu_{h}=\textstyle{\int}_{M}\mathrm{div}_{h}X\,{\mathrm{Vol}}_{h}=0.

Higher weak derivatives are defined iteratively and the
k
’th weak derivative is denoted
(D^{h})^{k}u
. Again, if
u
is smooth,
(D^{h})^{k}u=(\nabla^{h})^{k}u
.
We leave it to the reader to verify that
u
is
k
times weakly differentiable if and only if the components of
u\circ\mathsf{x}^{-1}
with respect to

\mathsf{x}
are
k
times weakly differentiable on
\mathsf{x}(U)
for any choice of local coordinates
(\mathsf{x},U)
. In particular, whether a function has a weak
derivative or not does not depend on the choice of Riemannian metric
h
(though the weak derivative
D^{h}u
of course does). Note also that, with
respect to local coordinates,
(D^{h})^{k}u
is given by replacing derivatives by weak derivatives in the expression for
(\nabla^{h})^{k}u
with respect to
local coordinates.

A.4.
Sobolev spaces

Let
1\leq q\in\mathbb{R}
,
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
u\in L^{q}[T^{(r,s)}TM;h]
. If
u
is
k
times weakly differentiable and

\textstyle{\int}_{M}|(D^{h})^{j}u|_{h}^{q}d\mu_{h}<\infty

for all
0\leq j\leq k
, then we say that
u\in W^{k,q}[T^{(r,s)}TM;h]
and use the notation

\|u\|_{W^{k,q}(h)}:=\big(\textstyle{\sum}_{j=0}^{k}\textstyle{\int}_{M}|(D^{h})^{j}u|_{h}^{q}d\mu_{h}\big)^{1/q}.

Next, let
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
u\in L^{\infty}[T^{(r,s)}TM;h]
. If
u
is
k
times weakly differentiable and

\|(D^{h})^{j}u\|_{L^{\infty}(h)}<\infty

for all
0\leq j\leq k
, then we say that
u\in W^{k,\infty}[T^{(r,s)}TM;h]
and use the notation

\|u\|_{W^{k,\infty}(h)}:=\textstyle{\sum}_{j=0}^{k}\|(D^{h})^{j}u\|_{L^{\infty}(h)}.

Define, in addition,
H^{k}[T^{(r,s)}TM;h]:=W^{k,2}[T^{(r,s)}TM;h]
and, for
u,v\in H^{k}[T^{(r,s)}TM;h]
,

(u,v)_{H^{k}(h)}:=\textstyle{\sum}_{j=0}^{k}\textstyle{\int}_{M}((D^{h})^{j}u,(D^{h})^{j}v)_{h}d\mu_{h}.

For the sake of brevity, we often simply write
H^{k}(h)
.

Sobolev spaces on closed manifolds.
Let
(M,h)
be a closed and oriented Riemannian manifold,
1\leq q\in\mathbb{R}
and
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. Let
\mathbb{P}
be a
finite partition of unity
\phi_{i}
,
i=1,\dots,l
, such that
\mathrm{supp}\phi_{i}\subset U_{i}
, where
(\mathsf{x}_{i},U_{i})
are local coordinates. Define, for

u\in W^{k,q}[T^{(r,s)}TM;h]
,

\|u\|_{W^{k,q}(M;\mathbb{P})}:=\big(\textstyle{\sum}_{i=1}^{l}\sum_{i_{1},\dots,i_{s}}\sum_{j_{1},\dots,j_{r}}\sum_{|\alpha|\leq k}\int_{\mathsf{x}_{i}(U_{i})}(\phi_{i}|\partial^{\alpha}u^{j_{1}\dots j_{r}}_{i_{1}\dots i_{s}}|^{q})\circ\mathsf{x}_{i}^{-1}d\mu\big)^{1/q}.

We leave it as an exercise to verify that the right hand side is finite for
u\in W^{k,q}[T^{(r,s)}TM;h]
. We also leave it as an exercise to verify
that
\|\cdot\|_{W^{k,q}(h)}
and
\|u\|_{W^{k,q}(M;\mathbb{P})}
are equivalent, irrespective of the choice of
h
and
\mathbb{P}
, but with constants depending on

h
and
\mathbb{P}
. Finally, we leave it to the reader to verify that
W^{k,q}[T^{(r,s)}TM;h]
and
H^{k}[T^{(r,s)}TM;h]
are Banach and Hilbert spaces,
respectively, and that
\mathcal{T}^{(r,s)}(M)
is dense in
W^{k,q}[T^{(r,s)}TM;h]
for
q<\infty
.

A.5.
Conventions concerning frames

Let
M
be a manifold. If
U\subset M
is open;
\{E_{i}\}_{i=1}^{l}
are smooth vector fields on
U
;
\psi\in C^{m}(U)
;
k\leq m
; and

\mathbf{I}=(i_{1},\dots,i_{k})
, where
i_{j}\in\{1,\dots,l\}
for
j=1,\dots,k
, then
E_{\mathbf{I}}\psi:=E_{i_{1}}\cdots E_{i_{k}}\psi
. If
\mathbf{I}=(i_{1},\dots,i_{k})
,
we also use the notation
|\mathbf{I}|=k
. In case
\mathbf{I}
is empty,
E_{\mathbf{I}}
denotes multiplication by
1
and we use the convention that
|\mathbf{I}|=0
.

In case
(M,h)
is a closed
n
-dimensional Riemannian manifold;
\mathbb{E}:=\{E_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
is a global frame on
M
; and
u\in H^{k}(h)
, we can define

E_{\mathbf{I}}u
for
|\mathbf{I}|\leq k
in terms of the weak derivatives of
u
(as well as the structure coefficients of the frame etc.). For
u\in H^{k}(h)
,
we then use the notation

(201)

\|u\|_{H^{k}(M;\mathbb{E})}:=\big(\textstyle{\sum}_{|\mathbf{I}|\leq k}\int_{M}|E_{\mathbf{I}}u|^{2}d\mu_{h}\big)^{1/2}.

We leave it to the reader to verify that this norm is equivalent to
\|\cdot\|_{H^{k}(h)}
.

A.6.
Moser estimates

In the proof of local existence, one essential tool is Moser estimates. In case
(M,h)
is a closed Riemannian manifold;
\mathbb{E}:=\{E_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
is
a global frame on
M
;
\psi_{1},\dots,\psi_{l}\in C^{\infty}(M)
; and
|\mathbf{I}_{1}|+\dots+|\mathbf{I}_{l}|=m
, the relevant estimate reads

(202)

\|E_{\mathbf{I}_{1}}\psi_{1}\cdots E_{\mathbf{I}_{l}}\psi_{l}\|_{L^{2}(h)}\leq C\textstyle{\sum}_{j=1}^{l}\|\psi_{j}\|_{H^{m}(M;\mathbb{E})}\prod_{i\neq j}\|\psi_{i}\|_{L^{\infty}(h)},

where the constant only depends on an upper bound on
|\mathrm{div}_{h}E_{i}|
,
n
and
m
(note that if
\mathbb{E}
is an orthonormal frame with respect to
h
,
then
|\mathrm{div}_{h}E_{i}|
can be estimated in terms of the structure coefficients of
\mathbb{E}
). This estimate is a special case of
[
20
, Corollary B.8, p. 215]
.

A.7.
Modified Moser estimates

When deriving elliptic estimates, we, for the sake of completeness, do not want to assume that we have a global frame. Let
(M,h)
be a closed
Riemannian manifold and
U\subseteq M
be an open subset on which there is an orthonormal frame
\{E_{i}\}
with respect to
h
. Let
\zeta\in C^{\infty}_{c}(U)
.
Then we can consider
E_{\zeta,i}:=\zeta E_{i}
to be a smooth vector field on
M
. Moreover, the vector fields
E_{\zeta,1},\dots,E_{\zeta,n}
satisfy the conditions
imposed on the vector fields
W_{i}
in
[
20
, Section B.1, p. 209]
(in the present setting the
W_{i}
have no time dependence). Given
\mathbf{I}=(i_{1},\dots,i_{l})
,
we use the notation
E_{\zeta,\mathbf{I}}:=E_{\zeta,i_{1}}\cdots E_{\zeta,i_{l}}
. Given
u\in H^{l}(h)
, we use the notation

\|\mathbf{E}^{\leq l}_{\zeta}u\|_{L^{2}(h)}:=\big(\textstyle{\sum}_{|\mathbf{I}|\leq l}\textstyle{\int}_{M}|E_{\zeta,\mathbf{I}}u|^{2}d\mu_{h}\big)^{1/2}.

In case
\psi_{1},\dots,\psi_{l}\in C^{\infty}(M)
and
|\mathbf{I}_{1}|+\dots+|\mathbf{I}_{l}|=m
,
[
20
, Corollary B.8, p. 215]
then implies that

(203)

\|E_{\zeta,\mathbf{I}_{1}}\psi_{1}\cdots E_{\zeta,\mathbf{I}_{l}}\psi_{l}\|_{L^{2}(h)}\leq C\textstyle{\sum}_{j=1}^{l}\|\mathbf{E}^{\leq m}_{\zeta}\psi_{j}\|_{L^{2}(h)}\prod_{i\neq j}\|\psi_{i}\|_{L^{\infty}(h)},

where the constant only depends on an upper bound on
|\mathrm{div}_{h}E_{\zeta,i}|
,
n
and
m
(due to (
246
) below, it follows that

|\mathrm{div}_{h}E_{\zeta,i}|
only depends on
\zeta
and
\{E_{i}\}
).

A.8.
Rellich-Kondrakhov theorem, Poincaré inequality

Next, we recall the Rellich-Kondrakhov theorem.

Theorem 72

(Rellich-Kondrakhov Theorem)
.

Let
(M,h)
be a closed Riemannian manifold of dimension
n\geq 2
. Then, for every
p,q\geq 1
,
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
l\in\mathbb{N}
satisfying

1/p>1/q-l/n
, the embedding
H^{k+l,q}(h)\hookrightarrow H^{k,p}(h)
is compact.

Remark 73
.

We are also interested in the case
n=1
and
M=\mathbb{S}^{1}
. In this case, the embedding from
H^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
into
C^{0,\alpha}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
is compact for

\alpha<1/2
; see, e.g,
[
6
, Theorem 1.4.13, p. 48]
. In particular, this means that the embedding from
H^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
into
L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
is
compact. Note also that
W^{1,1}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
is continuously embedded into
C^{0}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
and that
W^{1,1}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
is compactly embedded into

L^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
; see
[
6
, Proposition 1.2.5, p. 27]
. Combining these observations with
[
6
, Proposition 1.2.5, p. 27]
yields the
conclusion that
W^{1,1}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
is compactly embedded into
L^{p}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
for any
p\in[1,\infty)
.

Proof.

The proof of this statement can be found, e.g., in
[
14
, Theorem 3.6, p. 24]
and
[
6
, Theorem 1.2.12, p. 34]
.
∎

Appendix B
Hölder spaces on manifolds

Let
(M,g)
be a closed Riemannian manifold and
\gamma:I\rightarrow M
be a smooth curve. Then
\mathcal{T}^{(r,s)}(\gamma)
is the set of smooth functions

T:I\rightarrow T^{(r,s)}TM
such that the base point of
T(t)
is
\gamma(t)
. We refer to this set as the set of
(r,s)
-tensor fields on
\gamma
.
Note, in particular, that if
S\in\mathcal{T}^{(r,s)}(M)
, then
S\circ\gamma\in\mathcal{T}^{(r,s)}(\gamma)
. Conversely, if
t_{0}\in I
,
\gamma^{\prime}(t_{0})\neq 0
and
T\in\mathcal{T}^{(r,s)}(\gamma)
,
we can locally write
T
as a restriction of a smooth tensor field on an open neighbourhood of
\gamma(t_{0})
to
\gamma
. Next, we have a notion of induced covariant derivative,
just as in the case of vector fields on
\gamma
; cf., e.g.,
[
17
, Proposition 18, p. 65]
. In fact, if
\nabla
denotes the Levi-Civita connection of
(M,g)
,
there is a unique function taking
T\in\mathcal{T}^{(r,s)}(\gamma)
to
T^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}^{(r,s)}(\gamma)
, called the
induced covariant derivative
, such that

(1)

(aT_{1}+bT_{2})^{\prime}=aT_{1}^{\prime}+bT_{2}^{\prime}
for all
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
and
T_{1},T_{2}\in\mathcal{T}^{(r,s)}(\gamma)
,

(2)

(hT)^{\prime}=h^{\prime}T+hT^{\prime}
for all
h\in C^{\infty}(I)
and
T\in\mathcal{T}^{(r,s)}(\gamma)
,

(3)

(S\circ\gamma)^{\prime}(t)=\nabla_{\gamma^{\prime}(t)}S
for all
t\in I
and
S\in\mathcal{T}^{(r,s)}(M)
.

Moreover, the induced covariant derivative has the property that

(204)

\tfrac{d}{dt}(T_{1},T_{2})_{g}=(T_{1}^{\prime},T_{2})_{g}+(T_{1},T_{2}^{\prime})_{g}

for
T_{1},T_{2}\in\mathcal{T}^{(r,s)}(\gamma)
. The proof of these statements is similar to the proof of
[
17
, Proposition 18, p. 65]
. Writing the equation
T^{\prime}=0

in local coordinates, it is clear that it is a linear homogeneous system of ODE’s for the components of
T
with respect to the local coordinates. If
a\in I

and
\xi\in T^{(r,s)}TM
with base point
\gamma(a)
, there is thus a unique
T\in\mathcal{T}^{(r,s)}(\gamma)
such that
T^{\prime}=0
and
T(a)=\xi
. If
\gamma
