is injective
and
b\in I
, we then refer to
T(b)
as the
parallel transport of
\xi
from
\gamma(a)
to
\gamma(b)
along
\gamma
. Next, let

\mathscr{O}_{g}:=\{(p,q)\in M\times M\,|\,0<d_{g}(p,q)<\mathrm{inj}(M,g)\},

where
\mathrm{inj}(M,g)
denotes the injectivity radius of
(M,g)
and
d_{g}
denotes the topological metric on
M
induced by
g
. If
(p,q)\in\mathscr{O}_{g}
, then there is
an
r>0
satisfying
d_{g}(p,q)<r<\mathrm{inj}(M,g)
. This means that the exponential map based at
p
is a diffeomorphism from the open ball of radius
r
in

T_{p}M
onto its image. In particular, there is a corresponding geodesic
\sigma_{pq}
with the property that
\sigma_{pq}(0)=p
and
\sigma_{pq}(1)=q
; in fact,

\sigma_{pq}
is the unique length minimising geodesic from
p
to
q
in
M
; see
[
17
, Proposition 16, p. 134]
.
If
(p,q)\in\mathscr{O}_{g}
and
\xi\in T^{(r,s)}TM
with base point
p
, we, in what follows, denote by
P^{(r,s)}[\sigma_{pq}](\xi)
the parallel transport of
\xi
from
p
to

q
along
\sigma_{pq}
. Let
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
\alpha\in(0,1]
,
r,s\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and assume
S
to be a
C^{k}
section of
T^{(r,s)}TM
. Then we define

(205)

\displaystyle\|S\|_{C^{k}(g)}:=

\displaystyle\textstyle{\sup}_{p\in M}\sum_{j\leq k}|((\nabla^{g})^{j}S)(p)|_{g},

(206)

\displaystyle[S]_{C^{k,\alpha}(g)}:=

\displaystyle\textstyle{\sup}_{(p,q)\in\mathscr{O}_{g}}\tfrac{|P^{(r,s+k)}[\sigma_{pq}](((\nabla^{g})^{k}S)(p))-((\nabla^{g})^{k}S)(q)|_{g}}{[d_{g}(p,q)]^{\alpha}},

(207)

\displaystyle\|S\|_{C^{k,\alpha}(g)}:=

\displaystyle\|S\|_{C^{k}(g)}+[S]_{C^{k,\alpha}(g)}.

If
\|S\|_{C^{k,\alpha}(g)}
is bounded, we say that
S\in C^{k,\alpha}(g)
. When deriving Schauder estimates, it is convenient to use an equivalent norm, expressed in terms of
local coordinates. Next, we prove that there are appropriate coverings by coordinate neighbourhoods that can be used to define such a norm.

Lemma 74
.

Let
(M,g)
be a closed Riemannian manifold. Given
\epsilon\in(0,1/2)
, there is a finite cover
\{U_{i}\}_{i=1}^{l_{\epsilon}}
of
M
and local coordinates

\mathsf{x}_{i}:U_{i}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
with the following properties: the Christoffel symbols of
g
with respect to the coordinates
(\mathsf{x}_{i},U_{i})
are
bounded in absolute value by
\epsilon
on
U_{i}
for all
i\in\{1,\dots,l_{\epsilon}\}
;

(208)

\|\mathsf{g}_{\mathsf{x}_{i}}\circ\mathsf{x}_{i}^{-1}-{\mathrm{Id}}\|_{C^{0}(\mathsf{x}_{i}(U_{i}))}\leq\epsilon,\ \ \ \|\mathsf{g}_{\mathsf{x}_{i}}^{-1}\circ\mathsf{x}_{i}^{-1}-{\mathrm{Id}}\|_{C^{0}(\mathsf{x}_{i}(U_{i}))}\leq\epsilon

for all
i\in\{1,\dots,l_{\epsilon}\}
; the estimate

(209)

(1-\epsilon)d(p,q)\leq|\mathsf{x}_{i}(p)-\mathsf{x}_{i}(q)|\leq(1+\epsilon)d(p,q)

holds for all
p,q\in U_{i}
and
i\in\{1,\dots,l_{\epsilon}\}
; for any
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, there is a constant
C_{k}\in\mathbb{R}
such that

(210)

\|\mathsf{g}_{\mathsf{x}_{i}}\circ\mathsf{x}_{i}^{-1}\|_{C^{k}(\mathsf{x}_{i}(U))}\leq C_{k}

for all
i\in\{1,\dots,l_{\epsilon}\}
; and there is an
r>0
such that for any
p\in M
, the ball
B_{r}(p)
is contained in one of the neighbourhoods

U_{i}
.

Remark 75
.

The coordinates
(\mathsf{x}_{i},U_{i})
that we construct in the proof are normal coordinates. However, this is not of importance in what follows. In fact,
we wish to work with coordinates having the properties listed in the lemma but which are not necessarily normal coordinates.

Remark 76
.

For the sake of brevity, we use the notation
d:=d_{g}
in the statement of the lemma and the proof. The notation
\mathsf{g}_{\mathsf{x}_{i}}
is introduced in
(
198
). Note also that the last statement of the lemma is an immediate consequence of the assumptions and the Lebesgue Lemma;
cf., e.g.,
[
5
, Lemma 9.11, p. 28]
. However, we state it as a property of the covering for the sake of convenience.

Proof.

One essential feature of the coverings we wish to construct is the estimate (
209
). A natural class of coordinate systems
in which to obtain it is normal coordinates. If
\mathsf{x}
are normal coordinates, based at
p
, on a ball
B_{r}(p)
, then

(211)

d(p,q)=|\mathsf{x}(q)|=|\mathsf{x}(q)-\mathsf{x}(p)|

for
q\in B_{r}(p)
; see below. Since a convex neighbourhood is a normal neighbourhood of each of its points, it is natural, as a next step, to use
the corresponding family of normal neighbourhoods. However, we only obtain (
211
) if one of the points
p
,
q
is the base point
of the normal coordinates. Next, it is therefore natural to try to obtain uniform control of the family of normal coordinates in (a subset of) a convex
neighbourhood in terms of one of the normal coordinate systems.

Constructing a family of normal neighbourhoods.
To begin with, let
(\mathsf{y}_{i},V_{i})
,
i=1,\dots,l
, be a finite covering of
M
by local
coordinate systems. Let

\{E_{i,k}\}_{k=1}^{n}
be an orthonormal frame on
V_{i}
with respect to
g
for
i=1,\dots,l
. For each
x\in M
, there is a convex neighbourhood of
x

with respect to
g
, say
\mathscr{C}
, contained in one of the
V_{i}
; cf.
[
17
, Proposition 7, p. 130]
. Note that the map taking
p,q\in\mathscr{C}
to

\sigma^{\prime}_{pq}(0)
is a smooth map from
\mathscr{C}\times\mathscr{C}
to
TM
, where
\sigma_{pq}
is the unique geodesic in
\mathscr{C}
such that
\sigma_{pq}(0)=p
and

\sigma_{pq}(1)=q
; cf.
[
17
, Lemma 9, p. 131]
. For this reason, we obtain a smooth family of normal coordinates, say
\mathsf{x}_{p}
for

p\in\mathscr{C}
, defined as follows. For
p,q\in\mathscr{C}
, let

\mathsf{x}_{p}(q):=[\langle\sigma^{\prime}_{pq}(0),E_{i,1}|_{p}\rangle,\dots,\langle\sigma^{\prime}_{pq}(0),E_{i,n}|_{p}\rangle],

where
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle:=g
. Next, note that if
p\in\mathscr{C}
and
\rho>0
are such that
B_{\rho}(p)\subset\mathscr{C}
, then
B_{\rho}(p)
is a normal
neighbourhood of
p
(since
\mathscr{C}
is a normal neighbourhood of
p
, by definition, and the relevant subset of
T_{p}M
corresponding to
B_{\rho}(p)

is starshaped). Moreover, if
B_{\rho}(p)
is normal, then
d(p,q)=|\sigma^{\prime}_{pq}(0)|_{g}=|\mathsf{x}_{p}(q)|
for all
q\in B_{\rho}(p)
; cf.

[
17
, Proposition 16, p. 134]
. Here
|\cdot|
represents the standard norm in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

Uniform control of the family in terms of one coordinate system.
Next, note that if
o\in\mathscr{C}
, then

\mathsf{x}_{p}(q)=\mathsf{x}_{p}(q)-\mathsf{x}_{p}(p)=\mathsf{x}_{p}\circ\mathsf{x}_{o}^{-1}\circ\mathsf{x}_{o}(q)-\mathsf{x}_{p}\circ\mathsf{x}_{o}^{-1}\circ\mathsf{x}_{o}(p).

Assume now that
B_{2r}(o)\subset\mathscr{C}
and that
p,q\in B_{2r}(o)
for some
r>0
, so that the straight line between
\mathsf{x}_{o}(p)
and
\mathsf{x}_{o}(q)
is
a subset of the ball of radius
2r
and center at the origin in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Let
\xi(t):=t\mathsf{x}_{o}(q)+(1-t)\mathsf{x}_{o}(p)
. Then

\mathsf{x}_{p}\circ\mathsf{x}_{o}^{-1}\circ\mathsf{x}_{o}(q)-\mathsf{x}_{p}\circ\mathsf{x}_{o}^{-1}\circ\mathsf{x}_{o}(p)=\textstyle{\int}_{0}^{1}D(\mathsf{x}_{p}\circ\mathsf{x}_{o}^{-1})(\xi(t))dt[\mathsf{x}_{o}(q)-\mathsf{x}_{o}(p)].

Next, note that for
p=o
,
D(\mathsf{x}_{p}\circ\mathsf{x}_{o}^{-1})
is the identity mapping on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Letting
\gamma
be a length minimising geodesic with

\gamma(0)=o
and
\gamma(1)=p
, it then follows that

D(\mathsf{x}_{p}\circ\mathsf{x}_{o}^{-1})-\mathrm{Id}=\textstyle{\int}_{0}^{1}\tfrac{d}{dt}\left[D(\mathsf{x}_{\gamma(t)}\circ\mathsf{x}_{o}^{-1})\right]dt.

However, since
\mathsf{x}_{p}(q)
is smooth as a function of both
p
and
q
, there is, for each pair of compact subsets
K_{0},K_{1}\subset\mathscr{C}
, a constant
C
such
that

(212)

\big\|\tfrac{d}{dt}\big[D(\mathsf{x}_{\gamma(t)}\circ\mathsf{x}_{o}^{-1})\big](r)\big\|\leq C|\gamma^{\prime}(t)|_{g},

assuming
\gamma(t)\in K_{0}
and
r\in K_{1}
, where
\|\cdot\|
denotes the standard norm on
L(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. This means that, if
\gamma(t)\in K_{0}
for
t\in[0,1]
and
r\in K_{1}
,

(213)

\|D(\mathsf{x}_{p}\circ\mathsf{x}_{o}^{-1})(r)-\mathrm{Id}\|\leq Cd(o,p).

Constructing a covering.
Let
x\in M
and let
\mathscr{C}
be a convex neighbourhood of
x
as above. In particular,
\mathscr{C}\subset V_{i}
for some

i\in\{1,\dots,l\}
. Let now
r_{0}\in(0,1)
be such that
K_{1}:=\overline{B}_{2r_{0}}(x)
is compact and contained in
\mathscr{C}
and let
K_{0}:=\overline{B}_{r_{0}}(x)
.
Note that
K_{0}
is a compact subset of
\mathscr{C}
. Let
C\in(0,\infty)
be the associated constant such that (
212
) holds for
o=x
,

\gamma(t)\in K_{0}
and
r\in K_{1}
. Next, let
r\leq r_{0}
be such that
rC<\epsilon
. Letting
o=x
in the above arguments, and using the notation
\mathsf{x}:=\mathsf{x}_{x}
,

\mathsf{x}_{p}(q)=[\mathsf{x}(q)-\mathsf{x}(p)]+\textstyle{\int}_{0}^{1}\left[D(\mathsf{x}_{p}\circ\mathsf{x}^{-1})(\xi(t))-\mathrm{Id}\right]dt\ [\mathsf{x}(q)-\mathsf{x}(p)]

for
p,q\in B_{2r}(x)
. Appealing to (
213
) and the condition on
r
yields, in addition,

(214)

(1-\epsilon)|\mathsf{x}(q)-\mathsf{x}(p)|\leq d(p,q)\leq(1+\epsilon)|\mathsf{x}(q)-\mathsf{x}(p)|

for
p\in K:=\overline{B}_{r}(x)
and
q\in B_{2r}(x)
. Next, by decreasing
r
, if necessary, we can assume the absolute value of the Christoffel
symbols of
g
with respect to
\mathsf{x}
to be bounded by
\epsilon
on
B_{r}(x)
; cf.
[
17
, Proposition 33, p. 73]
. Similarly, we can assume estimates
analogous to (
208
) and (
210
) to hold. For each
x\in M
, we thus obtain local coordinates

(\mathsf{x}_{x},B_{r(x)}(x))
. By compactness of
M
, this covering can be reduced to a finite covering with the properties stated in the lemma.
∎

Given a covering of the type constructed in Lemma
74
, we can define alternate Hölder norms.

Definition 77
.

Let
(M,g)
be a closed Riemannian manifold; let
\mathbb{U}:=\{U_{i}\}
be a covering of the type constructed in Lemma
74
;
and let
\mathsf{x}_{i}
be the corresponding local coordinates on
U_{i}
. Define

(215a)

\displaystyle\|T\|_{C^{k}(M;\mathbb{U})}:=

\displaystyle\sup_{i\in\{1,\dots,l_{\epsilon}\}}\sup_{\underset{i_{1},\dots,i_{s}}{\overset{j_{1},\dots,j_{r}}{}}}\|{}^{\mathsf{x}_{i}}T^{j_{1}\cdots j_{r}}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{s}}\circ\mathsf{x}_{i}^{-1}\|_{C^{k}(\mathsf{x}_{i}(U_{i}))},

(215b)

\displaystyle[T]_{C^{k,\alpha}(M;\mathbb{U})}:=

\displaystyle\sup_{i\in\{1,\dots,l_{\epsilon}\}}\sup_{\underset{i_{1},\dots,i_{s}}{\overset{j_{1},\dots,j_{r}}{}}}\sup_{|\beta|=k}\sup_{\underset{p,q\in U_{i}}{\overset{p\neq q}{}}}\tfrac{|\partial^{\beta}({}^{\mathsf{x}_{i}}T^{j_{1}\cdots j_{r}}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{s}}\circ\mathsf{x}_{i}^{-1})(\mathsf{x}_{i}(p))-\partial^{\beta}({}^{\mathsf{x}_{i}}T^{j_{1}\cdots j_{r}}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{s}}\circ\mathsf{x}_{i}^{-1})(\mathsf{x}_{i}(q))|}{|\mathsf{x}_{i}(p)-\mathsf{x}_{i}(q)|^{\alpha}},

(215c)

\displaystyle\|T\|_{C^{k,\alpha}(M;\mathbb{U})}:=

\displaystyle\|T\|_{C^{k}(M;\mathbb{U})}+[T]_{C^{k,\alpha}(M;\mathbb{U})}

for
T\in C^{k,\alpha}(g)
.

Remark 78
.

In the definition, we use the convention that if
(\mathsf{x},U)
are local coordinates and
T
is a section of
T^{(r,s)}TM
, then

{}^{\mathsf{x}}T^{j_{1}\cdots j_{r}}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{s}}:=T(\partial_{\mathsf{x}^{i_{1}}},\dots,\partial_{\mathsf{x}^{i_{s}}},d\mathsf{x}^{j_{1}},\dots,d\mathsf{x}^{j_{r}}).

Given this definition, we can prove the following equivalence.

Lemma 79
.

Let
(M,g)
be a closed Riemannian manifold; let
\mathbb{U}:=\{U_{i}\}
be a covering of the type constructed in Lemma
74
;
and let
\mathsf{x}_{i}
be the corresponding local coordinates on
U_{i}
. Then, given the notation introduced in
Definition
77
, there is a constant
C>1
such that

(216)

C^{-1}\|S\|_{C^{k,\alpha}(M;\mathbb{U})}\leq\|S\|_{C^{k,\alpha}(g)}\leq C\|S\|_{C^{k,\alpha}(M;\mathbb{U})}

for all
S\in C^{k,\alpha}(g)
.

Remark 80
.

Due to (
216
), it is clear that
C^{k,\alpha}(g)
is complete; i.e., that it is a Banach space.

Proof.

In what follows, we, for the sake of brevity, write
\nabla
and
d
instead of
\nabla^{g}
and
d_{g}
, respectively. We leave it to the reader to
verify that the norms
\|\cdot\|_{C^{k}(M;\mathbb{U})}
and
\|\cdot\|_{C^{k}(g)}
are equivalent. In order to estimate
[S]_{C^{k,\alpha}(g)}
, see
(
206
), in terms of
\|S\|_{C^{k,\alpha}(M;\mathbb{U})}
, note first that if
d(p,q)\geq r>0
(where
r
is the number whose existence is
guaranteed in Lemma
74
), then

\begin{split}\tfrac{|P^{(r,s+k)}[\sigma_{pq}](\nabla^{k}S(p))-(\nabla^{k}S)(q)|_{g}}{[d(p,q)]^{\alpha}}\leq&r^{-\alpha}(|P^{(r,s+k)}[\sigma_{pq}](\nabla^{k}S(p))|_{g}+|(\nabla^{k}S)(q)|_{g})\\
=&r^{-\alpha}(|(\nabla^{k}S)(p)|_{g}+|(\nabla^{k}S)(q)|_{g})\leq C_{k}\|S\|_{C^{k}(M;\mathbb{U})},\end{split}

where we, in the second last step, used the fact that, due to (
204
), parallel transport preserves norms and in the last step used
the fact that the different
C^{k}
-norms are equivalent. In other words, we can assume that
q\in B_{r}(p)
. This means that there is an
i\in\{1,\dots,l_{\epsilon}\}

such that
p,q\in U_{i}
. This, in its turn, means that
d(p,q)\geq|\mathsf{x}_{i}(p)-\mathsf{x}_{i}(q)|/2
; cf. (
209
). It is therefore natural to
focus on the parallel transport of
\nabla^{k}S
. To this end, we solve the equation
T^{\prime}=0
along
\gamma
; i.e.,

(217)

\tfrac{d}{dt}T^{j_{1}\cdots j_{r}}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{s+k}}+\dot{\gamma}^{m}\textstyle{\sum}_{a=1}^{r}{}^{\mathsf{x}_{i}}\Gamma_{ml}^{j_{a}}\circ\gamma\cdot T^{j_{1}\cdots l\cdots j_{r}}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{s+k}}-\dot{\gamma}^{m}\textstyle{\sum}_{b=1}^{s+k}\ {}^{\mathsf{x}_{i}}\Gamma_{mi_{b}}^{l}\circ\gamma\cdot T^{j_{1}\cdots j_{r}}_{i_{1}\cdots l\cdots i_{s+k}}=0,

where
\gamma:=\sigma_{pq}
; and
{}^{\mathsf{x}_{i}}\Gamma^{m}_{jk}
are the Christoffel symbols of
g
with respect to the coordinates
(\mathsf{x}_{i},U_{i})
. We also impose the
initial condition

T^{j_{1}\cdots j_{r}}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{s+k}}(0)=(\nabla^{k}S)(\partial_{\mathsf{x}^{i_{1}}_{i}}|_{p},\dots,\partial_{\mathsf{x}^{i_{s+k}}_{i}}|_{p},d\mathsf{x}^{j_{1}}_{i}|_{p},\dots,d\mathsf{x}^{j_{r}}_{i}|_{p}).

In order to estimate the integral of the second and third terms on the left hand side of (
217
), note that

|{}^{\mathsf{x}_{i}}\Gamma^{m}_{jk}|\leq\epsilon
by assumption. Note also that, along the curve,

|T^{j_{1}\cdots j_{r}}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{s+2}}|\leq C|(\nabla^{k}S)(p)|_{g},

where
C
is a constant depending only on
r
and
s
. Here we used the fact that, due to (
204
), the
g
-norm of
T
is preserved along the curve and
the fact that (
208
) holds. Similarly,
|\dot{\gamma}^{m}|\leq C|\dot{\gamma}|_{g}
for some numerical constant
C
. Combining the above observations
yields the conclusion that

\begin{split}|T^{j_{1}\cdots j_{r}}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{s+2}}(1)-T^{j_{1}\cdots j_{r}}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{s+2}}(0)|\leq&C_{n,r,s}\epsilon|(\nabla^{k}S)(p)|_{g}\textstyle{\int}_{0}^{1}|\dot{\gamma}(\tau)|_{g}d\tau\\
=&C_{n,r,s}\epsilon|(\nabla^{k}S)(p)|_{g}d(p,q),\end{split}

where
C_{n,r,s}\in\mathbb{R}
is a constant depending only on
n
,
r
and
s
. Thus

|{}^{\mathsf{x}_{i}}[P^{(r,s+k)}[\sigma_{pq}](\nabla^{k}S(p))]^{j_{1}\cdots j_{r}}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{s+k}}-{}^{\mathsf{x}_{i}}(\nabla^{k}S)^{j_{1}\cdots j_{r}}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{s+k}}(p)|\leq C_{n,r,s}\epsilon|(\nabla^{k}S)(p)|_{g}d(p,q),

so that

\begin{split}&|{}^{\mathsf{x}_{i}}[P^{(r,s+k)}[\sigma_{pq}](\nabla^{k}S(p))]^{j_{1}\cdots j_{r}}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{s+k}}-{}^{\mathsf{x}_{i}}(\nabla^{k}S)^{j_{1}\cdots j_{r}}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{s+k}}(q)|\\
\leq&|{}^{\mathsf{x}_{i}}(\nabla^{k}S)^{j_{1}\cdots j_{r}}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{s+k}}(p)-{}^{\mathsf{x}_{i}}(\nabla^{k}S)^{j_{1}\cdots j_{r}}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{s+k}}(q)|+C_{n,r,s}\epsilon|(\nabla^{k}S)(p)|_{g}d(p,q).\end{split}

Dividing by
[d(p,q)]^{\alpha}
, the second term on the right hand side can be estimated by a constant times
\|S\|_{C^{k}(M;\mathbb{U})}
. What remains to be estimated is thus

\tfrac{|{}^{\mathsf{x}_{i}}(\nabla^{k}S)^{j_{1}\cdots j_{r}}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{s+k}}(p)-{}^{\mathsf{x}_{i}}(\nabla^{k}S)^{j_{1}\cdots j_{r}}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{s+k}}(q)|}{|\mathsf{x}_{i}(p)-\mathsf{x}_{i}(q)|^{\alpha}}.

We leave it to the reader to verify that this expression can be estimated by
\|S\|_{C^{k,\alpha}(M;\mathbb{U})}
. There is thus a constant
C_{k,\alpha}
such that the second
inequality in (
216
) holds. The proof of the first inequality is similar. We leave the details to the reader.
∎

Appendix C
Schauder estimates, elliptic model equation

In order to derive a continuation criterion for solutions to (
3
), we need Schauder estimates. Moreover, we need to have detailed
information concerning the dependence of the constants (appearing in the estimates) on the unknowns in (
3
). To this end, we
quote Gilbarg and Trudinger’s classical text
[
12
]
, more specifically
[
12
, Theorem 6.2, p. 90]
.
To be able to do so easily in the proofs to follow, it is convenient to introduce the relevant notation. Let
\Omega
be a proper open
subset of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. For
x,y\in\Omega
, define
d_{x}:=\mathrm{dist}(x,\partial\Omega)
(where the distance is measured with the standard Euclidean
metric) and
d_{x,y}:=\min\{d_{x},d_{y}\}
. For
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
u\in C^{k}(\Omega)
and
\sigma\in\mathbb{R}
, define

\displaystyle[u]_{k,0;\Omega}^{(\sigma)}:=

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in\Omega,|\beta|=k}d_{x}^{k+\sigma}|(\partial^{\beta}u)(x)|,

\displaystyle|u|_{k,0;\Omega}^{(\sigma)}:=

\displaystyle\textstyle{\sum}_{j=0}^{k}[u]_{j,0;\Omega}^{(\sigma)}.

In case
u\in C^{k,\alpha}(\Omega)
for some
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
0<\alpha\leq 1
, let
\mathscr{O}_{\Omega}:=\{(x,y)\in\Omega\times\Omega\,|\,x\neq y\}
and

\displaystyle[u]_{k,\alpha;\Omega}^{(\sigma)}:=

\displaystyle\sup_{(x,y)\in\mathscr{O}_{\Omega}}\sup_{|\beta|=k}d_{x,y}^{k+\alpha+\sigma}\tfrac{|\partial^{\beta}u(x)-\partial^{\beta}u(y)|}{|x-y|^{\alpha}},

\displaystyle|u|_{k,\alpha;\Omega}^{(\sigma)}:=

\displaystyle|u|_{k,0;\Omega}^{(\sigma)}+[u]_{k,\alpha;\Omega}^{(\sigma)}.

In case
\sigma=0
, we use the notation
*
instead of
(0)
in the above norms and semi-norms; e.g.,
|u|_{k,\alpha;\Omega}^{*}:=|u|_{k,\alpha;\Omega}^{(0)}
etc.
The statement of
[
12
, Theorem 6.2, p. 90]
is then the following:

Theorem 81

(Gilbarg and Trudinger, Theorem 6.2, p. 90)
.

Let
\Omega
be a proper and non-empty open subset of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
\alpha\in(0,1]
and let
u\in C^{2,\alpha}(\Omega)
be a bounded solution in
\Omega
of the
equation

(218)

a^{ij}\partial_{i}\partial_{j}u+b^{i}\partial_{i}u+cu=F,

where
F\in C^{\alpha}(\Omega)
and there are positive constants
\lambda
and
\Lambda
such that the coefficients satisfy

(219)

a^{ij}\xi_{i}\xi_{j}\geq\lambda|\xi|^{2}

for all
x\in\Omega
and
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and

|a^{ij}|^{*}_{0,\alpha;\Omega},\ |b^{i}|^{(1)}_{0,\alpha;\Omega},\ |c|^{(2)}_{0,\alpha;\Omega}\leq\Lambda.

Then

|u|_{2,\alpha;\Omega}^{*}\leq C(|u|_{0;\Omega}+|F|^{(2)}_{0,\alpha;\Omega}),

where
C=C(n,\alpha,\lambda,\Lambda)
and
|u|_{0;\Omega}:=\sup_{x\in\Omega}|u(x)|
.

Remark 82
.

In our applications, it will be of interest to keep in mind that the dependence of
C
on
\Lambda
can be assumed to be continuous and such that

C
is monotonically increasing with
\Lambda
. To justify the statement that
C
can be assumed to increase monotonically with
\Lambda
, note that

C
can be replaced by

C^{\prime}(n,\alpha,\lambda,\Lambda):=\textstyle{\inf}_{\eta\geq\Lambda}C(n,\alpha,\lambda,\eta),

which is clearly monotonically increasing. In order to justify the statement concerning continuity, assume
f:(0,\infty)\rightarrow(0,\infty)

to be an increasing function. Then
f
is Lebesgue integrable on compact subintervals of
(0,\infty)
, since it is measurable
and bounded on compact intervals (
f(t)\leq f(b)
for all
t\in[a,b]
). Define the function
g:(0,\infty)\rightarrow(0,\infty)
by the condition

g(t)=\textstyle{\int}_{t}^{t+1}f(s)ds.

Then
g(t)\geq f(t)
by construction. Moreover,
g
is increasing, since
f
is increasing. Finally,
g
is continuous, since
f
is uniformly bounded
on compact subsets. Applying this observation to
C^{\prime}(n,\alpha,\lambda,\cdot)
yields the desired conclusion.

Next, we state the Schauder estimates we need in our applications.

Theorem 83
.

Let
(\Sigma,h_{\mathrm{ref}})
be a closed Riemannian manifold and define Hölder spaces as at the beginning of
Appendix
B
with
(M,g)=(\Sigma,h_{\mathrm{ref}})
. Let
c_{1},c_{2}>1
be constants. Then there is a function

\mathfrak{S}:\mathbb{R}_{+}^{3}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
, depending only on
c_{1}
,
c_{2}
,
(\Sigma,h_{\mathrm{ref}})
and a covering of
(\Sigma,h_{\mathrm{ref}})
constructed in the
proof. Moreover,
\mathfrak{S}
is continuous, monotonically increasing in each of its arguments, and such that the following holds. Let

h\in C^{1,1}
be a Riemannian metric on
\Sigma
;
X\in C^{0,1}
be a vector field; and
f,a\in C^{0,1}
be real valued functions. Assume that

(220)

c_{1}^{-1}h_{\mathrm{ref}}\leq h\leq c_{1}h_{\mathrm{ref}},\ \ \ c_{2}^{-1}\leq a\leq c_{2}

and that
u\in C^{2,1}
is a solution to

(221)

-\Delta_{h}u+Xu+au=f.

Then

(222)

\|u\|_{C^{2,1}}\leq\mathfrak{S}(\|h\|_{C^{1,1}},\|X\|_{C^{0,1}},\|a\|_{C^{0,1}})\cdot\|f\|_{C^{0,1}}.

Remark 84
.

In the statement and proof, we, for the sake of brevity, use the notation
C^{k,\alpha}:=C^{k,\alpha}(h_{\mathrm{ref}})
and
\mathbb{R}_{+}:=[0,\infty)
.

Proof.

The idea of the proof is to apply Theorem
81
in local coordinates and to deduce the desired conclusion by appealing
to Lemma
79
. We therefore start by constructing appropriate local coordinates. We first apply
Lemma
74
with
(M,g)=(\Sigma,h_{\mathrm{ref}})
. Then
\{B_{r/2}(p)\}_{p\in\Sigma}
is an open covering of
\Sigma
, where
r>0
is as
in the statement of Lemma
74
. This means that there is a finite subcovering, say
\{B_{r/2}(p_{i})\}_{i=1}^{l}
. By
construction, there are local coordinates
\mathsf{x}_{i}
on
U_{i}:=B_{r}(p_{i})
for each
i
. Moreover,
(\mathsf{x}_{i},U_{i})
have the properties listed in
Lemma
74
(with a different
r>0
). Then, due to Lemma
79
, we know that, for each

i\in\{1,\dots,l\}
,

u\circ\mathsf{x}_{i}^{-1}\in C^{2,1}(\Omega_{i}),\ {}^{\mathsf{x}_{i}}h_{jm}\circ\mathsf{x}_{i}^{-1}\in C^{1,1}(\Omega_{i}),\ {}^{\mathsf{x}_{i}}X^{j}\circ\mathsf{x}_{i}^{-1},\ a\circ\mathsf{x}_{i}^{-1},\ f\circ\mathsf{x}_{i}^{-1}\in C^{0,1}(\Omega_{i}),

where
\Omega_{i}:=\mathsf{x}_{i}(B_{r}(p_{i}))
. Moreover, for
p\in B_{r}(p_{i})
and
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,

{}^{\mathsf{x}_{i}}h^{jm}(p)\xi_{j}\xi_{m}\geq\tfrac{1}{c_{1}}\,{}^{\mathsf{x}_{i}}h^{jm}_{\mathrm{ref}}(p)\xi_{j}\xi_{m}\geq\tfrac{1}{2c_{1}}|\xi|^{2},

where we used (
220
) in the first step and (
208
) in the second step. This means that we are in a
position to apply Theorem
81
. More specifically,
\Omega:=\Omega_{i}
(where
\Omega_{i}
by construction is open,
non-empty and bounded),
\alpha=1
and
a^{jm}:={}^{\mathsf{x}_{i}}h^{jm}\circ\mathsf{x}_{i}^{-1}
, so that (
219
) holds with
\lambda=1/(2c_{1})
.
In order to determine
b^{j}
, note that (
221
) can be written

{}^{\mathsf{x}_{i}}h^{jm}\partial_{\mathsf{x}^{j}_{i}}\partial_{\mathsf{x}^{m}_{i}}u-{}^{\mathsf{x}_{i}}\Gamma^{j}\partial_{\mathsf{x}^{j}_{i}}u-{}^{\mathsf{x}_{i}}X^{j}\partial_{\mathsf{x}^{j}_{i}}u-au=-f

on
B_{r}(p_{i})
, where
{}^{\mathsf{x}_{i}}\Gamma^{j}={}^{\mathsf{x}_{i}}h^{kl}\,{}^{\mathsf{x}_{i}}\Gamma^{j}_{kl}
are the contracted Christoffel symbols. In other words,
the function
u\circ\mathsf{x}_{i}^{-1}
satisfies (
218
) on
\Omega_{i}
with
a^{lm}
as above,

(223)

b^{j}:=-{}^{\mathsf{x}_{i}}\Gamma^{j}\circ\mathsf{x}_{i}^{-1}-{}^{\mathsf{x}_{i}}X^{j}\circ\mathsf{x}_{i}^{-1},

c:=-a\circ\mathsf{x}_{i}^{-1}
and
F:=-f\circ\mathsf{x}_{i}^{-1}
. Next, note that the distance from a point
p\in B_{r}(p_{i})
to
\partial B_{r}(p_{i})
(with respect to
the topological metric induced by
h_{\mathrm{ref}}
on
\Sigma
, say
d_{\mathrm{ref}}
) is bounded from above by
r
. Due to (
209
),
we conclude that if
d_{x}:=\mathrm{dist}(x,\partial\Omega_{i})
(where
\mathrm{dist}
denotes the distance with respect to the Euclidean metric on

\mathbb{R}^{n}
), then
d_{x}\leq 2r
for
x\in\Omega_{i}
. By a similar argument, if
\omega_{i}:=\mathsf{x}_{i}(B_{r/2}(p_{i}))
, then
d_{x}\geq r/4
for
x\in\omega_{i}
. Next,

|a^{jm}|_{0,1;\Omega_{i}}^{*}=|a^{jm}|_{0,0;\Omega_{i}}^{*}+[a^{jm}]_{0,1;\Omega_{i}}^{*}\leq 2c_{1}+\textstyle{\sup}_{(x,y)\in\mathscr{O}_{i}}d_{x,y}\tfrac{|a^{jm}(x)-a^{jm}(y)|}{|x-y|},

where we appealed to (
208
), (
220
) and used the notation
\mathscr{O}_{i}:=\{(x,y)\in\Omega_{i}\times\Omega_{i}\,|\,x\neq y\}
.
In order to estimate the second term on the far right hand side, let
(x,y)\in\mathscr{O}_{i}
and define, for
t\in[0,1]
,

\xi_{lm}(t):=t\cdot{}^{\mathsf{x}_{i}}h_{lm}\circ\mathsf{x}_{i}^{-1}(x)+(1-t)\cdot{}^{\mathsf{x}_{i}}h_{lm}\circ\mathsf{x}_{i}^{-1}(y).

Due to (
208
) and (
220
), the eigenvalues of the matrix with components
\xi_{lm}(t)
are bounded from below
by
1/(2c_{1})
and from above by
2c_{1}
. The matrix with components
\xi_{lm}(t)
is therefore invertible and the eigenvalues of the inverse
satisfy the same bounds. Let
\xi^{lm}(t)
denote the components of the inverse of the matrix with components
\xi_{lm}(t)
. Then

\begin{split}a^{lm}(x)-a^{lm}(y)=&\xi^{lm}(1)-\xi^{lm}(0)=-\textstyle{\int}_{0}^{1}\xi^{lj}(t)\xi^{mk}(t)\tfrac{d}{dt}\xi_{jk}(t)dt\\
=&-\textstyle{\int}_{0}^{1}\xi^{lj}(t)\xi^{mk}(t)dt[{}^{\mathsf{x}_{i}}h_{jk}\circ\mathsf{x}_{i}^{-1}(x)-{}^{\mathsf{x}_{i}}h_{jk}\circ\mathsf{x}_{i}^{-1}(y)].\end{split}

Combining the above observations with Lemma
79
and similar (but much simpler) arguments for
c
and
F
, it is clear that

|a^{jm}|_{0,1;\Omega_{i}}^{*}\leq C\|h\|_{C^{0,1}},\ \ \ |c|_{0,1;\Omega_{i}}^{(2)}\leq C\|a\|_{C^{0,1}},\ \ \ |F|_{0,1;\Omega_{i}}^{(2)}\leq C\|f\|_{C^{0,1}},

where the constants only depends on
r
and
c_{1}
. Turning to
b^{j}
introduced in (
223
), the argument concerning the second term
on the right hand side is similar to the above. Finally, consider

{}^{\mathsf{x}_{i}}\Gamma^{j}\circ\mathsf{x}_{i}^{-1}=\tfrac{1}{2}a^{kl}a^{jm}[\partial_{k}({}^{\mathsf{x}_{i}}h_{lm}\circ\mathsf{x}_{i}^{-1})+\partial_{l}({}^{\mathsf{x}_{i}}h_{km}\circ\mathsf{x}_{i}^{-1})-\partial_{m}({}^{\mathsf{x}_{i}}h_{kl}\circ\mathsf{x}_{i}^{-1})].

By arguments similar to the above, it can be verified that

|b^{j}|_{0,1;\Omega_{i}}^{(1)}\leq C\|h\|_{C^{0,1}}\|h\|_{C^{1}}+C\|h\|_{C^{1,1}}+C\|X\|_{C^{0,1}}

where the constants only depends on
r
and
c_{1}
.

By the above estimates and observations, we are allowed to apply Theorem
81
. This yields

|u\circ\mathsf{x}_{i}^{-1}|_{2,1;\Omega_{i}}^{*}\leq C(|u\circ\mathsf{x}_{i}^{-1}|_{0;\Omega_{i}}+|F|_{0,1;\Omega_{i}}^{(2)}),

where
C
only depends on
n
,
c_{1}
,
r
and continuously and monotonically increasingly (cf. Remark
82
)
on
\|h\|_{C^{1,1}}
,
\|X\|_{C^{0,1}}
and
\|a\|_{C^{0,1}}
. Next,
\|u\|_{C^{0}}\leq c_{2}\|f\|_{C^{0}}
due to the maximum principle. To summarise,

|u\circ\mathsf{x}_{i}^{-1}|_{2,1;\Omega_{i}}^{*}\leq C\|f\|_{C^{0,1}},

where
C
only depends on
n
,
c_{1}
,
c_{2}
,
r
and continuously and monotonically increasingly on
\|h\|_{C^{1,1}}
,
\|X\|_{C^{0,1}}
and

\|a\|_{C^{0,1}}
. Since
d_{x}\geq r/4
for
x\in\omega_{i}
, this estimate allows us to bound
\|u\|_{C^{2,1}(\Sigma;\mathbb{V})}
, where
\mathbb{V}:=\{V_{i}\}_{i=1}^{l}
is
given by
V_{i}:=B_{r/2}(p_{i})
; cf. (
215
). The desired estimate then follows by an application of
Lemma
79
.
∎

Appendix D
Sobolev estimates, elliptic model equation

Next, we need Sobolev estimates of solutions to the elliptic PDE’s appearing in (
3
). The estimates are essentially standard. However,
we here need detailed information concerning how the constants appearing in the estimates depend on the coefficients of the equation. The main result
of the present appendix is the estimate (
227
) below.

Theorem 85

(The elliptic estimates)
.

Let
(\Sigma,h_{\mathrm{ref}})
be a closed, connected and oriented Riemannian manifold of dimension
n
; let
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
; and let
c_{1},c_{2}>1
be real
numbers. Then there are
polynomials
\mathfrak{D}_{1}:\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
and
\mathfrak{D}_{2}:\mathbb{R}_{+}^{4}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
with non-negative coefficients; and a continuous function

\mathfrak{D}_{3}:\mathbb{R}_{+}^{3}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
which is monotonically increasing in each of its arguments. Here
\mathfrak{D}_{i}
,
i=1,2,3
, only depend on
c_{1}
,

c_{2}
,
n
,
k
and
(\Sigma,h_{\mathrm{ref}})
. Finally, let
h
and
a
be a Riemannian metric and a smooth function on
\Sigma
, respectively, such that

(224)

c_{1}^{-1}h_{\mathrm{ref}}\leq h\leq c_{1}h_{\mathrm{ref}},\ \ \ c_{2}^{-1}\leq a\leq c_{2}.

Then the following holds. First, if
f\in H^{k}
, and
u\in H^{1}
is a weak solution to

(-\Delta_{h}+a)u=f,

then
u\in H^{k+2}
. Second, for such
f
and
u
,

(225)

\left\|u\right\|_{H^{k+2}}\leq\mathfrak{D}_{1}[\left\|h\right\|_{W^{2,\infty}},\left\|a\right\|_{W^{1,\infty}}]\left(\left\|f\right\|_{H^{k}}+\left\|u\right\|_{W^{1,\infty}}\left\|h\right\|_{H^{k+1}}+\left\|u\right\|_{L^{\infty}}\left\|a\right\|_{H^{k}}\right).

Third, if, in addition to the above,
k>n/2
, then

(226)

\|u\|_{H^{k+2}}\leq\mathfrak{D}_{2}[\left\|h\right\|_{W^{2,\infty}},\left\|a\right\|_{W^{1,\infty}},\|a\|_{H^{k}},\|h\|_{H^{k+1}}]\cdot\|f\|_{H^{k}}.

Fourth, if, in addition to the above assumptions (except for the assumption that
k>n/2
),
u\in C^{2,1}
and
f\in C^{0,1}
, then

(227)

\left\|u\right\|_{H^{k+2}}\leq\mathfrak{D}_{3}[\left\|h\right\|_{W^{2,\infty}},\left\|a\right\|_{W^{1,\infty}},\left\|f\right\|_{C^{0,1}}]\cdot(\left\|f\right\|_{H^{k}}+\|h\|_{H^{k+1}}+\|a\|_{H^{k}}).

Remark 86
.

It is understood that we use the metric
h_{\mathrm{ref}}
to define all the spaces and corresponding norms.

Remark 87
.

When we say that the
\mathfrak{D}_{i}
depend on
(\Sigma,h_{\mathrm{ref}})
, we include dependence of coverings of
\Sigma
, partitions of unity, local orthonormal
frames with respect to
h_{\mathrm{ref}}
etc. constructed in the proof.

Remark 88
.

It is also useful to keep in mind that the following, more primitive, estimate holds

(228)

\|u\|_{H^{k+2}}\leq C\|Lu\|_{H^{k}},

where
C
only depends on
c_{1}
,
c_{2}
,
n
,
k
,
(\Sigma,h_{\mathrm{ref}})
, the
C^{l_{k}+1}
-norm of
h
, where
l_{k}:={\mathrm{max}}\{k,1\}
, and the
C^{l_{k}}
-norm
of
a
. This estimate is analogous to (
226
). However, it is better in the sense that we do not require
k>n/2
and it is worse
in the sense that we require control of
h
and
a
in
C^{l_{k}+1}
and
C^{l_{k}}
respectively.

The proof of Theorem
85
is to be found at the end of Subsection
D.5
and rests on several lemmas.
We begin with some basic observations.

D.1.
Basic estimates and observations

We begin by noting that the volume forms and measures associated with different Riemannian metrics are equivalent as far as estimates are concerned.

Lemma 89

(Equivalence of volume forms)
.

Let
(\Sigma,h_{\mathrm{ref}})
be a closed, connected and oriented Riemannian manifold of dimension
n
. Assume that
h
is a Riemannian metric on
\Sigma
and
that
c_{1}^{-1}h_{\mathrm{ref}}\leq h\leq c_{1}h_{\mathrm{ref}}
for some constant
c_{1}>1
. Then there is a constant
c
, depending only on
c_{1}
and
n
, such that

(229)

c^{-1}\textstyle{\int}_{\Sigma}|\phi|{\mathrm{Vol}}_{h_{\mathrm{ref}}}\leq\textstyle{\int}_{\Sigma}|\phi|{\mathrm{Vol}}_{h}\leq c\textstyle{\int}_{\Sigma}|\phi|{\mathrm{Vol}}_{h_{\mathrm{ref}}}

for all
\phi\in C(M)
. Similarly, if
\phi\in L^{1}(h_{\mathrm{ref}})
, then
\phi\in L^{1}(h)
and

(230)

c^{-1}\textstyle{\int}_{\Sigma}|\phi|d\mu_{h_{\mathrm{ref}}}\leq\textstyle{\int}_{\Sigma}|\phi|d\mu_{h}\leq c\textstyle{\int}_{\Sigma}|\phi|d\mu_{h_{\mathrm{ref}}}.

Proof.

Using a finite partition of unity, it is sufficient to prove (
229
) for functions

\phi
with support in a coordinate neighbourhood, say
U
. Let
\{E_{i}\}
be an orthonormal frame on
U
with respect to
h_{\mathrm{ref}}
. In
order to prove the second inequality in (
229
), it is sufficient to prove that the determinant of

h_{ij}:=h(E_{i},E_{j})
is bounded by the determinant of
h_{\mathrm{ref}}(E_{i},E_{j})=\delta_{ij}
(i.e.,
1
) times a factor that only depends on
c_{1}
and

n
. However,

\displaystyle|\det(h_{ij})|

\displaystyle=\left|\textstyle{\sum}_{\sigma\in S_{n}}\mathrm{sgn}(\sigma)\prod_{i=1}^{n}h_{i\sigma(i)}\right|\leq n!\cdot{\mathrm{max}}_{i,j=1,\ldots,n}|h_{ij}|^{n}\leq n!\cdot c_{1}^{n}.

The first inequality in (
229
) is proven similarly. The estimate (
230
) follows from
(
200
), (
229
) and a density argument.
∎

Lemma 90

(Equivalence of Sobolev norms)
.

Let
(\Sigma,h_{\mathrm{ref}})
be a closed, connected and oriented Riemannian manifold of dimension
n
. Assume that
h
is a Riemannian metric on
\Sigma

satisfying
c_{1}^{-1}h_{\mathrm{ref}}\leq h\leq c_{1}h_{\mathrm{ref}}
for some constant
c_{1}>1
. Then there is a constant
c>1
, depending only on

c_{1}
and
n
, such that

(231)

c^{-1}\left\|u\right\|_{H^{1}(h_{\mathrm{ref}})}\leq\left\|u\right\|_{H^{1}(h)}\leq c\left\|u\right\|_{H^{1}(h_{\mathrm{ref}})}

for all
u\in H^{1}(h)
.

Proof.

Due to Lemma
89
and the assumptions, there is a constant
c>1
, depending only on
c_{1}
and
n
, such that

\displaystyle c^{-1}\left\|u\right\|_{H^{1}(h_{\mathrm{ref}})}^{2}\leq

\displaystyle\textstyle{\int}_{\Sigma}[h^{ij}\nabla_{i}^{h}u\nabla^{h}_{j}u+u^{2}]{\mathrm{Vol}}_{h}\leq c\left\|u\right\|_{H^{1}(h_{\mathrm{ref}})}^{2},

where
\nabla^{h}
denotes the Levi-Civita connection of
(\Sigma,h)
. This proves (
231
) for
u\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma)
. Thus
(
231
) holds for
u\in H^{1}(h)
since
C^{\infty}(\Sigma)
is dense in
H^{1}(h)
.
∎

Lemma 91

(The Laplace operator)
.

Let
(\Sigma,h_{\mathrm{ref}})
be a Riemannian manifold and let
\{E_{i}\}
be a local orthonormal frame, defined on an open subset

U\subseteq\Sigma
. For a second Riemannian metric
h
on
\Sigma
and
u\in C^{\infty}(U)
, the Laplace operator is given by

(232)

\Delta_{h}u=h^{ij}E_{i}E_{j}u+\left(E_{i}h^{ik}\right)E_{k}u+\tfrac{1}{2}h^{ij}(E_{l}h_{ij})h^{lk}E_{k}u+h^{ij}\lambda_{ki}^{k}E_{j}u,

where
h_{ij}:=h(E_{i},E_{j})
;
h^{ij}
are the components of the inverse of the matrix with components
h_{ij}
; and

\lambda_{ij}^{k}
are defined by
[E_{i},E_{j}]=\lambda_{ij}^{k}E_{k}
.

Remark 92
.

In the applications of this observation, it is convenient to note that

(233)

h^{ij}E_{i}E_{j}u+\left(E_{i}h^{ik}\right)E_{k}u=E_{i}(h^{ij}E_{j}u).

Proof.

Note that
-\Delta_{h}=-h^{ij}(\nabla^{h})^{2}_{E_{i},E_{j}}=-h^{ij}E_{i}E_{j}+h^{ij}\nabla^{h}_{E_{i}}E_{j}
.
Moreover,

\displaystyle 2h(\nabla^{h}_{E_{i}}E_{j},E_{l})=

\displaystyle E_{i}h_{jl}+E_{j}h_{il}-E_{l}h_{ij}-\lambda_{ji}^{m}h_{ml}-\lambda_{il}^{m}h_{mj}-\lambda_{jl}^{m}h_{mi},

due to the Koszul formula. Next,
\nabla^{h}_{E_{i}}E_{j}=h(\nabla^{h}_{E_{i}}E_{j},E_{l})h^{lk}E_{k}
. Thus

\displaystyle-\Delta_{h}=

\displaystyle-h^{ij}E_{i}E_{j}+\tfrac{1}{2}h^{ij}\left(2E_{i}h_{jl}-E_{l}h_{ij}\right)h^{lk}E_{k}-\tfrac{1}{2}h^{ij}\left(\lambda_{il}^{m}h_{mj}+\lambda_{jl}^{m}h_{mi}\right)h^{lk}E_{k}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-h^{ij}E_{i}E_{j}-\left(E_{i}h^{ik}\right)E_{k}-\tfrac{1}{2}h^{ij}(E_{l}h_{ij})h^{lk}E_{k}-h^{lk}\lambda_{ml}^{m}E_{k}.

This concludes the proof.
∎

D.2.
The space
H^{-1}

Next we consider the space
H^{-1}(h):=\left(H^{1}(h)\right)^{*}
with norm

(234)

\left\|\lambda\right\|_{H^{-1}(h)}:=\sup\Big\{\tfrac{|\lambda(v)|}{\left\|v\right\|_{H^{1}(h)}}\,\Big|\,v\in H^{1}(h),\ v\neq 0\Big\}.

There is a natural embedding
\iota_{h}:L^{2}(h)\hookrightarrow H^{-1}(h)
defined by

\iota_{h}[u](v):=\textstyle{\int}_{\Sigma}vud\mu_{h}.

If
X
is a smooth vector field on
\Sigma
and
u\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma)
, it is of interest to estimate

(235)

\|\iota_{h}[Xu]\|_{H^{-1}(h)}=\sup\Big\{\tfrac{|\iota_{h}[Xu](v)|}{\left\|v\right\|_{H^{1}(h)}}\,|\,v\in H^{1}(h),\ v\neq 0\Big\}.

However, assuming
v
to be
C^{1}
(cf. (
245
) below),

(236)

\iota_{h}[Xu](v)=\textstyle{\int}_{\Sigma}vXu{\mathrm{Vol}}_{h}=-\textstyle{\int}_{\Sigma}vu(\mathrm{div}_{h}X){\mathrm{Vol}}_{h}-\textstyle{\int}_{\Sigma}(Xv)u{\mathrm{Vol}}_{h}.

Due to this equality we can define the left hand side via the far right hand side, given
u\in L^{2}(h)
,
v\in H^{1}(h)
and
a
C^{1}
-vector field
X
. We therefore, for
u\in L^{2}(h)
and a
C^{1}
-vector field
X
define
X[u]\in H^{-1}(h)
by

(237)

X[u](v):=-\textstyle{\int}_{\Sigma}vu(\mathrm{div}_{h}X)d\mu_{h}-\textstyle{\int}_{\Sigma}(Xv)ud\mu_{h}.

This means that

(238)

\begin{split}|X[u](v)|\leq&\|\mathrm{div}_{h}X\|_{C^{0}(h)}\|v\|_{L^{2}(h)}\|u\|_{L^{2}(h)}+\|Xv\|_{L^{2}(h)}\|u\|_{L^{2}(h)}\\
\leq&[\|\mathrm{div}_{h}X\|_{C^{0}(h)}^{2}+\|X\|_{C^{0}(h)}^{2}]^{1/2}\|v\|_{H^{1}(h)}\|u\|_{L^{2}(h)},\end{split}

where we use the notation introduced in (
205
). Combining (
238
) with (
234
) yields

(239)

\|X[u]\|_{H^{-1}(h)}\leq[\|\mathrm{div}_{h}X\|_{C^{0}(h)}^{2}+\|X\|_{C^{0}(h)}^{2}]^{1/2}\|u\|_{L^{2}(h)}.

Combining the above observations yields the conclusion that, given a
C^{1}
vector field
X
and an
L^{2}
-function
u
, we can think of

Xu
as an element of
H^{-1}(h)
.

D.3.
Estimating elliptic second order operators in
H^{-1}

Next, it is of interest to note that, given
u\in H^{1}(h)
and
L:=-\Delta_{h}+a
,
Lu
can be interpreted as an element of
H^{-1}(h)
. Assume, to this end,
first that
u
and
v
are smooth functions on
\Sigma
. Then

(240)

\textstyle{\int}_{\Sigma}vLu{\mathrm{Vol}}_{h}=\textstyle{\int}_{\Sigma}[h(\mathrm{grad}_{h}v,\mathrm{grad}_{h}u)+avu]{\mathrm{Vol}}_{h}=\textstyle{\int}_{\Sigma}[du(\mathrm{grad}_{h}v)+avu]{\mathrm{Vol}}_{h}.

In particular, it is clear that the right hand side of (
240
) is meaningful for

u,v\in H^{1}(h)
. Given
u\in H^{1}(h)
, we can therefore define
L[u]\in H^{-1}(h)
by

L[u](v):=\textstyle{\int}_{\Sigma}[h(\mathrm{grad}_{h}v,\mathrm{grad}_{h}u)+avu]d\mu_{h}.

Assume that there is a constant
c_{2}>1
such that
c_{2}^{-1}\leq a\leq c_{2}
on
\Sigma
. Then

c_{2}^{-1}\|u\|_{H^{1}(h)}^{2}\leq L[u](u)\leq\|L[u]\|_{H^{-1}(h)}\|u\|_{H^{1}(h)},

so that

\|u\|_{H^{1}(h)}\leq c_{2}\|L[u]\|_{H^{-1}(h)}.

Note, however, that
L[u]
depends on
h
in two ways. First,
\Delta_{h}
is of course the Laplace operator associated with
h
. However, there
is a second dependence on
h
arising from the choice of the volume form
{\mathrm{Vol}}_{h}
in (
240
).
The second dependence on
h
is, in a sense, artificial. One could also consider, for
u,v\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma)
,

\begin{split}\textstyle{\int}_{\Sigma}vLu{\mathrm{Vol}}_{h_{\mathrm{ref}}}=&\textstyle{\int}_{\Sigma}[h(\mathrm{grad}_{h}(\psi_{h}v),\mathrm{grad}_{h}u)+a\psi_{h}vu]{\mathrm{Vol}}_{h}\\
=&\textstyle{\int}_{\Sigma}[\psi_{h}^{-1}h(\mathrm{grad}_{h}(\psi_{h}v),\mathrm{grad}_{h}u)+avu]{\mathrm{Vol}}_{h_{\mathrm{ref}}},\end{split}

where
\psi_{h}
is defined by the condition that
{\mathrm{Vol}}_{h_{\mathrm{ref}}}=\psi_{h}{\mathrm{Vol}}_{h}
. Given
u,v\in H^{1}(h_{\mathrm{ref}})
, we can thus define

\begin{split}L_{\mathrm{ref}}[u](v):=&\textstyle{\int}_{\Sigma}[\psi_{h}^{-1}h(\mathrm{grad}_{h}(\psi_{h}v),\mathrm{grad}_{h}u)+avu]d\mu_{h_{\mathrm{ref}}}\\
=&\textstyle{\int}_{\Sigma}[du(\psi_{h}^{-1}\mathrm{grad}_{h}(\psi_{h}v))+avu]d\mu_{h_{\mathrm{ref}}}.\end{split}

Thus

\begin{split}|L_{\mathrm{ref}}[u](v)|\leq&\|\nabla^{h_{\mathrm{ref}}}u\|_{L^{2}(h_{\mathrm{ref}})}\|\psi_{h}^{-1}\mathrm{grad}_{h}(\psi_{h}v)\|_{L^{2}(h_{\mathrm{ref}})}+c_{2}\|v\|_{L^{2}(h_{\mathrm{ref}})}\|u\|_{L^{2}(h_{\mathrm{ref}})}\\
\leq&c_{1}\|\nabla^{h_{\mathrm{ref}}}u\|_{L^{2}(h_{\mathrm{ref}})}\|\psi_{h}^{-1}\mathrm{grad}_{h_{\mathrm{ref}}}(\psi_{h}v)\|_{L^{2}(h_{\mathrm{ref}})}+c_{2}\|v\|_{L^{2}(h_{\mathrm{ref}})}\|u\|_{L^{2}(h_{\mathrm{ref}})}\\
\leq&C\|h\|_{W^{1,\infty}(h_{\mathrm{ref}})}\|v\|_{H^{1}(h_{\mathrm{ref}})}\|u\|_{H^{1}(h_{\mathrm{ref}})},\end{split}

where
C
only depends on
c_{1}
,
c_{2}
and
n
. This means that

(241)

\|L_{\mathrm{ref}}[u]\|_{H^{-1}(h_{\mathrm{ref}})}\leq C\|h\|_{W^{1,\infty}(h_{\mathrm{ref}})}\|u\|_{H^{1}(h_{\mathrm{ref}})}.

Next, note that

(242)

\begin{split}L_{\mathrm{ref}}[u](\psi_{h}^{-1}u)=&\textstyle{\int}_{\Sigma}[\psi_{h}^{-1}h(\mathrm{grad}_{h}u,\mathrm{grad}_{h}u)+\psi_{h}^{-1}au^{2}]d\mu_{h_{\mathrm{ref}}}.\end{split}

There are thus constants
C
, depending only on
n
,
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
such that

\begin{split}\|u\|_{H^{1}(h_{\mathrm{ref}})}^{2}\leq&CL_{\mathrm{ref}}[u](\psi_{h}^{-1}u)\leq C\|L_{\mathrm{ref}}[u]\|_{H^{-1}(h_{\mathrm{ref}})}\|\psi_{h}^{-1}u\|_{H^{1}(h_{\mathrm{ref}})}\\
\leq&C\|h\|_{W^{1,\infty}(h_{\mathrm{ref}})}\|L_{\mathrm{ref}}[u]\|_{H^{-1}(h_{\mathrm{ref}})}\|u\|_{H^{1}(h_{\mathrm{ref}})}.\end{split}

Thus

(243)

\|u\|_{H^{1}(h_{\mathrm{ref}})}\leq C\|h\|_{W^{1,\infty}(h_{\mathrm{ref}})}\|L_{\mathrm{ref}}[u]\|_{H^{-1}(h_{\mathrm{ref}})},

where
C
is a constant depending only on
n
,
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
.

D.4.
Elliptic regularity

Next, we recall elliptic regularity.

Lemma 93
.

Let
(\Sigma,h)
be a closed, connected and oriented Riemannian manifold,
a\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma)
and
L:=-\Delta_{h}+a
. Assume
u\in H^{1}(h)
to be such
that
L[u]=\iota_{h}[f]
, where
f\in H^{k}(h)
and
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. Then
u\in H^{k+2}(h)
. Moreover,
Lu=f
a.e.

Remark 94
.

In this lemma, we do not assume
a
to be strictly positive. Moreover, analogous conclusions hold in case
u
satisfies

L[u]+X[u]=\iota_{h}[f]
for some smooth vector field
X
.

Proof.

Let
(\mathsf{x},U)
be local coordinates. Let
\zeta\in C^{\infty}_{c}(U)
and consider
L[\zeta u]
. Given
v\in H^{1}(h)
,

(244)

\begin{split}L[\zeta u](v)-L[u](\zeta v)=&\textstyle{\int}_{\Sigma}[d(\zeta u)(\mathrm{grad}_{h}v)-du(\mathrm{grad}_{h}(\zeta v))]d\mu_{h}\end{split}

Let
f_{1}:=-du(\mathrm{grad}_{h}\zeta)
. Then
f_{1}\in L^{2}(h)
and (
244
) can be written

L[\zeta u](v)-L[u](\zeta v)=\iota_{h}[f_{1}](v)+\textstyle{\int}_{\Sigma}ud\zeta(\mathrm{grad}_{h}v)d\mu_{h}.

In order to rewrite the second term on the right hand side, note that

\begin{split}ud\zeta(\mathrm{grad}_{h}v)=&uh(\mathrm{grad}_{h}\zeta,\mathrm{grad}_{h}v)=udv(\mathrm{grad}_{h}\zeta)\\
=&u(\mathrm{grad}_{h}\zeta)(v)=(\mathrm{grad}_{h}\zeta)(uv)-v(\mathrm{grad}_{h}\zeta)(u).\end{split}

On the other hand, due to Cartan’s magic formula (i.e.,
\mathcal{L}_{X}=d\circ\iota_{X}+\iota_{X}\circ d
), Stokes’ theorem and
the fact that
\mathcal{L}_{X}{\mathrm{Vol}}_{h}=(\mathrm{div}_{h}X){\mathrm{Vol}}_{h}
,

(245)

\textstyle{\int}_{\Sigma}X(\psi){\mathrm{Vol}}_{h}=-\textstyle{\int}_{\Sigma}\psi(\mathrm{div}_{h}X){\mathrm{Vol}}_{h}

for all
\psi\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma)
. In particular, we thus have

\textstyle{\int}_{\Sigma}(\mathrm{grad}_{h}\zeta)(uv)d\mu_{h}=-\textstyle{\int}_{\Sigma}uv\cdot\Delta_{h}\zeta d\mu_{h}.

Introducing

f_{2}:=-2du(\mathrm{grad}_{h}\zeta)-u\Delta_{h}\zeta,

it follows that

L[\zeta u](v)=L[u](\zeta v)+\iota_{h}[f_{2}](v)=\iota_{h}[f](\zeta v)+\iota_{h}[f_{2}](v)=\iota_{h}[\zeta f+f_{2}](v).

Due to this equality, which holds for all
v\in H^{1}(h)
, it follows that
(\zeta u)\circ\mathsf{x}^{-1}
is a weak solution
to an elliptic problem on
\mathsf{x}(U)
. Moreover, this weak solution belongs to
H^{1}(\mathsf{x}(U))
. Due to
[
8
, Theorem 2, p. 332]
,
it follows that
(\zeta u)\circ\mathsf{x}^{-1}\in H^{2}(\mathsf{x}(U))
. Thus
\zeta u\in H^{2}(h)
. Combining this observation with a partition
of unity, it follows that
u\in H^{2}(h)
, so that
Lu
is well defined and equals
f
. If
k\geq 1
, we can iterate this argument
in order to obtain the desired conclusion.
∎

D.5.
Local frames and commutators

Let
(\Sigma,h_{\mathrm{ref}})
be a closed, connected and oriented Riemannian manifold and let
\{E_{i}\}
be a local orthonormal frame with respect to

h_{\mathrm{ref}}
, defined on an open subset
U\subseteq\Sigma
as in the statement of Lemma
91
. Fix a
\zeta\in C^{\infty}_{c}(U)
and
a compact set
K\subset U
such that
\mathrm{supp}\zeta\subset K
. Assume, moreover, that
0\leq\zeta\leq 1
. Then

E_{\zeta,i}:=\zeta E_{i}
is a smooth vector field on
\Sigma
and
\|E_{\zeta,i}\|_{C^{0}(h_{\mathrm{ref}})}\leq 1
. Moreover,

(246)

\mathrm{div}_{h_{\mathrm{ref}}}E_{\zeta,i}=\textstyle{\sum}_{k}h_{\mathrm{ref}}(\nabla^{h_{\mathrm{ref}}}_{E_{k}}E_{\zeta,i},E_{k})=\textstyle{\sum}_{k}h_{\mathrm{ref}}([E_{k},E_{\zeta,i}],E_{k})=E_{i}(\zeta)+\sum_{k}\zeta\lambda^{k}_{ki},

where the structure coefficients
\lambda_{ij}^{k}
are defined by
[E_{i},E_{j}]=\lambda_{ij}^{k}E_{k}
. Combining these observations with
(
239
) (in which we replace
h
by
h_{\mathrm{ref}}
) yields

(247)

\|E_{\zeta,i}[u]\|_{H^{-1}(h_{\mathrm{ref}})}\leq C\|u\|_{L^{2}(h_{\mathrm{ref}})},

where
C
only depends on the frame
\{E_{i}\}
,
n
,
\zeta
and
K
.

Lemma 95

(
H^{-1}
-bounds on the commutator)
.

Let
(\Sigma,h_{\mathrm{ref}})
be a closed, connected and oriented Riemannian manifold of dimension
n
. Let
U
,
\{E_{i}\}
,
\zeta
and
K
be as
described prior to the statement of the lemma. Assume
h
to be a Riemannian metric on
\Sigma
such that
c_{1}^{-1}h_{\mathrm{ref}}\leq h\leq c_{1}h_{\mathrm{ref}}

for some constant
c_{1}>1
. Then there is a constant
C
, depending only on
c_{1}
,
n
,
\{E_{i}\}
,
\zeta
and
K
, such that

(248)

\begin{split}\|L_{\mathrm{ref}}[E_{\zeta,i}u]-E_{\zeta,i}[Lu]\|_{H^{-1}}\leq&C\big(\left\|h\right\|_{W^{2,\infty}}+\left\|h\right\|_{W^{1,\infty}}^{2}+\|a\|_{W^{1,\infty}}\big)\left\|u\right\|_{H^{1}}\end{split}

for all
u\in H^{2}
.

Remark 96
.

In the lemma, it is understood that
L=-\Delta_{h}+a
, where
a\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma)
, and that we use the metric
h_{\mathrm{ref}}
to define all the spaces
and corresponding norms. Moreover, in the definition of
E_{\zeta,i}[Lu]
, we take it for granted that
h
is replaced by
h_{\mathrm{ref}}
on the right hand
side of (
237
).

Proof.

Compute, for
u,v\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma)
,

(249)

\begin{split}L_{\mathrm{ref}}[E_{\zeta,i}u](v)-E_{\zeta,i}[Lu](v)=&\textstyle{\int}_{\Sigma}(LE_{\zeta,i}u-E_{\zeta,i}Lu)vd\mu_{h_{\mathrm{ref}}}.\end{split}

On the other hand

LE_{\zeta,i}u-E_{\zeta,i}Lu=E_{\zeta,i}\Delta_{h}u-\Delta_{h}E_{\zeta,i}u-E_{\zeta,i}(a)u.

The contribution to (
249
) arising from the last term on the right hand side can be estimated by

C\|a\|_{W^{1,\infty}}\left\|u\right\|_{L^{2}}\left\|v\right\|_{L^{2}},

where
C
is numerical; again, all norms are defined using
h_{\mathrm{ref}}
. Next, we focus on
[E_{\zeta,i},\Delta_{h}]
. Let us begin by considering the
commutator with the right hand side of (
233
):

(250)

\begin{split}&E_{\zeta,i}[E_{l}(h^{lj}E_{j}u)]-E_{l}(h^{lj}E_{j}E_{\zeta,i}u)\\
=&\zeta\lambda_{il}^{m}E_{m}(h^{lj}E_{j}u)+\zeta E_{l}(E_{i}(h^{lj}E_{j}u))-E_{l}(h^{lj}E_{j}E_{\zeta,i}u)\\
=&\zeta\lambda_{il}^{m}E_{m}(h^{lj}E_{j}u)+\zeta E_{l}(E_{i}(h^{lj})E_{j}u+h^{lj}[E_{i},E_{j}]u)+\zeta E_{l}(h^{lj}E_{j}E_{i}u)-E_{l}(h^{lj}E_{j}E_{\zeta,i}u).\end{split}

The first two terms on the far right hand side can be written as a sum of terms of the form
\varphi E_{l}(\psi E_{m}u)
. Here, and in what
follows, we assume that either
\mathrm{supp}\varphi\subset K
or
\mathrm{supp}\psi\subset K
. The corresponding contribution to (
249
) is

\begin{split}\textstyle{\int}_{\Sigma}\varphi E_{l}(\psi E_{m}u)vd\mu_{h_{\mathrm{ref}}}=&\textstyle{\int}_{\Sigma}E_{l}(v\varphi\psi E_{m}u)d\mu_{h_{\mathrm{ref}}}-\textstyle{\int}_{\Sigma}\psi E_{m}u\cdot E_{l}(\varphi v)d\mu_{h_{\mathrm{ref}}}.\end{split}

Recalling (
245
) (with
h
replaced by
h_{\mathrm{ref}}
) and (
246
) (with
\zeta=1
), it follows that

(251)

\big|\textstyle{\int}_{\Sigma}\varphi E_{l}(\psi E_{m}u)vd\mu_{h_{\mathrm{ref}}}\big|\leq C\|\varphi\|_{W^{1,\infty}}\|\psi\|_{L^{\infty}}\|u\|_{H^{1}}\|v\|_{H^{1}},

where
C
only depends on
\{E_{i}\}
and
K
. The contribution of the first two terms on the far right hand side of
(
250
) to the left hand side of (
249
) can thus be estimated by

(252)

C\|h\|_{W^{1,\infty}}\|u\|_{H^{1}}\|v\|_{H^{1}},

where
C
only depends on
c_{1}
,
n
,
\{E_{i}\}
,
\zeta
and
K
; here we used the fact that
|h^{ij}|\leq c_{1}
and the fact that

\|h\|_{L^{\infty}}
is bounded from below by a constant depending only on
c_{1}
and
n
. Next, the last two terms on the far right
hand side of
(
250
) can be written

-E_{l}(\zeta)h^{lj}E_{j}E_{i}u-E_{l}(h^{lj}E_{j}(\zeta)E_{i}u).

The corresponding contribution to the left hand side of (
249
) can be estimated by appealing to
(
251
). This results in terms of the form (
252
).

Next, consider the last term on the right hand side of (
232
). The corresponding commutator is

-h^{lj}\lambda_{kl}^{k}E_{j}E_{\zeta,i}u+E_{\zeta,i}(h^{lj}\lambda_{kl}^{k}E_{j}u).

These terms are of the same form as ones we have previously handled, and the resulting contribution to the estimate is
the same. Finally, we need to consider

(253)

\begin{split}&-\tfrac{1}{2}h^{kj}(E_{l}h_{kj})h^{lm}E_{m}E_{\zeta,i}u+\tfrac{1}{2}E_{\zeta,i}(h^{kj}(E_{l}h_{kj})h^{lm}E_{m}u)\\
=&\tfrac{1}{2}h^{kj}(E_{l}h_{kj})h^{lm}[E_{\zeta,i},E_{m}]u+\tfrac{1}{2}E_{\zeta,i}(h^{kj}(E_{l}h_{kj})h^{lm})E_{m}u.\end{split}

The contribution of both of these terms can be estimated directly. Combining the above observations yields the
estimate

\begin{split}&|L_{\mathrm{ref}}[E_{\zeta,i}u](v)-E_{\zeta,i}[Lu](v)|\\
\leq&C\big(\left\|h\right\|_{W^{2,\infty}}+\left\|h\right\|_{W^{1,\infty}}^{2}+\|a\|_{W^{1,\infty}}\big)\left\|u\right\|_{H^{1}}\left\|v\right\|_{H^{1}},\end{split}

where
C
only depends on
c_{1}
,
n
,
\{E_{i}\}
,
\zeta
and
K
. By density and the definition of the
H^{-1}
-norm,
the desired conclusion follows.
∎

Proposition 97
.

Let
(\Sigma,h_{\mathrm{ref}})
be a closed, connected and oriented Riemannian manifold of dimension
n
. Assume that
h
is a Riemannian metric on
\Sigma

and that
a
is a smooth function on
\Sigma
, such that
c_{1}^{-1}h_{\mathrm{ref}}\leq h\leq c_{1}h_{\mathrm{ref}}
and
c_{2}^{-1}\leq a\leq c_{2}
for some constants

c_{1},c_{2}>1
. Let
L:=-\Delta_{h}+a
. Assume
u\in H^{1}(h)
and
f\in L^{2}(h)
to be such that
L[u]=\iota_{h}[f]
. Then

u\in H^{2}(h)
and
Lu=f
a.e. Next, fix a finite partition of unity subordinate to a finite cover of
\Sigma
by coordinate neighbourhoods. Then
there is a constant
C
, depending only on
n
,
c_{1}
,
c_{2}
, the cover and the partition of unity such that

(254)

\left\|u\right\|_{H^{2}}\leq C\left\|h\right\|_{W^{1,\infty}}\big(\left\|h\right\|_{W^{1,\infty}}^{2}+\left\|h\right\|_{W^{2,\infty}}+\left\|a\right\|_{W^{1,\infty}}\big)\left\|Lu\right\|_{L^{2}},

where all the norms are defined using
h_{\mathrm{ref}}
.

Remark 98
.

In (
254
), it is sufficient to assume
u
to be
H^{2}
,
h
to be
C^{2}
and
a
to be
C^{1}
. In order to see this, it is
sufficient to take a
sequence of smooth metrics
h_{j}
converging to
h
in
C^{2}
; and a sequence of smooth functions
a_{j}
converging to
a
in
C^{1}
. Then
(
254
) applies with
h=h_{j}
;
a=a_{j}
; and
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
replaced by
2c_{1}
and
2c_{2}
respectively. Taking the
limit of this estimate yields the desired conclusion.

Proof.

The statements concerning the regularity of
u
follow from the assumptions and Lemma
93
. In order to prove
(
254
), we recall (
243
). Moreover, from now on, we drop all reference to
h_{\mathrm{ref}}
since all the
norms in the present proof are calculated with respect to
h_{\mathrm{ref}}
. Let
U
,
\{E_{i}\}
,
\zeta
,
E_{\zeta,i}
and
K
be as described at the
beginning of Subsection
D.5
. Applying (
243
) with
u
replaced by
E_{\zeta,i}u
yields

(255)

\begin{split}\left\|E_{\zeta,i}u\right\|_{H^{1}}\leq&C\|h\|_{W^{1,\infty}}\left\|E_{\zeta,i}[Lu]-L_{\mathrm{ref}}[E_{\zeta,i}u]\right\|_{H^{-1}}+C\|h\|_{W^{1,\infty}}\left\|E_{\zeta,i}[Lu]\right\|_{H^{-1}}.\end{split}

Combining this estimate with (
247
) and (
248
) yields

(256)

\begin{split}\left\|E_{\zeta,i}u\right\|_{H^{1}}\leq&C\|h\|_{W^{1,\infty}}\big(\left\|h\right\|_{W^{2,\infty}}+\left\|h\right\|_{W^{1,\infty}}^{2}+\|a\|_{W^{1,\infty}}\big)\left\|u\right\|_{H^{1}}\\
&+C\|h\|_{W^{1,\infty}}\left\|Lu\right\|_{L^{2}},\end{split}

where
C
only depends on
c_{1}
,
c_{2}
,
n
,
\{E_{i}\}
,
\zeta
and
K
. On the other hand, due to Lemma
90
,
(
240
) and Lemma
89
,

(257)

\|u\|_{H^{1}}^{2}\leq C\|u\|_{H^{1}(h)}^{2}\leq C\|Lu\|_{L^{2}}\|u\|_{L^{2}}\leq C\|Lu\|_{L^{2}}\|u\|_{H^{1}},

where
C
only depends on
c_{1}
,
c_{2}
and
n
. Combining (
256
) and (
257
) with a finite partition of unity
subordinate to a finite cover of
\Sigma
by coordinate neighbourhoods (on the coordinate neighbourhoods, we can use the Gram-Schmidt procedure
to construct an orthonormal frame using the coordinate vector fields) yields the desired conclusion.
∎

Lemma 99

(
L^{2}
-bounds on higher commutators)
.

Let
(\Sigma,h_{\mathrm{ref}})
be a closed, connected and oriented Riemannian manifold of dimension
n
. Let
U
,
\{E_{i}\}
,
\zeta
and
K
be as described
at the beginning of Subsection
D.5
. Let
\eta\in C^{\infty}_{c}(U)
be such that
0\leq\eta\leq 1
and such that
\eta(x)=1
for
all
x\in\mathrm{supp}\zeta
. Assume
h
to be a Riemannian metric on
\Sigma
and
a
to be a smooth function on
\Sigma
, such that

c_{1}^{-1}h_{\mathrm{ref}}\leq h\leq c_{1}h_{\mathrm{ref}}
and
c_{2}^{-1}\leq a\leq c_{2}
for some constants
c_{1},c_{2}>1
. Let
L:=-\Delta_{h}+a
. Then there is a
constant
C
, depending only on
c_{1}
,
c_{2}
,
n
,
k
,
\{E_{i}\}
,
\zeta
and
\eta
, such that if
|\mathbf{I}|\leq k
, then, using the notation
introduced in Subsection
A.7
,

(258)

\left\|[L,E_{\zeta,\mathbf{I}}]u\right\|_{L^{2}}\leq C\left(\left\|h\right\|_{W^{1,\infty}}\left\|u\right\|_{H^{k+1}}+\left\|h\right\|_{W^{1,\infty}}\left\|u\right\|_{W^{1,\infty}}\left\|h\right\|_{H^{k+1}}+\left\|u\right\|_{L^{\infty}}\left\|a\right\|_{H^{k}}\right)

for all
u\in H^{k+2}
, where all spaces and norms are defined with respect to
h_{\mathrm{ref}}
.

Remark 100
.

In some situations, it is useful to keep the following simpler estimate in mind:

(259)

\left\|[L,E_{\zeta,\mathbf{I}}]u\right\|_{L^{2}}\leq C\left\|u\right\|_{H^{k+1}}

for all
u\in H^{k+2}
, where all spaces and norms are defined with respect to
h_{\mathrm{ref}}
. Moreover, the constant
C
only depends on
c_{1}
,
c_{2}
,

n
,
k
,
\{E_{i}\}
,
\zeta
,
\eta
, the
C^{k+1}
-norm of
h
and the
C^{k}
norm of
a
.

Proof.

Combining (
232
) and (
233
) yields

(260)

\Delta_{h}u=E_{i}(h^{ij}E_{j}u)+\tfrac{1}{2}h^{ij}(E_{l}h_{ij})h^{lm}E_{m}u+h^{ij}\lambda_{mi}^{m}E_{j}u.

In order to estimate
[-\Delta_{h},E_{\zeta,\mathbf{I}}]u
, it is convenient to estimate the contribution from each of the terms on the right hand side of
(
260
). To begin with, we need to estimate

(261)

-E_{i}(h^{ij}E_{j}E_{\zeta,\mathbf{I}}u)+E_{\zeta,\mathbf{I}}E_{i}(h^{ij}E_{j}u)=[E_{\zeta,\mathbf{I}},E_{i}](h^{ij}E_{j}u)+E_{i}(E_{\zeta,\mathbf{I}}(h^{ij}E_{j}u)-h^{ij}E_{j}E_{\zeta,\mathbf{I}}u).

We leave it to the reader to verify that

[E_{\zeta,\mathbf{I}},E_{i}]=\textstyle{\sum}_{|\mathbf{J}|\leq|\mathbf{I}|}f_{\mathbf{I},i}^{\mathbf{J}}E_{\mathbf{J}}=\textstyle{\sum}_{|\mathbf{J}|\leq|\mathbf{I}|}f_{\mathbf{I},i}^{\mathbf{J}}E_{\eta,\mathbf{J}}

for some functions
f_{\mathbf{I},i}^{\mathbf{J}}
vanishing outside of the support of
\zeta
(a property which justifies the last equality) and depending only on
the frame and
\zeta
(including derivatives of
\zeta
). In order to estimate the first term on the right hand side of (
261
), it is thus
sufficient to estimate expressions of the form

\|E_{\eta,\mathbf{J}_{1}}h_{k_{1}l_{1}}\cdots E_{\eta,\mathbf{J}_{m}}h_{k_{m}l_{m}}\cdot E_{\eta,\mathbf{J}_{0}}E_{\eta,j}u\|_{L^{2}},

where
|\mathbf{J}_{0}|+\dots+|\mathbf{J}_{m}|\leq|\mathbf{I}|
and we used the fact that
|h^{ij}|
can be estimated by
c_{1}
. Appealing to (
203
), we
conclude that the first term on the right hand side of (
261
) can be estimated by

C\|u\|_{H^{k+1}}+C\|u\|_{W^{1,\infty}}\|h\|_{H^{k}},

where
C
only depends on
c_{1}
,
n
,
k
,
\{E_{i}\}

\zeta
and
\eta
, where we used the fact that
|h_{ij}|\leq c_{1}
. It can also be estimated
by the right hand side of (
259
). Expanding the second term on the right hand side of (
261
) yields two types of terms:

E_{i}(E_{\zeta,\mathbf{J}}(h^{ij})E_{\zeta,\mathbf{K}}E_{j}u),\ \ \ E_{i}(h^{ij}[E_{\zeta,\mathbf{I}},E_{j}]u),

where
0<|\mathbf{J}|\leq|\mathbf{I}|
and
|\mathbf{J}|+|\mathbf{K}|=|\mathbf{I}|
in the first expression. Due to (
203
) and arguments similar to the above, these
expressions can, in
L^{2}
, be estimated by

(262)

C\|h\|_{W^{1,\infty}}(\|u\|_{H^{k+1}}+\|u\|_{W^{1,\infty}}\|h\|_{H^{k+1}}),

where
C
only depends on
c_{1}
,
n
,
k
,
\{E_{i}\}

\zeta
and
\eta
. They can also be estimated by the right hand side of
(
259
). Next, we need to consider the commutator arising from the second term on the right hand side of
(
260
). In practice, we need to estimate

E_{\zeta,\mathbf{I}}(h^{ij}E_{l}(h_{ij})h^{lm}E_{m}u)-h^{ij}E_{l}(h_{ij})h^{lm}E_{m}E_{\zeta,\mathbf{I}}u.

By arguments similar to the above, this expression can be estimated by (
262
) in
L^{2}
. It can also be estimated
by the right hand side of (
259
). The estimate for the contribution to the commutator from the last term on the right
hand side of (
260
) is even better. Finally, the contribution from
[E_{\zeta,\mathbf{I}},a]
can be estimated by

C\|u\|_{H^{k}}+C\|u\|_{L^{\infty}}\|a\|_{H^{k}}

as well as by the right hand side of (
259
). The lemma and remark follow, keeping in mind that
\left\|h\right\|_{W^{1,\infty}}
is
bounded by below by a constant depending only on
c_{1}
and
n
.
∎

We are finally in a position to prove Theorem
85
:

Proof of Theorem
85
.

The regularity statement follows immediately from Lemma
93
. Next, applying Proposition
97
to

E_{\zeta,\mathbf{I}}u
yields

(263)

\begin{split}\left\|E_{\zeta,\mathbf{I}}u\right\|_{H^{2}}&\leq C\left\|LE_{\zeta,\mathbf{I}}u\right\|_{L^{2}}\leq C\left\|E_{\zeta,\mathbf{I}}Lu\right\|_{L^{2}}+C\left\|[L,E_{\zeta,\mathbf{I}}]u\right\|_{L^{2}},\end{split}

where
C
only depends on a cover of
\Sigma
, a partition of unity,
n
,
c_{1}
,
c_{2}
,
\|h\|_{W^{2,\infty}}
and
\|a\|_{W^{1,\infty}}
, and the
dependence on the last two quantities is polynomial. By Lemma
99
, it follows that if
|\mathbf{I}|\leq k
, then

(264)

\left\|E_{\zeta,\mathbf{I}}u\right\|_{H^{2}}\leq C\left(\left\|Lu\right\|_{H^{k}}+\left\|u\right\|_{H^{k+1}}+\left\|u\right\|_{W^{1,\infty}}\left\|h\right\|_{H^{k+1}}+\left\|u\right\|_{L^{\infty}}\left\|a\right\|_{H^{k}}\right)

for any
k\geq 0
, where
C
only depends on a cover of
\Sigma
, a partition of unity,
n
,
k
,
c_{1}
,
c_{2}
,
\{E_{i}\}
,
\zeta
,
\eta
,

\|h\|_{W^{2,\infty}}
and
\|a\|_{W^{1,\infty}}
, and the dependence on the last two quantities is polynomial. This means that

(265)

\left\|u\right\|_{H^{k+2}}\leq C\left(\left\|Lu\right\|_{H^{k}}+\left\|u\right\|_{W^{1,\infty}}\left\|h\right\|_{H^{k+1}}+\left\|u\right\|_{L^{\infty}}\left\|a\right\|_{H^{k}}+\left\|u\right\|_{H^{k+1}}\right),

where
C
only depends on
n
,
k
,
c_{1}
,
c_{2}
, a finite cover of
\Sigma
, a finite partition of unity,
\|h\|_{W^{2,\infty}}
and
\|a\|_{W^{1,\infty}}
,
the dependence
on the last two quantities being polynomial. Next, we wish to prove that (
265
) holds with the
H^{k+1}
-norm of
u
on the right hand
side replaced by the
H^{1}
norm. If
k=0
, this follows from (
265
). Assume that an estimate of this form holds for some
k\geq 0
.
Combining (
265
), with
k
replaced by
k+1
, with the inductive hypothesis proves that the inductive hypothesis is satisfied with

k
replaced by
k+1
. Thus the desired estimate holds for all
k\geq 0
. Estimating the

H^{1}
-norm of
u
appearing on the right hand side by appealing to Proposition
97
yields

(266)

\left\|u\right\|_{H^{k+2}}\leq C\left(\left\|Lu\right\|_{H^{k}}+\left\|u\right\|_{W^{1,\infty}}\left\|h\right\|_{H^{k+1}}+\left\|u\right\|_{L^{\infty}}\left\|a\right\|_{H^{k}}\right),

where
C
has the same dependence as in the case of (
265
). The estimate (
225
) follows. In order to take the step
from (
225
) to (
227
), it is sufficient to appeal to Theorem
83
.

In order to prove (
226
), note that the following interpolation estimate holds: Given integers
k
and
l
such that

0\leq l\leq k
and
k\geq 1
, there is a constant
C
, depending only on
k
,
l
,
(\Sigma,h_{\mathrm{ref}})
and a partition of unity, such that

(267)

\|\psi\|_{H^{l}}\leq C\|\psi\|_{2}^{1-l/k}\|\psi\|_{H^{k}}^{l/k}

for all
\psi\in H^{k}
on
\Sigma
. In the case of parallelisable manifolds, a proof of this statement is to be found in
[
13
, Lemma 120, p. 72]
. In
the general case, we can use a partition of unity to divide
\psi
into a finite number of parts that have compact support in sets that can be considered
to be subsets of the
n
-torus. Applying (
267
) in the case of the
n
-torus then yields (
267
) for closed manifolds that
are not parallelisable. Applying (
267
) with
l=k-1
and
\psi
replaced by
E_{\zeta,\mathbf{I}}\psi
for
|\mathbf{I}|\leq 2
yields

\|E_{\zeta,\mathbf{I}}\psi\|_{H^{k-1}}\leq C\|\psi\|_{H^{2}}^{1/k}\|\psi\|_{H^{k+2}}^{1-1/k}.

From this estimate, we deduce that

\|\psi\|_{H^{k+1}}\leq C\|\psi\|_{H^{2}}^{1/k}\|\psi\|_{H^{k+2}}^{1-1/k}.

Combining this estimate with (
266
) and Sobolev embedding yields, if
k>n/2
,

(268)

\begin{split}\left\|u\right\|_{H^{k+2}}\leq&C\left\|Lu\right\|_{H^{k}}+C(\left\|h\right\|_{H^{k+1}}+\left\|a\right\|_{H^{k}})\left\|u\right\|_{H^{k+1}}\\
\leq&C\left\|Lu\right\|_{H^{k}}+C(\left\|h\right\|_{H^{k+1}}+\left\|a\right\|_{H^{k}})\|u\|_{H^{2}}^{1/k}\|u\|_{H^{k+2}}^{1-1/k}\\
\leq&C\left\|Lu\right\|_{H^{k}}+\tfrac{1}{k}C^{k}(\left\|h\right\|_{H^{k+1}}+\left\|a\right\|_{H^{k}})^{k}\|u\|_{H^{2}}+\tfrac{k-1}{k}\|u\|_{H^{k+2}}.\end{split}

The second term on the far right hand side can be estimated by appealing to Proposition
97
. The third term can be
taken over to the left hand side. This proves (
226
).

In order to prove (
228
), note that combining (
263
) with (
259
) yields

(269)

\left\|u\right\|_{H^{k+2}}\leq C(\left\|Lu\right\|_{H^{k}}+\|u\|_{H^{k+1}}),

where
C
only depends on
n
,
k
,
c_{1}
,
c_{2}
, a finite cover of
\Sigma
, a finite partition of unity, the
C^{l_{k}+1}
-norm of
h
, where

l_{k}:={\mathrm{max}}\{k,1\}
, and the
C^{l_{k}}
-norm of
a
. At this point, an estimate analogous with (
268
) combined with
Proposition
97
yields (
228
).
∎

D.6.
Existence of solutions

Since existence of solutions to the elliptic equations of interest here is an immediate consequence of the observations made in the present
appendix, and since we did not find a good reference for the statements we need we, next, write down the relevant existence result, even though
it is standard.

Lemma 101
.

Let
(\Sigma,h_{\mathrm{ref}})
be a closed, connected and oriented Riemannian manifold of dimension
n
. Let
k>n/2+1
and assume

h\in H^{k+1}[T^{(0,2)}T\Sigma;h_{\mathrm{ref}}]
to be a Riemannian metric and
a\in H^{k}(h_{\mathrm{ref}})
to be strictly positive. Assume that
f\in L^{2}(h_{\mathrm{ref}})
. Then
there is a unique weak solution
u\in H^{1}(h_{\mathrm{ref}})
to
L[u]=\iota_{h}[f]
. Moreover, if
f\in H^{k}(h_{\mathrm{ref}})
, then
u\in H^{k+2}(h_{\mathrm{ref}})
.

Next, let
c_{1}>1
and
c_{2}>1
be constants and
(\Sigma,h_{\mathrm{ref}})
,
n
and
k
be as above. Then there are polynomials

\mathfrak{D}_{1}:\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
and
\mathfrak{D}_{2}:\mathbb{R}_{+}^{4}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
with non-negative coefficients; and a continuous function

\mathfrak{D}_{3}:\mathbb{R}_{+}^{3}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
which is monotonically increasing in each of its arguments. Here
\mathfrak{D}_{i}
,
i=1,2,3
, only depend on
c_{1}
,

c_{2}
,
n
,
k
and
(\Sigma,h_{\mathrm{ref}})
. Moreover, if
h
and
a
are as above;
c_{1}^{-1}h_{\mathrm{ref}}\leq h\leq c_{1}h_{\mathrm{ref}}
;
c_{2}^{-1}\leq a\leq c_{2}
;

f\in H^{k}(h_{\mathrm{ref}})
; and
u\in H^{k+2}(h_{\mathrm{ref}})
is the solution obtained in the first part of the lemma, then (
225
),
(
226
) and (
227
) hold.

Remark 102
.

By the assumptions and Sobolev embedding, we can assume
h
to be
C^{2}
and
a
to be
C^{1}
. In particular,
a
has a strictly positive minimum
and a finite maximum.

Remark 103
.

Due Lemma
93
, it follows that if
h
and
a
are smooth and
f\in H^{l}(h_{\mathrm{ref}})
for some
l\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, then

u\in H^{l+2}(h_{\mathrm{ref}})
. However, we are here mainly interested in the case that the coefficients are not smooth.

Remark 104
.

If
h
,
a
and
f
are all smooth, then an immediate consequence of the lemma is that
u
is smooth.

Proof.

As noted in Subsection
A.4
,
H:=H^{1}(h)
is a Hilbert space. Define
B:H\times H\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
by

B[u,v]:=\textstyle{\int}_{\Sigma}(h(\mathrm{grad}_{h}u,\mathrm{grad}_{h}v)+auv)d\mu_{h}.

By assumption, there is a constant
c_{2}>1
such that
c_{2}^{-1}\leq a\leq c_{2}
. Thus

|B[u,v]|\leq c_{2}\|u\|_{H}\|v\|_{H},\ \ \ B[u,u]\geq c_{2}^{-1}\|u\|_{H}^{2}.

Next, let
f\in L^{2}(h)
and define
F:H\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
by

F(v):=\textstyle{\int}_{\Sigma}fvd\mu_{h}.

Then
F
is a bounded linear functional. By the Lax-Milgram Theorem, see, e.g.,
[
8
, Theorem 1, p. 315–316]
, it follows that there is a
unique element
u\in H
such that
B[u,v]=F(v)
for all
v\in H
. Due to Lemma
93
, it follows that if
a
and
h
are smooth,
and
f\in H^{l}
for some
l\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, then
u\in H^{l+2}
.

Assume now that
h\in H^{k+1}[T^{(0,2)}T\Sigma;h_{\mathrm{ref}}]
is a Riemannian metric, that
a\in H^{k}(h_{\mathrm{ref}})
is strictly positive and that
f\in H^{k}(h_{\mathrm{ref}})
.
Then there are constants
c_{1}>1
and
c_{2}>1
such that

c_{1}^{-1}h_{\mathrm{ref}}\leq h\leq c_{1}h_{\mathrm{ref}}
and
c_{2}^{-1}\leq a\leq c_{2}
. Next, choose a sequence
h_{m}
of smooth Riemannian metrics converging to
h

in the
H^{k+1}
-norm and a sequence of smooth strictly positive functions
a_{m}
converging to
a
in the
H^{k}
-norm. Let
L_{m}:=-\Delta_{h_{m}}+a_{m}
. Then
there is, by the first part of the proof, functions
u_{m}\in H^{k+2}(h_{\mathrm{ref}})
such that
L_{m}u_{m}=f
. Letting
b_{i}=2c_{i}
,
i=1,2
, we can assume that

b_{1}^{-1}h_{\mathrm{ref}}\leq h_{m}\leq b_{1}h_{\mathrm{ref}},\ \ \ b_{2}^{-1}\leq a_{m}\leq b_{2}

for all
m
. Due to Theorem
85
, there is then a polynomial
\mathfrak{D}_{2}:\mathbb{R}_{+}^{4}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
with non-negative coefficients,
depending only on
b_{1}
,
b_{2}
,
n
,
k
and
(\Sigma,h_{\mathrm{ref}})
, such that

\left\|u_{m}\right\|_{H^{k+2}}\leq\mathfrak{D}_{2}[\left\|h_{m}\right\|_{W^{2,\infty}},\left\|a_{m}\right\|_{W^{1,\infty}},\left\|a_{m}\right\|_{H^{k}},\left\|h_{m}\right\|_{H^{k+1}}]\left\|f\right\|_{H^{k}}.

Since the unit ball in
H^{k}
is compact in the weak topology, there is a subsequence of
u_{m}
, which we still denote
u_{m}
, converging weakly to,
say,
u
. Moreover, due to the fact that
k>n/2+1
, Sobolev embedding and the fact that
\mathfrak{D}_{2}
is a polynomial, (
226
) holds.
Next, due to the Rellich-Kondrakhov theorem, Theorem
72
, and Remark
73
there is a
subsequence of
u_{m}
, which we still denote
u_{m}
, converging in
H^{k+1}
. This means that
u
is a solution to
Lu=f
. Moreover, combining the
above observations with Theorem
85
, we conclude that there is then a polynomial
\mathfrak{D}_{1}:\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
with
non-negative coefficients, depending only on
b_{1}
,
b_{2}
,
n
,
k
and
(\Sigma,h_{\mathrm{ref}})
, such that (
225
) holds.
Next, note that
u\in C^{3}
and
f\in C^{1}
. Moreover, due to Theorem
85
, there is a continuous function

\mathfrak{D}_{3}:\mathbb{R}_{+}^{3}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
, depending only on
b_{1}
,
b_{2}
,
n
,
k
and
(\Sigma,h_{\mathrm{ref}})
, such that (
227
) holds.
∎

Next, we prove that there is a complete set of eigenfunctions of the Laplacian.

Theorem 105
.

Let
(M,h)
be a closed, connected and oriented Riemannian manifold of dimension
n
. Then each eigenvalue of
-\Delta_{h}
is real and has finite
multiplicity. Moreover, repeating each eigenvalue according to its multiplicity, the set of eigenvalues is countably infinite and can be ordered

0=\lambda_{0}<\lambda_{1}\leq\lambda_{2}\leq\dots.

In addition,
\lambda_{k}\rightarrow\infty
as
k\rightarrow\infty
. Finally, there is an orthonormal basis
\{u_{k}\}_{k=0}^{\infty}
of
L^{2}(h)
,
where
u_{0}
is constant;
u_{k}\in H^{1}_{0}(h)
for
k\geq 1
; and
u_{k}
is an eigenfunction of
-\Delta_{h}
corresponding to
\lambda_{k}
.

Remark 106
.

Due to Lemma
93
, the eigenfunctions
u_{k}
are smooth.

Proof.

Due to Lemma
101
, we can solve the equation
-\Delta_{h}u+u=f
for
f\in L^{2}(h)
. Then

(270)

\textstyle{\int}_{M}[h(\mathrm{grad}_{h}v,\mathrm{grad}_{h}u)+uv]d\mu_{h}=\textstyle{\int}_{M}fvd\mu_{h}

for all
v\in H^{1}(h)
. In particular, we can define an operator
S:L^{2}(h)\rightarrow H^{1}(h)
taking
f
to the solution
u
to

-\Delta_{h}u+u=f
. Due to (
270
) with
v=u
and the Cauchy-Schwartz inequality, it is clear that
S
is
continuous. If we consider
S
to be an operator from
L^{2}(h)
to itself, it follows from Theorem
72

and Remark
73
that
S
is a bounded, linear and compact operator from
L^{2}(h)
to itself.
In order to prove that
S
is symmetric, let
f,g\in L^{2}(h)
;
u:=Sf
; and
v:=Sg
. Then

\textstyle{\int}_{M}Sf\cdot gd\mu_{h}=\textstyle{\int}_{M}u\cdot gd\mu_{h}=\textstyle{\int}_{M}[h(\mathrm{grad}_{h}v,\mathrm{grad}_{h}u)+uv]d\mu_{h}

and

\textstyle{\int}_{M}f\cdot Sgd\mu_{h}=\textstyle{\int}_{M}f\cdot vd\mu_{h}=\textstyle{\int}_{M}[h(\mathrm{grad}_{h}v,\mathrm{grad}_{h}u)+uv]d\mu_{h}.

Thus
S
is symmetric. Finally,
(Sf,f)\geq 0
, where
(\cdot,\cdot)
denotes the
L^{2}
-inner product. At this point we can, just as in
the proof of
[
8
, Theorem 1, p. 355]
, appeal to
[
8
, §D.6]
in order to obtain the desired conclusion. More specifically, note
that
\mathcal{H}:=L^{2}(h)
is an infinite dimensional separable Hilbert space. Due to
[
8
, Theorem 7, p. 728]
, there is a countable orthonormal
basis of
\mathcal{H}
consisting of eigenvectors of
S
. Let, as in the proof of
[
8
, Theorem 7, p. 728]
,
\{\eta_{k}\}
denote the sequence of distinct
eigenvalues of
S
, excepting
0
. On the other hand, if
Sf=\mu f
for some
\mu\in\mathbb{R}
and
f\in L^{2}(h)
, then, if
u:=Sf
,

(271)

\|Sf\|_{L^{2}(h)}^{2}\leq\|u\|_{H^{1}(h)}^{2}=(Sf,f)=\mu(f,f).

In particular,
\mu\geq 0
and if
\mu=0
, then
u=0
, so that
f=0
. Thus
0
is not an eigenvalue of
S
. If
\mu>0
, then
f=\mu^{-1}Sf
and,
since
f\neq 0

for an eigenvector, (
271
) yields the conclusion that
\mu\leq 1
. In other words, if
\mu
is an eigenvalue of
S
, it has
to satisfy
0<\mu\leq 1
. Next, let
\mathcal{H}_{k}:=N(S-\eta_{k}I)
denote the null space
of
S-\eta_{k}I
. Then
0<\dim\mathcal{H}_{k}<\infty
; see the proof of
[
8
, Theorem 7, p. 728]
. There are thus infinitely many eigenvalues and we
can order them
1\geq\eta_{1}>\eta_{2}>\cdots>0
. Due to
[
8
, Theorem 6, p. 727]
,
\eta_{k}
is a sequence tending to
0
. On the other hand,

\eta_{k}>0
is an eigenvalue of
S
if and only if
1/\eta_{k}-1
is an eigenvalue of
-\Delta_{h}
(and
u
is an eigenvector of
S
if and only if it
is an eigenvector of
-\Delta_{h}
). Note also that
-\Delta_{h}
only has one unit eigenfunction with zero eigenvalue. The theorem follows.
∎

Appendix E
A spectral definition of Sobolev spaces

Next, we give a spectral definition of Sobolev spaces and verify that this definition gives rise to equivalent norms in the case of non-negative
integer Sobolev exponents.

E.1.
A spectral definition of Sobolev spaces

Let
(\Sigma,h)
be a closed, connected and oriented Riemannian manifold of dimension
n
. Then
\Delta_{h}
has eigenvalues
\{-\lambda_{i}^{2}\}
,
where
0=\lambda_{0}<\lambda_{1}\leq\dots
, and a corresponding complete set of orthonormal eigenfunctions
\varphi_{i}
,
i=0,1,\dots
, with respect to
the
L^{2}(h)
-inner product; see Theorem
105
. Moreover, the
\varphi_{i}
are smooth; see Remark
106
.

We define a distribution on
\Sigma
to be a map from
C^{\infty}(\Sigma)
to
\mathbb{R}
, say
u
, such that there is a
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
with the
property that

|u(\psi)|\leq C\|\psi\|_{C^{k}(h)}

for all
\psi\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma)
. We denote the set of distributions on
\Sigma
by
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Sigma)
. We define the set of
\mathbb{R}^{l}
-valued
distributions similarly, and we denote them by
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Sigma,\mathbb{R}^{l})
. Given
u\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Sigma,\mathbb{R}^{l})
and
i\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, define

(272)

\hat{u}(i):=u(\varphi_{i}).

Let
s\in\mathbb{R}
. We say that
u\in H_{(s)}(h;\mathbb{R}^{l})
if
u\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Sigma,\mathbb{R}^{l})
and

\textstyle{\sum}_{i=0}^{\infty}\langle\lambda_{i}\rangle^{2s}|\hat{u}(i)|^{2}<\infty.

Moreover, we use the notation
H_{(s)}(h):=H_{(s)}(h;\mathbb{R})
and

(273)

\|u\|_{(s)}:=\left(\textstyle{\sum}_{i=0}^{\infty}\langle\lambda_{i}\rangle^{2s}|\hat{u}(i)|^{2}\right)^{1/2}.

Next, note that, since
\{\varphi_{i}\}
is complete, if
u,v\in L^{2}(h)
, then

(274)

(u,v)_{L^{2}(h)}:=\textstyle{\int}_{\Sigma}u\cdot vd\mu_{h}=\textstyle{\sum}_{i=0}^{\infty}\hat{u}(i)\cdot\hat{v}(i),

where the dot denotes the standard Euclidean inner product. This inner product naturally generalises to an inner product on
H_{(s)}(h;\mathbb{R}^{l})
:

(u,v)_{(s)}:=\textstyle{\sum}_{i=0}^{\infty}\langle\lambda_{i}\rangle^{2s}\hat{u}(i)\cdot\hat{v}(i).

Next, note that if
u\in L^{2}(h)
, then we can think of
u
as an element of
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Sigma)
via

u(\phi):=\textstyle{\int}_{\Sigma}\phi ud\mu_{h}.

Moreover, if
u\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma)
,
i,k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
u_{k}:=(-\Delta_{h}+1)^{k}u
, then, by partial integration,

\hat{u}_{k}(i)=\langle\lambda_{i}\rangle^{2k}\hat{u}(i)
. Thus, given
s\in\mathbb{R}
and
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
such that
s\leq 2k
,

(275)

\textstyle{\sum}_{i=0}^{\infty}\langle\lambda_{i}\rangle^{2s}|\hat{u}(i)|^{2}\leq\textstyle{\sum}_{i=0}^{\infty}\langle\lambda_{i}\rangle^{4k}|\hat{u}(i)|^{2}=\textstyle{\sum}_{i=0}^{\infty}|\hat{u}_{k}(i)|^{2}=\|u_{k}\|_{L^{2}(h)}^{2}<\infty.

In particular,
C^{\infty}(\Sigma)\subset H_{(s)}(h)
. Next, let
s\in\mathbb{R}
and
\alpha_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{l}
for
i\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
be such that

\left(\textstyle{\sum}_{i=0}^{\infty}\langle\lambda_{i}\rangle^{2s}|\alpha_{i}|^{2}\right)^{1/2}<\infty.

We then wish to prove that there is a
u\in H_{(s)}(h;\mathbb{R}^{l})
such that
\hat{u}(i)=\alpha_{i}
. Define, for
\psi\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma)
,

u(\psi):=\textstyle{\sum}_{i}\hat{\psi}(i)\alpha_{i}.

Given a
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
such that
-s\leq 2k
, Hölder’s inequality then yields

\begin{split}|u(\psi)|\leq&\left(\textstyle{\sum}_{i=0}^{\infty}\langle\lambda_{i}\rangle^{2s}|\alpha_{i}|^{2}\right)^{1/2}\left(\textstyle{\sum}_{i=0}^{\infty}\langle\lambda_{i}\rangle^{-2s}|\hat{\psi}(i)|^{2}\right)^{1/2}\\
\leq&\left(\textstyle{\sum}_{i=0}^{\infty}\langle\lambda_{i}\rangle^{2s}|\alpha_{i}|^{2}\right)^{1/2}\|(-\Delta_{h}+1)^{k}\psi\|_{L^{2}(h)},\end{split}

where we appealed to an estimate analogous to (
275
) in the last step. This means that
u\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\Sigma,\mathbb{R}^{l})
.
Moreover,
\hat{u}(i)=\alpha_{i}
, so that
u\in H_{(s)}(h;\mathbb{R}^{l})
. Due to the completeness of
\ell^{2}
, we conclude that
H_{(s)}(h;\mathbb{R}^{l})

is a Hilbert space. Note also that if
u\in H_{(s)}(h;\mathbb{R}^{l})
and

u_{k}:=\textstyle{\sum}_{i=0}^{k}\hat{u}(i)\varphi_{i}

for
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, then
u_{k}\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma,\mathbb{R}^{l})
and
\|u_{k}-u\|_{(s)}\rightarrow 0
as
k\rightarrow\infty
. Thus

C^{\infty}(\Sigma,\mathbb{R}^{l})
is dense in
H_{(s)}(h;\mathbb{R}^{l})
. Next, note that the pairing (
274
) can be generalised to
a pairing

\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle:H_{(s)}(h;\mathbb{R}^{l})\times H_{(-s)}(h;\mathbb{R}^{l})\rightarrow\mathbb{R},

defined, for
u\in H_{(s)}(h;\mathbb{R}^{l})
and
v\in H_{(-s)}(h;\mathbb{R}^{l})
, by

\langle u,v\rangle:=\textstyle{\sum}_{i=0}^{\infty}\hat{u}(i)\cdot\hat{v}(i),

where
\cdot
denotes the standard Euclidean inner product; due to Hölder’s inequality

|\langle u,v\rangle|\leq\|u\|_{(s)}\|v\|_{(-s)}.

Interpolation.
Say that
u\in H_{(s_{0})}(h;\mathbb{R}^{l})\cap H_{(s_{1})}(h;\mathbb{R}^{l})
, where
s_{0}<s_{1}
. Assume, moreover, that
s\in(s_{0},s_{1})
.
Then there is a
\tau\in(0,1)
such that
s=\tau s_{1}+(1-\tau)s_{0}
. Let
p:=1/\tau
and
q=1/(1-\tau)
. Then
p,q>1
and
1/p+1/q=1
. Appealing to
Hölder’s inequality then yields

\|u\|_{(s)}^{2}=\textstyle{\sum}_{i=0}^{\infty}\langle\lambda_{i}\rangle^{2\tau s_{1}}|\hat{u}(i)|^{2\tau}\langle\lambda_{i}\rangle^{2(1-\tau)s_{0}}|\hat{u}(i)|^{2(1-\tau)}\leq\|u\|_{(s_{1})}^{2\tau}\|u\|_{(s_{0})}^{2(1-\tau)}.

In particular,

(276)

\|u\|_{(s)}\leq\|u\|_{(s_{1})}^{\tau}\|u\|_{(s_{0})}^{1-\tau}.

The dual space.
Assume that
f
is an element of the dual of
H_{(s)}(h)
and let
\|f\|
denote the norm of
f
considered as a bounded
linear operator from
H_{(s)}(h)
to
\mathbb{R}
. Let, given
N\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,

\alpha_{i}:=\langle\lambda_{i}\rangle^{-2s}\hat{f}(i),\ \ \ \psi_{N}:=\textstyle{\sum}_{i=0}^{N}\alpha_{i}\varphi_{i};

since
C^{\infty}(\Sigma)\subset H_{(s)}(h)
, we can define
\hat{f}(i)
by (
272
). By definition,
|f(\psi_{N})|\leq\|f\|\cdot\|\psi_{N}\|_{(s)}
.
Moreover,

f(\psi_{N})=\textstyle{\sum}_{i=0}^{N}\langle\lambda_{i}\rangle^{2s}\alpha_{i}^{2},\ \ \ \|\psi_{N}\|_{(s)}=\big(\sum_{i=0}^{N}\langle\lambda_{i}\rangle^{2s}\alpha_{i}^{2}\big)^{1/2}.

Combining these observations yields
\|\psi_{N}\|_{(s)}\leq\|f\|
for all
N\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. This means that

\textstyle{\sum}_{i}\langle\lambda_{i}\rangle^{-2s}|\hat{f}(i)|^{2}\leq\|f\|^{2}<\infty,

so that
\chi:=\textstyle{\sum}_{i=0}^{\infty}\hat{f}(i)\varphi_{i}
defines an element of
H_{(-s)}(h)
with
\|\chi\|_{(-s)}\leq\|f\|
. Moreover, we can
associate an element
f_{\chi}
in the dual of
H_{(s)}(h)
with
\chi
; it is given by

(277)

f_{\chi}(\phi):=\langle\phi,\chi\rangle=\textstyle{\sum}_{i=0}^{\infty}\hat{\chi}(i)\hat{\phi}(i)=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\hat{f}(i)\hat{\phi}(i)=f(\phi).

Note also that
\|f_{\chi}\|\leq\|\chi\|_{(-s)}
. In other words, the dual of
H_{(s)}(h)
can be identified with
H_{(-s)}(h)
.

In case
f
is an element of the dual of
H_{(s)}(h;\mathbb{R}^{k})
, define
f_{j}
in the dual of
H_{(s)}(h)
by
f_{j}(\phi):=f(\phi e_{j})
, where

e_{j}
is the
j
’th element of the standard basis of
\mathbb{R}^{k}
. By the above observations, there is then a
\chi_{j}\in H_{(-s)}(h)
such
that
f_{j}(\phi)=\langle\phi,\chi_{j}\rangle
. This means that

(278)

f(\phi)=f\big(\textstyle{\sum}_{j}\phi_{j}e_{j}\big)=\textstyle{\sum}_{j}f_{j}(\phi_{j})=\textstyle{\sum}_{j}\langle\phi_{j},\chi_{j}\rangle=\textstyle{\sum}_{i}\hat{\chi}(i)\cdot\hat{\phi}(i)=:\langle\phi,\chi\rangle,

where
\chi:=\sum_{j}\chi_{j}e_{j}\in H_{(-s)}(h;\mathbb{R}^{k})
.

E.2.
Relating different definitions of Sobolev spaces

Let
(\Sigma,h)
be a closed, connected and oriented Riemannian manifold of dimension
n
. Above, we define the norm associated with the Sobolev
space
H_{(s)}(h)
and in Subsection
A.4
, we introduce the norm
\|\cdot\|_{H^{m}(h)}
. It is of interest to relate the two
when
s=m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. Note, to this end, that for
\psi\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma)
,

\langle(-\Delta_{h}+1)^{m}\psi,\varphi_{i}\rangle=\langle\lambda_{i}\rangle^{2m}\hat{\psi}(i).

This means that

\|\psi\|_{(m)}^{2}=\textstyle{\sum}_{i}\langle\lambda_{i}\rangle^{2m}|\hat{\psi}(i)|^{2}=\langle(-\Delta_{h}+1)^{m}\psi,\psi\rangle.

If
m
is even, we can integrate by parts
m/2
times in order to obtain
\|\psi\|_{(m)}\leq C\|\psi\|_{H^{m}(h)}
for a constant
C
depending
only on
m
and
(\Sigma,h)
. In case
m=2k+1
, we can integrate by parts
k
times in order to obtain

(279)

\begin{split}\|\psi\|_{(m)}^{2}=&\textstyle{\int}_{\Sigma}(-\Delta_{h}+1)^{k+1}\psi\cdot(-\Delta_{h}+1)^{k}\psi{\mathrm{Vol}}_{h}\\
=&\textstyle{\int}_{\Sigma}(-\Delta_{h}+1)^{k}\psi\cdot(-\Delta_{h}+1)^{k}\psi{\mathrm{Vol}}_{h}+\textstyle{\int}_{\Sigma}|\nabla^{h}(-\Delta_{h}+1)^{k}\psi|^{2}{\mathrm{Vol}}_{h}.\end{split}

Again, we obtain the desired estimate:

\|\psi\|_{(m)}\leq C\|\psi\|_{H^{m}(h)}

for a constant
C
depending only on
m
and
(\Sigma,h)
. Turning to the opposite estimate, if
m=2k
, we can appeal to (
228
)
k

times. This yields

(280)

\|\psi\|_{H^{m}(h)}\leq C\|(-\Delta_{h}+1)^{k}\psi\|_{2}\leq C\|\psi\|_{(m)}

for a constant
C
depending only on
m
and
(\Sigma,h)
. Assume now that
m=2k+1
. Then, due to (
280
),

(281)

\|\psi\|_{H^{2k}(h)}\leq C\|\psi\|_{(2k)}\leq C\|\psi\|_{(m)}

for a constant
C
depending only on
m
and
(\Sigma,h)
. Next, let
U
,
\{E_{i}\}
,
\zeta
and
K
be as described at the beginning
of Subsection
D.5
. Then, due to (
280
),

\begin{split}\|E_{\zeta,i}\psi\|_{H^{2k}(h)}\leq&C\|E_{\zeta,i}(-\Delta_{h}+1)^{k}\psi\|_{2}+C\|[(-\Delta_{h}+1)^{k},E_{\zeta,i}]\psi\|_{2}\\
\leq&C\|\nabla^{h}(-\Delta_{h}+1)^{k}\psi\|_{2}+C\|\psi\|_{H^{2k}(h)}.\end{split}

Combining this estimate with (
279
) and (
281
