) yields the conclusion that

\|\psi\|_{H^{m}(h)}\leq C\|\psi\|_{(m)}
holds when
m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
is odd. Combining the above estimates with a density argument yields the conclusion
that, given
m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, there is a constant
C_{m}>1
depending only on
m
and
(\Sigma,h)
such that

(282)

C_{m}^{-1}\|\psi\|_{H^{m}(h)}\leq\|\psi\|_{(m)}\leq C_{m}\|\psi\|_{H^{m}(h)}

for all
\psi\in H^{m}(h)
.

Appendix F
Smooth dependence on coefficients, elliptic model equation

Let
(\Sigma,h_{\mathrm{ref}})
be a closed, connected and oriented Riemannian manifold of dimension
n
. In what follows, we denote the set of elements in

H^{k}[T^{(0,m)}T\Sigma;h_{\mathrm{ref}}]
that take their values in the set of symmetric covariant
m
-tensor fields by
H^{k}[S^{(0,m)}T\Sigma;h_{\mathrm{ref}}]
.

Theorem 107
.

Let
(\Sigma,h_{\mathrm{ref}})
be a closed, connected and oriented Riemannian manifold of dimension
n
. Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
satisfy
k>n/2+1
; let

h_{0}\in H^{k+1}[S^{(0,2)}T\Sigma;h_{\mathrm{ref}}]
be a Riemannian metric; let
a_{0}\in H^{k}(h_{\mathrm{ref}})
be a strictly positive function; and let

f_{0}\in H^{k}(h_{\mathrm{ref}})
. Then there is an open subset

{\mathcal{U}}\subseteq H^{k+1}[S^{(0,2)}T\Sigma;h_{\mathrm{ref}}]\oplus H^{k}(h_{\mathrm{ref}})\oplus H^{k}(h_{\mathrm{ref}})

with
(h_{0},a_{0},f_{0})\in{\mathcal{U}}
, and a smooth map
\Phi:{\mathcal{U}}\to H^{k+2}(h_{\mathrm{ref}})
such that

\left(-\Delta_{h}+a\right)\Phi(h,a,f)=f.

Remark 108
.

Since, due to Lemma
101
,
\Phi
is well defined on the set of all

(h,a,f)\in H^{k+1}[S^{(0,2)}T\Sigma;h_{\mathrm{ref}}]\oplus H^{k}(h_{\mathrm{ref}})\oplus H^{k}(h_{\mathrm{ref}})

such that
h
is a Riemannian metric and
a
is strictly positive, we can assume
{\mathcal{U}}
to coincide with this set.

Theorem
107
implies for example the following:

Corollary 109
.

Let
(\Sigma,h_{\mathrm{ref}})
be a closed, connected and oriented Riemannian manifold of dimension
n
. Let
k
be an integer such that
k>n/2+1
, let

m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, let
t_{1}<t_{2}
be real numbers and let

\displaystyle\gamma:(t_{1},t_{2})

\displaystyle\to H^{k+1}[S^{(0,2)}T\Sigma;h_{\mathrm{ref}}]\oplus H^{k}(h_{\mathrm{ref}})\oplus H^{k}(h_{\mathrm{ref}})

be
C^{m}
. Assume, moreover, that if
\gamma(t)=(h_{t},a_{t},f_{t})
, then
h_{t}
is positive definite and
a_{t}
is strictly positive for all

t\in(t_{1},t_{2})
. Let
\lambda:(t_{1},t_{2})\to H^{k+2}(\Sigma)
, be defined as follows: for
t\in(t_{1},t_{2})
,
\lambda(t)
is the unique solution

v
to
\left(-\Delta_{h_{t}}+a_{t}\right)v=f_{t}
. Then
\lambda
is
C^{m}
.

Proof of Corollary
109
, assuming Theorem
107
.

That
\lambda
is well defined follows from Lemma
101
. Given any
\tau\in(t_{1},t_{2})
, the map
\Phi
in
Theorem
107
is smooth near
(h_{\tau},a_{\tau},f_{\tau})
, so the map

\displaystyle(\tau-\delta,\tau+\delta)

\displaystyle\to H^{k+2}(\Sigma);\ \ \ t\mapsto\lambda(t)=\Phi(h_{t},a_{t},f_{t}),

is of class
C^{m}
for some small
\delta=\delta(\tau)>0
. Since this is true for all
\tau\in(t_{1},t_{2})
, the desired statement follows.
∎

The main ingredient in the proof of Theorem
107
is the following:

Lemma 110

(The implicit function theorem in Banach spaces, see
[
1
]
, Theorem 2.5.7, p. 121)
.

Let
\mathrm{E}
,
\mathrm{F}
,
\mathrm{G}
be Banach spaces and let
{\mathcal{U}}\subset\mathrm{E}
,
{\mathcal{V}}\subset\mathrm{F}
be open subsets.
Let
\Psi:{\mathcal{U}}\times{\mathcal{V}}\to\mathrm{G}
be of class
C^{r}
for some
r\geq 1
.
For some
x_{0}\in{\mathcal{U}}
,
y_{0}\in{\mathcal{V}}
, assume that

(283)

D_{2}\Psi(x_{0},y_{0}):\mathrm{F}\to\mathrm{G}

is an isomorphism. Then there are neighborhoods
{\mathcal{U}}_{0}
of
x_{0}
and
{\mathcal{W}}_{0}
of
\Psi(x_{0},y_{0})
and a unique
C^{r}
map
\Phi:{\mathcal{U}}_{0}\times{\mathcal{W}}_{0}\to{\mathcal{V}}
,
such that for all
(x,w)\in{\mathcal{U}}_{0}\times{\mathcal{W}}_{0}
, the equality
\Psi(x,\Phi(x,w))=w
holds.

Proof of Theorem
107
.

The proof consists of an application of Lemma
110
. We begin by choosing

\displaystyle\mathrm{E}:=H^{k+1}[S^{(0,2)}T\Sigma;h_{\mathrm{ref}}]\oplus H^{k}(h_{\mathrm{ref}}),\ \ \ \mathrm{F}:=H^{k+2}(h_{\mathrm{ref}}),\ \ \ \mathrm{G}:=H^{k}(h_{\mathrm{ref}}).

We continue by choosing
{\mathcal{V}}=\mathrm{F}
and
{\mathcal{U}}
to be the set of
(h,a)\in\mathrm{E}
such that
h
is a Riemannian metric and
a
is strictly
positive. Let us now define the map
\Psi:{\mathcal{U}}\times{\mathcal{V}}\to\mathrm{G}
by

\Psi((h,a),v):=\left(-\Delta_{h}+a\right)v.

We leave it to the reader to verify that
\Psi
is a smooth map. Next, let
x_{0}=(h_{0},a_{0})\in{\mathcal{U}}
and
y_{0}:=v_{0}\in{\mathcal{V}}
, where
v_{0}
is
the unique solution to

\left(-\Delta_{h_{0}}+a_{0}\right)v_{0}=f_{0};

see Lemma
101
. Compute

D_{2}\Psi(x_{0},y_{0})(v)=\left(-\Delta_{h_{0}}+a_{0}\right)v.

Thus
D_{2}\Psi(x_{0},y_{0}):\mathrm{F}\to\mathrm{G}
is an isomorphism due to Lemma
101
.
The implicit function theorem, Lemma
110
, gives a smooth map

\Phi:{\mathcal{U}}_{0}\times{\mathcal{W}}_{0}\subset\mathrm{E}\oplus\mathrm{G}\to\mathrm{F},

where
{\mathcal{U}}_{0}
is an open neighborhood of
x_{0}=(h_{0},a_{0})
and
{\mathcal{W}}_{0}
is an open neighborhood of
\Psi(x_{0},y_{0})=f_{0}
, such that

\Psi((h,a),\Phi((h,a),f))=f,

for all
(h,a)\in{\mathcal{U}}_{0}
and
f\in{\mathcal{W}}_{0}
. In other words,
\Phi
takes
((h,a),f)
to the unique solution
v
to

\left(-\Delta_{h}+a\right)v=f.

This completes the proof.
∎
